Entanglement in von Neumann algebraic quantum information theory
Lauritz van Luijk

Abstract




Entanglement in von Neumann algebraic

quantum information theory

Von der Fakultät für Mathematik und Physik

der Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz Universität Hannover zur Erlangung des Grades

Doktor der Naturwissenschaften

Dr. rer. nat.

genehmigte Dissertation von

Lauritz van Luijk

2025

Abstract

In quantum systems with infinitely many degrees of freedom, states can be infinitely entangled across a pair of subsystems. But are there
different
forms of infinite entanglement? To address this, we use a rigorous framework for studying information-theoretic properties in infinite systems, formulated in terms of von Neumann algebras. We show that there are operational tasks that distinguish different forms of infinite entanglement. In fact, we find that the classification of von Neumann factors into types and subtypes is in one-to-one correspondence with a family of operational entanglement properties. For instance, Connes’ classification of type III factors corresponds to the smallest achievable error when
“embezzling”
entanglement from the system. Our findings promote the type classification from algebraic bookkeeping to a classification of infinite quantum systems based on their operational entanglement properties.

Contents

1
Introduction

Quantum entanglement lies at the heart of quantum information theory, underpinning a range of phenomena from teleportation to quantum cryptography and quantum computation
[
10
,
11
,
12
,
13
]
.
While the structure and manipulation of entanglement are well understood in finite systems, many foundational and physically relevant settings involve infinitely many degrees of freedom.

In systems with infinitely many degrees of freedom, states can be infinitely entangled across a pair of subsystems.
Examples range from the vacua of relativistic quantum field theories
[
14
,
15
,
16
]
to ground states in infinite spin chains
[
17
]
.
But does that mean that these states are all the same from the point of view of entanglement theory?
More specifically:

Are there different kinds of infinite entanglement?

If so, can we distinguish them operationally?

The natural mathematical framework for modeling systems with infinitely many degrees of freedom is that of von Neumann algebras
[
18
,
19
,
16
,
20
,
21
]
.
The study of quantum entanglement in the von Neumann algebraic setting was initiated in the seminal works of Summers and Werner on the violation of Bell’s inequalities in quantum field theory
[
15
,
22
,
23
,
24
,
25
]
.
While several aspects have been considered since then
[
16
,
26
,
27
,
28
,
29
,
30
,
31
,
32
]
, there is, to this point, no systematic study of entanglement theory in the von Neumann algebraic setting.
This work aims to fill this gap.
In doing so, we will discover tight connections between operational entanglement properties and the classification theory for von Neumann algebras.

This thesis is structured as follows:

Section

2
summarizes the setup and the main results of our work;
section

3
collects the necessary mathematical prerequisites on von Neumann algebras;
section

4
explains the operational setup of von Neumann algebraic quantum information theory;
section

5
studies catalytic states of von Neumann algebras, the monopartite analog of embezzlement of entanglement;
section

6
collects the basic structure results for bipartite systems.
Finally, with all preparations out of the way,
sections

7
and
8
study entanglement theory in von Neumann algebraic bipartite systems.

2
Main results

In this section, we give an overview of the results obtained in this work.
We focus on conceptual ideas, the technical details fill the sections to come.
We start off with the setup of von Neumann algebraic quantum information theory, before turning to the central focus of this work: entanglement theory.

Setup.

Let us consider an agent with partial access to a quantum system, potentially having infinitely many degrees of freedom.
This means that the agent can implement a subset
{\mathcal{O}}
of the set of all operations on the full system.
The prototypical example is that of an agent who can solely act on a
subsystem
of the full system.
On purely operational grounds, a subsystem is not more than the set of operations that act on it.

The basic assumption underlying von Neumann algebraic quantum information theory is that the agent’s operations are described by a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
, called the
observable algebra
, on the Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
of the full system.
The operations that the agent can implement are those whose Kraus operators are elements of
{\mathcal{M}}
, or equivalently, those that can be implemented by preparing an ancillary system
{\mathcal{K}}
, applying a unitary from the amplified observable algebra
{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})
, and then discarding/measuring the ancilla.
We refer to this class of operations as the
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner
operations, denoted
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M}}
.

This assumption applies to agents who can implement operations that interact solely with specific subsystems of a full system, even if the latter has infinitely many degrees of freedom.
More precisely, the von Neumann algebraic description applies when one considers subsystems in a fixed sector, e.g., the ground state sector of a local Hamiltonian on a spin system or the vacuum sector of a field theory, of the full system
[
19
,
20
,
17
,
16
,
21
,
27
,
18
]
.
We discuss these settings in
section

4.9
.

In order to further justify the assumption, we consider a set of operational axioms.
Apart from the obvious consistency assumption that the composition of implementable operations is implementable, we assume the following:

1.

Limits: The agent’s operations are topologically closed.

2.

Ancillas: The agent can freely prepare, manipulate, and discard ancillary systems.

3.

Dilations:
Each of the agent’s channels can be implemented by preparing an ancillary system, performing a global unitary, and discarding the ancilla.

4.

Reversibility: For every unitary channel, the agent can also implement the inverse.

5.

Postselection: An instrument that could be implemented by postselecting outcomes of implementable instruments is implementable.

If we identify agents with the subsystems they act on, these axioms form an operational approach to the question:
What is a subsystem?

Axioms
3
and
4
correspond to the idea that the only source of irreversibility is the loss of information, see, e.g.,
[
33
,
34
]
.
We refer to
section

4
for a more detailed discussion and mathematically precise versions of these axioms, explaining, e.g., the topology in
1
.

If
{\mathcal{M}}
is a von Neumann algebra on
{\mathcal{H}}
, then the set
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M}}
of
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner operations satisfies the axioms
1
to
5
.
In fact, we show the converse:

Theorem A

(See
section

4.3
)
.

The axioms above hold if and only if there is a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
on
{\mathcal{H}}
such that the agent’s operations are exactly the
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner ones.

A
, shows that the mathematical assumption that the agent’s operations are the
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner ones is, in fact, a fully operational assumption in disguise.

Apart from these axioms, there are other reasons to consider the setting of operations determined by a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
on a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
.
Motivations come from symmetry considerations, e.g., in the setting of (quantum) reference frames.
Moreover, the von Neumann algebraic setting can always be regarded as an idealization: For every collection
{\mathcal{O}}
of operations on the full system, there is a unique smallest von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
such that
{\mathcal{O}}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M}}
(see
section

4
).

For a good understanding of von Neumann algebraic quantum information theory, we need three main ingredients:

1.

extending ideas and results from finite-dimensional quantum information theory to the von Neumann algebraic setting;

2.

understanding the relation between mathematical (von Neumann algebraic) properties of the observable algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
and operational properties of the agent;

3.

knowing the von Neumann algebraic properties of the observable algebras in many concrete physical models.

With a good understanding of each of these points, we can understand the operational properties of concrete physical models and recognize the ultimate limits of how quantum information can be manipulated in systems with infinitely many degrees of freedom.
What makes the latter interesting is that there are quantitatively and qualitatively new information-theoretic properties in systems with infinitely many degrees of freedom, e.g., the phenomenon of ‘embezzlement of entanglement’ (see below).
For each of the three aspects, there is important previous work, e.g.,
[
35
,
16
,
36
,
37
,
32
,
30
,
27
,
38
]
for
1
,
[
24
,
15
,
39
,
27
,
40
,
41
]
for
2
, and
[
19
,
18
,
42
,
43
]
for
3
to name a few.
The main focus of this work is on item
2
above. We will show that the type classification of von Neumann algebras is in one-to-one correspondence with operational properties in entanglement theory.

A priori, it is not clear that there should be any interesting correspondence between mathematical properties of the observable algebras on the one hand and operational properties on the other hand.
In finite dimensions, interesting operational properties are always properties of individual states, not properties of the whole system.
In a way, this is because all finite-dimensional systems are
“the same”
up to the dimension of the Hilbert space.
In the general case, however, the rich mathematical structure of von Neumann algebras creates a vast zoo of non-equivalent setups, and item
2
above essentially asks to what degree they can be classified in terms of their operational properties.
Roughly speaking, the
type classification
of von Neumann algebras takes over the role of the dimension in the usual setup of quantum information theory.
General von Neumann algebras can be decomposed into
factors
, i.e., von Neumann algebras with trivial center
{\mathcal{M}}\cap{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}={\mathbb{C}}1
, where
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}=\{a^{\prime}:[a,a^{\prime}]=0\ \forall a\in{\mathcal{M}}\}
denotes the
commutant
.
Factors are classified into types
{\mathrm{I}}
,
{\mathrm{II}}
, and
{\mathrm{III}}
, each further divided into subtypes, see
fig.

1
.
Type
{\mathrm{I}}
factors are finite or infinite matrix algebras.
They arise in cases where only finitely many degrees of freedom contribute to the entanglement across a pair of subsystems, e.g., if the full system consists of finitely many qubits or is a system of finitely many bosonic modes.
Types
{\mathrm{II}}
and
{\mathrm{III}}
are more exotic and only arise in systems with infinitely many degrees of freedom.

Figure 1
:
The classification of factors into types
{\mathrm{I}}
,
{\mathrm{II}}
,
{\mathrm{III}}
, and their respective subtypes (see
section

3
for details).

The simplest example of a one-to-one correspondence between an operational and an algebraic property is the
no information without disturbance
principle, which states that no information can be gained about a quantum system without disturbing its state.
The principle applies to the agent’s subsystem if and only if the observable algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
has the algebraic property of being a factor.
To give a less trivial example, it was shown in
[
31
]
that counterexamples to Tsirelson’s problem in entanglement theory require so-called non-AFD observable algebras (see
section

4
).

Entanglement theory.

We now turn our attention to entanglement.
We consider a bipartite system consisting of two agents, conventionally called Alice and Bob, with commuting observable algebras
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
.
Let us consider operational axioms for bipartite systems.

1.

Tomography: States of the full system can be determined uniquely with correlation experiments.

Independently of the ‘no information without disturbance’ principle,
1
is equivalent to the statement that
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are factors that jointly generated
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
.
In general,
1
is insufficient to imply the following:

2.

Duality: Alice can implement all operations that commute with all of Bob’s operations and vice versa.

3.

Uniqueness of purifications: If two pure states of the full system have the same
A
-marginal, then they are connected by a partial isometry of
B
.

Theorem B

(see
section

6.6
)
.

The operational axioms
2
and
3
are equivalent. They hold if and only if

{\mathcal{M}}_{A}={\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}.

(2.1)

The property
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}={\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}
, stating that Alice’s observable algebra is the commutant of Bob’s, is known as
Haag duality

[
17
]
after a similar property in quantum field theory
[
19
]
.
From now on, we consider a bipartite system satisfying the axioms
1
,
2
, and
3
.
I.e., we consider a pair
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B})
of commuting factors in Haag duality.
We aim to answer the following question:

What is the relationship between pure state entanglement theory

and the algebraic properties of the bipartite system?

To warm up, let us consider the finite-dimensional case.
Here, the Hilbert space decomposes as
{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{A}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{B}
and the local observable algebras are
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}={\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{A})\otimes 1
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}=1\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{B})
.
Up to unitary equivalence, the setup is fully determined by the dimensions
(n_{A},n_{B})
of the local Hilbert spaces.
The minimal local dimension
n=\min(n_{A},n_{B})
can be determined from entanglement theory: It is the maximal Schmidt rank that a pure state of the full system can have.
Moreover, the Schmidt decomposition tells us that there is no entanglement property that a pure state can have, which is not shared by a pure state on a bipartite system with local dimensions
(n,n)
.
Therefore, the minimal local dimension completely describes the collection of entanglement properties that the pure states of the full system have.

The finite-dimensional case considered in the previous paragraph is, of course, rather boring.
The reason is that finite-dimensional quantum systems only differ in their local dimensions—a fact that dramatically changes when passing to infinite quantum systems described by von Neumann algebras.
As stated above, the appropriate generalization of the local dimension is the type classification of factors.
The classification, in particular the type
{\mathrm{III}}
case
[
44
,
45
]
, is a deep mathematical result, and we will profit greatly from the tools that have been developed in this line of research.
Generalizing the minimum local dimension, we consider the minimum of the types of
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
with respect to the following naive ordering

{\mathrm{I}}_{1}<{\mathrm{I}}_{2}<\ldots<{\mathrm{I}}_{\infty}<{\mathrm{II}}_{1}<{\mathrm{II}}_{\infty}<{\mathrm{III}}_{\lambda}<{\mathrm{III}}_{\lambda^{\prime}},\qquad 0\leq\lambda<\lambda^{\prime}\leq 1.

(2.2)

We refer to this as the
minimal
type of a bipartite system.
In general,
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
is of type
{\mathrm{I}}
,
{\mathrm{II}}
, or
{\mathrm{III}}
if and only if
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
is. Thus, we can speak of bipartite systems of type
{\mathrm{I}}
,
{\mathrm{II}}
, or
{\mathrm{III}}
.
Moreover, in the type
{\mathrm{III}}
case,
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
always have the same subtype.
We can now state the main result of this work:

Theorem C

(see
sections

8.1
and
7.5
)
.

The minimal type is in one-to-one correspondence with operational properties in entanglement theory.

To understand entanglement operationally, we need to extend the concept of local operations and classical communication (LOCC) to the von Neumann algebraic setting
[
16
]
.
After doing so, we show Nielsen’s theorem
[
46
]
for von Neumann algebraic bipartite systems, which was already generalized to the type
{\mathrm{II}}
setting in
[
30
]
.

In the following, we sketch the operational entanglement properties that are needed for
C
(see
table

1
).
The easiest is the
one-shot entanglement
.
In the type
{\mathrm{I}}
case, every state has finite one-shot entanglement in the sense that only a finite number of Bell pairs can be distilled from it at once.
This is quite different for bipartite systems of types
{\mathrm{II}}
and
{\mathrm{III}}
, where
all
states have infinite one-shot entanglement.
To distinguish the type
{\mathrm{II}}
subtypes, we consider the existence of
maximally entangled states
.
Type
{\mathrm{III}}
is singled out among the other types by the
trivialization of LOCC
, meaning that all pure states of the full system are approximately LOCC equivalent.
This provides a one-to-one correspondence between operational properties in entanglement theory and the minimal type, except for the subtypes of type
{\mathrm{III}}
.

Table 1
:
One-to-one correspondence between entanglement properties and the minimal type.
In the table, we have
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\lambda\in(0,1)
, and
f(\lambda)=2\frac{1-\sqrt{\lambda}}{1+\sqrt{\lambda}}
, which is a bijection of
[0,1]
onto
[0,2]
.
Some type
{\mathrm{III}}_{0}
systems host embezzling states (
\kappa_{\min}=0
); the others maximally fail doing so (
\kappa_{\min}=2
).

In order to distinguish these, we use the phenomenon of
embezzlement of entanglement
, discovered by van Dam and Hayden in
[
47
]
.
Consider two agents, Alice and Bob, sharing an entangled ‘resource’ state
\Phi_{AB}
, and a state
\Omega_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}
with little entanglement, which they want to transform into a more entangled state
\Psi_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}
.
However, they are only allowed to act locally, may not use classical communication, and the resource system has to return to its original state.
I.e., they want to implement the state transition

\Phi_{AB}\otimes\Omega_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}\to\Phi_{AB}\otimes\Psi_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}

(2.3)

with local unitaries, at least up to arbitrary precision.
As stated, this is, of course, impossible with finite-dimensional resource systems. In fact, it is still impossible if the resource system is a von Neumann algebraic bipartite system of types
{\mathrm{I}}_{\infty}
or
{\mathrm{II}}
.
In the type
{\mathrm{III}}
case, however, embezzlement of entanglement becomes possible.
To quantify this phenomenon, we consider the worst error up to which the resource state
\Phi_{AB}
allows embezzlement of
arbitrary
entangled states
\Psi_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}
given an arbitrary initial state
\Omega_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}
.
This quantity, denoted
\kappa(\Phi_{AB})
, is zero if and only if arbitrary entangled states can be embezzled from
\Phi_{AB}
. We refer to such states as
embezzling states
.
For resource states in bipartite systems not of type
{\mathrm{III}}
,
\kappa(\Phi_{AB})
attains its maximal value.
To discuss system properties instead of state properties, we consider the optimal values

\kappa_{\min}({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B}):=\inf_{\Phi_{AB}}\ \kappa(\Phi_{AB}),\qquad\kappa_{\max}({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B}):=\sup_{\Phi_{AB}}\ \kappa(\Phi_{AB}),

(2.4)

where the optimization runs over pure states of the full system.
For bipartite systems of type
{\mathrm{III}}_{\lambda}
factors,
0\leq\lambda\leq 1
, we have

\kappa_{\max}({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B})=2\;\!\frac{1-\lambda^{1/2}}{1+\lambda^{1/2}}.

(2.5)

The RHS of (
2.5
) is an invertible continuous function of
\lambda
.
Its inverse allows us to recover the subtype from the operational quantifier
\kappa_{\max}
.
This completes the one-to-one correspondence mentioned in
C
, which is summarized in
table

1
, which, in addition, contains the values of
\kappa_{\min}
showing that embezzling states exist for all subtypes, although they fail to exist in some type
{\mathrm{III}}_{0}
cases.

Strong forms of infinite entanglement.

One of the motivating questions for this work was whether there are interesting operationally distinct forms of infinite entanglement.
We have seen above that all pure states in bipartite systems of types
{\mathrm{II}}
and
{\mathrm{III}}
have infinite one-shot entanglement (this was first observed in
[
27
]
).
But that does not yet mean that all states in these systems have the same entanglement properties.
The latter would essentially require all bipartite pure states to be equivalent up to local unitaries (LU), at least up to arbitrary precision.
We call a bipartite system where this is the case
LU transitive
.
These are precisely the type
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}
systems:

Theorem D

(see
section

8.3
)
.

The following are equivalent:

1.

the bipartite system is LU transitive;

2.

the bipartite system is a
universal embezzler
: every pure state of the full system is embezzling (equivalently,
\kappa_{\max}({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B})=0
);

3.

{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
, and therefore
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
, are of type
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}
.

Examples of these highly entangled systems can be found in well-studied physical models.
Indeed, the observable algebras in conformal field theories and relativistic quantum field theories are generally of type
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}

[
19
,
18
]
.
Moreover, we expect the ground state sectors of critical many-body systems in 1D to yield type
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}
bipartite systems in the left-right bipartition.
This has been shown rigorously for the XY model
[
17
]
, the transverse-field Ising model
[
5
]
, and critical free fermion chains
[
5
]
.

Additionally, we show the (mathematical) existence of multipartite analogs of these systems.
This is remarkable given how hard it is to say anything substantial about multipartite entanglement.
To do this, we can take the multipartite embezzling family of Leung, Toner, and Watrous and form the restricted infinite Hilbert space tensor product.
For any number
N\geq 2
of parties, this yields a collection
({\mathcal{M}}_{x})_{x=1}^{N}
of pairwise commuting type
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}
factors, which fulfil Haag duality in every bipartition, and satisfy the
N
-partite analog of LU transitivity.
We show that LU transitivity implies that the system is a
multipartite universal embezzler
: For every pure state
\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
and every pair
\Omega,\Psi\in({\mathbb{C}}^{d})^{\otimes N}
of finite-dimensional
N
-partite pure states, the state transition
\Phi\otimes\Omega\to\Phi\otimes\Psi
can be realized with local unitaries up to arbitrary precision.
In contrast to the bipartite case, we do not know of any physical realizations of multipartite universal embezzlers.

Discussion and outlook

The results explained above allow us to fully characterize mathematical concepts such as the definition of von Neumann algebras (see
A
), Haag duality (see
B
), or the type classification of factors (see
C
) in terms of information-theoretic ones, and vice versa.
Since the former are known for the observable algebras in various models, we discover previously unknown operational properties of well-studied models.

For instance, we learn that the entanglement properties of the ground states of, say, the toric code
[
48
]
and a Levin-Wen model
[
49
]
with nontrivial quantum dimensions, are qualitatively different.
Indeed, if we partition the lattice into a cone and its complement, the former has type
{\mathrm{II}}_{\infty}

[
50
]
and, for a suitable fusion category, the latter has
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}

[
43
]
.
Hence, embezzlement of entanglement is possible if the resource system is a suitable Levin-Wen model but impossible if it is the toric code.

It was recently argued that observable algebras in semiclassical quantum gravity can be of type
{\mathrm{II}}
, in contrast to the type
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}
algebras ubiquitous in relativistic quantum field theory
[
51
]
(see also
[
52
,
53
]
).
With our results, we can interpret this type reduction from an operational entanglement point of view.
Again, the phenomenon of embezzlement of entanglement distinguishes the two cases.

It would be fascinating to further explore the correspondence between operational properties on the one hand and von Neumann algebraic properties on the other.
Concrete open problems in this direction are mentioned at several points throughout this work.

Moreover, it would be great to have a better understanding of the von Neumann algebraic properties in concrete models (see
section

4.9.1
).
Especially, Haag duality is only known to hold for a handful of cases.
For many important models, e.g., the Heisenberg antiferromagnet, neither the type nor Haag duality are known.

3
Preliminaries on von Neumann algebras

This section collects the mathematical prerequisites for this work.
We give an overview of a substantial part of the theory of von Neumann algebras, based on Refs.
[
54
,
55
,
56
,
57
]
.
This section can be skimmed through on a first read, as we will refer back to it in the later sections.
In particular, only the basic definitions are necessary to understand
section

4
, where the setup of von Neumann algebraic quantum information theory is explained.
With few exceptions, this section is kept purely mathematical. Physical relevance and interpretation are discussed in the later sections, in particular, in
section

4
.

Let us begin with a historic remark.
The theory of von Neumann algebras was initiated by von Neumann together with Murray in a series of papers
[
58
,
59
,
60
,
61
,
62
,
63
]
.
Von Neumann’s motivation was rooted in applications to quantum mechanics, whose mathematical formulation he had developed earlier
[
64
]
.
He was dissatisfied with the Hilbert space-based formulation of quantum mechanics because it was not singled out among the more general descriptions where a quantum system is described by what we now call a von Neumann algebra
[
65
]
.
Indeed, it is known today that this more general mathematical description of quantum systems becomes necessary for describing systems with infinitely many degrees of freedom, like quantum field theories
[
19
]
or quantum many-body systems in the thermodynamic limit
[
20
]
.
This is precisely why we care about von Neumann algebras in this work.
Von Neumann algebras became one of the most active research areas of pure mathematics in the late 20th century.
Perhaps, the biggest breakthrough in the theory was the discovery of Tomita-Takesaki modular theory in the 1970s
[
66
]
.

3.1
The basics

3.1.1
Hilbert spaces and bounded operators

A Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
is a complex inner product space that is complete with respect to the induced norm
\lVert\Psi\rVert=\langle\Psi,\Psi\rangle^{1/2}
.
All Hilbert spaces in this work are assumed to be separable.
Assuming basic familiarity with Hilbert spaces, we only recall a few essentials to fix notation.
A linear operator
a:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{K}}
between Hilbert spaces is continuous if and only if it is bounded in the sense that
\lVert a\Psi\rVert\leq C\lVert\Psi\rVert
for some
C>0
and all
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
.
The optimal constant in this estimate is the operator norm
\lVert a\rVert
.
Perhaps the most important fact about Hilbert spaces is the Riesz representation theorem, asserting that
\Psi\mapsto\langle\Psi|:=\langle\Psi,{\,\cdot\,}\rangle
is an anti-isomorphism between a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
and its dual
{\mathcal{H}}^{*}
.
As a consequence, the adjoint of a bounded operator
a:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{K}}
is defined as the operator
a^{*}:{\mathcal{K}}\to{\mathcal{H}}
with

\langle\Psi,a\Phi\rangle=\langle a^{*}\Psi,\Phi\rangle,\qquad\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}},\ \Phi\in{\mathcal{K}}.

A shorter way to write this formula is
\langle\Psi|a=\langle a^{*}\Psi|
.
The adjoint is anti-linear and satisfies
\lVert a^{*}\rVert=\lVert a\rVert
,
(ab)^{*}=b^{*}a^{*}
.
An operator
a
is called hermitian if
a^{*}=a
, a projection if
a=a^{*}a
, an isometry if
a^{*}a=1
, and unitary if
a
and
a^{*}
are both isometries.
Moreover, an operator
a
is positive, written
a\geq 0
, if
a=a^{*}
and
\langle\Psi,a\Psi\rangle\geq 0
,
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
, and an operator inequality
a\geq b
is defined via
a-b\geq 0
.

Although we use the ‘bra’ symbol
\langle\Psi|
from Dirac’s bra-ket notation for the canonical covector associated with a vector
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
, we do not use ‘ket’ to denote general vectors.
Instead, we follow the convention of
[
67
]
where the symbol
|\Psi\rangle
,
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
, is used to denote the operator
{\mathbb{C}}\ni\lambda\mapsto\lambda\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
.
This way
|\Psi\rangle
and
\langle\Psi|
are adjoints of each other and the operator product
\langle\Psi||\Phi\rangle
is simply the scalar
\langle\Psi,\Phi\rangle
whereas the reversed product
|\Psi\rangle\langle\Phi|
is the rank 1 operator
\langle\Phi,{\,\cdot\,}\rangle\Psi
.
In particular, if
\Psi
is a unit vector, then the orthogonal projection onto its span is
|\Psi\rangle\langle\Psi|
.
Moreover, we write
|j\rangle
,
j=0,1,\ldots
, to denote vectors of an orthonormal basis (ONB).

Given a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
, we let
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
denote the space of bounded operators on
{\mathcal{H}}
.
The product, adjoint, and operator norm turn
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
into a unital Banach *-algebra.
The unit is, of course, the identity operator on
{\mathcal{H}}
, which we simply denote by
1
or
1_{\mathcal{H}}
if we want to emphasize the Hilbert space.
A particularly important property of
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
is the monotone convergence theorem:
A uniformly bounded increasing net
(a_{\alpha})
of hermitian elements has a unique least upper bound
a
to which it converges
\sigma
-strongly (see below).

The trace on
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
is denoted
\tr
.
It is initially defined as an
\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
-valued functional on the cone
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})^{+}
of positive bounded operators, where
\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{+}=[0,\infty]
.
The trace class
{\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{H}})
is the subspace of
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
of operators on which the trace norm
\lVert\rho\rVert_{1}=\tr|\rho|
is finite, where
|\rho|=(\rho^{*}\rho)^{1/2}
.
This norm turns
{\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{H}})
into a Banach space on which the trace extends linearly to a continuous linear functional.
Moreover,
{\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{H}})
is a two-sided ideal in
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
, and we have
\tr(a\rho)=\tr(\rho a)
for
a\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
,
\rho\in{\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{H}})
.
In fact, the bilinear map
(a,\rho)\mapsto\tr(a\rho)
induces an isomorphism

{\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{H}})^{*}\cong{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}),

which identifies
{\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{H}})
as the pre-dual
{\mathcal{B}}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{H}})
of
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})

[
54
, Sec. II.1]
.
Positive operators with unit trace on
{\mathcal{H}}
are called
density operators
.
They span the trace class and, in case the Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
describes a quantum system, they describe the (mixed) states of this system (see
section

4
).

Next, we recall locally convex operator topologies on
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
.
We refer to
[
54
, Sec. II.1]
for a detailed treatment.
The
weak
operator topology on
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
is the initial topology of the functionals
a\mapsto\langle\Psi,a\Phi\rangle
,
\Psi,\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
, and the
strong
operator topology is the topology induced by the maps
a\mapsto a\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
.
The
\sigma
-weak
operator topology is the initial topology of the maps
a\mapsto\phi(a)
,
\phi\in{\mathcal{B}}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{H}})
, whereas the
\sigma
-strong
topology is the initial topology of the maps
a\mapsto\phi(a^{*}a)
.
The
(
\sigma\text{-}
)strong*
operator topology is defined like the (
\sigma
-)strong operator topology, but the initial topology is formed also with respect to the maps where
a^{*}
is used instead of
a
.
Each of these topologies makes
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
a locally-convex topological vector space.
The weak, strong, and strong* topologies coincide with their respective
\sigma
-variants on norm-bounded subsets of
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
.
The adjoint operation is continuous for the weak,
\sigma
-weak, strong*, and
\sigma
-strong* topologies, but discontinuous for the strong and
\sigma
-strong topologies.

When discussing linear maps on
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
, we need the
point-
\sigma
-weak topology
, i.e., the topology of pointwise convergence in the
\sigma
-weak operator topology, where a net of bounded linear maps
(T_{\alpha})
converges to a linear map
T
if and only if
T_{\alpha}(a)
converges to
T(a)
in the
\sigma
-weak operator topology.
By definition of the
\sigma
-weak operator topology, this is equivalent to
\lim_{\alpha}\phi(T_{\alpha}(a))=\phi(T(a))
for all
a\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
,
\phi\in{\mathcal{B}}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{H}})
.

3.1.2
Von Neumann algebras

An analogy often used to describe von Neumann algebras is that they represent
“noncommutative measure spaces”

[
68
]
. More specifically, the idea is that a von Neumann algebra is the
L^{\infty}
-space of a noncommutative measure space.
This metaphor is not merely due to the fact that abelian von Neumann algebras are isomorphic to actual
L^{\infty}
-spaces over measure spaces, but summarizes the general observation that concepts in measure theory transfer to the noncommutative setting.
We will encounter many examples of this in the following, including monotone convergence theorems,
L^{p}
-spaces, and Radon-Nikodym derivatives.

A von Neumann algebra on a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
is a unital *-algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
of bounded operators on
{\mathcal{H}}
that is closed in the strong operator topology.
For instance,
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
is a von Neumann algebra.
If
\mathcal{R}
is a set of bounded operators that is closed under adjoints, then the
commutant

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=\{x\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}):[a,x]=0\ \forall a\in{\mathcal{M}}\}

(3.1)

is a von Neumann algebra, where
[x,a]:=xa-ax
.
In fact, every von Neumann algebra arises this way.
This is a consequent of the
bicommutant theorem
asserting that a *-algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
is a von Neumann algebra if and only if it is its own bicommutant

{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{M}}^{\prime\prime}.

(3.2)

The bicommutant theorem tells us that von Neumann algebras always come in pairs
({\mathcal{M}},{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})
.
Another consequence is that a von Neumann algebra contains the spectral projections and the functional calculi of each of its hermitian elements.
Indeed, this follows from (
3.2
) because the spectral projections and functional calculi of an operator
a=a^{*}\in{\mathcal{M}}
commute with all operators that commute with
a
.

Von Neumann algebras are monotonely closed: The limit of a uniformly bounded increasing net
(a_{\alpha})
of hermitian elements in
{\mathcal{M}}
is in
{\mathcal{M}}
.
Moreover, von Neumann algebras are closed in each of the operator topologies mentioned in the previous subsection.
Each of these topologies is defined with respect to the Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
. However, the norm,
\sigma
-weak,
\sigma
-strong, and
\sigma
-strong* topologies only depend on the algebraic structure of the von Neumann algebra:
If
{\mathcal{M}}_{j}\subset{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{j})
are von Neumann algebras and if
\pi:{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
is a *-isomorphism, i.e., a linear bijection preserving products and the adjoint operation, then
\pi
is bicontinuous for the norm,
\sigma
-weak,
\sigma
-strong and
\sigma
-strong* topologies.
Importantly, the positive cone
{\mathcal{M}}^{+}
of a von Neumann algebra is an algebraic property as well.
Indeed, an operator
a\in{\mathcal{M}}
is positive if and only if
a=b^{*}b
for some
b\in{\mathcal{M}}
.

The above definition takes von Neumann algebras as algebras of operators on Hilbert spaces.
In the previous paragraph, we have seen that the most relevant properties of von Neumann algebras depend only on the algebraic structure but not on the Hilbert space on which they act.
In fact, there is an equivalent approach in which von Neumann algebras are defined abstractly:
An
abstract
von Neumann algebra (or W*-algebra)
{\mathcal{M}}
is a Banach *-algebra, which satisfies the C*-property
\lVert a^{*}a\rVert=\lVert a\rVert^{2}
and is itself the dual space of a Banach space
{\mathcal{M}}_{*}
, known as the predual
[
54
, Sec. III.3]
.
Every abstract von Neumann algebra has a faithful representation, i.e., is *-isomorphic to a von Neumann algebra on a Hilbert space.
The predual of a concrete von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}\subset{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
is the space
{\mathcal{M}}_{*}
of
\sigma
-weakly continuous linear functionals on
{\mathcal{M}}
.
Conversely, the
\sigma
-weak operator topology on
{\mathcal{M}}
is the weak*-topology induced by the predual.
In addition to the
\sigma
-weak topology, the
\sigma
-strong and
\sigma
-strong* topologies do not depend on the given representation and can be defined for abstract von Neumann algebras.
We will only consider abstract von Neumann algebras with separable preduals.
These are exactly the abstract von Neumann algebras that can be faithfully represented on a separable Hilbert space.

The elements of the predual
{\mathcal{M}}_{*}
are known as
normal
linear functionals.
Among general linear functionals, they are characterized as those that are compatible with monotone limits, i.e., if
(a_{\alpha})
is a bounded increasing net in
{\mathcal{M}}
and
\phi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}
then
\lim_{\alpha}\phi(a_{\alpha})=\phi(\lim_{\alpha}a_{\alpha})
.
A (normal)
state
on a von Neumann algebra is a (normal) positive linear functional
\phi
on
{\mathcal{M}}
that is normalized
\phi(1)=1
.
If a von Neumann algebra acts on a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
, then every unit vector
\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
, induces a normal state on
{\mathcal{M}}
via

\phi(a)=\langle\Phi,a\Phi\rangle,\qquad a\in{\mathcal{M}}.

(3.3)

The states form a convex set whose extremal points are called
pure states
.
In general, however, normal pure states may fail to exist.
We denote the normal state space of
{\mathcal{M}}
by
\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{M}})
.
In the analogy with measure spaces, normal states correspond to absolutely continuous probability measures.

There are a few operations that combine von Neumann algebras into new ones.
If
{\mathcal{M}}
and
{\mathcal{N}}
are von Neumann algebras on a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
, their intersection
{\mathcal{M}}\cap{\mathcal{N}}
is again a von Neumann algebra.
Moreover, there is a unique smallest von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}\vee{\mathcal{N}}
that contains both
{\mathcal{M}}
and
{\mathcal{N}}
. It is given by
{\mathcal{M}}\vee{\mathcal{N}}=({\mathcal{M}}\cup{\mathcal{N}})^{\prime\prime}
.
Together with the commutant, these operations satisfy de Morgan’s rule

({\mathcal{M}}\vee{\mathcal{N}})^{\prime}={\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\cap{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}.

(3.4)

However, the distributivity law fails
[
58
]
. I.e., in general
{\mathcal{M}}\cap({\mathcal{N}}_{1}\vee{\mathcal{N}}_{2})\neq({\mathcal{M}}\cap{\mathcal{N}}_{1})\vee({\mathcal{M}}\cap{\mathcal{N}}_{2})
.
Now suppose that
{\mathcal{M}}
and
{\mathcal{N}}
are von Neumann algebras on Hilbert spaces
{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathcal{K}}
, respectively.
The
direct sum

{\mathcal{M}}\oplus{\mathcal{N}}=\{x\oplus y:x\in{\mathcal{M}},y\in{\mathcal{N}}\}
is a von Neumann algebra on
{\mathcal{H}}\oplus{\mathcal{K}}
whose commutant is
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\oplus{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}
.
The more interesting cousin of the direct sum is the
tensor product

{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{N}}=\{x\otimes y:x\in{\mathcal{M}},y\in{\mathcal{N}}\}^{\prime\prime}
acting on
{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}
with commutant
({\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{N}})^{\prime}={\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}
.
Direct sums can be generalized to so-called
direct integrals

\int^{\oplus}{\mathcal{M}}_{x}\,d\mu(x)
, where the role of the index set is now played by a measure space
(X,\mu)
and the map
x\mapsto{\mathcal{M}}_{x}
must satisfy certain measurability criteria (see
[
54
, Sec. IV.8]
for details).
These operations can be defined on the level of abstract von Neumann algebras
[
54
]
.

3.1.3
Classification of von Neumann algebras

We now sketch the basic structure theory of von Neumann algebras.
The
center
of a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
is

Z({\mathcal{M}})=\{z\in{\mathcal{M}}:[z,x]=0\ \forall x\in{\mathcal{M}}\}={\mathcal{M}}\cap{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}.

(3.5)

As mentioned above, abelian von Neumann algebras are *-isomorphic with
L^{\infty}
-spaces.
A von Neumann algebra with trivial center
Z({\mathcal{M}})={\mathbb{C}}1
is called a
factor
.
Factors are the building blocks of von Neumann algebras:
If
(X,\mu)
is a standard measure space such that
Z({\mathcal{M}})\cong L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
, there are measurable fields of factors
{\mathcal{M}}_{x}\subset{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{x})
,
x\in X
, such that

{\mathcal{H}}=\int^{\oplus}_{X}{\mathcal{H}}_{x}\,d\mu(x),\qquad{\mathcal{M}}=\int_{X}^{\oplus}{\mathcal{M}}_{x}\,d\mu(x),

(3.6)

see
[
54
, Sec. IV.8]
for details. This decomposition of
({\mathcal{M}},{\mathcal{H}})
is unique up to unitary isomorphism and the usual degeneracies arising from null sets.
Moreover, the decomposition of
{\mathcal{M}}
into factors is intrinsic, i.e., independent of the Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
that
{\mathcal{M}}
acts on.
This procedure, known as
disintegration
, reduces the structure theory of von Neumann algebras to that of factors.

Before discussing the classification of factors, we briefly collect a few facts about the set
\proj({\mathcal{M}})
of projections in a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
.
It follows from the bicommutant theorem that the projection
[V]
onto a closed subspace
V\subset{\mathcal{H}}
is in
{\mathcal{M}}
if and only if
V
is an invariant subspace for
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
:

[V]\in{\mathcal{M}}\iff{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}V\subset V.

(3.7)

For every projection
p\in\proj({\mathcal{M}})
, a von Neumann algebra also contains the complementary projection
p^{\perp}=1-p
, and we say that a projection
q
is orthogonal to
p
if
p+q
is a projection or, equivalently, if
q\leq p^{\perp}
.
The classification rests on the so-called
Murray-von Neumann comparison theory
of projections: Projections
p
and
q
are said to be equivalent, written
p\sim q
, if there exists an operator
v\in{\mathcal{M}}
such that
p=v^{*}v
and
q=vv^{*}
, and one says that
p
dominates
q
, written
q\preceq p
, if
q\leq p^{\prime}\sim p
for some
p^{\prime}
.
An operator
v
such that
vv^{*}
,
v^{*}v
are projections is called a
partial isometry
and the set of partial isometries in
{\mathcal{M}}
is denoted
{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{M}})
.
A projection
p
is
minimal
if
0\neq q\leq p\implies q=p
, and it is
finite
if
p\sim q\leq p\implies p=q
.
A von Neumann algebra is said to be finite if the unit
1\in{\mathcal{M}}
is a finite projection.

The type classification of factors goes as follows
[
54
]
(see
fig.

1
for a visualization):

•

Type
{\mathrm{I}}
: The factor contains a nonzero minimal projection. Type
{\mathrm{I}}
factors are further sub-classified into:

–

Type
{\mathrm{I}}_{n}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{\infty\}
: The maximal number of mutually nonzero orthogonal projections is
n
.
Up to isomorphism, every type
{\mathrm{I}}_{n}
factor is isomorphic to
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
for an
n
-dimensional Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
.

•

Type
{\mathrm{II}}
: The factor contains nonzero finite projections but no nonzero minimal projections. Type
{\mathrm{II}}
factors are sub-classified into:

–

Type
{\mathrm{II}}_{1}
: The unit
1\in{\mathcal{M}}
is a finite projection.

–

Type
{\mathrm{II}}_{\infty}
: The unit
1\in{\mathcal{M}}
is an infinite, i.e., non-finite, projection.

•

Type
{\mathrm{III}}
: The factor contains no nonzero finite projections. Equivalently,
p\sim q
for all pairs of nonzero projections
p,q\in{\mathcal{M}}
. Type
{\mathrm{III}}
factors are further sub-classified into:

–

Type
{\mathrm{III}}_{\lambda}
,
0\leq\lambda\leq 1
: The number
\lambda
is given by

\displaystyle\lambda=\left(\frac{2-\diam\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{M}})/_{\sim}}{2+\diam\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{M}})/_{\sim}}\right)^{2},

where

\displaystyle\diam\,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{M}})/_{\sim}\ :=\adjustlimits{\sup}_{\psi,\phi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{M}})}{\inf}_{u\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}})}\lVert\psi-u\phi u^{*}\rVert

(3.8)

is the
diameter of the state space
modulo unitary equivalence
[
69
,
57
]
with
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}})
denoting the group of unitaries in
{\mathcal{M}}
.

Every factor has exactly one of the specified types and exactly one of the specified subtypes.
A von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
is said to be of type
\rm X
, if it is a direct integral of type
\rm X
factors (see (
3.6
)).
The definition of type
{\mathrm{III}}_{\lambda}
factors in terms of the diameter of the state space is taken from
[
69
]
, where it is shown that
\diam\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{M}})/_{\sim}\,=2\frac{1-\lambda^{1/2}}{1+\lambda^{1/2}}
.
While this is very different from the usual definitions found in textbooks, which we will discuss later on, it has the benefit of being explicitly stated without introducing modular theory.
Moreover, the diameter of the state space will be important in
sections

5
and
8
, where we will show that it has an operational meaning in quantum entanglement theory.

The classification of factors into type and subtype is almost a complete classification of
approximately finite-dimensional
(AFD) factors.
A von Neumann algebra is AFD if it is generated by an increasing sequence
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\subset{\mathcal{M}}_{2}\subset\ldots\subset{\mathcal{M}}
of finite-dimensional subalgebras, i.e.,
{\mathcal{M}}=(\cup_{n}{\mathcal{M}}_{n})^{\prime\prime}
.
Apart from type
{\mathrm{III}}_{0}
factors, the isomorphism classes of AFD factors are in one-to-one correspondence with type and subtype
[
70
, Ch. XVIII]
.
For approximately finite-dimensional type
{\mathrm{III}}_{0}
factors, a complete invariant is given by the flow of weights (see
section

3.3.4
).
To the best of our knowledge, all von Neumann algebras that appear as observable algebras in models of physical systems are AFD.
1
1

1

This is particularly relevant in the context of quantum entanglement theory since only non-AFD algebras can give rise to counterexamples to Tsirelson’s problem, see
section

4.10
.

3.1.4
Weights and states

A
normal semifinite weight
on a von Neumann
{\mathcal{M}}
is an affine
\sigma
-weakly lower semicontinuous map

\omega:{\mathcal{M}}^{+}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{+}

such that the left-ideal
\mathfrak{n}_{\omega}:=\{a\in{\mathcal{M}}:\omega(a^{*}a)<\infty\}
is
\sigma
-weakly dense.
In the analogy with measure spaces, weights are noncommutative generalizations of
\sigma
-finite measures.
A weight is called
faithful
if
\omega(a^{*}a)=0\implies a=0
and
finite
if
\omega(a^{*}a)<\infty
for all
a\in{\mathcal{M}}
.
A normal semifinite weight extends to a densely-defined linear functional with domain
\mathfrak{m}_{\omega}:=\lin\{a^{*}b:a,b\in\mathfrak{n}_{\omega}\}
.
Note that normal states are precisely normal semifinite weights with
\omega(1)=1
.
Like normal states, normal semifinite weights respect monotone limits, which can be seen as a generalization of the monotone convergence theorem.
If
a\in{\mathcal{M}}
, then
a\omega a^{*}
denotes the normal semifinite weight

a\omega a^{*}(b)=\omega(a^{*}ba),\qquad b\in{\mathcal{M}}^{+}.

(3.9)

The
support projection

\supp(\omega)
of a normal semifinite weight
\omega
is defined as the smallest projection
p
such that
\omega=p\omega p
.
2
2

2

For every collection
\{p_{\alpha}\}
of projections in
{\mathcal{M}}
, there exist a unique greatest lower bound
p=:\wedge_{\alpha}p_{\alpha}\in\proj({\mathcal{M}})
, which satisfies
q\leq p\leq p_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha
and all projections that satisfy
q\leq p_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha
.
Similarly, there is a least upper bound
\vee_{\alpha}p_{\alpha}
and the two are related by
(\vee_{\alpha}p)^{\perp}=\wedge_{\alpha}p_{\alpha}^{\perp}
.

If
\omega
is finite, the definition is equivalent to defining
\supp(\omega)
as the smallest projection such that
\omega(p)=\omega(1)
.
If
\phi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
is implemented by a vector
\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
(as in (
3.3
)), the support projection is given by
3
3

3

Since this fact will often be used in this work, we give a short proof:
[{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\Phi]\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime\prime}={\mathcal{M}}
holds because
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\Phi
is an
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
-invariant subspace (see (
3.7
)).
The inequality
[{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\Phi]\geq\supp(\phi)
follows from
\phi([{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\Phi])=1
.
If
p\in\proj({\mathcal{M}})
also satisfies
\phi(p)=1
, then
\Phi=p\Phi\in p{\mathcal{H}}
, which implies
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\Phi\subset{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}p{\mathcal{H}}=p{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}{\mathcal{H}}\subset p{\mathcal{H}}
and, hence,
p\geq[{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\Phi]
.

\supp(\phi)=[{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\Phi].

(3.10)

Faithfulness of a normal semifinite weight
\omega
is equivalent to
\supp(\omega)=1
.
A vector
\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
is called
separating
for
{\mathcal{M}}
if the normal positive linear functional
\phi
that it implements is faithful.
Equivalently, if
\lVert a\Phi\rVert=0\implies a=0
for
a\in{\mathcal{M}}
.
A vector
\Phi
is
cyclic
for
{\mathcal{M}}
if
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}\Phi}={\mathcal{H}}
.
Being cyclic and being separating are dual notions:

Lemma 3.1

(
[
54
, Prop. II.3.17]
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a von Neumann algebra on
{\mathcal{H}}
and let
\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
.
Then
\Phi
is cyclic for
{\mathcal{M}}
if and only if
\Phi
is separating for
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
.

A particularly important role in the theory of von Neumann algebras is played by vectors that are both cyclic and separating.
Lemma

3.1
implies that a vector is cyclic and separating relative to
{\mathcal{M}}
if and only if it has the same property relative to
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
.

The von Neumann algebra acts on the predual by left and right multiplication.
If
\phi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}
, the left multiplication
a\phi
by an operator
a\in{\mathcal{M}}
is defined as

a\phi(b)=\phi(ba),\qquad b\in{\mathcal{M}}.

(3.11)

The right multiplication is defined similarly.
The left (resp. right) support projection
\supp_{l}(\phi)
(resp.
\supp_{r}(\phi)
) of
\phi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}
is the smallest projection
p
such that
p\phi=\phi
(resp.
\phi p=\phi
).
There is a
polar decomposition
theorem for
{\mathcal{M}}_{*}
(see
[
54
, Sec. III.4]
), which states that every
\phi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}
is of the form

\phi=v\lvert\phi\rvert

(3.12)

for a unique pair
(v,\lvert\phi\rvert)
of a partial isometry
v\in{\mathcal{M}}
and positive linear functional
\lvert\phi\rvert\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
with
v^{*}v=\supp(\lvert\phi\rvert)=\supp_{r}(\phi)
and
vv^{*}=\supp_{l}(\phi)
and a positive functional
\lvert\phi\rvert\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
.

3.2
Semifinite von Neumann algebras

A (normal semifinite faithful)
trace

\tau
on a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
is a normal semifinite faithful weight, which is
tracial
in the sense that

\tau(a^{*}a)=\tau(aa^{*}),\qquad a\in{\mathcal{M}},

(3.13)

where both sides may be infinite.
Equivalently, traciality can be defined as unitary invariance:
\tau=u\tau u^{*}
for all unitaries
u\in{\mathcal{M}}
.

The theory of traces on factors is directly connected to the Murray-von Neumann comparison theory for projections, which can be used to show that there is a map
\tau:\proj({\mathcal{M}})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
such that

p\preceq q\quad\iff\quad\tau(p)\leq\tau(q),

(3.14)

which is unique up to positive scalar multiples.
If
{\mathcal{M}}
is a type
{\mathrm{I}}
factor, we fix the arbitrary scaling by setting
\tau(p)=1
for some minimal projection
p
. For
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
this implies
\tau(p)=\tr(p)=\dim(p{\mathcal{H}})
.
If
{\mathcal{M}}
is a type
{\mathrm{II}}_{1}
factor, it is customary to fix the scaling by setting
\tau(1)=1
.
In type
{\mathrm{II}}_{\infty}
, there is no canonical way to choose the scaling.
In type
{\mathrm{III}}
factors, where all nonzero projections are equivalent, (
3.14
) necessarily implies that
\tau(p)=\infty
for all nonzero projections.
With these conventions, the classification of type
{\mathrm{I}}
and
{\mathrm{II}}
factors can be phrased in terms of the set of possible dimensions
D=\{\tau(p):p\in\proj({\mathcal{M}})\}

[
56
, Sec. 1.4]
:

•

D=\{1,\ldots,n\}
if and only if
{\mathcal{M}}
has type
{\mathrm{I}}_{n}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{\infty\}
.

•

D=[0,1]
if and only if
{\mathcal{M}}
has type
{\mathrm{II}}_{1}
,

•

D=\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
if and only if
{\mathcal{M}}
has type
{\mathrm{II}}_{\infty}
,

•

D=\{0,\infty\}
if and only if
{\mathcal{M}}
has type
{\mathrm{III}}
.

Using the spectral theorem, the map
\tau
defined on projections can be extended to a normal semifinite faithful trace, i.e., a normal semifinite faithful weight assigning the same value to
a^{*}a
and to
aa^{*}
for all
a\in{\mathcal{M}}
, on
{\mathcal{M}}
.
A factor is semifinite if and only if it is not of type
{\mathrm{III}}
, i.e., of type
{\mathrm{I}}
or
{\mathrm{II}}
.
A factor is finite if and only if it is of type
{\mathrm{I}}_{n}
with
n<\infty
or of type
{\mathrm{II}}_{1}
.

A general von Neumann algebra is said to be
(semi-)finite
if it admits a normal faithful tracial state, which is the case if and only if it is a direct integral of (semi-)finite factors.
In the case of an abelian von Neumann algebra
L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
, the possible normal semifinite faithful traces correspond to the
\sigma
-finite measures
\nu\sim\mu
via
\tau(f)=\int_{X}f\,d\nu
.

3.2.1
L^{p}
-spaces

As for measure spaces, one can define
L^{p}
-spaces over von Neumann algebras.
In the following, we will discuss the construction of
L^{p}
-spaces for semifinite von Neumann algebras, which, in particular, requires treating unbounded operators
“in”
von Neumann algebras.

We fix a semifinite von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
with a normal semifinite faithful trace
\tau
.
In order to define the
L^{p}
-spaces properly, we need to include unbounded operators.
A closed operator
a
on a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
that
{\mathcal{M}}
acts on is
affiliated with
{\mathcal{M}}
, written

a\ \tilde{\hskip-0.5pt\in\hskip 0.5pt}\,\;\!{\mathcal{M}},

if the spectral projections of the positive self-adjoint operator
\lvert a\rvert=(a^{*}a)^{1/2}
and the partial isometry
v
appearing in the polar decomposition
a=v\lvert a\rvert
are elements of
{\mathcal{M}}

[
56
, Sec. 4.1]
.
Equivalently,
a
is affiliated with
{\mathcal{M}}
if and only if
u^{\prime*}au^{\prime}=a
for all unitaries
u^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
.
An operator
a\ \tilde{\hskip-0.5pt\in\hskip 0.5pt}\,\;\!{\mathcal{M}}
is
\tau
-measurable
if
\lim_{t\to\infty}\tau(1_{(t,\infty)}(|a|))\to 0

[
56
, Sec. 4.1]
, where
1_{A}
denotes the indicator function of a set
A
.
The set of
\tau
-measurable operators will be denoted

L^{0}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)

Clearly,
a^{*}\ \tilde{\hskip-0.5pt\in\hskip 0.5pt}\,\;\!{\mathcal{M}}
if and only if
a\ \tilde{\hskip-0.5pt\in\hskip 0.5pt}\,\;\!{\mathcal{M}}
, and the same holds for
\tau
-measurability.
If
a,b
are
\tau
-measurable, then
a+b
and
ab
are closable and their closures are
\tau
-measurable, which turns
L^{0}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)
into a *-algebra
[
56
, Sec. 4.1]
.
The trace extends to positive operators
0\leq a\in L^{0}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)
via
\tau(a)=\lim_{t\to\infty}\tau(a1_{[0,t)}(a))
.
The
L^{p}
-norm for
1\leq p<\infty
is defined on
L^{0}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)
as

\lVert a\rVert_{L^{p}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)}=\tau(\lvert a\rvert^{p})^{1/p}\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{+}.

(3.15)

The
L^{p}
-space
L^{p}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)
is now simply the subspace of
L^{0}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)
on which the
L^{p}
-norm is finite.
For
p=\infty
, we set
L^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)={\mathcal{M}}
.
The
L^{p}
-spaces enjoy the usual properties. For instance, they are complete, and Hölder’s inequality holds, i.e., if
p^{-1}+q^{-1}=r^{-1}
for
1\leq p,q,r\leq\infty
, then
[
56
, Prop. 4.43]

\lVert ab\rVert_{L^{r}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)}\leq\lVert a\rVert_{L^{p}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)}\lVert b\rVert_{L^{q}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)},\qquad a,b\in L^{0}({\mathcal{M}},\tau).

(3.16)

In particular, the
L^{p}
-spaces are closed under left and right multiplication with elements of
{\mathcal{M}}
.
The trace
\tau
has a unique extension to a bounded linear function on
L^{1}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)
such that
\lvert\tau(\rho)\rvert\leq\tau(\lvert\rho\rvert)=\lVert\rho\rVert_{L^{1}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)}
,
\rho\in L^{1}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)
.
For
\rho\in L^{1}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)
, the linear functional
\tau(\rho\;\!{\,\cdot\,}):{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
is normal, i.e., an element of
{\mathcal{M}}_{*}
, and the map

L^{1}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)\ni\rho\mapsto\tau(\rho\;\!{\,\cdot\,})\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}

(3.17)

is an isomorphism of ordered Banach spaces.
Its inverse is the
Radon-Nikodym derivative
:

\phi=\tau(\rho_{\phi}({\,\cdot\,})),\qquad\rho_{\phi}=\frac{d\phi}{d\tau}\in L^{1}({\mathcal{M}},\tau),\ \phi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}.

(3.18)

This bijection between
{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
and
L^{1}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)^{+}
extends to weights: If
\phi
is a normal semifinite weight, there is an operator
0\leq\rho_{\phi}=d\phi/d\tau\ \tilde{\hskip-0.5pt\in\hskip 0.5pt}\,\;\!{\mathcal{M}}
such that
\phi(a)=\tau(\rho_{\phi}^{1/2}a\rho_{\phi}^{1/2})
,
a\geq 0
.

Let us also mention that the
L^{2}
-space
L^{2}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)
is a Hilbert space with the inner product

\langle\xi,\eta\rangle_{L^{2}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)}=\tau(\xi^{*}\eta),\qquad\xi,\eta\in L^{2}({\mathcal{M}},\tau),

(3.19)

on which
{\mathcal{M}}
acts by left multiplication.

We briefly discuss
L^{p}
-spaces for two basic examples of abelian von Neumann algebras and type
{\mathrm{I}}
factors.
The
L^{p}
-spaces of an abelian von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}=L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
relative to the trace
\tau=\int{\,\cdot\,}\,d\mu
are simply the standard
L^{p}
-spaces
L^{p}(X,\mu)
.
The
L^{p}
-spaces of a type
{\mathrm{I}}
factor
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
relative to the standard trace
\tr
are the Schatten classes
L^{p}({\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}),\tr)={\mathcal{T}}^{p}({\mathcal{H}})

[
56
, Ex. 4.34]
.
In particular,
L^{1}({\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}),\tr)
is the trace class
{\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{H}})
.

3.2.2
Spectral scales and majorization

In semifinite factors, we can use the identification
{\mathcal{M}}_{*}\cong L^{1}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)
to define spectral notions for normal states relative to a choice of normal semifinite faithful trace
\tau
.
We begin by discussing spectral notions of
\tau
-measurable operators.

For positive operators
0\leq a\in L^{0}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)
, the
distribution function

D_{a}
and the
spectral scale

\lambda_{a}
are given by

\begin{aligned} D_{a}(t)&=\tau(1_{(t,\infty)}(a)),\\
\lambda_{a}(t)&=\inf\{s>0:D_{a}(s)\leq t\},\end{aligned}\qquad\qquad t>0.

(3.20)

For general
a\in L^{0}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)
,
\mu_{a}(t)=\lambda_{\lvert a\rvert}(t)
,
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, are called the generalized singular numbers of
a

[
56
, Sec. 4.2]
.
4
4

4

These generalize the concept of singular values of a matrix in the same way that the spectral scales are a generalization of the eigenvalues of a matrix, see below.

The functions
D_{a},\lambda_{a}:(0,\infty)\to{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
are non-increasing and right-continuous.
If
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{+}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
is a continuous non-decreasing function with
f(0)=0
, then
0\leq f(a)\in L^{0}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)
and

\tau(f(a))=\int_{0}^{\infty}f(\lambda_{a}(t))\,dt.

(3.21)

For instance, this implies
\lVert a\rVert_{L^{p}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)}=\lVert\lambda_{\lvert a\rvert}\rVert_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}})}
and
\tau(1_{A}(a))=\lvert\lambda_{a}^{-1}(A)\rvert
, where
\lvert{\,\cdot\,}\rvert
denotes the Lebesgue measure.
5
5

5

Thus, the push-forward measure of the Lebesgue measure
dt
by the map
\lambda_{a}:{\mathbb{R}}^{+}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
is precisely the spectral measure of
a
, evaluated with the trace
\tau
.

We consider the spectral scale in the two basic examples:

Example 3.2
.

1.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}=L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
with
\tau=\int{\,\cdot\,}\,d\mu
, and let
f\in L^{1}(X,\mu)^{+}
.
Then
\lambda_{f}=f^{\downarrow}\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{+})
is the
\mu
-equimeasurable decreasing rearrangement of
f

[
71
, Rem. 2.3.1]
.

2.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
and
\tau=\tr
.
Let
a
be a positive trace class operator and let
(\alpha_{n})
be its eigenvalues (ordered non-increasingly and repeated according to their multiplicity).
Then

\lambda_{a}(t)=\sum_{n}\alpha_{n}\,1_{[n-1,n)}(t),\qquad t>0.

(3.22)

Following
[
72
,
73
]
,
majorization

\rho\preceq\sigma
is defined for
\tau
-measurable positive operations
\rho,\sigma\in L^{0}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)^{+}
via majorization of the spectral scales
\lambda_{\rho}\preceq\lambda_{\sigma}
, i.e.,
\rho\preceq\sigma
if and only if

\int_{0}^{t}\lambda_{\rho}(s)\,ds\geq\int_{0}^{t}\lambda_{\sigma}(s)\,ds,\qquad t>0,

(3.23)

with equality for
t=\infty
. The latter condition is equivalent to
\tau(\rho)=\tau(\sigma)
.
Note that the definition depends on the choice of normal semifinite faithful trace
\tau
.
For factors, however, majorization is intrinsically defined because there is a unique normal semifinite faithful trace up to scalar multiples.

Of the many things that can be said about majorization theory in von Neumann algebras (see
[
74
,
73
,
72
,
75
,
71
,
76
]
), we only need the structure of majorization for states on semifinite factors.
Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a semifinite factor with normal semifinite faithful trace
\tau
.
For normal states
\psi
on
{\mathcal{M}}
, we define

\lambda_{\psi}:=\lambda_{\rho_{\psi}},\quad D_{\psi}=D_{\rho_{\psi}},\qquad\rho_{\psi}=\frac{d\psi}{d\tau}.

(3.24)

The distribution function and spectral scale relative to a scaled trace
\tau^{\prime}=c\tau
,
c>0
, are dilations
D_{\psi}^{\prime}(t)=cD_{\psi}(ct)
and
\lambda^{\prime}_{\psi}(t)=c^{-1}\lambda_{\psi}(c^{-1}t)
.
6
6

6

Indeed,
\rho^{\prime}_{\psi}=d\psi/d\tau^{\prime}=c^{-1}d\psi/d\tau=c^{-1}\rho_{\psi}
,
D^{\prime}_{\psi}(t)=\tau^{\prime}(1_{(t,\infty)}(\rho_{\psi}^{\prime})=c\tau(1_{(t,\infty)}(c^{-1}\rho_{\psi}))=cD_{\psi}(ct)
, and
\lambda_{\psi}^{\prime}(t)=\inf\{s>0:D^{\prime}_{\psi}(s)\leq t\}=\inf\{c^{-1}s>0:D_{\psi}(s)\leq c^{-1}t\}=c^{-1}\lambda_{\psi}(c^{-1}t)
.

If
\phi
is another normal state on
{\mathcal{M}}
, we define majorization
\psi\preceq\phi
via majorization of the density operators
\rho_{\psi}\preceq\rho_{\phi}
or, equivalently, majorization of the spectral scales
\lambda_{\psi}\preceq\lambda_{\phi}
.
Note that the majorization of states is independent from the choice of normal semifinite faithful trace.

Theorem 3.3

(
[
73
, Thm. 2.5]
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a semifinite factor with normal semifinite faithful trace
\tau
.
Let
\psi,\phi
be normal states on
{\mathcal{M}}
.
The following are equivalent:

1.

\psi\succeq\phi
, i.e.,
\lambda_{\psi}\succeq\lambda_{\phi}
;

2.

\tau(f(\rho_{\psi}))\geq\tau(f(\rho_{\phi}))
for all continuous non-decreasing convex functions
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{+}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
;

3.

\psi\in\overline{\conv}\,\{\,u\phi u^{*}:u\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}})\,\}
.

Moreover, the distance of the unitary orbits of
\psi
and
\phi
is given by:

\inf_{u\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}})}\,\lVert\psi-u\phi u^{*}\rVert=\lVert\lambda_{\psi}-\lambda_{\phi}\rVert_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{+})}=\lVert D_{\psi}-D_{\phi}\rVert_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{+})}.

(3.25)

We conclude our discussion with a construction that shows that every possible spectral scale occurs as the spectral scale of a normal state.
Since this clear in type
{\mathrm{I}}
(see
example

3.2
), we consider a type
{\mathrm{II}}
factor
{\mathcal{M}}
with a normal semifinite faithful trace
\tau
.
Then we can find an increasing family of projections
e_{t}
,
0\leq t\leq\tau(1)
with

\tau(e_{t})=t,\qquad\vee_{s<t}e_{s}=e_{t},\qquad e_{\tau(1)}=1

(3.26)

(see
[
77
, Sec. III.1.7]
or
[
57
, Lem. 4.1]
for a construction).
Now if
\lambda:(0,\infty)\to{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
is a non-increasing right-continuous function, the Lebesgue-Stieltjes integral

\rho=\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda(t)\,de_{t}

(3.27)

defines a positive operator
\rho\in L^{0}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)
with
\lambda_{a}(t)=\lambda(t)
.
In particular, if
\lambda
is a probability density, then
\rho\in L^{1}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)
and the normal state
\psi=\tr(\rho({\,\cdot\,}))
has spectral scale
\lambda
.

3.3
Modular theory

Modular theory was initiated by Tomita and Takesaki
[
66
]
and led to many breakthroughs in the theory of von Neumann algebras in the early 1970s.
Perhaps its most important application is the classification of type
{\mathrm{III}}
factors, due to Connes
[
44
]
.
In the following, we give a brief exposition roughly following
[
56
]
.
For a complete account, we refer to
[
55
]
or
[
78
]
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a von Neumann algebra on
{\mathcal{H}}
and let
\Omega
be a cyclic separating vector.
The operator
S_{\Omega}x\Omega=x^{*}\Omega
defines a closable anti-linear operator on
D(S_{\Omega})={\mathcal{M}}\Omega
.
The positive self-adjoint operator
\Delta_{\Omega}
and the conjugation
7
7

7

A conjugation on a Hilbert space is an anti-unitary involution, i.e., an anti-linear operator
J:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}
such that
J^{2}=1
and
\langle J\Psi,J\Phi\rangle=\overline{\langle\Psi,\Phi\rangle}
for all
\Psi,\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
.

J_{\Omega}
such that
\bar{S}_{\Omega}=J_{\Omega}\Delta_{\Omega}^{1/2}
is the polar decomposition of
\bar{S}_{\Omega}
are called the
modular operator
and the
modular conjugation
associated with
\Omega
.
It follows that
J_{\Omega}\Omega=\Delta_{\Omega}\Omega=\Omega
.
Tomita’s Theorem (see
[
56
, Sec. 2.1]
) asserts that

J_{\Omega}{\mathcal{M}}J_{\Omega}={\mathcal{M}}^{\prime},\quad\text{and}\quad\Delta_{\Omega}^{it}{\mathcal{M}}\Delta_{\Omega}^{-it}={\mathcal{M}},\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(3.28)

The one-parameter group
\sigma^{\omega}
of automorphisms given by

\sigma^{\omega}_{t}(a)=\Delta_{\Omega}^{it}a\Delta_{\Omega}^{-it},\qquad a\in{\mathcal{M}},\ t\in{\mathbb{R}},

(3.29)

which only depends on the marginal state
\omega
that
\Omega
induces on
{\mathcal{M}}
, is the so-called
modular automorphism group
.
The state
\omega
is invariant under its own modular automorphism group, i.e.,
\omega\circ\sigma^{\omega}_{t}=\omega
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
(this follows from
\Delta_{\Omega}\Omega=\Omega
).
8
8

8

The modular automorphism group is uniquely determined by the properties that (a) it is point-
\sigma
-weakly continuous (see
section

3.1.1
), and (b) it satisfies the
\omega
-KMS condition at inverse temperature
\beta=-1

[
55
]
.

Connes showed that the modular automorphism groups of different states are cocycle equivalent (see
[
56
, Sec. 6.2]
).
To understand this, we need a few definitions.
The modular flow is a one-parameter group of automorphisms that is point-
\sigma
-weakly continuous (see
section

3.1.1
).
Equivalently, we can view it as an action of the group
{\mathbb{R}}
on the von Neumann algebra.
9
9

9

An action
\theta:G\curvearrowright{\mathcal{M}}
on a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
is a group homomorphism
G\ni\theta\mapsto\theta_{g}\in\Aut({\mathcal{M}})
.

A
cocycle
for an action
\theta:{\mathbb{R}}\curvearrowright{\mathcal{M}}
(a
\theta
-cocyle for short) is a
\sigma
-strongly continuous one-parameter family of unitaries
(u_{t})
such that

u_{t+s}=u_{t}\theta_{t}(u_{s}),\qquad t,s\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(3.30)

If
\varphi
is another faithful state, then the
{\mathbb{R}}
-actions
\sigma^{\varphi}
and
\sigma^{\omega}
are cocycle equivalent in the sense that there exists a
\sigma^{\varphi}
-cocycle
(u_{t})
such that

u_{t}\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}(a)u_{t}^{*}=\sigma^{\omega}_{t}(a),\qquad a\in{\mathcal{M}},\ t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(3.31)

The so-called
Connes cocycle

(D\omega:D\varphi)_{t}:=u_{t}
is uniquely determined by these properties.
Therefore, the equivalence class
\delta_{t}:=[\sigma^{\omega}_{t}]
in the outer automorphism group
\Out({\mathcal{M}})=\Aut({\mathcal{M}})/\Inn({\mathcal{M}})
does not depend on
\omega
.
This defines, for every von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
, a unique continuous group homomorphism,

\delta_{\mathcal{M}}:{\mathbb{R}}\to\Out{\mathcal{M}},

(3.32)

called the
modular homomorphism
. It can be used to define the classification of type
{\mathrm{III}}
factors (see
[
78
, Sec. I.3.1]
).
We briefly look at the concepts above in a familiar example:

Example 3.4
.

Consider the Hilbert space
\mathrm{HS}({\mathcal{K}})
of Hilbert-Schmidt operators on a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{K}}
.
Let
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})
, which acts on
\mathrm{HS}({\mathcal{K}})
via left multiplication.
If
\rho
is a faithful density operator on
{\mathcal{K}}
, then its square root
\rho^{1/2}
is a unit vector in
\mathrm{HS}({\mathcal{K}})
, which is cyclic separating for
{\mathcal{M}}
.
The resulting modular objects are

J_{\rho^{1/2}}\xi=\xi^{*},\qquad\Delta_{\rho^{1/2}}\xi=\rho\xi\rho^{-1},\qquad\xi\in\mathrm{HS}({\mathcal{K}}).

(3.33)

The modular automorphism group acts on
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})
as
\sigma^{\rho}_{t}(a)=\rho^{it}a\rho^{-it}
, where
\omega=\tr\rho({\,\cdot\,})
.
If
\sigma
is another faithful density operator, then the Connes cocycle is
(D\sigma:D\rho)_{t}=\sigma^{it}\rho^{-it}
.

3.3.1
The standard form

The theory of standard forms is due to Haagerup
[
79
]
.
The standard form of a von Neumann algebra is a canonical representation, which for type
{\mathrm{I}}
factors
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
reduces to the left multiplication on the Hilbert space of Hilbert-Schmidt operators (see
example

3.4
).

Theorem 3.5

(Haagerup
[
79
]
10
10

10

In
[
79
]
, uniqueness is shown assuming
4
, but in
[
80
, Lem. 4.9]
,
4
is shown to follow from
1
to
3
.
)
.

For every abstract von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
, there is a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
carrying a faithful representation
{\mathcal{M}}\subset{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
, a self-dual positive cone
\mathcal{P}\subset{\mathcal{H}}
and a conjugation
J
such that:

1.

J{\mathcal{M}}J={\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
,

2.

aJa\mathcal{P}\subset\mathcal{P}
for all
a\in{\mathcal{M}}
,

3.

J\Omega=\Omega
for all
\Omega\in\mathcal{P}
.

These properties determine
({\mathcal{H}},J,\mathcal{P})
up to unitary equivalence.
Moreover, the following hold:

4.

JzJ=z^{*}
for all
z\in Z({\mathcal{M}})
.

5.

For
\phi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
, there exists a unique vector
\Phi\in\mathcal{P}
such that
\phi(a)=\langle\Phi,a\Phi\rangle
for all
a\in{\mathcal{M}}
.

\Phi
is cyclic separating if and only if
\phi
is faithful.
If
\Psi,\Phi\in\mathcal{P}
correspond to
\psi,\phi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
then

\lVert\Psi-\Phi\rVert^{2}\leq\lVert\psi-\phi\rVert\leq\lVert\Psi-\Phi\rVert\lVert\Psi+\Phi\rVert.

(3.34)

6.

For every automorphism
\alpha
on
{\mathcal{M}}
, there exists a unique unitary
u_{\alpha}
such that
u_{\alpha}J=Ju_{\alpha}
,
u_{\alpha}\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}
and
u_{\alpha}au_{\alpha}^{*}=\alpha(a)
.

7.

If
\Omega\in\mathcal{P}
is cyclic separating for
{\mathcal{M}}
, then the modular conjugation is
J_{\Omega}=J
.

A triple
({\mathcal{H}},J,\mathcal{P})
satisfying the specified properties is called a
standard form
of
{\mathcal{M}}
.
We will later see that there is a canonical way to construct a standard form, which we refer to as
the
standard form and which we denote

(L^{2}({\mathcal{M}}),J,L^{2}({\mathcal{M}})^{+}).

Note that items
1
to
3
are invariant under swapping
{\mathcal{M}}
and
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
. Thus,
({\mathcal{H}},J,\mathcal{P})
is a standard form of
{\mathcal{M}}
if and only if it is a standard form of
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
.
A representation of
{\mathcal{M}}
on a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
is a
standard representation
if there exists a self-dual positive cone
\mathcal{P}
and a conjugation
J
such that
({\mathcal{H}},J,\mathcal{P})
is a standard form of
{\mathcal{M}}
.

Example 3.6
.

A standard form of
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
is given by the action of left multiplication on the Hilbert-Schmidt class
\mathrm{HS}({\mathcal{H}})
with the modular conjugation given by the adjoint operation
J\xi=\xi^{*}
and the self-dual positive cone is given by
\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{HS}({\mathcal{H}})^{+}
, the cone of positive Hilbert-Schmidt operators.
The vector implementing a normal state
\rho
is the square root
\rho^{1/2}
(cp.
example

3.4
).

This generalizes to semifinite von Neumann algebras:

Example 3.7
.

A standard form of a semifinite von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
with normal semifinite faithful trace
\tau
is given by the action of left multiplication on
L^{2}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)
with the modular conjugation given by the adjoint operation
J\xi=\xi^{*}
and the self-dual positive cone
\mathcal{P}=L^{2}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)^{+}
.
The vector implementing a normal state
\omega
is the square root
\rho^{1/2}
of the density operator
\rho=d\omega/d\tau\in L^{1}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)
.

Lemma 3.8
.

A von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
on a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
is in standard representation if and only if it admits a cyclic separating vector.
11
11

11

This Lemma needs our standing convention that Hilbert spaces are separable. It holds for arbitrary Hilbert spaces if the von Neumann algebra is
\sigma
-finite, which holds automatically if the Hilbert space is separable.

Proof sketch.

If
\Omega
is a cyclic separating vector, then a standard form
({\mathcal{H}},J,\mathcal{P})
is given by

J=J_{\Omega},\qquad\mathcal{P}=\overline{\{aJa\Omega:a\in{\mathcal{M}}\}}.

(3.35)

To see this, one has to check that
\mathcal{P}
is a self-dual positive cone (this requires some work, see
[
79
, Thm. 1.1]
) and that items
1
to
3
of
theorem

3.5
hold.
Item
1
follows from Tomita’s theorem (see (
3.28
)), and the other two items are clear.
Conversely, if
({\mathcal{H}},J,\mathcal{P})
is a standard form, consider a unit vector
\Omega\in\mathcal{P}
that implements a faithful state
\omega
on
{\mathcal{M}}
.
Then
\Omega
is faithful for
{\mathcal{M}}
, which is equivalent to
\Omega
being cyclic for
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
.
Since
\Omega
is in the positive cone, it is
J
-invariant which implies that it is cyclic for
{\mathcal{M}}
:
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}\Omega}=\overline{J{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}J\Omega}=\overline{J{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\Omega}=J{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}
.
∎

3.3.2
The core of a von Neumann algebra

Roughly speaking, the core of a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
is a canonical semifinite extension
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\supset{\mathcal{M}}
equipped with a canonical trace and a measure scaling
{\mathbb{R}}
-action.
This structure, which can be constructed from modular theory, allows for the construction of canonical
L^{p}
-spaces associated with an arbitrary von Neumann algebra (see
section

3.3.3
).
The latter is of great significance, in particular, for applications to quantum information theory, since the
L^{p}
-spaces allow for direct generalizations for expressions such as
F(\rho,\sigma)=\lVert\rho^{1/2}\sigma^{1/2}\rVert_{1}
(the fidelity of density operators
\rho
and
\sigma
, see
[
81
]
or
section

4.7
) even if
\rho
and
\sigma
are states on a general von Neumann algebra
[
35
]
.
What is even more important for us is that the core of a von Neumann algebra gives rise to the so-called flow of weights (see
section

3.3.4
), which will be an indispensable tool for understanding embezzlement of entanglement (see
section

8
).

Theorem 3.9

(
[
82
]
)
.

For every von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
, there is a semifinite extension
12
12

12

Here, semifinite extension simply means that
{\mathcal{M}}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
is a subalgebra and that
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
is semifinite.

\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\supset{\mathcal{M}}
, a normal semifinite faithful trace
\tau
on
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
, and a point-
\sigma
-weakly continuous action
\tilde{\theta}:{\mathbb{R}}\curvearrowright\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
that scales the trace

\tau\circ\tilde{\theta}_{s}=e^{-s}\tau,\qquad s\in{\mathbb{R}},

(3.36)

and has
{\mathcal{M}}
as its invariant subalgebra

{\mathcal{M}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\tilde{\theta}}:=\{y\in\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}:\tilde{\theta}_{t}(y)=y\ \forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}\}.

(3.37)

The triple
c({\mathcal{M}})=(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}},\tilde{\theta},\tau)
can be constructed canonically and is uniquely determined by the properties above up to isomorphism and cocycle equivalence (see (
3.31
)).
Moreover:

1.

The relative commutant of
{\mathcal{M}}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
is the center:
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}=Z(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}})

2.

{\mathcal{M}}
is a factor if and only if
\widetilde{\theta}
is centrally ergodic, i.e.,
Z(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}})^{\theta}={\mathbb{C}}1
.

Apart from a canonical way of constructing the triple
(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}},\tilde{\theta},\tau)
in
[
82
]
, which involves many high-level concepts, there is the traditional, more pedestrian, way:
To start off, we have to pick a faithful normal state
\omega
(or weight) on a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
.
One then considers the
crossed product

\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{M}}\rtimes_{\sigma^{\omega}}{\mathbb{R}}
relative to the modular flow
\sigma^{\omega}
.
In general, if
\sigma:G\curvearrowright{\mathcal{M}}
is an action of a locally abelian group
G
, e.g.,
G={\mathbb{R}}
, then the crossed product is a canonical extension
{\mathcal{M}}\rtimes_{\sigma}G\supset{\mathcal{M}}
in which the action
\sigma
becomes inner, i.e., there are unitaries
(u_{g})_{g\in G}
such that
\sigma_{g}(a)=u_{g}^{*}au_{g}
(see
[
56
, Sec. 8.1]
).
The crossed product naturally comes with a dual action
\hat{\sigma}
of the dual group
\hat{G}
whose fixed points are precisely elements of
{\mathcal{M}}
.
The modular structure admits the construction of a normal semifinite faithful trace
\tau
on
{\mathcal{M}}\rtimes_{\sigma^{\omega}}{\mathbb{R}}
, which is scaled by the dual action
\tilde{\theta}=\hat{\sigma}^{\omega}
of
\hat{\mathbb{R}}={\mathbb{R}}
.
Moreover, the fact that all modular automorphism groups are cocycle equivalent implies that the triple
({\mathcal{M}}\rtimes_{\sigma^{\omega}},\tilde{\theta},\tau)
is independent of
\omega
up to an appropriate notion of equivalence
[
55
, Thm. X.1.7]
.

In the following, we refer to a triple
c({\mathcal{M}})=(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}},\tilde{\theta},\tau)
with the properties in
theorem

3.9
as a
core
for
{\mathcal{M}}
.
The name
“core”
for the triple
(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}},\tilde{\theta},\tau)
is due to the following:
A type
{\mathrm{III}}
von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
is, up to isomorphism, given as the crossed product of the semifinite algebra
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
by the action
\tilde{\theta}
:
13
13

13

The isomorphism in (
3.38
) is not canonical. There is, however, a canonical isomorphism
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\rtimes_{\tilde{\theta}}{\mathbb{R}}\cong\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}))
, which gives rise to (
3.38
) since type
{\mathrm{III}}
von Neumann algebras absorb type
{\mathrm{I}}_{\infty}
factors.

{\mathcal{M}}\cong\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\rtimes_{\tilde{\theta}}{\mathbb{R}},

(3.38)

The core of the trivial von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathbb{C}}
is

c({\mathbb{C}})=(L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}),\theta,\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\,\cdot\,}\,e^{-s}ds),

(3.39)

where
\theta:{\mathbb{R}}\curvearrowright L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
is the action by translations
\theta_{t}(f)(s)=f(s-t)
.
