Indeed, (
3.36
) and (
3.37
) hold trivially for
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathbb{C}}
so that the claim follows from the uniqueness of the core.
The core respects direct integration:

Lemma 3.10

(
[
57
, Prop. 8.1]
)
.

Let
(X,\mu)
be a
\sigma
-finite measure space and let
x\mapsto{\mathcal{M}}_{x}
be a measurable field of von Neumann algebras.
Then
x\mapsto c({\mathcal{M}}_{x})
is appropriately measurable in each entry.
If
{\mathcal{M}}=\int^{\oplus}_{X}{\mathcal{M}}_{x}\,d\mu(x)
is a direct integral of von Neumann algebras, then

c({\mathcal{M}})=\int^{\oplus}_{X}c({\mathcal{M}}_{x})\,d\mu(x)

(3.40)

is a core for
{\mathcal{M}}
, where the direct integral is entry-wise.

In contrast to direct sums, tensor products are, in general, not preserved by the core.
However, if one of the tensor factors is semifinite, we have:

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
{\mathcal{N}}
be a semifinite von Neumann algebra with normal semifinite faithful trace
\tau_{0}
and let
{\mathcal{M}}
be another von Neumann algebra.
Then a core of
{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{N}}
is

c({\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{N}})=c({\mathcal{M}})\otimes({\mathcal{N}},\id,\tau_{0}),

(3.41)

where the tensor product is component-wise, e.g.,
{({\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{N}})}^{\sim}=\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{N}}
.

Proof.

Clearly,
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{N}}
is a semifinite extension of
{\mathcal{M}}\otimes{\mathcal{N}}
with
\tau\otimes\tau_{0}
-scaling action
\tilde{\theta}\otimes\id
.
Moreover, the fixed-point algebra is

(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{N}})^{\tilde{\theta}\otimes\id}=\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\tilde{\theta}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{N}}^{\id}={\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{N}}.

(3.42)

Thus,
theorem

3.9
implies that
c({\mathcal{M}})\otimes({\mathcal{N}},\id,\tau_{0})
is a core for
{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{N}}
.
∎

Since
{\mathcal{N}}={\mathcal{N}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}
, this lets us construct a core of a semifinite von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{N}}
with normal semifinite faithful trace
\tau_{0}
via

c({\mathcal{N}})=\big({\mathcal{N}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}),\id\otimes\,\theta,\tau_{0}\otimes\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\,\cdot\,}\,e^{-s}ds\big).

(3.43)

Note that this concrete realization of the core depends on the choice of
\tau_{0}
(the dependence is only up to isomorphism and cocycle-equivalence, see
theorem

3.9
).

3.3.3
Haagerup
L^{p}
-spaces

Following
[
57
, Sec. 3]
, we associate with every weight
\psi
on
{\mathcal{M}}
a
dual weight

\tilde{\psi}
on the core
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
by:

\tilde{\psi}(y)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\psi(\tilde{\theta}_{s}(y))\,ds,\qquad y\in{\mathcal{N}}^{+}.

(3.44)

Relative to the trace
\tau
, the dual weight has a density
0\leq h_{\psi}=\frac{d\tilde{\psi}}{d\tau}\ \tilde{\hskip-0.5pt\in\hskip 0.5pt}\,\;\!{\mathcal{N}}
(see
section

3.2
).
These constructions are covariant and affine in the sense that
(a\psi a^{*}+\phi)^{\sim}=a\tilde{\psi}a^{*}+\tilde{\phi}
and
h_{a\psi a^{*}+\phi}=ah_{\psi}a^{*}+h_{\phi}
for
a\in{\mathcal{M}}
.
Since, by definition, the dual weight
\tilde{\psi}
is
\tilde{\theta}
-invariant, the trace-scaling property implies

\tilde{\theta}_{s}(h_{\psi})=e^{-s}h_{\psi}.

(3.45)

An operator
h\ \tilde{\hskip-0.5pt\in\hskip 0.5pt}\,\;\!\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
is said to have
grade
\alpha>0
if
\tilde{\theta}_{t}(h)=e^{\alpha t}h
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
The space of
\tau
-measurable operators of grade
p^{-1}
is known as the
Haagerup
L^{p}
-space

L^{p}({\mathcal{M}})
.
It is clear from the definition that these are vector spaces and that the products of operators in
L^{p}({\mathcal{M}})
with operators in
L^{q}({\mathcal{M}})
are in
L^{r}({\mathcal{M}})
with
r^{-1}=p^{-1}+q^{-1}
(simply because the grade is additive under multiplication).
The structure of the
L^{1}
-space is described by the following:

Theorem 3.12

(The Haagerup
L^{1}
-space
[
56
, Sec. 9.1]
)
.

The operator
h_{\psi}
is
\tau
-measurable if and only if
\psi
is finite, i.e., in
{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
.
The map
\psi\mapsto h_{\psi}
is affine. Its linear extension gives a bijection:

{\mathcal{M}}_{*}\ni\psi\mapsto h_{\psi}\in L^{1}({\mathcal{M}}).

(3.46)

The following properties hold:

1.

h_{\psi}^{*}=h_{\psi^{*}}
, where
\psi^{*}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
is the linear functional
a\mapsto\overline{\psi(a^{*})}
,

2.

if
a,b\in{\mathcal{M}}
, then
h_{a\psi b}=ah_{\psi}b
,

3.

if
\psi=v\lvert\psi\rvert
is the polar decomposition of
\psi
, then
h_{\psi}=vh_{\lvert\psi\rvert}
is the polar decomposition of
h_{\psi}
. In particular,
\lvert h_{\psi}\rvert=h_{\lvert\psi\rvert}
,
h_{\psi}\geq 0\iff\psi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
, and
\supp(h_{\psi})=\supp(\psi)
.

Thus, if we set
\Tr h_{\psi}=\psi(1)
for
\psi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}
, then

\lVert h\rVert_{L^{1}({\mathcal{M}})}=\tr|h|,\qquad h\in L^{1}({\mathcal{M}}),

(3.47)

turns
L^{1}({\mathcal{M}})
into a Banach space, such that (
3.46
) is an isometric isomorphism.

The map
\tr:L^{1}({\mathcal{M}})\to{\mathbb{C}}
is called the
Haagerup trace
.
The Haagerup
L^{p}
-spaces are Banach spaces with the norm

\lVert h\rVert_{L^{p}({\mathcal{M}})}=\big(\tr|h|^{p}\big)^{1/p},

(3.48)

which is justified by
\lvert h\rvert^{p}\in L^{1}({\mathcal{M}})\iff h\in L^{p}({\mathcal{M}})

[
56
, Sec. 9.2]
.
More generally, an element
h\in L^{0}({\mathcal{M}},\tau)
is in
L^{p}({\mathcal{M}})
if and only if its polar decomposition is
h=v\lvert h\rvert
with
v\in{\mathcal{M}}
and
\lvert h\rvert^{p}\in L^{1}({\mathcal{M}})
.
We refer to
[
56
, Sec. 9]
for an overview of the properties of the Haagerup
L^{p}
-spaces.
We only mention the core essentials:
The Hölder inequality holds
[
56
, Sec. 9.2]
: If
r^{-1}=p^{-1}+q^{-1}
, then

\lVert hk\rVert_{L^{r}({\mathcal{M}})}\leq\lVert h\rVert_{L^{p}({\mathcal{M}})}\lVert k\rVert_{L^{q}({\mathcal{M}})}.

(3.49)

In the case that
p^{-1}+q^{-1}=1
, the products
hk,kh\in L^{1}({\mathcal{M}})
have the same trace
\tr(hk)=\tr(kh)

[
56
, Prop. 9.22]
.
In fact, the
L^{p}({\mathcal{M}})
and
L^{q}({\mathcal{M}})
are dual spaces with the dual pairing
(h,k)\mapsto\tr(hk)
if
1<p<\infty
and
p^{-1}+q^{-1}=1
.
This defines an inner product on
L^{2}({\mathcal{M}})
, which turns it into a Hilbert space.
The action of
{\mathcal{M}}
by left multiplication on
L^{2}({\mathcal{M}})
is a standard form of
{\mathcal{M}}
with
J
and
\mathcal{P}
given by

J\xi=\xi^{*},\qquad\mathcal{P}=L^{2}({\mathcal{M}})^{+}.

(3.50)

Another neat feature of the theory of Haagerup
L^{p}
-spaces is that it lets us describe the modular objects directly through the operators
h_{\psi}
.
For instance, it holds that if
\psi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
is faithful, then
[
56
, Lem. 10.5]
:

\sigma^{\psi}_{t}(a)=h_{\psi}^{it}ah_{\psi}^{-it},\qquad a\in{\mathcal{M}}.

(3.51)

Moreover, if
\phi
is another faithful element
\varphi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
, then Connes cocycle is given by
[
56
, Lem. 10.13]

(D\psi:D\phi)_{t}=h_{\psi}^{it}h_{\phi}^{-it}.

(3.52)

Another nice application is to the definition of
commutativity
of positive linear functionals
\psi,\phi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
.
Given the Haagerup
L^{1}
-space, the obvious definition is that
h_{\psi}
and
h_{\phi}
commute and, indeed, this is equivalent to the traditional definitions in terms of modular theory such as
\phi\circ\sigma^{\psi}_{t}=\phi
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
(see
[
56
, Prop. 10.18]
for details).

Before we move on, we discuss how the Haagerup
L^{p}
-spaces relate to the
L^{p}
-spaces relative to a fixed trace on a semifinite von Neumann algebra.
Recall that we can write the core
c({\mathcal{M}})
of a semifinite von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
with normal semifinite faithful trace
\tau_{0}
as
({\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}),\id\otimes\,\theta,\tau_{0}\otimes\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\,\cdot\,}e^{-s}ds)
above (see (
3.43
)).
Since the flow acts trivially on
{\mathcal{M}}\otimes 1
, the operators
h\ \tilde{\hskip-0.5pt\in\hskip 0.5pt}\,\;\!\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
that have grade
p^{-1}
are precisely the operators of the form
a\otimes e^{-t/p}
(where
t
is the
“dummy variable”
for functions on
{\mathbb{R}}
).
Thus, we may write the Haagerup
L^{p}
-space of the semifinite von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
as:

L^{p}({\mathcal{M}})=L^{p}({\mathcal{M}},\tau_{0})\otimes e^{-t/p}.

(3.53)

If
\psi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
and if
\rho_{\psi}=d\psi/d\tau_{0}
is its density operator in
L^{1}({\mathcal{M}},\tau_{0})
, then the corresponding operator
h_{\psi}
in the canonical
L^{1}
-space
L^{1}({\mathcal{M}})
is given by

h_{\psi}=\rho_{\psi}\otimes e^{-t}.

(3.54)

While the standard
L^{p}
-spaces relative to a trace are, of course, much more accessible, the abstract approach via Haagerup
L^{p}
-spaces has the structural benefit that it is canonical, i.e., does not require the choice of a trace.
The measure theory analog of this are the canonical
L^{p}
-spaces of nonsingular spaces, i.e., standard Borel spaces equipped with an equivalence class of, which do not depend on the choice of a measure
\sigma
-finite measures (see
[
83
, Sec. 4.3]
).

3.3.4
The flow of weights and the Haagerup-Størmer spectral state

The flow of weights is a canonical classical dynamical system that can be assigned to a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
.
Working in the category of von Neumann algebras, we describe a classical dynamical system by a flow
\theta:{\mathbb{R}}\curvearrowright{\mathcal{A}}
on an abelian von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
.
The flow of weights is the central restriction of the trace-scaling flow on the core of the von Neumann algebra, i.e., if
c({\mathcal{M}})=(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}},\tilde{\theta},\tau)
is the core of
{\mathcal{M}}
, then the flow of weights is:

{\mathcal{A}}=Z(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}),\qquad\theta_{t}=\tilde{\theta}_{t}\restriction Z(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}).

(3.55)

By
theorem

3.9
, the flow of weights is ergodic if and only if the von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
is a factor.
By (
3.43
), the flow of weights of all semifinite factors is given by the translations on
{\mathbb{R}}
.
For type
{\mathrm{III}}
factors, however, the flow of weights captures the subtype (see
[
55
, Sec. XII.1]
):

•

Type
{\mathrm{III}}_{0}
: The flow of weights is aperiodic.
14
14

14

Type
{\mathrm{III}}_{0}
factors are the only type
{\mathrm{III}}
factors with aperiodic flow weights. Among all factors, type
{\mathrm{III}}_{0}
factors are singled out by having a properly ergodic flow of weights.

In this case, the flow of weights is
{\mathcal{A}}=L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
and
\theta_{t}(f)(x)=f(F_{t}(x))
for some properly ergodic flow
(F_{t})
on
(X,\mu)
.
15
15

15

An ergodic flow
(F_{t})
on
(X,\mu)
is properly ergodic if
X
is not equal to the orbit of a single point (up to null sets)
[
55
, Sec. XII.3]
. For instance, the irrational line flow on the torus is properly ergodic.

•

Type
{\mathrm{III}}_{\lambda}
,
0<\lambda<1
: The flow of weights is periodic with minimal period
T=-\log\lambda
. In this case,
{\mathcal{A}}\cong L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}/T{\mathbb{Z}})
with
\theta
corresponding to the periodic shift.

•

Type
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}
: The flow of weights is trivial
\theta\equiv\id
and
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathbb{C}}
.

In the following, we review a construction due Haagerup and Størmer
[
57
]
, that associates with a normal positive linear function
\psi
on
{\mathcal{M}}
a positive linear functional
\hat{\psi}
on the flow of weights.
If
\psi
is a normal semifinite weight on
{\mathcal{M}}
, we denote the spectral projection of the operator
h_{\psi}=d\tilde{\psi}/d\tau
onto the interval
(1,\infty)
by
e_{\psi}
, i.e.,
e_{\psi}=1_{(1,\infty)}(h_{\psi})
.

Lemma 3.13

(
[
57
, Lem. 3.1]
)
.

Let
\psi
be a normal semifinite weight on
{\mathcal{M}}
and let
(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}},\tilde{\theta},\tau)
be the core of
{\mathcal{M}}
.
Then

\tau(e_{\psi})=\psi(1).

(3.56)

Because of its central importance, we sketch the proof given in
[
57
]
:

Proof.

The identity
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{t}s^{-1}1_{(1,\infty)}(e^{-t})\,dt=1_{(0,\infty)}(s)
shows
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{t}h_{\psi}^{-1}1_{(1,\infty)}(e^{-t}h_{\psi})\,dt=\supp(h_{\psi})=\supp\psi
, where we used item
3
of
theorem

3.12
.
Hence, using
\tilde{\theta}_{t}(h_{\psi})=e^{-t}h_{\psi}
, we find

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tilde{\theta}_{t}(e_{\psi}h_{\psi}^{-1})\,dt=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{t}h_{\psi}^{-1}1_{(1,\infty)}(e^{-t}h_{\psi})\,dt=\supp(\psi).

Since
\tilde{\psi}
is
\tilde{\theta}
-invariant, we have

\displaystyle\tau(ae_{\psi})=\tilde{\psi}(ae_{\psi}h_{\psi}^{-1})

\displaystyle=\psi\bigg(\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tilde{\theta}_{t}(ae_{\psi}h_{\psi}^{-1})\,dt\bigg)=\psi\bigg(a\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tilde{\theta}_{t}(e_{\psi}h_{\psi}^{-1})\,dt\bigg)=\psi(a)

for
a\in{\mathcal{M}}
,
where we used
1_{(0,\infty)}(h_{\psi})=\supp(h_{\psi})=\supp(\psi)
.
Setting
a=1
implies the claim.
∎

Definition 3.14
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a von Neumann algebra with flow of weights
\theta:{\mathbb{R}}\curvearrowright{\mathcal{A}}
.
The
spectral functional
of
\psi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
is the normal positive linear functional
\hat{\psi}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{*}^{+}
defined by

\hat{\psi}(a)=\tau(e_{\psi}a),\qquad a\in{\mathcal{A}}=Z(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}).

(3.57)

If
\psi
is a state, we call
\hat{\psi}
the
spectral state
of
\psi
.
We list a few basic properties of the map
\psi\mapsto\hat{\psi}
.
By
lemma

3.13
, this map is norm-preserving.
Moreover, it is monotonic, i.e.,

\psi\leq\phi\implies\hat{\psi}\leq\hat{\phi}.

(3.58)

The flow of the spectral state is

\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{t}=e^{-t}\,\widehat{e^{t}\psi},\qquad t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(3.59)

Monotonicity and (
3.59
) imply

\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{t}\geq e^{-t}\hat{\psi},\qquad t>0.

(3.60)

We refer to this property as
\theta
-regularity
.
We denote the
\theta
-regular state space on
{\mathcal{A}}
as

\mathfrak{S}_{*}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}})=\{\chi\in{\mathcal{A}}_{*}^{+}:\chi\circ\theta_{t}\geq e^{-t}\chi,\ t>0\}.

(3.61)

The following is the main theorem of
[
57
]
:

Theorem 3.15

(
[
57
]
)
.

The distance of the unitary orbits of
\psi,\phi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
is

\inf_{u\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}})}\,\lVert\psi-u\phi u^{*}\rVert=\lVert\hat{\psi}-\hat{\phi}\rVert.

(3.62)

If
{\mathcal{M}}
is type
{\mathrm{III}}
, then
\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{M}})\ni\psi\mapsto\hat{\psi}\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}})
is surjective.

In particular,
theorem

3.15
states that two normal states
\psi,\phi
on
{\mathcal{M}}
are approximately unitarily equivalent if and only if
\hat{\psi}=\hat{\phi}
, where approximate unitary equivalence is defined as

\psi\sim\phi\quad:\iff\quad\inf_{u\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}})}\,\lVert\psi-u\phi u^{*}\rVert=0.

(3.63)

Comparing
theorem

3.15
with
theorem

3.3
shows that the spectral states on general von Neumann algebras play a similar role to that of the spectral scale and distribution function in semifinite factors.
In fact, the distribution function is just a special representation of the spectral states (see below).

Another important consequence of
theorem

3.15
is that it bridges the gap between the classification of type
{\mathrm{III}}
factors in terms of the state space diameter (see
section

3.1
) and the textbook version of the classification in terms of the flow of weights, as explained at the beginning of this subsection.
Indeed,
theorem

3.15
shows that the state space diameter (see (
3.8
)) of a type
{\mathrm{III}}
factor is

\diam\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{M}})/\!\sim\ =\diam\mathfrak{S}_{*}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}}),

(3.64)

where the diameter of the
\theta
-regular state space is taken with respect to the norm distance.
The right-hand side (RHS) of (
3.8
) can be computed from the flow of weights.
It is given by
2(1-e^{-T/2})/(1+e^{-T/2})
, where
T\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
is the minimal period of
\theta
.
Thus, (
3.8
) follows from
\lambda=e^{-T}
with
e^{-\infty}=0
.

To better understand the Haagerup-Størmer spectral states, we consider the case of a semifinite factor
{\mathcal{M}}
with a normal semifinite faithful trace
\tau_{0}
.
Recall the description of the core from (
3.43
) and the description of the Haagerup
L^{1}
-space from (
3.53
) and (
3.54
).
If
\psi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
, then (
3.54
) implies
e_{\psi}=1_{(1,\infty)}(\int^{\oplus}_{\mathbb{R}}\rho_{\psi}e^{-t}\,dt)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}^{\oplus}1_{(e^{t},\infty)}(\rho_{\psi})\,dt
(where we changed from
“dummy variable notation”
to direct integral notation, i.e.,
\rho_{\psi}\otimes e^{t}\equiv\int^{\oplus}_{\mathbb{R}}e^{t}\rho_{\psi}\,dt
).
Thus, if
f\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})=Z(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}})
, then

\hat{\psi}(f)=\tau(fe_{\psi})=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tau_{0}(1_{(e^{t},\infty)}(\rho_{\psi}))f(t)\,e^{-t}dt=\int_{0}^{\infty}D_{\psi}(t)f(\log t)\,dt,

where
D_{\psi}
denotes the distribution function of
\psi
relative to
\tau_{0}
(see
section

3.3.4
).
Therefore, if we use the map
f\mapsto f\circ\log
to represent the flow of weights
{\mathcal{A}}=L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{+})
with dilations
\theta_{t}(f)(s)=f(se^{-t})
, then the density of the normal positive linear functional
\hat{\psi}
with respect to the Lebesgue measure
dt
, is precisely the distribution function
D_{\psi}
.
Thus,
theorem

3.15
reduces to the statement
\inf_{u\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}})}\lVert u\psi u^{*}-\phi\rVert=\lVert D_{\psi}-D_{\phi}\rVert_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{+})}
that we already know from
theorem

3.3
.

3.4
Useful Lemmas and their consequences

In this section, we present a collection of useful tools for our later analysis of quantum entanglement and von Neumann algebraic quantum information theory.

3.4.1
Partial isometries vs. unitaries

In the following, we collect results summarizing the interplay of contractions, partial isometries, and unitaries with regard to their action on vectors and states.
Recall that we denote by
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}})
the unitary group and by
{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{M}})
the set of partial isometries in a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
.
The set of contractions in
{\mathcal{M}}
, i.e., the unit ball, is denoted
B({\mathcal{M}})
.
We say that an operator
a
on a Hilbert space acts isometrically on a vector
\Phi
if
\lVert a\Phi\rVert=\lVert\Phi\rVert
.

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
s\in{\mathcal{M}}
be a contraction with polar decomposition
s=v\lvert s\rvert
and let
\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
be a unit vector. Then

1.

If
s
acts almost isometrically on
\Phi
, then
s\Phi\approx v\Phi
. Quantitatively,

\lVert s\Phi-v\Phi\rVert\leq\big(2-2\lVert s\Phi\rVert^{2}\big)^{1/2}.

(3.65)

If
s
acts isometrically on
\Phi
, then it acts isometrically on
a^{\prime}\Phi
and
sa^{\prime}\Phi=va^{\prime}\Phi
for all
a^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
.

2.

If
s
acts almost isometrically on
\Phi
, it can be approximated with unitaries:
If
\varepsilon>0
is such that
\lVert s\Phi\rVert\geq 1-\varepsilon
, there is a
u\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}})
with

\lVert s\Phi-u\Phi\rVert\leq 3\varepsilon^{1/2}.

(3.66)

Proof.

1
: We set
\delta=1-\lVert s\Phi\rVert^{2}
.
Then
1-\delta=\lVert s\Phi\rVert^{2}\leq\lVert\lvert s\rvert\Phi\rVert^{2}=\langle\Phi,\lvert s\rvert^{2}\Phi\rangle\leq\langle\Phi,\lvert s\rvert\Phi\rangle
.
Thus, we have
\lVert s\Phi-v\Phi\rVert^{2}\leq\lVert v(\lvert s\rvert-1)\Phi\rVert^{2}\leq\lVert\lvert s\rvert\Phi-\Phi\rVert^{2}\leq 1+\lVert\lvert s\rvert\Phi\rVert^{2}-2\langle\Phi,\lvert s\rvert\Phi\rangle\leq 2(1-\langle\Phi,\lvert s\rvert\Phi\rangle)\leq 2\delta
. Rearranging shows (
3.65
).
If
s
acts isometrically and if
a^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
, we have
sa^{\prime}\Phi=a^{\prime}s\Phi=a^{\prime}v\Phi
, which implies
\lVert a^{\prime}v\Phi\rVert^{2}=\langle\Phi,a^{\prime*}a^{\prime}v^{*}v\Phi\rangle=\langle\Phi,a^{\prime*}a^{\prime}\Phi\rangle=\lVert a^{\prime}\Phi\rVert
.

2
:
The argument is based on
[
57
, Lem. 2.4]
.
Let
\delta=\varepsilon/2
. By the Russo-Dye Theorem
[
84
]
(see also
[
85
]
), we have
(1-\delta)s=\sum_{i}p_{i}u_{i}
for a finite probability distribution
(p_{i})
and unitaries
u_{i}
.
Then

\displaystyle\sum p_{i}\lVert u_{i}\Phi-(1-\delta)s\Phi\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum p_{i}\big(1+(1-\delta)^{2}\lVert s\Phi\rVert^{2}-2(1-\delta)\mathrm{Re}\langle u_{i}\Phi,s\Phi\rangle\big)

\displaystyle=1+(1-\delta)^{2}\lVert s\Phi\rVert^{2}-2(1-\delta)\mathrm{Re}\langle(1-\delta)s\Phi,s\Phi\rangle

\displaystyle=1-(1-\delta)^{2}\lVert s\Phi\rVert^{2}\leq 1-(1-\varepsilon/2)^{2}(1-\varepsilon)^{2}\leq 4\varepsilon.

Thus, there is an index
i
such that
u=u_{i}
satisfies
\lVert u\Phi-(1-\delta)s\Phi\rVert\leq 2\varepsilon^{1/2}
and, hence,

\lVert u\Phi-s\Phi\rVert^{2}\leq(\lVert u\Phi-(1-\delta)s\Phi\rVert+\delta)^{2}\leq(2\varepsilon^{1/2}+\varepsilon/2)^{2}\leq 9\varepsilon.\qed

As a consequence, we have:

Corollary 3.17
.

1.

If
v
is a partial isometry in a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
on
{\mathcal{H}}
such that
v
acts isometrically on a vector
\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
, then for every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a unitary
u\in{\mathcal{M}}
such that
\lVert u\Phi-v\Phi\rVert<\varepsilon
.

2.

If
v
is a partial isometry and
\phi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
such that
\phi(v^{*}v)=\phi(1)
(
v\phi v^{*}
and
\phi
have the same norm), then, for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists a unitary
u\in{\mathcal{M}}
such that
\lVert u\phi u^{*}-v\phi v^{*}\rVert<\varepsilon
.

Proof.

The first item is clear from
item

2
of
lemma

3.16
.
The second item follows from the first item by considering the representing vector
\Phi\in\mathcal{P}
in a standard form
({\mathcal{H}},J,\mathcal{P})
of
{\mathcal{M}}
(or any other purification of
\phi
):
The assumption
\phi(v^{*}v)=\phi(1)
implies
v^{*}v\geq\supp(\phi)
, which implies
\lVert v\Phi\rVert=\lVert\Phi\rVert
.
Thus, there exists a unitary
u
such that
\lVert(v-u)\Phi\rVert<\varepsilon/(2\lVert\Phi\rVert)
.
Consequently, we have
\lVert v\phi v^{*}-u\phi u^{*}\rVert\leq\lVert(v-u)\Phi\rVert\lVert(v+u)\Phi\rVert<\varepsilon
.
∎

The next Lemma shows that every isometrically acting isometry can be replaced by a unitary on a larger Hilbert space:

Lemma 3.18
.

Let
v\in{\mathcal{M}}
be a partial isometry.
If
{\mathcal{K}}
is an infinite-dimensional Hilbert space and
\Omega\in{\mathcal{K}}
is a unit vector, then there is a unitary
u\in{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})
such that

v\otimes|\Omega\rangle=u(v^{*}v\otimes|\Omega\rangle).

(3.67)

Proof.

By disintegration, we may assume that
{\mathcal{M}}
is a factor.
Recall that a partial isometry
w
can be extended to a unitary if and only if
1-ww^{*}
and
1-w^{*}w
are Murray-von Neumann equivalent.
We set
w=v\otimes|\Omega\rangle\langle\Omega|
. Then
1-w^{*}w=1-v^{*}v\otimes|\Omega\rangle\langle\Omega|
and
1-ww^{*}=1-vv^{*}\otimes|\Omega\rangle\langle\Omega|
are both infinite projections in a factor and, hence, Murray-von Neumann equivalent.
Hence, there is a unitary
u\in{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})
such that
w=w^{*}wu=uw^{*}w
.
Thus, we have
(v\otimes|\Omega\rangle)\Psi=w(\Psi\otimes\Omega)=uw^{*}w(\Psi\otimes\Omega)=u((vv^{*}\Psi)\otimes\Omega)=u(v^{*}v\otimes|\Omega\rangle)\Psi
.
∎

To state our next Lemma, we introduce a bit of notation that will be useful in later sections.
For elements
x,y
of a metric space
(X,d)
, we write
x\approx_{\varepsilon}y
if
d(x,y)<\varepsilon
.
We will use this notation freely in cases where the metric is clear from the context.
For instance, for normal states
\phi,\psi
on a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
,
\psi\approx_{\varepsilon}\phi
means
\lVert\psi-\phi\rVert<\varepsilon
.

Lemma 3.19

(
[
57
, Thm. 2.2 & Lem. 2.4]
)
.

Let
\phi,\psi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{M}})
. The following are equivalent:

1.

approximate unitary equivalence: for all
\varepsilon>0
, there exist unitaries
u\in{\mathcal{M}}
with
u\psi u^{*}\approx_{\varepsilon}\phi
;

2.

for all
\varepsilon>0
, there is a
v\in{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{M}})
with
vv^{*}=\supp(\phi)
,
v^{*}v=\supp(\psi)
, and
v\psi v^{*}\approx_{\varepsilon}\phi
;

3.

for all
\varepsilon>0
, there is a contraction
s\in{\mathcal{M}}
such that
s\psi s^{*}\approx_{\varepsilon}\phi
.

We also note the following fact about the topology and the completions of the unitary group:

Lemma 3.20

(
[
77
, Par. I.3.2.9]
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a von Neumann algebra on
{\mathcal{H}}
.
The weak, strong, and strong* topologies coincide on the unitary group
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}})
.
However, the closures of
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}})
in
{\mathcal{M}}
are different:

1.

the weak closure is the unit ball
B({\mathcal{M}})
;

2.

the strong closure is the set of isometries in
{\mathcal{M}}
;

3.

the unitary group is closed in the strong* topology.

3.4.2
Purifications

A
purification
of a normal positive linear functional
\phi
on a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
, is a triple
(\pi,{\mathcal{H}},\Phi)
of a representation
(\pi,{\mathcal{H}})
of
{\mathcal{M}}
and a vector
\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
such that

\phi(a)=\langle\Phi,\pi(a)\Phi\rangle,\qquad a\in{\mathcal{M}}.

If
(\pi,{\mathcal{H}})
is a fixed representation then a
purification in
{\mathcal{H}}
is a vector
\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
such that
(\pi,{\mathcal{H}},\Phi)
is a purification.
Every normal positive linear functional
\phi
on
{\mathcal{M}}
has a purification.
In particular, it has a
canonical purification
, namely the triple
(\pi,L^{2}({\mathcal{M}}),\Omega_{\phi})
of the standard representation
\pi:{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}({\mathcal{M}}))
with
\Omega_{\phi}\in L^{2}({\mathcal{M}})^{+}
the unique implementing vector of
\phi
in the positive cone.
In addition to the canonical purification, every
\phi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
has a unique minimal purification, namely its GNS representation (see below).

The following simple Lemma will become an indispensable tool for our analysis of pure state entanglement:

Lemma 3.21
.

Let
(\pi_{i},{\mathcal{H}}_{i},\Phi_{i})
,
i=1,2
, purifications of
\phi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
.
Then, there is a unique partial isometry
v^{\prime}:{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
with the following properties:
v^{\prime}\Phi_{1}=\Phi_{2}
,
v^{\prime*}v^{\prime}=[\pi_{1}({\mathcal{M}})\Phi_{1}]
,
v^{\prime}v^{\prime*}=[\pi_{2}({\mathcal{M}})\Phi_{2}]
, and

\pi_{2}(a)v^{\prime}=v^{\prime}\pi_{1}(a),\qquad a\in{\mathcal{M}}.

(3.68)

Proof.

We define
v^{\prime}
on
\overline{\pi_{1}({\mathcal{M}})\Phi_{1}}
as
v^{\prime}\pi_{1}(a)\Phi_{1}=\pi_{2}(a)\Phi_{2}
,
a\in{\mathcal{M}}
.
This is well-defined and isometric since
\lVert\pi_{2}(a)\Phi_{2}\rVert=\langle\pi_{2}(a)\Phi_{2},\pi_{2}(a)\Phi_{2}\rangle^{1/2}=\phi(a^{*}a)^{1/2}=\lVert\pi_{1}(a)\Phi_{1}\rVert
,
a\in{\mathcal{M}}
.
We extend
v^{\prime}
trivially onto the orthogonal complement of
\overline{\pi_{1}({\mathcal{M}})\Phi_{1}}
.
By construction,
v^{\prime}
is a partial isometry
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
satisfying the specified properties.
If
u
is another partial isometry with the specified properties and
\Phi=\pi_{1}(a)\Phi_{1}+\Phi^{\perp}
, where
\Phi^{\perp}\perp[\pi_{1}({\mathcal{M}})\Phi_{1}]
, then
u\Phi=u\pi_{1}(a)\Phi_{1}=\pi_{2}(a)u\Phi_{1}=\pi_{2}(a)\Phi_{2}=u\pi_{2}(a)\Phi_{1}=v^{\prime}\Phi
,
a\in{\mathcal{M}}
.
∎

We note three important consequences of this Lemma.
The first one states that vectors in
{\mathcal{H}}
have a canonical polar decomposition relative to a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
on
{\mathcal{H}}
:

Corollary 3.22

(Polar decomposition)
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a von Neumann algebra of operators on
{\mathcal{H}}
.
Let
\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
be a vector and let
\phi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
be the induced positive linear functional.
Let
\pi
be the standard representation of
{\mathcal{M}}
on
L^{2}({\mathcal{M}})
.
There exists a unique partial isometry
v^{\prime}:L^{2}({\mathcal{M}})\to{\mathcal{H}}
such that

1.

v^{\prime}
maps the canonical purification
\Omega_{\phi}
to
\Phi
, i.e.,
v\Omega_{\phi}=\Phi
;

2.

v^{\prime}
is
{\mathcal{M}}
-linear, i.e.,
v^{\prime}\pi(a)=av^{\prime}
for all
a\in{\mathcal{M}}
;

3.

v^{\prime}v^{\prime*}=[{\mathcal{M}}\Phi]
,
v^{\prime*}v^{\prime}=[\pi({\mathcal{M}})\Omega_{\phi}]=J\pi(\supp(\phi))J
.

Proof.

Apply
lemma

3.21
to the canonical purification
(\pi,L^{2}({\mathcal{M}}),\Omega_{\phi})
and
(\id,{\mathcal{H}},\Phi)
.
∎

Next, we consider the structure of vectors on a Hilbert space inducing the same functional on a fixed von Neumann algebra:

Corollary 3.23
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a von Neumann algebra on
{\mathcal{H}}
.
If
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}\in{\mathcal{H}}
are vectors of the same norm.
The following are equivalent:

1.

\Phi_{1}
and
\Phi_{2}
induce the same functional
\phi
on
{\mathcal{M}}
;

2.

\Phi_{1}=v^{\prime}\Phi_{2}
for a partial isometry
v^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
;

3.

for all
\varepsilon>0
, there is a unitary
u^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
such that
\Phi_{1}\approx_{\varepsilon}u^{\prime}\Phi_{2}
.

Therefore, if
\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
is a purification of
\phi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
, then the set of all purifications in
{\mathcal{H}}
is

\{v^{\prime}\Phi:v^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}),\ \lVert v^{\prime}\Phi\rVert=\lVert\Phi\rVert\}=\overline{\{u^{\prime}\Phi:u^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})\}}.

(3.69)

Proof.

Equivalence of the first two items is direct from
lemma

3.21
. Equivalence with the third item follows from
corollary

3.17
.
∎

We emphasize that the partial isometry
v^{\prime}
appearing in
corollary

3.23
necessarily acts isometrically on
\Phi_{2}
.
Finally, we note that among all purifications, there is a unique minimal one:

Corollary 3.24

(GNS representation)
.

Up to unitary equivalence, a normal positive linear functional
\phi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
has a unique purification
(\pi,{\mathcal{H}},\Phi)
, which is minimal in the sense that
{\mathcal{H}}=\overline{\pi({\mathcal{M}})\Phi}
.

Proof.

Uniqueness
:
Lemma

3.21
implies that any two minimal purifications are unitarily equivalent.

Existence
: We restrict the canonical purification to the invariant subspace
\overline{\pi({\mathcal{M}})\Phi}
.
∎

The unique purification in
corollary

3.24
is the GNS representation
[
54
, Thm. I.9.14]
.

3.4.3
{\mathcal{M}}
-operators and inner completely positive maps

In this subsection, we consider inner completely positive maps between different amplifications of a given representation of a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
.
To do so, we consider properties and characterizations of the space
{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
.

A
completely positive
(cp) map
T:{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathcal{N}}
between von Neumann algebras
{\mathcal{M}}
and
{\mathcal{N}}
is a linear map such that

T\otimes\id:{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})\to{\mathcal{N}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})

(3.70)

is positive for all (finite-dimensional) Hilbert spaces
{\mathcal{K}}

[
86
]
.
Equivalently,
T
is cp if for finite collections
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in{\mathcal{N}}
,
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}\in{\mathcal{M}}
, we have
\sum_{ij=1}^{n}a_{i}^{*}T(b_{i}^{*}b_{j})a_{j}\geq 0
, see
[
54
, Sec. 4.3]
.
It is
normal
if it is continuous for the respective
\sigma
-weak operator topologies.
This is the case if and only if there is a predual map
T_{*}:{\mathcal{N}}_{*}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{*}
.
A cp map is
subunital
if
T(1)\leq 1
. Every bounded cp map is subunital up to a scalar multiple, namely its norm.
In the following, we only consider cp maps between type
{\mathrm{I}}
factors, which is the only case that we need in this work.
Every normal subunital cp map
T:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{1})
has a
Kraus decomposition

T=\sum_{x}k_{x}^{*}({\,\cdot\,})k_{x},\qquad\sum_{x}k_{x}^{*}k_{x}\leq 1,

(3.71)

for a collection
\{k_{x}\}
of so-called
Kraus operators

k_{x}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{1},{\mathcal{H}}_{2})

[
87
]
.
Conversely, (
3.71
) defines a subunital normal cp map, which is unital if and only if equality holds on the RHS.
Moreover, every normal cp map has a
Stinespring dilation
.
The Stinespring dilation for unital cp maps together with two of its important properties is summarized in the following Lemma (see, e.g.,
[
88
]
).
To state it, we need the
cp-order
. If
T,S
are cp maps, we say that
T
cp-dominates
S
, written
S\leq_{cp}T
, if
T-S
is cp.
The cp-order interval
[0,T]
is the convex set of cp maps
S\leq_{cp}T
.

Lemma 3.25

(Stinespring dilation)
.

Let
T:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{1})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{2})
be normal ucp map.

1.

There is a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{K}}
and an isometry
v:{\mathcal{H}}_{2}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}
such that
T=v^{*}({\,\cdot\,}\otimes 1)v
and
\overline{({\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{1})\otimes 1)v{\mathcal{H}}_{2}}={\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}
. These properties determine
({\mathcal{K}},v)
up to a unitary on
{\mathcal{K}}
.

2.

For every isometry
v_{1}:{\mathcal{H}}_{2}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
such that
T=v_{1}^{*}({\,\cdot\,}\otimes 1)v_{1}
, there is an isometry
w:{\mathcal{K}}\to{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
such that
v_{1}=(1\otimes w)v
.

3.

The map
q\mapsto S=v^{*}({\,\cdot\,}\otimes q)v
is an affine bijection between the order interval
[0,1]_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})}
and the cp-order interval
[0,T]_{cp}
such that
q\leq q^{\prime}\iff S\leq_{cp}S^{\prime}
.

We refer to isometries
v:{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{2}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}
with
T=v^{*}({\,\cdot\,}\otimes 1)v
as
Stinespring isometries
and to the unique isometry in the first item as the
minimal
Stinespring isometry
As a consequence of the cp-order isomorphism property, a Kraus decomposition (see (
3.71
)) is in one-to-one correspondence with a family of one-dimensional projections
q_{x}=|\Omega_{x}\rangle\langle\Omega_{x}|
such that
\sum_{x}q_{x}=1
and

k_{x}=(1\otimes\langle\Omega_{x}|)v.

(3.72)

Moreover, the second item of
lemma

3.25
shows that for every pair
v_{j}:{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{2}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{j}
,
j=1,2
, of Stinespring isometries, there is a partial isometry
w:{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
such that

v_{2}=(1\otimes w)v_{1}.

(3.73)

For the remainder of this section, we consider a fixed von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
on a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
.
A cp map
T
on
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
is said to be
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner if it has a Kraus representation
T=\sum_{x}k_{x}^{*}({\,\cdot\,})k_{x}
with Kraus operators
k_{x}
in
{\mathcal{M}}
.
For our applications in
section

4
, we need to generalize this notion to cp maps between Hilbert spaces of the form
{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{j}
,
j=1,2
.
To do so, we consider the strongly closed linear subspace

{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})\subset{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2})

(3.74)

generated by operators of the form
a\otimes b
,
a\in{\mathcal{M}}
,
b\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
.
We refer to the elements of
{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
as
{\mathcal{M}}
-operators
.
Identifying,
{\mathcal{H}}
with
{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}
, we obtain a notion of
{\mathcal{M}}
-operators
{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}
and
{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}\to{\mathcal{H}}
.
The following Lemma collects equivalent characterizations of the set of
{\mathcal{M}}
-operators.

Lemma 3.26
.

Let
a\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
.
The following are equivalent:

1.

a\in{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
.

2.

(1\otimes\langle\Omega_{2}|)a(1\otimes|\Omega_{1}\rangle)\in{\mathcal{M}}
for all
\Omega_{1}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
,
\Omega_{2}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
.

3.

(a^{\prime}\otimes 1_{{\mathcal{K}}_{2}})a=a(a^{\prime}\otimes 1_{{\mathcal{K}}_{1}})
for all
a^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
.

4.

a
can be written as an
{\mathcal{M}}
-valued matrix, i.e., if
\{|i\rangle_{t}\}_{i}
are ONBs of
{\mathcal{K}}_{t}
,
t=1,2
, then

a=\sum_{ij}a_{ij}\otimes|i\rangle_{2}\langle j|_{1},\qquad a_{ij}\in{\mathcal{M}}.

5.

a^{*}(a^{\prime}\otimes 1_{{\mathcal{K}}_{2}})a=a^{*}a(a^{\prime}\otimes 1_{{\mathcal{K}}_{1}})
for all
a^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

For notational ease, we suppress the indices indicating the Hilbert spaces
{\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
.
The implications
1

\Rightarrow

2

\Rightarrow

4

\Rightarrow

1
, and
4

\Rightarrow

3

\Rightarrow

5
are clear.

3

\Rightarrow

2
:
This follows from the bicommutant theorem: Let
a^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
, then

\displaystyle a^{\prime}(1\otimes\langle\Omega_{2}|)a(1\otimes|\Omega_{1}\rangle)

\displaystyle=(1\otimes\langle\Omega_{2}|)(a^{\prime}\otimes 1)a(1\otimes|\Omega_{1}\rangle)

\displaystyle=(1\otimes\langle\Omega_{2}|)a(a^{\prime}\otimes 1)(1\otimes|\Omega_{1}\rangle)=(1\otimes\langle\Omega_{2}|)a(1\otimes|\Omega_{1}\rangle)a^{\prime}.

5

\Rightarrow

3
:
By applying the assumption to self-adjoint elements, it follows that
a^{*}a\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\otimes 1
.
Let
a^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
. We have to show that
x=(a^{\prime}\otimes 1)a-a(a^{\prime}\otimes 1)
vanishes. This follows from

\displaystyle x^{*}x

\displaystyle=a^{*}(a^{\prime*}a^{\prime}\otimes 1)a-a^{*}(a^{\prime*}\otimes 1)a(a^{\prime}\otimes 1)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}\ -(a^{\prime*}\otimes 1)a^{*}(a^{\prime}\otimes 1)a+(a^{\prime*}\otimes 1)a^{*}a(a^{\prime}\otimes 1)=0.\qed

As a consequence of
lemma

3.26
, we note the following properties of
{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
:

•

{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
is closed in the weak, (
\sigma
-)strong and (
\sigma
-)strong* topologies,

•

a\in{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
if and only if
a^{*}\in{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{2},{\mathcal{K}}_{1})

•

the class of
{\mathcal{M}}
-operators is closed under products, whenever they make sense.

We return to discussing cp maps.
We say that a cp map
T:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1})
is
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner
if it has a Kraus decomposition with
{\mathcal{M}}
-operators, i.e., if

T=\sum_{x}k_{x}^{*}({\,\cdot\,})k_{x},\qquad\{k_{x}\}\subset{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2}).

(3.75)

Lemma 3.27
.

The following are equivalent for a normal ucp map
T:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1})
:

1.

T
is
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner,

2.

T(a^{\prime}\otimes 1_{{\mathcal{K}}_{2}})=(a^{\prime}\otimes 1_{{\mathcal{K}}_{1}})
for all
a^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
,

3.

The minimal Stinespring isometry
v
of
T
is an
{\mathcal{M}}
-operator,

In this case, all Stinespring isometries
w
and all Kraus operators
k_{x}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
for
T
are
{\mathcal{M}}
-operators.
Moreover,
T
satisfies

T\big(a(a^{\prime}\otimes 1_{{\mathcal{K}}_{2}})\big)=T(a)(a^{\prime}\otimes 1_{{\mathcal{K}}_{1}}),\qquad a\in{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{2}),\ a^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}.

(3.76)

Proof.

The implication
1

\Rightarrow

2
is clear.

2

\Rightarrow

3
: If
w
is a Stinespring isometry, then
item

5
of
lemma

3.26
holds and, thus, the same Lemma implies that
w
is an
{\mathcal{M}}
-operator.

3

\Rightarrow

1
: Let
v:{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}
be the minimal Stinespring isometry and let
\Omega_{x}\in{\mathcal{K}}
be vectors such that
\sum_{x}|\Omega_{x}\rangle\langle\Omega_{x}|=1
.
Set
k_{x}=(1\otimes\langle\Omega_{x}|)v
. This is by definition an
{\mathcal{M}}
-operator, and we have

\sum_{x}k_{x}^{*}({\,\cdot\,})k_{x}=\sum_{x}v^{*}({\,\cdot\,}\otimes|\Omega_{x}\rangle\langle\Omega_{x}|)v=v^{*}({\,\cdot\,}\otimes 1)v=T
.

If
\{k_{x}\}
is a family of Kraus operators for
T
then
w=\sum_{x}k_{x}\otimes|x\rangle:{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2}\otimes\ell^{2}_{X}
is a Stinespring isometry and, hence, an
{\mathcal{M}}
-operator by the argument in
2

\Rightarrow

3
above. Thus, each
k_{x}=(1\otimes\langle x|)w\in{\mathcal{M}}
.

Equation

3.76
follows directly from items
1
or
3
.
∎

For our purposes, we need
unitary
dilations in addition to the Stinespring dilation.
A unitary dilation of a normal ucp map
T:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{1})
is pair
(u,\Omega)
of a unitary
u:{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{2}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
for suitable Hilbert spaces
{\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
, together with a unit vector
\Omega\in{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
such that

T=(1\otimes\langle\Omega|)u^{*}({\,\cdot\,}\otimes 1)u(1\otimes|\Omega\rangle).

(3.77)

Lemma 3.28
.

Let
T:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
be a normal ucp map.
Then
T
has a unitary dilation
(u,\Omega)
with an
{\mathcal{M}}
-unitary
u
.
If
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}={\mathcal{K}}_{2}={\mathbb{C}}
, then
(u,\Omega)
is of the form
\Omega\in{\mathcal{K}}
,
u\in{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})
.

Proof.

For notational simplicity, we write
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
for Hilbert space tensor products
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
in this proof.
Let
v:{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{K}}_{2}{\mathcal{K}}
be the minimal Stinespring isometry of
T
, which is
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner by
lemma

3.27
.
Pick unit vectors
\Psi_{j}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{j}
,
j=1,2
,
\Psi\in{\mathcal{K}}
, and denote the first canonical basis vector of
\ell^{2}=\ell^{2}({\mathbb{N}}_{0})
by
|0\rangle
.
Consider the partial isometry
w=(v\otimes|\Psi_{1}\rangle)(1_{{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{K}}_{1}}\otimes\langle\Psi_{2}\otimes\Psi|)\in{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{1}{\mathcal{K}}_{2}{\mathcal{K}})
(with the obvious implicit reordering of the tensor factors).
By
lemma

3.18
, there is a unitary
u\in{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{1}{\mathcal{K}}_{2}{\mathcal{K}}\ell^{2})
such that
w\otimes|0\rangle=u(ww^{*}\otimes|0\rangle)
.
Thus, we have

v\otimes|\Psi_{1}\rangle\otimes|0\rangle=(w\otimes|0\rangle)\big(1_{{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{K}}_{1}}\otimes|\Psi_{2}\otimes\Psi\rangle\big)=u\big(1_{{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{K}}_{1}}\otimes|\Psi_{2}\rangle\otimes|\Psi\rangle\otimes|0\rangle\big).

(3.78)

Set
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{1}={\mathcal{K}}_{2}{\mathcal{K}}\ell^{2}
,
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{2}={\mathcal{K}}_{1}{\mathcal{K}}\ell^{2}
,
\Omega=\Psi_{2}\otimes\Psi\otimes|0\rangle\in\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
. Then
u
is an operator
{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{K}}_{2}\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
and (
3.78
) implies (
3.77
).
∎

Lemma 3.29
.

The following are equivalent for a normal subunital cp map
T:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1})
:

1.

there is an
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner subunital cp map
\hat{T}:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1})
with
T=\hat{T}(1_{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\,\cdot\,})
;

2.

the range of
T
is contained in
{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{1})
;

3.

for some/every basis
\{|i\rangle\}
of
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
, there is a collection of operators
k_{x,i}\in{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{1})
such that
T(a)=\sum_{xij}a_{ij}\,k_{x,i}^{*}k_{x,j}
, where
a\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{2})
and
a_{ij}=\langle i|a|j\rangle
.

Moreover,
T
is unital if and only if
\hat{T}
is unital if and only if
\sum_{xij}k_{x,i}^{*}k_{x,j}=1
.

Proof.

1

\Rightarrow

2
is trivial.
(
3
,
“some”
)
\Rightarrow

1
:
We set
k_{x}=\sum_{i}k_{x,i}\otimes|i\rangle\in{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
.
Subunitality of
T
gives
\sum_{x}k_{x}^{*}k_{x}=\sum_{x,i}k_{x,i}^{*}k_{x,i}\leq 1
with equality if and only if
T
is unital.
We set

\hat{T}=\sum_{x}k_{x}^{*}({\,\cdot\,}\otimes 1_{{\mathcal{K}}_{1}}\otimes{\,\cdot\,})k_{x}:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1}).

By construction,
\hat{T}
is an
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner subunital cp map with
\hat{T}(1_{\mathcal{H}}\otimes|i\rangle\langle j|)=\sum_{x}k_{x}^{*}(1_{{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{K}}_{1}}\otimes|i\rangle\langle j|)k_{x}=k_{x,i}^{*}k_{x,j}=T(|i\rangle\langle j|)
.
Hence, the claim holds.

2

\Rightarrow
(
3
,
“every”
):
Let
\{|i\rangle\}
be a basis for
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
and let
\lambda_{i}>0
be such that
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}=1
.
We use the Choi isomorphism.
Consider the contraction
t=\sum_{ij}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}T(|i\rangle\langle j|)\otimes|i\rangle\langle j|
on
{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
.
Our assumption implies
t\in{\mathcal{M}}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{1}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
and complete positivity of
T
implies that is positive.
Thus,
t
is of the form
t=\sum_{x}l_{x,i}^{*}l_{x,j}\otimes|i\rangle\langle j|
for a collection of operators
l_{x,i}\in{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{1})
.
Set
k_{x,i}=\lambda_{i}^{-1}l_{x,i}
.
It follows that
T(|i\rangle\langle j|)=\sum_{x}k_{x,i}^{*}k_{x,j}
, which implies the claim.
∎

4
Basics of von Neumann algebraic quantum information theory

In this section, we describe and discuss the framework, which we use to study quantum systems with infinitely many degrees of freedom from an information-theoretic point of view.
The technical results of this section are mostly known (a notable exception is the axiomatic approach in
section

4.3
), but the operational point of view that we adopt is new.

Two of the fundamental questions in quantum information theory are
[
89
]

What information can we write into and read from quantum systems?

Depending on the context, these questions involve constraints that must be fulfilled when interacting with quantum systems.
We like to think of a hypothetical agent that has access to operations that are compatible with these constraints.
The prototypical example is a
subsystem constraint
, which means that the agent can only act on a subsystem of the full system.
We study how well agents with partial access to a full system perform at
operational tasks
, e.g., distinguishing a given pair of states on the full system.
Under suitable assumptions, these agents are modeled by von Neumann algebras of operators on the full system’s Hilbert space.
In particular, this assumption applies to the case where an agent has access to the operations on a subsystem.

Our motivation for studying von Neumann algebraic quantum information theory is not mere curiosity about quantum information in a most general setting, in which quantitatively and qualitatively new effects become possible.
We are interested in information-theoretic properties of concrete physical models of systems with infinitely many degrees of freedom.
These are naturally described by von Neumann algebras
[
20
,
19
,
18
]
, and it is often possible to understand their mathematical properties explicitly, e.g., the type classification
[
19
,
18
,
42
,
43
,
5
]
or the index of some subfactor inclusion
[
90
,
90
,
91
,
92
,
93
]
.
A good understanding of the interplay of algebraic properties on one hand and operational ones on the other hand yields novel insights about physical models
[
15
,
2
,
5
]
.

4.1
Operations on full quantum systems

In this subsection, we recall the mathematical formulation of the operational building blocks of Quantum Theory, which remains untouched since the early days of
[
94
,
95
,
87
,
96
,
97
,
98
]
.

Let us start with the usual opening words: A full quantum system is described by a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
.
We have added the word ‘full’ to this standard textbook sentence, as we will later consider more general quantum systems that cannot be described in the purely Hilbert space-based formulation of quantum mechanics.
However, each of these more general quantum systems arises as a subsystem of a full quantum system, hence the name.

The
states
of a full quantum system are described either by density operators
\rho
on the Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
or by normal states
\phi:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})\to{\mathbb{C}}
on the von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
.
These points of view are equivalent via
\phi=\tr\rho({\,\cdot\,})
.
We adopt the latter as it suits our purposes better, and write
\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{H}})
for the normal state space of
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
.
Binary measurements, i.e., measurements with two outcomes, labeled
“yes”
and
“no”
, are described by operators
0\leq f\leq 1
on
{\mathcal{H}}
called
effects
.
If a binary measurement with effect
f
is measured when the system’s state is
\phi
, the success probability, i.e., the probability of the outcome
“yes”
, is

\phi(f)\in[0,1].

(4.1)

The effect
1-f
measures the probability of the outcome
“no”
. Indeed,
\phi(1-f)=\phi(1)-\phi(f)=1-\phi(f)
.
There are two trivial effects:
f=0
and
f=1
corresponding to the trivial measurements that output
“no”
and
“yes”
deterministically for all states
\phi
.
16
16

16

More generally, a scalar effect corresponds to flipping a biased coin. These are precisely the effects for which the success probability does not depend on the state of the system.

We now discuss general measurements, but restrict ourselves to the case where the outcome space
X
is discrete (and countable).
A measurement
m
is described by a collection
(m_{x})_{x\in X}
of effects
m_{x}
describing the binary measurement
“has the outcome
x
been measured?”
.
Linearity of (
4.1
) in
f
shows that the sum
\sum_{x\in A}m_{x}
is the effect corresponding to whether the outcome is in the set
A
.
Since every application of the measurement results in some output, we have
\sum_{x}m_{x}=1
.
The resulting object
(m_{x})
is called a positive operator-valued measure (POVM).
In this formulation, a binary measurement is described by the POVM
(f,1-f)
.
As a consequence of (
4.1
), the outcomes of the measurement with POVM
(m_{x})_{x\in X}
are distributed according to the probability distribution
(p_{x})_{x\in X}
,
p_{x}=\phi(m_{x})
, where
\phi
is the state of the system.

Quantum channels on the system are described by normal unital cp maps
T
on
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
(Heisenberg picture) or by normalization-preserving cp maps
T_{*}
on the predual
{\mathcal{B}}_{*}\hskip-1.0pt({\mathcal{H}})
(Schrödinger picture).
17
17

17

Under the identification of
{\mathcal{B}}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{H}})
with the trace class
{\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{H}})
, these become precisely the trace-preserving cp maps.

The two pictures are related by the equation

T_{*}(\phi)=\phi\circ T.

(4.2)

For our purposes, it will be more natural to work in the Heisenberg picture; we will use the term
“quantum channel”
synonymously with
“normal ucp map”
.
Every quantum channel has a unitary dilation: It can be implemented by preparing an ancillary quantum system
{\mathcal{K}}
in a pure state
\Omega\in{\mathcal{K}}
, applying a unitary
u
on the joint system
{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}
, and then discarding the ancilla (see
lemma

3.28
), i.e.,

T=(1\otimes\langle\Omega|)u^{*}({\,\cdot\,}\otimes 1)u(1\otimes|\Omega\rangle).

(4.3)

Setting
v=u(1\otimes|\Omega\rangle):{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}
, one obtains a, not necessarily minimal, Stinespring dilation
T=v^{*}({\,\cdot\,}\otimes 1)v
(see
section

3.4.3
).
18
18

18

While it is true that every channels has a unitary dilation, it is not guaranteed that the minimal Stinespring dilation arises this way. As an example, consider
T=v^{*}({\,\cdot\,})v
with
v
a proper isometry, e.g., the right shift on
\ell^{2}
.

Moreover, every quantum channel has a Kraus decomposition

T=\sum_{x}k_{x}^{*}({\,\cdot\,})k_{x},\qquad\sum_{x}k_{x}^{*}k_{x}=1,

(4.4)

with Kraus operators
\{k_{x}\}
(see
section

3.4.3
).
The smallest number of Kraus operators is the
Kraus rank
. Equivalently, the Kraus rank is the dimension of the Hilbert space
{\mathcal{K}}
appearing in the minimal Stinespring dilation (see
lemma

3.25
) of
T
.

Consider again a quantum channel
T
with unitary dilation
u\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}})
,
\Omega\in{\mathcal{K}}
(cp. (
4.3
)).
We now consider what happens when an agent performs a measurement
(n_{x})
on the ancillary system instead of discarding it.
Since the measurement outcome depends on the state of the main system, this defines a measurement
(m_{x})
on
{\mathcal{H}}
, which is readily checked to be given by

m_{x}=v^{*}(1\otimes n_{x})v,\qquad v=u(1\otimes|\Omega\rangle).

(4.5)

In addition to a measurement on
{\mathcal{H}}
, this setup includes a description of the
post-measurement states
:
If
\phi
is the initial state of the system and if
x
is the observed outcome, then the post-measurement state
\phi_{x}^{\prime}
is

\phi_{x}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{p_{x}}(T_{x})_{*}(\phi),

(4.6)

where
p_{x}=\phi(m_{x})
is the probability of the outcome
x
, and
(T_{x})_{*}
is the Schrödinger picture version of the subunital cp map

T_{x}(a)=v^{*}(a\otimes n_{x})v,\qquad a\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}).

(4.7)

The maps
T_{x}
satisfy
\sum_{x}T_{x}=T
and include a description of the measurement
m_{x}
via
m_{x}=T_{x}(1)
.
This structure is known as an
instrument
: An instrument is a collection of cp maps
(T_{x})_{x\in X}
such that
T=\sum_{x}T_{x}
is a quantum channel.
19
19

19

We have assumed that the post-measurement states are states of the given system. More generally, they could be states of other systems. In particular, measurements are instruments where the output system is trivial.

It describes a general measurement apparatus, including a description of post-measurement states.
Importantly, every instrument is of the above form: If
T=v^{*}({\,\cdot\,}\otimes 1)v
is a Stinespring dilation of
T
, then there exists a POVM
(n_{x})
on the ancilla such that (
4.7
) holds.
Every instrument
(T_{x})
has a Kraus representation: There exist Kraus operators
\{k_{y|x}\}
such that

T_{x}=\sum_{y}k_{y|x}^{*}({\,\cdot\,})k_{y|x},\qquad\sum_{y}k_{y|x}^{*}k_{y|x}=T_{x}(1).

(4.8)

In the case that each
T_{x}
has Kraus rank
1
, i.e.,
T_{x}=k_{x}^{*}({\,\cdot\,})k_{x}
for some
k_{x}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
, we call
(T_{x})
an
instrument with Kraus rank 1
.
These instruments have the property that the post-measurement states of all pure states are pure.
By (
4.8
), every instrument arises from an instrument with Kraus rank 1 via coarse-graining the output space.

Finally, we discuss topologies on the set of operations.
Let us begin with effects: A net
(f_{n})
converges to an effect
f
in expectation values, i.e.,
\lim_{n}\phi(f_{n})=\phi(f)
for all
\phi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{H}})
, if and only if
f_{n}\to f
in the
\sigma
-weak operator topology.
Indeed, this follows because
{\mathcal{B}}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{H}})
is spanned by the normal state space
\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{H}})
.
Similarly, a net
(m_{n;x})
of POVMs with outcome space
X
(independent of
n
), converges to a POVM
(m_{x})
in expectation values if and only if
m_{m;x}\to m_{x}
in
\sigma
-weak operator topology for all
x\in X
.
On the set of channels, we have two natural topologies:

•

the
topology of convergence in expectation values
, where a net
(T_{n})
converges to a channel
T
if
\lim_{n}\phi(T_{n}(a))=\phi(T(a))
for all
a\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
,
\phi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{H}})
, and

•

the
topology of norm convergence on states
, where a net
(T_{n})
converges to a channel
T
if
\lim_{n}\lVert(T_{n})_{*}(\phi)-T_{*}(\phi)\rVert=0
for all
\phi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{H}})
.

Clearly, the former is a stronger topology than the latter.
By the above, we identify the topology of convergence in expectation values as the
point-
\sigma
-weak topology
(see
section

3.1.1
).
Likewise, the topology of norm convergence on states, is the point-norm topology of the predual maps
T_{*}
.
Both of these topologies natural extend to the set of instruments with a fixed outcome space
X
.

4.2
What is a subsystem and why is it a von Neumann algebra?

The systems that are considered in quantum (information) theory are often effective systems rather than fundamental ones.
Examples range from effective qubit systems, e.g., transmon qubits
[
99
]
, to the code spaces in quantum error correction
[
48
,
100
]
or quasi-particles like phonons or anyons.
In the mathematical analysis, it does not matter how a system is realized physically.
An electron’s spin degree of freedom is just as much a
“qubit”
as every other effective two-dimensional system.
Whenever emergent systems arise, they arise as subsystems of larger quantum systems that are composed of fundamental systems.

We aim for an operational treatment of quantum systems that are more general than those described by a Hilbert space alone, which we call
full
quantum systems.
Here, we consider quantum systems that arise as
subsystems
of full quantum systems.
As we will see later, this is the most general case.
But what qualifies as a subsystem? — As discussed in the previous paragraph, this does not have a unique answer.
In the following, we adopt a purely operational point of view by considering hypothetical
agents with partial access to a full quantum system
.
Such agents are specified by the collection
{\mathcal{O}}
of operations (measurements, channels, and instruments) that they can implement on the full system.
We identify a subsystem with an agent that can perform those operations on the full subsystem that only act on the subsystem.
If the set of operations of the agent behaves like the set of operations on a subsystem (this will be made precise), we regard the agent’s operations as those that only act on a (virtual) subsystem of the full system.

In von Neumann algebraic quantum information theory, we consider agents with partial access to a full quantum system whose implementable operations are captured by a von Neumann algebra, called the
observable algebra
, on the Hilbert space of the full quantum system.
Before we discuss the justification and limitations of this assumption, particularly in regard to describing subsystems, we describe the resulting set of implementable operations.

Let us denote by
{\mathcal{H}}
, the Hilbert space of the full quantum system, and let
{\mathcal{M}}\subset{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
be the observable algebra associated with an agent with partial access to the full system.
We begin by specifying the agent’s instruments as follows:

The agent can implement instruments by preparing an ancillary system with Hilbert

space
{\mathcal{K}}
, applying a unitary in
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}))
, and then measuring the ancilla.

The implementable measurements and channels are derived from this:
The agent can implement channels/measurements by discarding the classical/quantum output of an instrument they can implement.
Let us repeat the above in mathematical terms: The agent can implement all instruments
(T_{x})
on
{\mathcal{H}}
that are of the form

T_{x}=(1\otimes\langle\Omega|)u^{*}({\,\cdot\,}\otimes n_{x})u(1\otimes|\Omega\rangle),

(4.9)

for some Hilbert space
{\mathcal{K}}
, a unit vector
\Omega\in{\mathcal{K}}
, a POVM
(n_{x})
on
{\mathcal{K}}
and a unitary
u\in{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})
.
Here, we have assumed without loss of generality that the ancilla is prepared in a pure state. Indeed, if the state of the ancilla were mixed, one could pass to a purification to obtain a pure state-unitary dilation of the instrument.
The repeatable measurements and channels are then given by (
4.5
) and (
4.3
), respectively.

As a consequence of the results in
section

3.4.3
, in particular,
lemma

3.28
, we will show that the agent can implement precisely the set
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M}}
of
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner
operations.
We have already discussed
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner channels (under the name ‘normal ucp maps’) in
section

3.4.3
.
An instrument
(T_{x})
is
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner if each
T_{x}
is an
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner cp map.
This means that the instrument has a Kraus decomposition with Kraus operators
\{k_{y|x}\}
in
{\mathcal{M}}
(see (
4.8
)).
A POVM
(m_{x})
on
{\mathcal{H}}
is
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner if
m_{x}\in{\mathcal{M}}
for all
x
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Consider an agent with observable algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
.
Then the agent’s operations, as defined above, are exactly the
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner ones.

Proof.

By definition, all operations of the agent are
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner. We have to show the converse.
The Kraus decomposition tells us that
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner channels arise from discarding the classical output of an
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner instrument.
If
(m_{x})
is a
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner POVM, then
T_{x}=m_{x}^{1/2}({\,\cdot\,})m_{x}^{1/2}
is an
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner instrument from which
(m_{x})
arises by discarding the quantum output.
It remains to show that every
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner instrument
(T_{x})
arises as in (
4.9
).
Let
v:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}
be the minimal Stinespring isometry of
T=\sum_{x}T_{x}
.
By
lemma

3.25
, there is a POVM
(n_{x})
on
{\mathcal{K}}
such that
T_{x}=v^{*}({\,\cdot\,}\otimes n_{x})v
for all
x
.
By
lemma

3.28
, the
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner channel
T
has a unitary dilation
T=(1\otimes\langle\Omega|)u^{*}({\,\cdot\,}\otimes 1)u(1\otimes|\Omega\rangle)
for some unit vector
\Omega\in\tilde{\mathcal{K}}
, and
u\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{N}}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{\mathcal{K}}))
.
Since
u(1\otimes|\Omega\rangle)
is a Stinespring isometry for
T
,
lemma

3.25
implies an isometry
w:{\mathcal{K}}\to\tilde{\mathcal{K}}
with
(1\otimes w)v=u(1\otimes|\Omega\rangle)
.
Let
(p_{x})
be a probability distribution and set
\tilde{n}_{x}=wq_{x}w^{*}+p_{x}(1-w^{*}w)
.
Then
(\tilde{n}_{x})
is a POVM on
{\mathcal{K}}
and
T_{x}=(1\otimes\langle\Omega|)u^{*}({\,\cdot\,}\otimes\tilde{n}_{x})u(1\otimes|\Omega\rangle)
.
∎

We highlight some consequences of
proposition

4.1
before we come to the discussion of the assumption that the agent’s operations are described by a von Neumann algebra.
The name ‘observable algebra’ for the von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
associated with an agent is due to the following:
Since the agent’s measurements are described by the POVMs in
{\mathcal{M}}
, the set of effects that the agent can implement is precisely the unit interval

[0,1]_{\mathcal{M}}=\{0\leq f\leq 1\ :\ f\in{\mathcal{M}}\}.

(4.10)

Hence,
{\mathcal{M}}
is generated by the agent’s observables (= measurements).
In
section

3.4.3
, we discussed equivalent characterization of
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner channels.
A notable one is the following: A channel
T
on the full system is
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner if and only if it acts trivially on the commutant
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
. Hence,
proposition

4.1
shows

\text{$T$ is implementable}\quad\iff\quad T\restriction{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}=\id.

(4.11)

Moreover, we see that one agent is more powerful (i.e., can implement more operations) than another one, if and only if the other agent’s observable algebra is a subalgebra:

{\mathcal{M}}\subset{\mathcal{N}}\quad\iff\quad{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M}}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{N}}.

(4.12)

Thus, the study of inclusions of von Neumann algebras corresponds to the study of a pair of agents where one is more powerful than the other.
Let us also mention that the set of
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner operations is closed in the topology of convergence in expectation values, and, hence, closed in the topology of norm convergence on states.
Indeed, if
T_{n}
is a net of
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner channels that converges to
T
in expectation values, then
T_{n}\restriction{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}=\id
implies
T\restriction{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}=\id
, which shows that is
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner as well (see
lemma

3.27
).
The analogous statement holds for instruments.
If
(f_{n})
is a net of effects in
{\mathcal{M}}
and
f
is an effect such that
f_{n}
converges to
f
in expectation values, i.e.,
\lim_{n}\phi(f_{n})=\phi(f)
for all
\phi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{H}})
, i.e.,
f_{n}\to f
in the
\sigma
-weak operator topology, then
f\in{\mathcal{M}}
since von Neumann algebras are
\sigma
-weakly closed.
Applying this to all effects in a POVM, we see that the set of measurements is closed in the topology of convergence in expectation values.

Having discussed the mathematical consequences, we now come to the motivation and justification of the assumption that the operations of an agent with partial access to a full quantum system are described by a von Neumann algebra as above.
In the following, we focus on a few key points. A purely operational characterization of the assumption will be shown in
section

4.3
.

Let us begin by saying that we neither claim that every von Neumann algebra corresponds to a physically relevant setup, nor that all settings in which some agent has partial access to a quantum system should be modeled with a von Neumann algebra.
20
20

20

The latter is obviously false for an agent that can only choose between two measurements.

However, we believe that many relevant settings exist in which the above description is appropriate, particularly as a description of subsystems.

Perhaps, the strongest motivation is an abundance of examples.
The prime source of nontrivial examples comes from studying sectors of quantum systems with infinitely many degrees of freedom.
The choice of sector determines a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
, describing the ‘full system’, and subsystems, e.g., field excitations in certain localization regions, are described by von Neumann algebras on
{\mathcal{H}}

[
19
,
20
,
18
]
.
We discuss this class of examples in detail in the settings of quantum many-body systems and quantum field theory in
sections

4.9.1
and
4.9.2
, respectively.
In
section

4.9.3
, we study how the idealization of having infinitely many entangled states in quantum information theory can be studied rigorously in the von Neumann algebraic setting.

The existence of many examples does not rule out the possibility that the set of
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner operations might have certain undesirable features for sufficiently weird von Neumann algebras.
Moreover, it does not suddenly turn the mathematical assumption that an agent with partial access to a full quantum system is modeled by a von Neumann algebra into an operational one.
We address these points by showing that this mathematical assumption is, in fact, equivalent to a set of purely operational axioms.
As the arguments require some space, we devote a separate subsection to this (see
section

4.3
).
The upshot is that we can arrive at our assumption, and, hence, at the definition of a von Neumann algebra in purely operational terms.

Next, we show that the assumption that an agent has access to the set of
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner operations for some von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
can always be regarded as an idealization.

Proposition 4.2
.

For every set
{\mathcal{O}}
of operations on a quantum system with Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
, there is a unique smallest von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
such that every operation in
{\mathcal{O}}
is
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner.

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be the von Neumann algebra generated by the Kraus operators of channels or instruments in
{\mathcal{O}}
and by the effects of measurements in
{\mathcal{O}}
.
Clearly, this makes every operation in
{\mathcal{O}}
an
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner one.
Conversely, every von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{N}}
such that
{\mathcal{O}}\subseteq{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{N}}
must contain these elements (see
section

3.4.3
). Thus, we have
{\mathcal{M}}\subset{\mathcal{N}}
.
∎

A setting in which a description via von Neumann algebras arises naturally is that of
symmetries
, where the implementable operations are those that commute with a symmetry group
G
of unitaries on
{\mathcal{H}}
.
This point of view arises in various contexts in physics, e.g., in the setting of quantum reference frames
[
101
,
53
,
102
,
103
,
104
]
.
The set of operations that have a unitary implementation which is covariant with respect to the trivial group representation on the ancilla system, is precisely the set of
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner operations with
{\mathcal{M}}
the commutant of the symmetry group:

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
G
be a group and let
g\mapsto v_{g}
be a unitary representation on
{\mathcal{H}}
. The following are equivalent for an instrument
(T_{x})
:

1.

(T_{x})
has a covariant unitary dilation: There is a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{K}}
, a unitary
u\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}})
commuting with
\{v_{g}\otimes 1\}_{g\in G}
, a POVM
(n_{x})
on
{\mathcal{K}}
, and a vector
\Omega\in{\mathcal{K}}
such that (
4.9
) holds.

2.

(T_{x})
is
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner with
{\mathcal{M}}=\{v_{g}:g\in G\}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Since
\{v_{g}\otimes 1:g\in G\}={\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})
, the commutativity assumption in
1
is equivalent to
u\in{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
. Thus, the statement follows from
proposition

4.1
.
∎

We now consider a general setup of approximately implementable operations.
This is an abstraction of what happens in the examples discussed in
sections

4.9.1
and
4.9.3
.
We consider a sequence (or a net) of agents
A_{n}
with partial access to a full quantum system with Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
, e.g., each agent could have access to a finite-dimensional subsystem.
We assume the agents to be of increasing capability, i.e., if
n\geq m
, then the agent
A_{n}
can implement all operations of the agent
A_{m}
.
By (
4.12
), this corresponds to an increasing sequence (net)
({\mathcal{M}}_{n})
of von Neumann algebras on
{\mathcal{H}}
.

We say that a quantum channel
T
can be
implemented approximately
by the agents
(A_{n})_{n}
in a topology on the set of channels, if there is a net
(T_{\alpha})
of channels such that each
T_{\alpha}
is
{\mathcal{M}}_{n_{\alpha}}
-inner for some
n_{\alpha}
(and hence
{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
-inner or all
k\geq n_{\alpha}
) and such that
T_{\alpha}
converges to
T
in the chosen topology.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
T
be a quantum channel.
The following are equivalent:

1.

T
can be implemented approximately by the agents
(A_{n})
in the topology of convergence in expectation values (= the point-
\sigma
-weak topology),

2.

T
can be implemented approximately by the agents
(A_{n})
in the topology of norm convergence on states,

3.

T
is
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner, where
{\mathcal{M}}=\bigvee_{n}{\mathcal{M}}_{n}
the von Neumann algebra generated by the sequence (net)
({\mathcal{M}}_{n})
.

Proof.

2

\Rightarrow

1
is clear.
1

\Rightarrow

3
follows from the point-
\sigma
-weak closure of the set of
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner channels (and the fact that all
{\mathcal{M}}_{n}
-inner operations are
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner).
It remains to show
3

\Rightarrow

2
:
We let
{\mathcal{M}}_{0}
denote the unital *-algebra generated by the von Neumann algebras
({\mathcal{M}}_{n})
.
The point-
\sigma
-weak closure of the set of
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner channels implies the
“only if”
statement.
To see the converse, consider a unitary dilation
T=(1\otimes\langle\Omega|)u^{*}({\,\cdot\,}\otimes 1)u(1\otimes|\Omega\rangle)
with
u\in{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})
,
\Omega\in{\mathcal{K}}
.
By Kaplansky’s density theorem
[
55
, Thm. II.4.8]
, we can approximate
u
with a net of contraction
(u_{\alpha})
in
{\mathcal{M}}_{0}\odot{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})
.
We consider the subunital cp maps
S_{\alpha}=(1\otimes\langle\Omega|)u_{\alpha}^{*}({\,\cdot\,}\otimes 1)u_{\alpha}(1\otimes|\Omega\rangle)
and
\omega=\langle\Omega,({\,\cdot\,})\Omega\rangle
.
Then

\lVert T_{*}(\phi)-(S_{\alpha})_{*}(\phi)\rVert\leq\lVert u(\phi\otimes\omega)u-u_{\alpha}(\phi\otimes\omega)u_{\alpha}^{*}\rVert\to 0

for all
\phi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{H}})
.
By construction each
S_{\alpha}
is
{\mathcal{M}}_{n_{\alpha}}
-inner for some
n_{\alpha}
.
However,
S_{\alpha}
is not a channel since it might be subunital.
We can, however, turn it into a subunital channel by considering
T_{\alpha}=S_{\alpha}+k_{\alpha}
,
k_{\alpha}=(1-S_{\alpha}(1))^{1/2}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{n_{\alpha}}
.
By construction,
T_{\alpha}
is an
{\mathcal{M}}_{n_{\alpha}}
-inner channel.
Since
k_{\alpha}=[(1\otimes\langle\Omega|)(1-u_{\alpha}^{*}u_{\alpha})(1\otimes|\Omega\rangle)]^{1/2}
, the
\sigma
-strong* convergence
u_{\alpha}\to u
implies that
k_{\alpha}\to 0

\sigma
-strongly.
Indeed, this follows from the strong continuity of the functional calculus (see
[
54
, Thm. II.4.7]
) and the fact that the strong and
\sigma
-strong operator topologies coincide on the unit ball (see
[
54
, Lem. II.2.5]
).
Thus, we have
\lVert(S_{\alpha})_{*}(\phi)-(T_{\alpha})_{*}(\phi)\rVert\to 0
for all
\phi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{H}})
, which shows that
T_{\alpha}
converges to
T
as specified.
∎

The assertion in
lemma

4.4
can be extended to instruments.
In fact, the same proof applies if one accounts for the measurements
(n_{x})
on the ancillary system.
As a consequence, we obtain an approximation statement for measurements:
A measurement
(m_{x})
of the full system can be approximated by measurements of the agents
(A_{n})
in the topology of convergence in expectation values if and only if it is
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner, i.e.,
m_{x}\in{\mathcal{M}}
for all
x
.

4.3
Axiomatic approach

In this subsection, we consider a set of operational axioms describing what an agent with partial access to a full quantum system can do.
We show that these axioms are equivalent to the assumption that the agent’s operations are captured by a von Neumann algebra as discussed in
section

4.2
.
Our axioms are formulated in terms of a collection of instruments the agent can implement.
Quantum channels are identified with instruments that have a single classical outcome.
x
Our axioms allow the agent to freely act on ancillary quantum systems, including preparing and discarding ancillas.
This means that they can implement operations whose input and output systems have Hilbert spaces
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}={\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{2}={\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
, where we identify
{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}
with
{\mathcal{H}}
.
In this more general setting, a quantum instrument
(T_{x})
is a collection of subunital cp maps
T_{x}:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{1})
and a quantum channel is a normal ucp map
T:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{1})
.

Our axioms are as follows:

0.

Consistency with the statistical interpretation:
The set of operations is closed under composition.

1.

Limits: The set of implementable channels is closed in the topology of convergence in expectation values.

2.

Full control over ancillary systems:
The agent can prepare and discard ancillary systems freely, and they can perform arbitrary operations between ancillary systems.

3.

Reversibility: The agent can implement the inverse of every implementable unitary quantum channel.

4.

Dilations:
Every implementable quantum channel can be implemented by preparing an ancillary system, applying a unitary quantum channel, and discarding the ancillary system.

5.

Selection-completeness: Let
T^{(y)}=(T_{x}^{(y)})
,
y\in Y
, be a finite collection of implementable instruments. Write
P_{\phi}(x|y)
for the probability of the outcome
x
if the measurement
T^{(y)}
was applied to the state
\phi
.
For every
“selection”
map
y\mapsto x_{y}
such that

\sum_{y}P_{\phi}(x_{y}|y)\leq 1\qquad\text{for all states $\phi$},

(4.13)

there is an implementable instrument
(S_{y})
with outcome space
Y\cup\{\perp\}
such that, for every input state, the probability and post-measurement state of an outcome
y\in Y
of
(S_{y})
are the same as for the outcome
x_{y}
of
T^{(y)}
.

Some remarks are in order.
The convex combinations mentioned in
0
correspond to statistical mixtures.
This assumption could be dropped here as it can be deduced from
5
.
Axioms
4
and
3
both encapsulate the idea that the only source of irreversibility is the loss of information (see, e.g.,
[
33
,
34
]
).
Roughly speaking, axiom
5
says that any operation that can be implemented by means of postselection can also be implemented at once.

To state the main theorem, we need to extend the notion of
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner operations to the setting with ancillas.
An instrument
(T_{x})
with
T_{x}:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1})
is
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner if it has a Kraus decomposition (
4.8
) with Kraus operators
k_{y|x}
in
{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
.
In particular, this applies to quantum channels, which are identified with instruments with a single output.

Theorem 4.5
.

The operational axioms
0
to
5
hold if and only if there is a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
such that the agent can implement exactly those operations that are
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner.

We say that an isometry
v:{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
is implementable by the agent if the quantum channel
T=\Ad(v^{*})
is implementable.
In particular, we use this language for unitaries. By
3
, the set of implementable unitaries is closed under adjoints.

We emphasize that axiom
4
also makes sense (and is assumed) in the presence of ancillas.
To be precise, the mathematical statement of
4
in the presence of ancillary systems is:

(A4)

For every implementable channel
T:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1})
, there exist Hilbert spaces
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{1},\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
, a unit vector
\Omega\in\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
and an implementable unitary
u:{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\otimes\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2}\otimes\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
such that
T=(1\otimes\langle\Omega|)u^{*}({\,\cdot\,}\otimes 1)u(1\otimes|\Omega\rangle)
.
21
21

21

This formulation, in which the input and output and output ancillary systems
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
may be different, is equivalent to the same statement with
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{1}=\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
if
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}={\mathcal{K}}_{2}
.

For clarity, we formulate
5
in explicit mathematical language:

(A5)

If
T^{(y)}=(T_{x}^{(y)})
,
y\in Y
, is a finite collection of implementable instruments and
y\mapsto x_{y}
is a selection of outcomes with
\sum_{y}T_{x_{y}}^{(y)}
subunital, there is an implementable instrument
(S_{y})
with outcome space
Y\cup\{\perp\}
and
S_{y}=T_{x_{y}}^{(y)}
.

This formulation is, of course, equivalent to the operational formulation presented above.

We are now starting the proof of the
“only if”
statement in
theorem

4.5
