. Afterward, we show the converse, which is essentially contained in
proposition

4.1
.
Until further notice, we consider an agent with partial access to a full system with Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
, and assume that the axioms
0
to
5
hold.
In the following, the Hilbert spaces of ancillary systems are denoted
{\mathcal{K}},{\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2},\ldots
.
We say that the agent can implement an isometry
v:{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
if they can implement the quantum channel
\Ad(v^{*}):{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1})
.
In particular, this applies to unitaries.

Lemma 4.6
.

The agent can implement the identity channel on
{\mathcal{H}}

Proof.

By
4
, the agent can implement a unitary on a larger system and by
3
, they can implement the inverse of this unitary.
Thus, by
0
, they can implement the identity on a larger system.
In combination with
2
, this implies that they can implement the identity on
{\mathcal{H}}
.
∎

Since the agent has full control over ancillary systems (see axiom
2
) and since they can implement the identity on
{\mathcal{H}}
, axiom
0
ensures that they can implement all quantum channels of the form

T=\id\otimes\;\!S:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1}),\qquad S:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{1}).

Lemma 4.7
.

The agent can implement the minimal Stinespring isometry of every channel they can implement.

Proof.

Let
T:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1})
be an implementable channel.
By
4
, there are Hilbert spaces
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{1},\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
and an implementable unitary
u:{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\otimes\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2}\otimes\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
such that
T=s^{*}({\,\cdot\,}\otimes 1_{\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{2}})s
, where
s=u(1\otimes|\Omega\rangle)
for some unit vector
\Omega\in\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
.
By
0
,
s
is an implementable isometry.
Let
v:{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{2}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}
be the minimal Stinespring isometry of
T
.
By
lemma

3.25
, there is an isometry
w:{\mathcal{K}}\to\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
such that
s=(1\otimes w)v
.
Hence, we have
v=(1\otimes w^{*})s
.
Let
S:{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})
be some quantum channel such that
S\geq w^{*}({\,\cdot\,})w
.
By
2
, the agent can implement the channel
S
.
Consequently, they can implement the product
\Ad(s^{*})\circ(\id\otimes S)=\Ad(s^{*})\circ\Ad(1\otimes w)=\Ad(v^{*})
, i.e., they can implement the Stinespring isometry
v
.
∎

Lemma 4.8
.

If the agent can implement a quantum channel
T
, they can implement every instrument
(T_{x})
with
\sum_{x}T_{x}=T
.

Proof.

This follows from
2
,
lemma

4.7
, and the cp-order interval property of the Stinespring dilation (see
lemma

3.25
), which implies that every instrument
(T_{x})
with
\sum_{x}T_{x}=T
arises by measuring the ancilla system in the Stinespring dilation of
T
.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

The quantum channels
T:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
that the agent can implement form a compact set in the point-
\sigma
-weak topology.

Proof.

Recall that the unit ball of a von Neumann algebra is
\sigma
-weakly compact (this follows from the Banach-Alaoglu theorem).
This implies the well-known fact that the set of normal ucp maps on
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
is compact in the point-
\sigma
-weak topology (indeed, this follows from Tychonov’s theorem).
By
1
, the set of implementable maps is a closed subset and, hence, itself compact.
∎

We say that an operator
k:{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
is a Kraus operator of a quantum channel
T:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{1})
, if
T
has a Kraus decomposition
T=\sum_{x}k_{x}^{*}({\,\cdot\,})k_{x}
with
k_{x}=k
for some index.
The existence of a Kraus decomposition for general cp maps (see
[
87
]
) implies that
k
is a Kraus operator of
T
if and only if
k^{*}({\,\cdot\,})k\leq_{cp}T
.

We denote by
K({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
the set of contractions
k:{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
that are Kraus operators of some quantum channel
T:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1})
that the agent can implement.
Identifying
{\mathcal{H}}
with
{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}
, the symbol
K({\mathbb{C}},{\mathbb{C}})
denotes the possible Kraus operators of implementable quantum channels that only act on the system. We introduce the shorthand
K:=K({\mathbb{C}},{\mathbb{C}})
.

Lemma 4.10
.

1.

K({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
contains all contractions of the form
1\otimes k_{0}
,
k_{0}:{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
.
In particular,
K({\mathcal{K}},{\mathcal{K}})
contains all scalars with absolute value
\leq 1
.

2.

If
k\in K({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
and
h\in K({\mathcal{K}}_{2},{\mathcal{K}}_{3})
, then
hk\in K({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{3})
.

3.

A quantum channel
T:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1})
can be implemented by the agent if and only if it has a Kraus decomposition with Kraus operators in
K({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
.

4.

If
k\in K({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
then
k^{*}\in K({\mathcal{K}}_{2},{\mathcal{K}}_{1})
.

5.

K({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
is closed in the strong operator topology.

6.

If
k_{1},\ldots k_{n}\in K({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
,
\lambda_{2},\ldots\lambda_{n}\in{\mathbb{C}}
satisfy
\lVert\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}k_{i}\rVert\leq 1
, then
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}k_{i}\in K
.

Proof.

1
is clear from
2
, and
2
is clear from
0
(consider the composition of channels that have
k
and
h
as their Kraus operators).

3
: The only
“only if”
implication holds by definition of the set
K({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
.
We show the converse using
lemma

4.8
and
5
.
Let
(k_{y})
,
k_{y}\in K({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
, be a collection of Kraus operators for
T
.
Then, by definition of
K({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
there exist quantum channels
S^{(y)}
,
y\in Y
, such that
k_{y}
is a Kraus operator of
S^{(y)}
.
By
lemma

4.8
, the agent can implement the two-outcome instrument
(S_{i}^{(y)})_{i=1,2}
with
S_{1}^{(y)}=k_{y}^{*}({\,\cdot\,})k_{y}
and
S_{2}^{(y)}=S^{(y)}-S_{1}^{(y)}
.
Since
\sum_{y}S_{1}^{(y)}(1)=\sum_{y}k_{y}^{*}k_{y}=1
,
5
implies that there is an implementable instrument
(T_{y})
with
T_{y}=S_{1}^{(y)}=k_{y}^{*}({\,\cdot\,})k_{y}
.
The implementability of
(T_{y})
implies that
T=\sum_{y}T_{y}
is implementable.

4
:
Let
0\neq k\in K({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
and let
T
be an implementable quantum channel with Kraus operators
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},\ldots
and
k_{1}=k
.
Our goal is to construct an implementable channel that has
k^{*}
as one of its Kraus operators.
Let
v:{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}
be the mnimal Stinespring isometry of
T
.
By the cp-order interval property of the Stinespring dilation, we can find rank-1 operators
q_{x}=|\Omega_{x}\rangle\langle\Omega_{x}|
with
\sum_{x}q_{x}=1
and
k_{x}^{*}({\,\cdot\,})k_{x}=v^{*}({\,\cdot\,}\otimes q_{x})
.
Adjusting the phases of the
\Omega_{x}
, we have
k_{x}=(1\otimes\langle\Omega_{x}|)v
.
Now
4
implies that there are Hilbert spaces
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{1},\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
, a vector
\Omega\in\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
and an implementable unitary
u:{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\otimes\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2}\otimes\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
such that
T=(1\otimes\langle\Omega|)u^{*}({\,\cdot\,})u(1\otimes|\Omega\rangle)
.
By
lemma

3.25
, there is an isometry
w:{\mathcal{K}}\to\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
such that
(1\otimes w)v=u(1\otimes|\Omega\rangle)
.
Setting
\Omega_{x}^{\prime}=w\Omega_{x}\in\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
, we find
k_{x}=(1\otimes\langle\Omega_{x}|)u(1\otimes|\Omega\rangle)
.
We set
\Phi=\lVert\Omega_{1}^{\prime}\rVert^{-1}\Omega_{1}^{\prime}\in\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
.
Let
\{\Psi_{y}\}_{y=1}^{\infty}
, be a collection of vectors in
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
with
\Psi_{1}=\lVert\Omega_{1}^{\prime}\rVert\Omega
and
\sum_{y}|\Psi_{y}\rangle\langle\Psi_{y}|=1
.
We set
m_{y}=(1\otimes\langle\Psi_{y}|)u^{*}(1\otimes|\Phi\rangle)
and note
m_{1}=k_{1}^{*}=k^{*}
.
By construction,

S=\sum_{y}m_{y}^{*}({\,\cdot\,})m_{y}=\sum_{y}(1\otimes\langle\Phi|)u({\,\cdot\,}\otimes|\Psi_{y}\rangle\langle\Psi_{y}|)u^{*}(1\otimes|\Phi\rangle)=(1\otimes\langle\Phi|)u({\,\cdot\,}\otimes 1)u^{*}(1\otimes|\Phi\rangle)

is a quantum channel.
The channel
S
is implementable since the unitary
u^{-1}
is, which is ensured by
3
. Since
k^{*}
is a Kraus operator of
S
, the claim follows.

5
:
Let
(k_{n})
be a strongly convergent net of operators in
K({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
and let
k
denote its limit.
Let
(T_{n}):{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1})
be a net of implementable quantum channels such that
k_{n}
is a Kraus operator of
T_{n}
.
By
lemma

4.9
, there is a point-
\sigma
-weakly convergent subnet
(T_{n_{m}})
and, by
1
, the agent can implement its limit
T
.
Then
T_{n}\geq_{cp}k_{n}^{*}({\,\cdot\,})k_{n}
for all
n
implies
T\geq_{cp}k^{*}({\,\cdot\,})k
.
Thus,
k
is a Kraus operator of
T
and, thus, an element of
K({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
.

6
:
We start by showing the following two claims:

1.

If
\sum_{i}\lvert\lambda_{i}\rvert^{2}=1
and
\sum_{i}k_{i}^{*}k_{i}\leq 1
, then
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}k_{i}\in K
.

2.

If
k\in K({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
,
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
and
\lVert\lambda k\rVert\leq 1
, then
\lambda k\in K
.

1
:
We can find a unitary
n\times n
matrix
(u_{ij})
with
u_{1j}=\lambda_{j}
.
By
item

3
, there is an implementable channel
T=\sum_{i}k_{i}^{*}({\,\cdot\,})k_{i}+T_{\perp}
. Set
l_{i}=\sum_{j}u_{ij}k_{j}
. Then
T=\sum_{i}l_{i}^{*}({\,\cdot\,})l_{i}+T_{\perp}
shows
l_{i}\in K({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
, and we have
l_{1}=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}k_{i}
.

2
:
By
1
,
\lVert k\rVert\leq 2^{-1/2}
implies
2^{1/2}k\in K
(take
(\lambda_{i})=(2^{-1/2},2^{-1/2},0,0\ldots)
).
By
1
and
2
, we have
\lambda k\in K({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
for all
k\in K({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
and
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
with
\lvert\lambda\rvert\leq 1
.
Together, these two observations imply the claim since every complex number
\lambda
is of the form
2^{n/2}\mu
with,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\mu\in{\mathbb{C}}
, and
\lvert\mu\rvert\leq 1
.

We
1
and
2
proven, we are now in the position to show
6
. Set
\hat{k}_{i}=rk_{i}
for sufficiently small
r>0
such that
\sum_{i}\hat{k}_{i}^{*}\hat{k}_{i}\leq 1
, and set
\hat{\lambda}_{i}=\lVert(\lambda_{i})\rVert_{\ell^{2}}^{-1}\lambda_{i}
.
Using
1
, we have
\sum_{i}\hat{\lambda}_{i}\hat{k}_{i}\in K({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
.
Thus,
2
implies that
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}k_{i}=(\lVert(\lambda_{i})\rVert_{\ell^{2}}/r)\sum_{i}\hat{\lambda}_{i}\hat{k}_{i}\in K
.
∎

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
K_{0}\subset{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
be a set of contractions with
1\in K_{0}
.
Suppose that

•

K
is closed under products and adjoints;

•

if
k,h\in K_{0}
,
\lambda,\mu\in{\mathbb{C}}
and
\lVert\lambda k+\mu h\rVert\leq 1
, then
\lambda k+\mu h\in K_{0}
;

Then the positive scalar multiples
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathbb{R}}^{+}\cdot K_{0}
form a unital *-algebra of bounded operators whose unit ball is
K_{0}
.
If
K_{0}
is closed in one of the locally convex operator topologies, then
{\mathcal{M}}
is a von Neumann algebra.

Proof.

The properties of a *-algebra are easily checked.
That
K_{0}
is exactly the unit ball of
{\mathcal{M}}
follows from the second item.
The second claim follows from Kaplansky’s density theorem
[
54
, Thm. II.4.8]
, which states that the unit ball of a *-algebra is
\sigma
-strongly* dense in the unit ball of the von Neumann algebra it generates.
∎

Corollary 4.12
.

{\mathcal{M}}={\mathbb{R}}^{+}\cdot K
is a von Neumann algebra on
{\mathcal{H}}
and the following hold:

1.

K({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
is precisely the set of
{\mathcal{M}}
-contractions
k:{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
.

2.

The agent can implement an instrument
(T_{x})
with
T_{x}:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1})
if and only if it is
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner.

Proof.

Lemma

4.11
shows that the properties of
K
that are listed in
lemma

4.10
make
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathbb{R}}^{+}\cdot K
a von Neumann algebra whose unit ball is precisely
K
.

1
:
The case
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}={\mathcal{K}}_{2}={\mathbb{C}}
is simply the statement above that
K
is the unit ball of
{\mathcal{M}}
.
By
item

1
of
lemma

4.10
, we have
1\otimes\langle\Omega|\in K({\mathcal{K}},{\mathbb{C}})
and
1\otimes|\Omega\rangle\in K({\mathbb{C}},{\mathcal{K}})
for all unit vectors
\Omega\in{\mathcal{K}}
.
Thus
item

2
of
lemma

4.10
imply that if
k\in K({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
, then
(1\otimes\langle\Omega_{2}|)k(1\otimes|\Omega_{1}\rangle)\in{\mathcal{M}}
for all
\Omega_{j}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{j}
,
j=1,2
.
This shows that
K({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
is a subset of the set of
{\mathcal{M}}
-operators.
Conversely, let
k
be an
{\mathcal{M}}
-operator.
Picking bases
\{|x\rangle_{j}\}
of
{\mathcal{K}}_{j}
,
j=1,2
, we can write
k=\sum k_{ij}\otimes|i\rangle\langle j|
with the sum being strongly convergent.
Thus, we can approximate
k
with linear combinations of operators of the form
k_{0}\otimes|i\rangle\langle j|
with
k_{0}\in{\mathcal{M}}
a contraction.
By
items

5
and
6
of
lemma

4.10
, it suffices to show that these operators are in
K({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
.
Let
T_{0}
be a channel on
T
which has
k_{0}
as one of its Kraus operators.
Let
S:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{1})
be a quantum channel, which has
|i\rangle\langle j|
as one of its Kraus operators, e.g.,
S=\langle i|({\,\cdot\,})|i\rangle 1_{{\mathcal{K}}_{2}}
.
Since
k_{0}\otimes|\Omega_{1}\rangle\langle\Omega_{2}|
is a Kraus operator of the channel
T_{0}\otimes S
, which is implementable by
2
, we have
k\in K({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
.

2
:
This follows from
item

3
of
lemma

4.10
.
∎

Proof of
theorem

4.5
.

Corollary

4.12
shows the
“only if”
implication of
theorem

4.5
.

It remains to show the converse:
We consider an agent who can implement precisely the
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner operations
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1})
for arbitrary
{\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
, and show that the axioms
0
to
5
hold:
Axioms
0
holds since the class of
{\mathcal{M}}
-operators is closed under products.
Axiom
1
is checked using
lemma

3.27
: If
T_{n}:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1})
is a net of
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner channels converging to
T
in the topology of convergence in expectation values (the point-
\sigma
-weak topology), then
T_{n}(a^{\prime}\otimes 1_{{\mathcal{K}}_{2}})=a^{\prime}\otimes 1_{{\mathcal{K}}_{1}}
for all
a^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
implies
T(a^{\prime}\otimes 1_{{\mathcal{K}}_{2}})=a^{\prime}\otimes 1_{{\mathcal{K}}_{1}}
.
Hence,
T
is
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner by
lemma

3.27
.
Axiom
2
is clear since all instruments
(T_{x})
of the form
T_{x}=\id_{{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})}\otimes S_{x}
, for an instrument
(S_{x})
with
S_{x}:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{1})
, are
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner.
Axiom
3
is clear: If
u\in{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
is a unitary, then its inverse
u^{-1}=u^{*}
is in
{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{2},{\mathcal{K}}_{1})
.
Axiom

is shown in

3.28
.
It remains to check axiom
5
.
Let
(T_{x}^{(y)})
be
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner instruments with
T_{x}^{(y)}:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1})
. If (
4.13
) holds for a selection map
y\mapsto x_{y}
, then the
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner cp map
S_{Y}=\sum_{y}T_{x_{y}}^{(y)}
is subunital.
Let
L:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{1})
be some
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner quantum channel, e.g.,
\id_{\mathcal{H}}\otimes\omega({\,\cdot\,})1
for some

, and set
S_{\perp}=(1-S_{Y}(1))^{1/2}L({\,\cdot\,})(1-S_{Y}(1))^{1/2}
.
Then
(S_{y})_{y\in\tilde{Y}}
with
\tilde{Y}=Y\cup\{\perp\}
is indeed an
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner instrument.
∎

4.4
No information without disturbance

We consider an agent
A
and a referee
R
, both of whom have partial access to a quantum system with Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
.
We assume that the state of the full quantum system is known to the referee but unknown to the agent.
We ask:

What can the agent measure that cannot be detected by the referee?

If the referee has access to the full quantum system, this question reduces to the classic question
What can be measured without disturbing the system?
with the well-known answer
Nothing

[
105
,
106
,
107
]
.
The assumption that the state of the full system is unknown to the agent is crucial. If the agent has partial knowledge about the system’s state, e.g., knows that the state commutes with certain observables, the agent can perform undetectable operations that reveal further information.
22
22

22

Assume, for instance, that both the agent and the referee have full access to the system and assume that the agent knows that the system’s state
\phi
commutes with a self-adjoint operator
h
on
{\mathcal{H}}
.
If
p
is a spectral projection of
h
, consider the instrument
(T_{1},T_{2})=(p({\,\cdot\,})p,p^{\perp}({\,\cdot\,})p^{\perp}))
.
Since
p
commutes with
\phi
, we have
T_{*}(\phi)=\phi
, i.e., the agent can measure without disturbing the system.

We will characterize the information that can be extracted secretly by an agent
A
as what is detectable with measurements in the von Neumann algebra

{\mathcal{M}}_{A}\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{R}^{\prime},

(4.14)

where
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{R}
are the von Neumann algebras associated with the agent and the referee, respectively.
This has two important consequences for us:

•

We understand the
center

Z({\mathcal{M}}_{A})={\mathcal{M}}_{A}\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime}
of the observable algebra
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
of the agent
A
as describing the information that can be extracted without disturbance relative to the agent (see also
[
38
]
).
Thus, the observable algebra
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
is a
factor
if and only if the agent cannot extract information without disturbing the state, relative to the agent’s observables.

•

In
cryptographic
settings, it is necessary to understand what an eavesdropper is able to learn about a given subsystem in secret.
Here, the roles of the agent and the referee are swapped:
We assume that the full system is in a state
\phi
, known to the referee (i.e., the referee knows the marginal state
\phi|_{{\mathcal{M}}_{R}}
), and that the referee encodes classical information
y
by choosing an operation, e.g., a unitary
u_{y}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{R}
.
The agent, who plays the role of an eavesdropper, wishes to learn the information
y
and performs an instrument
(T_{x})
.
This attack cannot be detected by the referee precisely when
(T_{x})
is
{\mathcal{M}}_{R}^{\prime}
-inner.

The precise setup is as follows:
The full system is in a state
\phi
, unknown to the agent, but known to the referee (i.e., the referee knows the marginal state
\phi|_{{\mathcal{M}}_{R}}
).
The referee’s goal is to decide whether the agent performs an instrument
(T_{x})
or not.
Since the referee would not know the agent’s measurement outcome, applying the instrument would, from the referee’s point of view, result in applying the quantum channel
T=\sum_{x}T_{x}
.
Thus, the referee’s job is to decide whether the system’s state is
\phi
or
T_{*}(\phi)
.
This is possible if and only if the referee can implement a measurement whose outcomes are distributed differently for these two states.
(We consider quantitative notions of state distinguishability in
section

4.7
.)
Since the referee can implement any POVM in
{\mathcal{M}}_{R}
, this is equivalent to
\phi|_{{\mathcal{M}}_{R}}\neq T_{*}(\phi)|_{{\mathcal{M}}_{R}}
.
Let us note this key fact as a Lemma:

Lemma 4.13
.

A pair of normal states
\phi,\psi
on a full quantum system can be distinguished by an agent with observable algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
if and only if
\phi|_{\mathcal{M}}\neq\psi|_{\mathcal{M}}
.

Returning to our problem, we conclude that
\phi|_{{\mathcal{M}}_{R}}\neq T_{*}(\phi)|_{{\mathcal{M}}_{R}}
must hold for all normal states
\phi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{H}})
. Otherwise, the agent, who does not know the state of the system, cannot have a guarantee that the referee does not know of his actions.
This property is much easier to state in the Heisenberg picture, where it simply takes the simple form
T\restriction{\mathcal{M}}_{R}=\id
.
By
lemma

4.8
, the requirement
T\restriction{\mathcal{M}}_{R}
is equivalent to
{\mathcal{M}}_{R}^{\prime}
-innerness of
(T_{x})
.
Thus, we have

\text{$(T_{x})$ cannot be detected}\quad\iff\quad(T_{x})\ \text{is ${\mathcal{M}}_{R}^{\prime}$-inner.}

(4.15)

If the referee has full access, i.e.,
{\mathcal{M}}_{R}={\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
, this implies the usual statement: If an instrument
(T_{x})
does not disturb the system in the sense that
\sum_{x}T_{x}=\id
, then
(T_{x})
is inner for
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})^{\prime}={\mathbb{C}}1
, and, therefore, the measurement that it implements cannot reveal information about the state of the system since
T_{x}(1)\propto 1
for all
x
.

In the general case, where both the agent and the referee have partial access to the full system, (
4.15
), tells us that the instruments which the agent can implement without detection from the referee are the
({\mathcal{M}}_{A}\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{R}^{\prime})
-inner ones, i.e., instruments of the form

T_{x}=\sum_{y}k_{y|x}^{*}({\,\cdot\,})k_{y|x},\qquad k_{y|x}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{A}\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{R}^{\prime}.

(4.16)

If the referee and the agent have access to the same observables, i.e., if
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}={\mathcal{M}}_{R}
, then the application of an instrument
(T_{x})
is undetectable to the agent if and only if the instrument is
central
in the sense that the Kraus operators
k_{y|x}
are contained in the center
Z({\mathcal{M}}_{A})={\mathcal{M}}_{A}\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime}
.

For the case that the referee and the agent have access to the same observables, an
information-disturbance trade-off
, i.e., a quantitative version of the above statement, was obtained in
[
106
,
107
]
.
It would be interesting to see whether this can be generalized to the case of a referee with partial access to the full system.

4.5
Bipartite systems, product states and local state preparation

We want to understand when two subsystems can be regarded as independent in the sense that measurements on one subsystem can be performed jointly with measurements on the other subsystem.
This is the case if and only if all measurements on one system commute with all measurements on the other system
[
108
]
.
More generally, we say that two agents
A
and
B
, called Alice and Bob, which both have constrained access to the same full quantum system, are
independent
if all measurements of Alice commute with all measurements of Bob.
Clearly, this is the case if and only if their observable algebras
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
commute, and it follows that

T_{A}\circ T_{B}=T_{B}\circ T_{A}

(4.17)

for all channels
T_{A/B}
the agents can implement, respectively.
Of course, the analogous statement holds for instruments.
Independent agents can perform
correlation experiments
on the full quantum system:
If
\phi
is a state of the full system, and Alice and Bob perform measurements
(m_{x}^{A})
,
(m_{y}^{B})
, respectively, then the outcomes are distributed according to the probability distribution

p(x,y)=\phi(m_{x}^{A}\,m_{y}^{B}).

(4.18)

If we fix the agent Alice, then the most powerful agent
B
that is independent from Alice is the one whose observable algebra is the commutant
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}={\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime}
of Alice’s observable algebra (see (
4.12
)).
Equivalently, independence of a pair of agents can be defined by saying that agent neither of the two agents can detect whether the other agent has applied an operation.
Indeed, the equivalence immediately follows from the results in
section

4.4
(see (
4.15
)).

A common paradigm in quantum information theory is that of
separated labs
, which describes the idea that Alice and Bob are spatially separated and are free to do whatever they want in their respective labs.
For instance, it is generally assumed that the local agents can discard their share of an entangled state and locally prepare a new state, which results in a product state of the full system.
The notion of independence given above is generally not sufficient to allow for such seemingly trivial maneuvers.
In fact, there are
no product states
in general:

Lemma 4.14

(
[
26
]
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
be commuting factors on a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
.
The following are equivalent:

1.

Product states: For some pair (resp. all pairs) of normal states
\phi_{A/B}
on
{\mathcal{M}}_{A/B}
, there is a state
\phi
of the full system, such that

\phi(ab)=\phi_{A}(a)\phi_{B}(b),\qquad(a,b)\in{\mathcal{M}}_{A}\times{\mathcal{M}}_{B}.

(4.19)

2.

Tensor product: There are Hilbert spaces
{\mathcal{H}}_{A/B}
with factors
{\mathcal{N}}_{A/B}\subset{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{A/B})
such that

({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B})\cong({\mathcal{H}}_{A}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{B},{\mathcal{N}}_{A}\otimes 1,1\otimes{\mathcal{N}}_{B}).

(4.20)

3.

Split property: here is a type
{\mathrm{I}}
factor
{\mathcal{N}}
such that
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}\subset{\mathcal{N}}\subset{\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The implications
1

\Leftarrow

2

\Leftrightarrow

3
are clear.

1

\Rightarrow

3
: By
[
54
, Ex. IV.5.1.(b)]
,
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}\vee{\mathcal{M}}_{B}\cong{\mathcal{M}}_{A}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
. By
[
26
, Thm. 3.9]
, this implies
3
.
∎

This tells us that product states only exist if and only if the observable algebras of the two agents live on distinct tensor factors of the underlying Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
.
Even if product states exist, they cannot be prepared locally relative to all observables of the respective agents, unless the von Neumann algebras are type
{\mathrm{I}}
factors.
To understand this, we begin with the following result, shown by Werner in the context of quantum field theory in
[
39
]
, which characterizes the milder requirement of preparing a state only for a subalgebra:

Lemma 4.15

(
[
39
]
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}_{0}\subset{\mathcal{M}}
be factors. The following are equivalent:

1.

For some/every normal state
\phi_{0}
on
{\mathcal{M}}_{0}
there is an inner ucp map
T:{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathcal{M}}
such that
T|_{{\mathcal{M}}_{0}}=\phi_{0}({\,\cdot\,})1

2.

Split property: There is a type
{\mathrm{I}}
factor
{\mathcal{N}}
such that
{\mathcal{M}}_{0}\subset{\mathcal{N}}\subset{\mathcal{M}}
.

Proof.

(
1
,
“some”
)
\Rightarrow

2
:
Let
{\mathcal{M}}
act on a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
and extend
T
to a normal ucp map on
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
by using a Kraus decomposition in
{\mathcal{M}}
.
Now let
\psi
be a normal state on
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
and let
\phi=T_{*}(\psi)
(
=\psi\circ T
).
Then
\phi(a_{0}a^{\prime})=\psi(T(a_{0}a^{\prime})=\psi(T(a_{0})a^{\prime})=\phi_{0}(a_{0})\psi(a^{\prime})
,
(a_{0},a^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{M}}_{0}\times{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
shows that
\phi
is a product state for the commuting algebras
{\mathcal{M}}_{0}
and
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
.
By
lemma

4.14
, the split property holds.

2

\Rightarrow
(
1
,
“every”
):
Given a normal state
\phi_{0}
on
{\mathcal{M}}_{0}
, pick a normal state
\phi
on
{\mathcal{N}}
extending
\phi_{0}
.
Since
{\mathcal{N}}
is a type
{\mathrm{I}}
factor, there are Kraus operators
\{k_{x}\}
in
{\mathcal{N}}
with
T(a):=\sum_{x}k_{x}^{*}(a)k_{x}=\phi(a)1
for all
a\in{\mathcal{N}}
and, hence,
T(a)=\phi_{0}(a)1
for
a\in{\mathcal{M}}_{0}
.
Clearly,
T
is
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner and unital.
∎

As a consequence, we learn that it is impossible to prepare states with inner operations on the full von Neumann algebra, unless the latter is a type
{\mathrm{I}}
factor:

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a von Neumann algebra and assume that there is an inner ucp map
T:{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathcal{M}}
of the form
T=\phi({\,\cdot\,})1
for a normal state
\phi
on
{\mathcal{M}}
.
Then
{\mathcal{M}}
is a type
{\mathrm{I}}
factor.

Proof.

Since
T
is inner, we have
T\restriction{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}=\id
, which implies that
T\restriction Z({\mathcal{M}})=\id
.
Thus, we have
Z({\mathcal{M}})=T(Z({\mathcal{M}}))=\phi(Z({\mathcal{M}}))1={\mathbb{C}}1
, which shows that the center
Z({\mathcal{M}})
is trivial, i.e.,
{\mathcal{M}}
is a factor.
By
lemma

4.15
(applied to
{\mathcal{M}}_{0}={\mathcal{M}}
), it follows that
{\mathcal{M}}
is type
{\mathrm{I}}
.
∎

We return to independent agents with partial access to a full quantum system.
The following result characterizes when correlation experiments fully determine the states of the full quantum system.
In particular, this is only possible if both
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are factors:

Lemma 4.17
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
be commuting von Neumann algebras on
{\mathcal{H}}
.
The following are equivalent:
23
23

23

The Lemma and its proof generalize verbatim to any finite number of pairwise commuting von Neumann algebras.

1.

A state of the full quantum system is completely determined through correlation experiments of
A
and
B
, i.e., if
\phi
and
\psi
are states of the full system, then
\psi=\phi
if and only if

\phi(m^{A}_{x}\,m^{B}_{y})=\psi(m^{A}_{x}\,m^{B}_{y})

(4.21)

for all measurements
(m_{x}^{A})
,
(m_{y}^{B})
of the respective agents.

2.

The only measurements that commute with all measurements of
A
and
B
are the trivial ones. I.e.,
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime}\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}={\mathbb{C}}1
or, what is equivalent,

{\mathcal{M}}_{A}\vee{\mathcal{M}}_{B}={\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}).

(4.22)

3.

{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are irreducible, i.e., there is no proper closed subspace of
{\mathcal{H}}
that is both
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
- and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
-invariant.

In this case,
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are necessarily factors.

Proof.

2

\Leftrightarrow

3
is clear because the projections onto jointly invariant closed subspaces are precisely the projections of the von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime}\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}=({\mathcal{M}}_{A}\vee{\mathcal{M}}_{B})^{\prime}
.

1

\Rightarrow

2
:
Let
u\in({\mathcal{M}}_{A}\vee{\mathcal{M}}_{B})^{\prime}
be a unitary and let
\phi
be a normal state on
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
.
Then
\phi
and
u\phi u^{*}
cannot be distinguished with correlation experiments, hence
1
forces
u=1
. Thus,
({\mathcal{M}}_{A}\vee{\mathcal{M}}_{B})^{\prime}={\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime}\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}={\mathbb{C}}1
.

2

\Rightarrow

1
:
Let
\psi,\phi
be normal states on
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
.
By assumption the *-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
generated by
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
is dense.
Every element of
{\mathcal{A}}
is a linear combination of products of the form
ab
for effects
a\in[0,1]_{{\mathcal{M}}_{A}}
and
b\in[0,1]_{{\mathcal{M}}_{B}}
.
Thus, (
4.21
) is equivalent to the statement that
\psi
and
\phi
are equal on a
\sigma
-weakly dense *-subalgebra. By normality, this is equivalent to
\psi=\phi
.

The fact that
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are factors follows from (
4.22
) because
Z({\mathcal{M}}_{A})\subset Z({\mathcal{M}}_{A}\vee{\mathcal{M}}_{B})=Z({\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}))={\mathbb{C}}1
.
∎

Based on the above, we now discuss what it means to have a
bipartite system
.
Of course, a precise formalization is not achievable without a proper definition of what constitutes a subsystem.
In all cases, the observables that are localized on the respective subsystem will be commuting.
In our framework, this results in commuting von Neumann algebras
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
,
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
on
{\mathcal{H}}
.
Apart from commutativity, the following two requirements are natural:

1.

The full system should be composed of the two subsystems, in the sense of the following equivalent properties (see
lemma

4.17
):

(a)

states of the full system are fully determined by correlation experiments;

(b)

measurements that commute with all measurements of both subsystems are trivial.

2.

The two subsystems should be
“complementary”
in the sense that one subsystem describes precisely those observables of the full system that commute with the other system, i.e.,
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are commutants:

{\mathcal{M}}_{A}={\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}.

(4.23)

We refer to the first property as
irreducibility
, which is justified by
lemma

4.17
.
The second property is known as
Haag duality

[
17
]
, named after a similar property with the same name in quantum field theory
[
19
]
(see also
section

4.9.2
).
Neither of these two properties implies the other: Irreducibility requires that
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are factors, which does not follow from Haag duality.
On the other hand, Haag duality is not automatic from irreducibility.
If Haag duality holds, then
Z({\mathcal{M}}_{A})=Z({\mathcal{M}}_{B})
, and irreducibility is equivalent to factoriality of
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and, therefore, also equivalent to factoriality of
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
.
If the first property holds, then
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}\subset{\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}\subset{\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime}
are so-called irreducible subfactor inclusions, which means that the relative commutant
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime}\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}={\mathbb{C}}1
is trivial (see
lemma

4.17
)
[
109
,
90
,
110
]
.

Haag duality is sometimes described as a
“technical assumption”

[
17
,
111
,
20
]
.
It is the opinion of the author that this falls somewhat short.
There are cases where the failure of Haag duality is understood on physical grounds (see, e.g.,
[
91
,
40
,
92
,
93
]
).
In
section

6.6
, we show that Haag duality has an operational interpretation that only refers to local operations:
24
24

24

This is not the case for the formulation above. Indeed, suppose that
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}\subset{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{B}
is a proper inclusion. Then the above refers to those operations that are not in
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
.
,
25
25

25

This operational interpretation does not apply to the weaker version of Haag duality studied in
[
112
,
113
,
114
]
.

Haag duality holds if and only if pure states with the same marginals on one subsystem are related by unitaries of the other subsystem.

We analyze bipartite systems and, in particular, the resulting entanglement properties, in
sections

6
,
7
and
8
in great detail.
Even though we regard both of the assumptions above as natural, we often assume Haag duality without factoriality because many of our theorems will not need this assumption.

We close this subsection by combining what we learned in the above and
section

4.4
about the information-theoretic properties that correspond to factoriality of the observable algebra:

Corollary 4.18
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
be a von Neumann algebra on
{\mathcal{H}}
.
The following are equivalent:

1.

{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
is a factor;

2.

there is von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
that commutes with
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
such that correlation experiments of the pair
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B})
(see (
4.18
)) provide full information about normal states on
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
;
26
26

26

While the choice
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}={\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime}
always is an example if
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
is a factor, it is not the unique choice for
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
.
Indeed, every irreducible subfactor
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}\subset{\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime}
is just as good.

3.

there is a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
that commutes with
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
such that the full system does not have nontrivial operations that commute with the operations of
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
;

4.

it is not possible to extract information about the state of the full system with
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
-inner operations in a way that cannot be detected with measurements in
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
.

4.6
Entanglement and LOCC

In the following, we go through the basics of entanglement theory in the von Neumann algebraic setting.
An in-depth analysis of bipartite entanglement under the assumption of Haag duality will be carried out in
sections

6
,
7
and
8
.
Entanglement is nowadays understood as the resource that LOCC cannot create
[
13
,
115
]
.
Thus, in order to talk about entanglement, we need to make sense of LOCC in our settings.

We consider a full quantum system with Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
together with a finite number of agents
A_{1},\ldots A_{N}
, each of which has partial access to the full system.
We assume the agents to be pairwise independent, so that the corresponding von Neumann algebras
{\mathcal{M}}_{j}={\mathcal{M}}_{A_{j}}\subset{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
are pairwise commuting.

A general LOCC protocol is of the following form:
In the first round, one of the agents performs an instrument and then communicates whatever outcome they measure to the other agents.
In each subsequent round, some agent chooses an instrument based on the outcomes of the previous rounds, applies it, and communicates the results to the other agents.
The protocol terminates after a finite number
r
of rounds.
The protocol itself is the algorithm that determines the order in which the agents act and which operations they apply based on which messages they receive.
Thus, in each run and for each initial state
\psi
, an LOCC protocol produces a string
x=x_{1}\cdots x_{r}
of outcomes and an output state
\psi_{x}^{\prime}
.
Therefore, the protocol defines an instrument
(T_{x})
whose outcome space
X
is the set of all possible strings
x_{1}\cdots x_{r}
.

If
\psi
and
\phi
are states of the full system, we say that
\psi
can be transformed to
\phi
with LOCC by the of agents
A_{1},\ldots A_{N}
if there exists an LOCC protocol with instrument
\{T_{x}\}_{x\in X}
such that, on average, the instrument takes
\psi
to
\phi
, i.e.,
T_{*}(\psi)=\phi
, where
T=\sum_{x}T_{x}
.
We denote this by

\psi\xrightarrow{\mathrm{LOCC}}\phi.

Note that we only ask that
\psi
is taken to
\phi
on average.
However, in the case that
\phi
is a pure state, it follows that each of the post-measurement states
\psi_{x}^{\prime}
is equal to
\phi
.
More precisely,
\psi_{x}^{\prime}=\phi
if
p_{x}>0
(otherwise, the post-measurement states are not defined).

As we said above, entanglement is defined as the resource that LOCC cannot create.
This defines comparative entanglement theory: A state is more entangled than a given state if the former can be reached from the latter with LOCC, at least approximately.
We can, of course, just take this definition and apply it to a collection of pairwise independent agents with constrained access to the full system.
It is a matter of taste whether one feels comfortable in calling the resulting relation entanglement, even in cases where the agents do not correspond to independent subsystems of the whole system.
In any case, it does not really matter as the concept of LOCC transitions is meaningful even in the general setting of pairwise independent agents with constrained access to the full system.

4.7
State distinguishability

We discuss two ways to operationally quantify the distance of quantum states relative to an agent with partial access to the full system.
These two quantifications are the
norm distance
and the
fidelity
.
To make sense of the latter, we discuss Uhlmann’s Theorem.

Norm distance

In order to distinguish a pair
\psi,\phi
of states of the full system, the agent performs measurements, which result in outcome distributions that are as different as possible for the two states
\psi
and
\phi
.
Since the agents wish to distinguish between two hypotheses, they only need to consider binary measurements.
The simplest form to do so is to find an effect
f
for which the two success probabilities
\psi(f)
and
\phi(f)
are as different as possible.
Mathematically, this amounts to optimizing
\lvert\psi(f)-\phi(f)\rvert
over all effects that are accessible to the agent.
Using the map
f\mapsto 2f-1
, which is an affine bijection between
[0,1]_{\mathcal{M}}
and
[-1,1]_{\mathcal{M}}
(the set of self-adjoint contractions in
{\mathcal{M}}
), we recognize this as half the norm distance of the induced states on
{\mathcal{M}}
:

\sup_{f\in[0,1]_{\mathcal{M}}}\lvert\psi(f)-\phi(f)\rvert=\frac{1}{2}\sup_{f\in[-1,1]_{\mathcal{M}}}\lvert(\psi-\phi)(f)\rvert=\frac{1}{2}\lVert\psi|_{\mathcal{M}}-\phi|_{\mathcal{M}}\rVert.

(4.24)

This tells us that the norm distance of states on
{\mathcal{M}}
has an operational interpretation in terms of state distinguishability.
If the agent has full access, i.e., if
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
, this corresponds precisely to the operational interpretation of the trace distance
[
81
, Sec. 9.2.1]
.

A central property of the norm distance is that it is monotone under inner quantum channels.
In fact, for normal ucp maps
T:{\mathcal{N}}\to{\mathcal{M}}
between von Neumann algebras
{\mathcal{M}}
and
{\mathcal{N}}
, which we call outer quantum channels (see
section

4.8
), it holds

\lVert T_{*}(\omega_{1})-T_{*}(\omega_{2})\rVert\leq\lVert\omega_{1}-\omega_{2}\rVert,\qquad\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{M}}).

(4.25)

This is called the
data processing inequality
(for the norm distance) in the quantum information theory literature
[
81
]
.
This reflects the fact that a quantum channel cannot increase the distinguishability of two states.
Mathematically, (
4.25
) is a direct consequence of the fact that ucp maps, and hence their preduals, are norm contractions.
If
\Psi
and
\Phi
are unit vectors and if
\psi,\phi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{H}})
denote the corresponding normal pure states, then

\lVert\psi|_{\mathcal{M}}-\phi|_{\mathcal{M}}\rVert\leq 2\lVert\Psi-\Phi\rVert.

(4.26)

Fidelity

We begin by recalling how the fidelity is defined in the usual framework of quantum information theory.
We then follow Hiai’s definition of the fidelity for normal states on a von Neumann algebra via Haagerup
L^{p}
-spaces
[
35
]
.
Afterward, we generalize Uhlmann’s Theorem, which connects the intrinsically defined fidelity with any given representation of the von Neumann algebra.
As a consequence of Uhlmann’s theorem, the equivalence with Uhlmann’s original definition of the fidelity
[
116
]
follows.
Moreover, we prove the Fuchs-van de Graaf inequalities, which we use to show that convergence in fidelity is equivalent to norm convergence.
Most, if not all, of these results here are well-known in the literature.

The fidelity of a pair
\psi,\phi
of normal states on
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
is defined as
F(\psi,\phi)=\lVert\rho_{\psi}^{1/2}\rho_{\phi}^{1/2}\rVert_{1}^{2}
, where
\rho_{\phi},\rho_{\psi}
denote the corresponding density operators in
{\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{H}})

[
81
]
.
In the case that
\psi
and
\phi
are pure states with implementing vectors
\Psi,\Phi
, the Fidelity becomes

F(\psi,\phi)=\lvert\langle\Psi,\Phi\rangle\rvert^{2}.

(4.27)

This quantity has an operational interpretation in terms of state distinguishability.
Indeed, this follows from the operational interpretation of the norm distance, here applied to pure states, since
\lVert\psi-\phi\rVert=\lVert|\Psi\rangle\langle\Psi|-|\Phi\rangle\langle\Phi|\rVert_{1}=2\sqrt{1-\lvert\langle\Psi,\Phi\rangle\rvert^{2}}=2\sqrt{1-F(\psi,\phi)}
.
The operational interpretation of the fidelity for pure states is extended to arbitrary normal states on
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
by Uhlmann’s theorem and its consequences
[
81
,
117
]
.

Following Hiai
[
35
]
, the fidelity of a pair
\psi,\phi
of normal states on a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
is defined through the framework of Haagerup
L^{p}
-spaces (see
section

3.3.3
)

F(\psi,\phi)=\lVert h_{\psi}^{1/2}h_{\phi}^{1/2}\rVert_{L^{1}({\mathcal{M}})}^{2}.

(4.28)

Since the Haagerup
L^{p}
-spaces are isomorphic to the Schatten classes for
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
, (
4.28
) reduces to the standard definition in this case.
The fidelity is 1 if and only if the states are equal:

F(\psi,\phi)=1\iff\psi=\phi
.
The
Fuchs-van de Graaf inequalities

[
118
]

1-F(\psi,\phi)^{1/2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\lVert\psi-\phi\rVert\leq(1-F(\psi,\phi))^{1/2},

(4.29)

\psi,\phi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{M}})
, show that convergence in fidelity is equivalent to convergence in norm. We will prove (
4.29
) as a consequence of Uhlmann’s Theorem below.
Like the norm distance, the fidelity satisfies a data processing inequality: If
T:{\mathcal{N}}\to{\mathcal{M}}
is a normal ucp map, then
[
35
, Rem. 3.15 & Thm. 3.16]
:

\displaystyle F(T_{*}(\psi),T_{*}(\phi))\geq F(\psi,\phi)

(4.30)

for all pairs of normal states on
{\mathcal{M}}
.
In particular, the fidelity of states on
{\mathcal{M}}
is monotone under
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner quantum channels.

We will prove two versions of Uhlmann’s theorem:

Theorem 4.19
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a von Neumann algebra on
{\mathcal{H}}
and let
\psi,\phi
be normal states on
{\mathcal{M}}
.
Then

F(\psi,\phi)=\sup_{\tilde{\psi},\tilde{\phi}}\,F(\tilde{\psi},\tilde{\phi}),

(4.31)

where the optimization is over normal states
\tilde{\psi},\tilde{\phi}
on
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
that extend
\psi
and
\phi
, respectively.
In fact, for every fixed extension
\tilde{\psi}
of
\psi
, we have
F(\psi,\phi)=\sup_{\tilde{\phi}}F(\tilde{\psi},\tilde{\phi})
.

A purification in
{\mathcal{H}}
of a state
\psi
on
{\mathcal{M}}
is a vector
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
such that
\psi(a)=\langle\Psi,a\Psi\rangle
,
a\in{\mathcal{M}}
.
If the states
\psi
and
\phi
admit purifications in
{\mathcal{H}}
, we have:

Theorem 4.20
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a von Neumann algebra on
{\mathcal{H}}
.
Let
\psi,\phi
be states on
{\mathcal{M}}
that have purifications in
{\mathcal{H}}
.
Then

F(\psi,\phi)=\sup_{\Psi,\Phi}\,\lvert\langle\Psi,\Phi\rangle\rvert^{2},

(4.32)

where the optimization is over all purifications
\Psi
of
\psi
and
\Phi
of
\phi
in
{\mathcal{H}}
.
If
\Psi
is a fixed purification, we have
F(\psi,\phi)=\sup_{\Phi}\lvert\langle\Psi,\Phi\rangle\rvert
.

We emphasize that the RHSs of
eqs.

4.31
and
4.32
depend on the representation
{\mathcal{H}}
, but that the LHSs are defined intrinsically.
Uhlmann’s theorem extends the operational meaning of the fidelity of normal states on
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
(see above) to states on a von Neumann algebra.

Theorem

4.20
is essentially contained in the literature:
It follows from first applying
[
119
, Cor. 2]
of Alberti to the standard representation, where the equivalence of (
4.28
) and the RHS of (
4.32
) can be checked explicitly, and then to a general representation.
The result does not follow directly from
[
119
, Cor. 2]
because the definition of the fidelity in (
4.32
) is different from Uhlmann’s definition
[
116
]
, which is used by Alberti.
In the following, we give a self-contained proof of
theorems

4.20
and
4.19
.

Lemma 4.21
.

Under the assumptions of
theorem

4.20
, let
\Psi_{0},\Phi_{0}
be a pair of purifications.
Then

\sup_{\Psi,\Phi}\,\lvert\langle\Psi,\Phi\rangle\rvert=\sup_{u^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})}\lvert\langle\Psi_{0},u^{\prime}\Phi_{0}\rangle\rvert=\sup_{s^{\prime}\in B({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})}\lvert\langle\Psi_{0},s^{\prime}\Phi_{0}\rangle\rvert,

(4.33)

where the optimization on the LHS is as in
theorem

4.20
.

Proof.

The first equality follows from
corollary

3.23
, and the second equality follows from the fact that the weak closure of
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}})
is
B({\mathcal{M}})
(see
lemma

3.20
).
∎

Proof of
theorem

4.20
.

For the sake of this proof, we denote the RHS of (
4.32
) by
F_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi,\phi)
.

Step 1.
We assume that
{\mathcal{M}}
acts by left multiplication on the Haagerup
L^{2}
-space
{\mathcal{H}}=L^{2}({\mathcal{M}})
.
Then
h_{\psi}^{1/2}
and
h_{\phi}^{1/2}
are purifications of
\psi
and
\phi
, respectively, so that
lemma

4.21
implies

\displaystyle F_{L^{2}({\mathcal{M}})}(\psi,\phi)=\sup_{s^{\prime}\in B({\mathcal{M}})}\lvert\langle h_{\psi}^{1/2},s^{\prime}h_{\phi}^{1/2}\rangle_{L^{2}({\mathcal{M}})}\rvert^{2}=\sup_{s\in B({\mathcal{M}})}\lvert\tr(h_{\psi}^{1/2}h_{\phi}^{1/2}s)\rvert^{2}=\lVert h_{\psi}^{1/2}h_{\phi}^{1/2}\rVert_{L^{1}({\mathcal{M}})}^{2},

where we used the following facts: the commutant of
{\mathcal{M}}
is the action of
{\mathcal{M}}
on
L^{2}({\mathcal{M}})
by right multiplication
[
56
, Thm. 9.29]
, and the
L^{1}
-norm of an operator
h\in L^{1}({\mathcal{M}})
equals
\sup_{s\in B({\mathcal{M}})}\lvert\tr(hs)\rvert

[
56
, Lem. 9.23]
.

Step 2.
We show that
F_{\mathcal{H}}
does not depend on
{\mathcal{H}}
.
Combined with the first step, this shows (
4.32
).
Let
(\pi_{i},{\mathcal{H}}_{i})
,
i=1,2
, be representations of
{\mathcal{M}}
and let
\Psi_{i},\Phi_{i}
be purifications of
\psi,\phi
, respectively, in
{\mathcal{H}}_{i}
,
i=1,2
.
Let
u,v
be the
\pi_{1}
-
\pi_{2}
-intertwining partial isometries from
lemma

3.21
such that
u\Psi_{1}=\Psi_{2}
,
v\Phi_{1}=\Phi_{2}
.
Since
uv^{*}
is a contraction in the commutant of
\pi_{2}({\mathcal{M}})
, we have

\lvert\langle\Psi_{1},\Phi_{1}\rangle\rvert=\lvert\langle\Psi_{2},uv^{*}\Phi_{2}\rangle\rvert\leq\sup_{s^{\prime}\in B(\pi_{2}({\mathcal{M}})^{\prime})}\lvert\langle\Psi_{2},s^{\prime}\Phi_{2}\rangle\rvert=F_{{\mathcal{H}}_{2}}(\psi,\phi)^{1/2}.

(4.34)

Thus,
F_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}(\psi,\phi)=\sup_{\Psi_{1},\Phi_{1}}\lvert\langle\Psi_{1},\Phi_{1}\rangle\rvert^{2}\leq F_{{\mathcal{H}}_{2}}(\psi,\phi)
.
The converse inequality follows from the same arguments with exchanged indices
1\leftrightarrow 2
.
The last claim follows from
lemma

4.21
.
∎

When
{\mathcal{M}}_{0}\subset{\mathcal{M}}
is a von Neumann subalgebra and
\psi,\phi
are normal states on
{\mathcal{M}}
, Uhlmann’s Theorem implies the following special case of (
4.30
)

F(\psi|_{{\mathcal{M}}_{0}},\phi|_{{\mathcal{M}}_{0}})\geq F(\psi,\phi).

(4.35)

Indeed, this follows directly from the fact that purifications of functional
\psi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
are, in particular, purifications of its restriction
\psi|_{{\mathcal{M}}_{0}}
to a subalgebra
{\mathcal{M}}_{0}\subset{\mathcal{M}}
.
As a consequence, we observe:

Corollary 4.22
.

Let
\psi
and
\phi
be normal states on
{\mathcal{M}}
and let
{\mathcal{N}}\supset{\mathcal{M}}
be an extension of
{\mathcal{M}}
.
For every extension
\tilde{\psi}
of
\psi
and every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists an extension
\tilde{\phi}
of
\phi
such that

F(\psi,\phi)\approx_{\varepsilon}F(\tilde{\psi},\tilde{\phi}).

(4.36)

Proof.

We may take
{\mathcal{N}}
to be in standard representation and let
\Psi
be a purification of
\tilde{\psi}
.
By
theorem

4.20
, there is a purification
\Phi
of
\phi
such that
F(\psi,\phi)\approx_{\varepsilon}\lvert\langle\Psi,\Phi\rangle\rvert^{2}
.
We let
\tilde{\phi}
be denote the state that
\Phi
induces on
{\mathcal{N}}
.
Then (
4.35
) implies

F(\psi,\phi)\approx_{\varepsilon}F(\Psi,\Phi)\geq F(\tilde{\psi},\tilde{\phi})\geq F(\psi,\phi).\qed

In particular,
theorem

4.19
follows from
corollary

4.22
.
We conclude by proving the Fuchs-van de Graaf inequality based on Uhlmann’s Theorem.

Proof of (
4.29
).

Let
({\mathcal{H}},J,\mathcal{P})
be a standard form of
{\mathcal{M}}
.
Let
\varepsilon>0
. By
theorem

4.20
, there are purifications
\Psi,\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
of the states
\psi,\phi
such that
F(\psi,\phi)\leq\lvert\langle\Psi,\Phi\rangle\rvert^{2}+\varepsilon
.
Let
\omega_{\Psi},\omega_{\Phi}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{H}})
denote the vector states induced by
\Psi,\Phi
, respectively.
Then

\lVert\psi-\phi\rVert\leq\lVert\omega_{\Psi}-\omega_{\Phi}\rVert=2(1-\lvert\langle\Psi,\Phi\rangle\rvert^{2})^{1/2}\leq 2(1-F(\psi,\phi)+\varepsilon)^{1/2}.

Thus, the upper bound follows by taking
\varepsilon\to 0
.
To see the lower bound, we consider the canonical purifications
\Omega_{\psi},\Omega_{\phi}\in\mathcal{P}
.
Using the inequality
\lVert\Omega_{\psi}-\Omega_{\phi}\rVert^{2}\leq\lVert\psi-\phi\rVert
(see (
3.34
)) and
theorem

4.20
, we have

1-F(\psi,\phi)^{1/2}\leq 1-\langle\Omega_{\psi},\Omega_{\phi}\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\lVert\Omega_{\psi}-\Omega_{\phi}\rVert^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\lVert\psi-\phi\rVert.\qed

In addition to the fidelity and the norm distance, there are many more, typically asymmetric, distance measures such as the relative entropy and other quantum divergences, most of which have been considered in the context of von Neumann algebras, see
section

4.10
or
[
120
,
35
,
36
,
121
]
.

4.8
Intrinsic description of quantum systems and outer quantum channels

In
section

4.2
, we considered subsystems of the full quantum systems and described them through their observable algebras.
Since every subsystem is a system in its own right, there should be an intrinsic description that bypasses the surrounding full quantum system.
Mathematically, it seems obvious what this description is:
We take the observable algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
of the system and forget the Hilbert space that it acted on.
Then the states of the system are the normal states on
{\mathcal{M}}
, measurements are
{\mathcal{M}}
-valued POVMs, and operations on the quantum system are the
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner ones.
This makes sense because
proposition

4.1
characterizes the implementable operations in a way that is intrinsic to
{\mathcal{M}}
.
Moreover, it does not yield a larger class of systems because all abstract von Neumann algebras have faithful representations.

In order to justify such an intrinsic description of quantum systems, we need to argue that the observable algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
of a subsystem of a full quantum system indeed captures all operational properties that are intrinsic to the subsystem.
Although this seems obvious, we give an explicit argument.
Let us consider an abstract von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
and a pair of full quantum systems with Hilbert spaces
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
.
We assume that both of these have subsystems with observable algebras
\pi_{1}({\mathcal{M}})
and
\pi_{2}({\mathcal{M}})
, where
\pi_{j}:{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
,
j=1,2
, are faithful representations of
{\mathcal{M}}
.
As a first step, we ask whether the possible reduced states of the two setups are different.
This is not the case: Whenever a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
acts on a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
, then all of its normal states are induced by normal states on the surrounding
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
.
As a consequence, the two environments cannot be distinguished without further knowledge of the states of the respective full quantum systems.
Indeed, let
(m_{x})
be a measurement that the agent performs in the two setups, i.e., they perform
(\pi_{j}(m_{x}))
on the system with Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}_{j}
,
j=1,2
.
It is clear that if
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
are states of the respective full systems such that
\phi_{1}\circ\pi_{1}=\phi_{2}\circ\pi_{2}
, then the output distributions on both systems are the same

\displaystyle p_{x}=\phi_{1}(\pi_{1}(m_{x}))=\phi_{2}(\pi_{2}(m_{x}))\qquad\forall x.

(4.37)

Thus, all measurements on system 1 can be reproduced by measurements on system 2 and vice versa.
We conclude that the agent cannot learn anything about the environment by measuring the system.

The point of view that a von Neumann algebra describes an abstract quantum system is, of course, not new.
In fact, it dates back all the way to von Neumann himself, for whom finding the most general mathematical description of quantum systems was one of the initial motivations for studying von Neumann algebras
[
65
]
.

We may now supplement the sentence
“a full quantum system is described by a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
”
with
a quantum system is described by a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
.
We emphasize again that we do not claim (nor believe) that every von Neumann algebra arises as the observable algebra of an actual quantum system.
Nonetheless, there is no clear separation between those von Neumann algebras that qualify as models of physical systems and those that do not.
This makes it even more interesting to understand the interplay between operational properties and von Neumann algebraic properties of the von Neumann algebra.

We caution the reader that, even though abstract von Neumann algebras can be used to describe classical systems or quantum-classical hybrids
[
122
]
, this is only partially covered by our framework.
The reason is that in our framework, only inner quantum channels, which always act trivially on the center
Z({\mathcal{M}})
, are implementable operations.
However, in a truly classical system, the observable algebra would be abelian so that all inner operations would be trivial.
To understand this, we recall that our conclusion that only inner operations can be applied was deduced from the assumption that we are dealing with subsystems of full quantum systems (or, more generally, agents with partial access).
This assumption does not apply to classical systems.
Instead, it applies to cases where the center of the von Neumann algebras describes
superselection rules
such as the parity superselection rule in fermionic systems.
27
27

27

The observable algebra associated with a finite number of fermionic modes is the
even
part of CAR algebra.
When considering a fixed sector of a systems with infinitely many modes,
the observable algebra is the von Neumann algebra generated by the even part of the CAR algebra in the sector under consideration.

If one wants to describe a quantum-classical hybrid system, one has to specify which operations should be regarded as implementable since the observable algebra itself does not yet know what kind of system it describes.
28
28

28

Another aspect to be cautious about when discussing quantum-classical hybrids is that a direct integral
\int_{X}{\mathcal{M}}_{x}\,d\mu(x)
with fibers
{\mathcal{M}}_{x}\cong{\mathcal{M}}
does not carry the same information as a tensor product
L^{\infty}(X,\mu)\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{M}}
. In both cases, it is clear what the classical subsystem is, but only in the latter case do we know what the quantum subsystem is (assuming
{\mathcal{M}}
is a factor). Identifying the two requires choosing coordinates for every
x\in X
, and this choice is unique up to a unitary section
x_{\mapsto}u_{x}
.

In
section

4.2
, we identified the operations of an agent with observable algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
as the
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner.
It is sometimes natural to consider a larger class of quantum channels that we call
outer quantum channels
, as opposed to inner ones.
In particular, these allow us to go from one system to another.
Consider a pair of von Neumann algebras
{\mathcal{M}}
and
{\mathcal{N}}
.
Then, in the Heisenberg picture, an outer quantum channel from is a normal ucp map

T:{\mathcal{N}}\to{\mathcal{M}}.

The corresponding Schrödinger picture version is that of a normalization-preserving cp map
T_{*}:{\mathcal{M}}_{*}\to{\mathcal{N}}_{*}
.
The two are connected via
T_{*}(\phi)=\phi\circ T
,
\phi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}
.
Outer quantum channels describe abstract state transformations that respect stochastic mixtures, i.e., convex combinations of states, and are compatible with innocent bystanders.
29
29

29

This means that
T\otimes\id
is still an outer channel if
\id
denotes the identity on some system
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})
, the innocent bystander, that does not take part in the transformation.

Outer quantum channels have a dilation theory known as Paschke dilations
[
88
]
.
Importantly, many natural constructions of new (outer) quantum channels from old ones preserve the subset of inner channels, e.g., the Petz recovery map
[
120
,
35
]
of an inner channel is easily seen to be an inner channel and the the Paschke dilation reduces to the inner Stinespring dislation for inner channels (see
lemma

3.27
).

We believe that the implementability of outer channels
T:{\mathcal{N}}\to{\mathcal{M}}
by an agent whose observable algebra is
{\mathcal{M}}
(or
{\mathcal{N}}
) is highly questionable in general, even if
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{N}}
.
Implementability is, however, not strictly necessary for a quantum channel to describe operationally meaningful state transformations of a quantum system.
For instance, the channel could describe a coarse-graining procedure, or it could a form of noise that affects states if the agent does nothing for an extended period of time.
To illustrate this, we consider a class of examples where outer channels arise from symmetries:
Consider a subsystem with observable algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
of a full quantum system with Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
.
Let
U
be unitary on
{\mathcal{H}}
with the property that
U^{*}{\mathcal{M}}U={\mathcal{M}}
.
Then
T=\Ad(U)\restriction{\mathcal{M}}
is an automorphism of
{\mathcal{M}}
and, hence, an outer quantum channel, which is, in general, not
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner.
For
T
to be inner, it is necessary and sufficient that
U
decomposes as a product
U=uu^{\prime}
of unitaries
u^{(\prime)}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{(\prime)}
.
A concrete example in the context of quantum field theory, where
U
implements a Lorentz transformation, is discussed in
section

4.9.2
.

We close our discussion of outer channels with remarks on cases, where channels between different von Neumann algebras can be regarded as implementable.
We fix an agent with observable algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
.
If
{\mathcal{M}}
and
{\mathcal{N}}
are type
{\mathrm{I}}
factor, every outer quantum channel
T:{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathcal{M}}
is inner
[
87
, Thm. 3.3]
.
In
section

4.3
, we have considered implementable channels
T:{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{1})
in the presence of ancillary systems, and identified them as the
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner channels.
If
{\mathcal{N}}\subset{\mathcal{M}}
is a von Neumann subalgebra of an agent’s observable algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
, then the inclusion map
j:{\mathcal{N}}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{M}}
corresponds to discarding all operations that are not
{\mathcal{N}}
-inner.
Since the agent can always decide to discard some of their operations, the inclusion map implementable.
Afterward, the agent’s observable algebra is the subalgebra
{\mathcal{N}}
.
If the agent wishes to discard the full system, they choose
{\mathcal{N}}={\mathbb{C}}1
.
However, in general, the agent cannot apply an inclusion map
{\mathcal{M}}\hookrightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{N}}
into a larger algebra, as this would give them access to new observables.
The following result shows that every channel whose output system (in the Schrödinger picture) is a full quantum system can be implemented:

Lemma 4.23
.

An outer quantum channel
T:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})\to{\mathcal{M}}
(more generally,
T:{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})
) can be implementable by an agent who has access to the set of
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner operations by applying an
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner operation and then discarding the system, instead of the ancilla.

Proof.

By
lemma

3.29
there is an
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner quantum channel
\hat{T}:{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{1})
such that
T=\hat{T}(1_{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\,\cdot\,})
.
∎

4.9
Examples of von Neumann algebraic quantum systems

In the following, we consider three physical scenarios in which quantum systems with infinitely many degrees of freedom are modelled with von Neumann algebras.

4.9.1
Ground state sectors of quantum many-body systems

We consider a quantum many-body system of spins localized on the sites of a lattice
\Gamma\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, e.g.,
\Gamma={\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
.
To every finite region
A\subset\Gamma
, we associate the Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}_{A}=\bigotimes_{x\in A}{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
and the unital *-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}_{A}={\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{A})
.
If
B\subset A
is a smaller region, we use the product form
{\mathcal{H}}_{A}={\mathcal{H}}_{B}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{A\setminus B}
to identify
{\mathcal{A}}_{B}
with the subalgebra
{\mathcal{A}}(B)\otimes 1_{A\setminus B}
of
{\mathcal{A}}_{A}
.
In this way, we obtain a directed family of unital *-algebras
({\mathcal{A}}_{A})_{A\Subset\Gamma}
(where
\Subset
indicates a finite subset), and, upon taking their union, we get a unital *-algebra

{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}=\bigcup_{A\Subset\Gamma}{\mathcal{A}}_{A}

(4.38)

which describes the collection of all operators with finite support.
This *-algebra can be equipped naturally with a
C^{*}
-norm via
\lVert a\rVert=\lVert a\rVert_{{\mathcal{B}}(\otimes_{x\in A}{\mathbb{C}}^{d})}
for some/all
A\Subset\Gamma
with
a\in A_{A}
, and if we take the completion
\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}
what we obtain the so-called quasi-local (
C^{*}
-) algebra
[
123
]
.
30
30

30

In fact, we have just described its standard construction.

By construction, the unital *-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}
and the quasi-local
C^{*}
-algebra
\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}
have the same algebraic states and the same representation theory.

The algebraic structure of finitely localized operators, which only depends on the dimension
d
of the local spins and the lattice
\Gamma
, is the same for many distinct physical models.
For instance, both the Heisenberg antiferromagnet and a collection of uncorrelated spins are described by
\Gamma={\mathbb{Z}}
and
d=2
.
Different physical models are specified by their Hamiltonians, which are formally written as

H=\sum_{A\Subset\Gamma}\Phi(A),\qquad\Phi(A)=\Phi(A)^{*}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{A},

(4.39)

where the interaction terms
\Phi(A)
decay sufficiently fast when
A
becomes large.
To make this rigorous, one associates to every finite region
A\Subset\Gamma
, the Hamiltonian
H_{A}=\sum_{A^{\prime}\Subset\Gamma}\Phi(A^{\prime})
and the dynamics
\tau_{t}^{A}(a)=e^{itH_{A}}({\,\cdot\,})e^{-itH_{A}}
.
If the interaction strength
\lVert\Phi(A)\rVert
decays sufficiently fast at large distances, the limits
\lim_{A\uparrow\Gamma}\tau_{t}^{A}(a)
(along the directed set of finite regions) exists in
\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}
for all
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}
and define a strongly continuous dynamics
\tau:{\mathbb{R}}\curvearrowright\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}
(see
[
123
,
124
,
125
,
126
]
).
In the following, we make the additional assumption that the infinitesimal generator
\delta
of
\tau
contains
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}
as a core on which it is given by the norm-convergent series
\delta(a)=\sum_{A\Subset\Gamma}i[\Phi(A),a]=\text{``}i[H,a]\text{''}
.
31
31

31

These assumptions are quite mild (see
[
123
,
124
,
125
]
).
For instance, they hold if the interaction has finite range, i.e.,
\Phi(A)=0
whenever the diameter of
A
exceeds some fixed number
\ell<\infty
.

A
ground state

\omega
is a state
\omega
on
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}
such that

-i\omega(a^{*}\delta(a))\geq 0,\qquad a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}

(4.40)

(see
[
112
, Def. 1.9]
or
[
123
]
).
The ground space (meaning the set of ground states) is a convex
w^{*}
-compact set.
If we assume the ground state to be unique, it is a pure state on
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}
.

In this work, we will mean by a
sector
of the many-body system a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
carrying an irreducible representation of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}
.
32
32

32

Every representation of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}
is faithful since
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}
is a simple unital *-algebra, i.e., it does not have nontrivial ideals. We also remark that every representation of the unital *-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}
(by bounded operators) extends to a representation of the quasi-local algebra
\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}
.
,
33
33

33

Two irreducible representations describe the same sector of the many-body system if they are unitarily equivalent.

In the following, we suppress the representation and regard
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}
as a subalgebra of
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
.
A
ground state sector
is a sector that arises via the GNS representation with respect to a pure ground state
\omega

[
123
]
.
The normal state space
\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{H}})
describes those states of the many-body system that are finite excitations above the ground state.
More precisely,
\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{H}})
is the set of states of the many-body system that can be approximated by acting on the ground state with operations of finite support, where we say that an operation has support in a finite region
A\Subset\Gamma
if it can be written with Kraus operators in
{\mathcal{A}}_{A}
.

From now on, we consider a fixed sector
{\mathcal{H}}
.
For a region
A\subset\Gamma
, we think of an agent, also denoted
A
, that can implement operations with
approximately finite support
in
A
.
By this, we mean operations that are finitely localized in
A
, and any limits thereof in the topology of pointwise norm-convergence on states or the topology of convergence in expectation values.
Based on
lemma

4.4
, both topologies lead to the same class of operations, namely those that are
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
-inner, where
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
is the von Neumann algebra

{\mathcal{M}}_{A}={\mathcal{A}}_{A}^{\prime\prime}.

(4.41)

With this definition, we have
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}={\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{\emptyset}={\mathbb{C}}1
.
We note the above as a Lemma:

Lemma 4.24
.

An operation on the Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
of the sector is approximately finitely localized in a region
A\subset\Gamma
if and only if it is
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
-inner.
In particular, all operations are approximately localized in
\Gamma
.

The irreducibility of the action of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}
on
{\mathcal{H}}
implies that
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}\vee{\mathcal{M}}_{A^{c}}={\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
for all
A\subset\Gamma
.
By
lemma

4.17
, this has the consequence that the von Neumann algebras
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
are, in fact, factors for all regions
A\subset\Gamma
.
Moreover, the factors
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
are, by construction, AFD.
The fact that finitely localized operators in disjoint regions commute implies that the factors
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
commute if
A\cap B=\emptyset
.

Figure 2
:
Bipartition of an infinite quantum spin system into a cone and its complement.

Let us now discuss entanglement theory in the sector
{\mathcal{H}}
.
To do this, we fix an
N
-partition
\Gamma=A_{1}\cup\ldots A_{N}
of the lattice, which gives us an
N
-tuple of pairwise commuting von Neumann algebras on
{\mathcal{H}}
.
We consider a pair of normal states
\psi,\phi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{H}})
.
By
lemma

4.24
, the von Neumann algebraic definition of

\psi\xrightarrow{\mathrm{LOCC}}\phi

(4.42)

in the sense of
section

4.6
is physically meaningful.
It encodes that
\psi
can be reached from
\phi
with classical operations and local operations of approximately finite support.
In this sense, the entanglement theory in the ground state is fully captured by the von Neumann algebras in (
4.41
).

We conclude with a brief summary of what is known about Haag duality and the type of the factors
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
,
A\subset\Gamma
, in some concrete models.

Spin chains.

For spin chains, the lattice is given by
\Gamma={\mathbb{Z}}
, and we pick the bipartition
R={\mathbb{N}}
,
L={\mathbb{Z}}\setminus{\mathbb{N}}
.
We begin by considering the ground state sector of translation-invariant Hamiltonians with a uniform energy gap.
Building on the work of Hastings
[
127
]
, Matsui showed in
[
128
]
that the factors
{\mathcal{M}}_{L}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{R}
are of type
{\mathrm{I}}
.
In particular, this shows that Haag duality holds, i.e.,
{\mathcal{M}}_{L}={\mathcal{M}}_{R}^{\prime}
.
We now turn to general, non-gapped, translation-invariant spin chains.
In
[
17
]
, it was conjectured that the only possible types for
{\mathcal{M}}_{L}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{R}
are type
{\mathrm{I}}
and type
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}
.
So far, no counterexamples have been found and no models violating Haag duality for the left-right bipartition are known.
The critical XX model, and the transverse-field Ising models are known to have type
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}
factors
{\mathcal{M}}_{L/R}
and to satisfy Haag duality (see
[
17
]
and
[
5
]
, respectively).
In both cases, this was shown by using a Jordan-Wigner isomorphism to reduce the problem to a problem about free fermion chains (see the next paragraph).

Critical fermion chains.

Following
[
5
]
, we consider the ground state sector of a 1D fermion chain with a quadratic translation-invariant Hamiltonian

H=\sum_{x,y}h(x-y)\,a_{x}^{\dagger}a_{y}.

(4.43)

We assume here that the interactions are not too long-ranged in the sense that
\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}h(n)<\infty
, and assume that the model is critical in the sense that the energy band
\hat{h}(k)
, i.e., the Fourier transform
\hat{h}(k)=\sum_{n}e^{ink}h(n)
, takes on both signs.
Moreover, for simplicity, let us assume that the model has a unique ground state.
Then the fermionic observable algebras
{\mathcal{M}}_{L}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{R}
associated with a left-right bipartition of the chain are of type
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}
and satisfy Haag duality (see
[
5
]
for details).

Gapped systems in 2D.

We consider ground state sectors of translation invariant gapped Hamiltonians on a 2D lattice
\Gamma={\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
.
For simplicity, we assume the ground state to be unique.
In the following, we list some known type properties and how they depend on topological order
[
129
]
, which are compiled from the works
[
42
,
130
,
20
,
50
,
113
,
43
,
131
,
132
,
7
]
.

•

If a region
A
is
“properly infinite”
in the sense that it contains balls of arbitrary diameter, then the corresponding factor
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
is properly infinite, i.e., of type
{\mathrm{I}}_{\infty}
,
{\mathrm{II}}_{\infty}
, or
{\mathrm{III}}
(see
[
7
, App. D]
, which uses an argument from
[
42
,
17
]
).

•

In topologically trivial systems, all types that were not excluded in the previous item occur:
In
[
7
]
, a family of models is constructed in which the factors
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
of all infinite regions
A\subset\Gamma
have the same type, which can be chosen to be
{\mathrm{I}}_{\infty}
,
{\mathrm{II}}_{\infty}
, or
{\mathrm{III}}_{\lambda}
with
0<\lambda\leq 1
by adjusting the parameters of the model. Moreover, these models satisfy Haag duality in arbitrary bipartitions.

•

In topologically ordered systems, the factors
{\mathcal{M}}_{C}
associated with cones are not of type
{\mathrm{I}}

[
113
]
.

•

In Kitaev’s quantum double model for abelian groups, the factors associated with cones have been shown to be of
{\mathrm{II}}_{\infty}

[
50
]
.
For cones, Haag duality is shown in
[
130
]
.
For regions
A=C_{1}\cup C_{2}
that are unions of distanced cones, Haag duality is known to fail.
In this case, the failure can be understood explicitly: Ribbon operators creating pairs of anyonic excitations in the respective cones are unitaries that commute with
{\mathcal{M}}_{A^{c}}
but are not elements of
{\mathcal{M}}_{C_{1}}\vee{\mathcal{M}}_{C_{2}}

[
41
]
.

•

In Levin-Wen models
[
49
]
, the observable algebras associated with cones are of type
{\mathrm{II}}_{\infty}
if the quantum dimensions of the simple objects of the unitary fusion category are all
1

[
43
]
.
In all other cases, they are of type
{\mathrm{III}}
and the subtypes can be computed from the fusion rules of the unitary fusion category (a proof due to Izumi can be found in the appendix of
[
43
]
).
Haag duality for cones is shown in
[
133
]
.

4.9.2
Relativistic quantum fields

In algebraic quantum field theory
[
19
]
, a fixed sector of a quantum field theory on a spacetime
\mathbb{M}
is described by a net

{\mathcal{O}}\mapsto{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}})

of von Neumann algebras
{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}})
, defined for open regions
{\mathcal{O}}\subset\mathbb{M}
, on the sector’s Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
.
The von Neumann algebras
{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}})
describe the collection of smeared quantum fields in
{\mathcal{O}}
.
The net
{\mathcal{O}}\mapsto{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}})
has to satisfy certain axioms which reflect physical properties.
For instance, the
causality
axiom states that
{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}}_{1})
and
{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}}_{2})
commute whenever
{\mathcal{O}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{2}
are causally separated.
The axiom of
isotony
states that observables in a smaller region
{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{2}
are, in particular, also observables of the larger region:
{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}}_{1})\subset{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}}_{2})
.
In general, there are a variety of further axioms, depending on the context.
We briefly discuss the most common ones:

Causal completeness
states that the observable algebras
{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}})
and
{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime\prime})
of a region
{\mathcal{O}}
and its causal closure
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime\prime}
are equal (
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}
denotes the causal complement and, hence,
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime\prime}
is the causal closure).

Additivity
states that the observable algebra
{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{O}}_{2})
of a union is
{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}}_{1})\vee{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}}_{2})
.

Haag duality
states that the observable algebra
{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime})
of the causal complement of a region
{\mathcal{O}}
is given by the commutant of
{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}})
:

{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}})^{\prime}={\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}).

(4.44)

Note that Haag duality implies both causality and causal completeness.
These axioms (and a few more that we do not discuss here) imply that the von Neumann algebras
{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}})
are AFD factors of type
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}
factors
[
21
,
134
,
135
]
.

Due to the causality axiom, a pair
{\mathcal{O}}_{A},{\mathcal{O}}_{B}\subset\mathbb{M}
of causally separated regions gives rise to a pair of commuting von Neumann algebras
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}={\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}}_{A})
,
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}={\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}}_{B})
on
{\mathcal{H}}
.
If the Haag duality axiom holds and if the regions
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
are causal complements of each other, i.e.,
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}={\mathcal{O}}_{B}^{\prime}
(see
fig.

3
), then the von Neumann algebraic bipartite system
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
satisfies Haag duality.

Figure 3
:
Penrose diagram of Minkowski spacetime with one spatial dimension, partitioned into a pair
{\mathcal{O}}_{A},{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
of causally separated regions with
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}={\mathcal{O}}_{B}^{\prime}
.
