Let us now consider the simple example of the vacuum sector of a free scalar field theory of mass
m
on the Minkowski spacetime
{\mathbb{R}}^{1,3}
.
The Lorentz group is implemented by a unitary representation
g\mapsto U_{g}
with respect to which the local von Neumann algebras are covariant
{\mathcal{A}}(g{\mathcal{O}})=U_{g}{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}})U_{g}^{*}
.
We now consider a region
{\mathcal{O}}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{1,3}
together with a Lorentz transformation
g
that leaves
{\mathcal{O}}
invariant, e.g.,
g
may be a spatial rotation and
{\mathcal{O}}
a causal diamond over a rotation-symmetric base, or a Lorentz boost and
{\mathcal{O}}=W
a suitable Rindler wedge with
gW=W
.
Invariance of
{\mathcal{O}}
under
g
then implies
{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}})=U_{g}{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}})U_{g}^{*}
.
Thus, the restriction of
\Ad(U_{g})
defines an automorphism on
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}})
, which is, in general, non-inner.
34
34

34

As an example, consider as the region
{\mathcal{O}}
a Rindler wedge
W
. Then the modular automorphism group of the vacuum state is known to coincide with a one-parameter group of Lorentz boosts
g_{t}
with
g_{t}W=W

[
136
]
. Since the factors are type
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}
, the modular flow (= the Lorentz boost) is not inner
[
55
]
.

In the language of
section

4.8
, this provides examples of outer quantum channel that are not inner.

We caution the reader that the QFT setting described above does not fit perfectly into the framework developed in this chapter, in particular,
section

4.2
.
Although the von Neumann algebras
{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}})
are interpreted as the observable algebras of the fields localized in the region
{\mathcal{O}}
, we cannot assume that all
{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}})
-inner operations are physically implementable by an agent localized in
{\mathcal{O}}
.
This assumption necessarily leads to violations of causality as pointed out in
[
137
]
(see also
[
138
]
).
For instance, it implies that the outcomes of a local measurement depend on the application of a unitary in a causally separated region
[
139
,
140
]
.
We refer to the works
[
141
,
140
,
142
]
for a recently proposed solution to the problem of finding a mathematical description of local operations and measurements.
Roughly speaking, their idea is to define local operations as those that can be constructed by introducing another field theory (the
“probe”
), allowing a localized interaction, followed by a measurement of the probe.
Note that this follows the same philosophy as our definition of implementable instruments via unitary dilations in
section

4.2
, but gives a constructive description of the allowed unitaries
u
, which is in accordance with principles of relativistic field theory.
Nonetheless, the assumption that all
{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{O}})
-inner operations are implementable is often made (sometimes implicitly)
[
19
,
15
,
26
,
24
,
23
,
25
,
16
,
2
,
32
]
. In this case, the
“operational”
statements that follow should be taken with a grain of salt.
In addition to the above, there is the problem that classical communication, which the notion of LOCC and, hence, our operational understanding of entanglement, is built upon, for causally separated systems does not make any sense physically (see
[
16
]
for a nice discussion of this point).

None of the problems above apply to non-relativistic quantum field theories, where the notion of localization is purely spatial (as opposed to the spatio-temporal localization in relativistic field theory).
As an example, consider conformal field theories on the circle, which describe critical many-body systems in the thermodynamic systems.

4.9.3
Infinitely many entangled pure states

We consider a setting in which a finite number of agents
A_{1},\ldots A_{N}
share an infinite number of finite-dimensional
N
-partite systems
{\mathcal{H}}_{j}={\mathcal{H}}_{1|j}\otimes\ldots{\mathcal{H}}_{N|j}
in pure states

\Psi_{j}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{j},\qquad j\in{\mathbb{N}}.

(4.45)

The full system is an
N
-partite system with infinitely many degrees of freedom, see
fig.

4
for a visualization with
N=3
.
We assume that agents can only act on finitely many of the infinite
N
-partite systems.
This assumption allows for a description of the full system in terms of a Hilbert space, namely the restricted infinite tensor product of the Hilbert spaces
{\mathcal{H}}_{j}
relative to the sequence
\Psi_{j}

[
70
, Sec. XIV.1]
:

{\mathcal{H}}=\bigotimes_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}\,({\mathcal{H}}_{j};\Psi_{j}).

(4.46)

This Hilbert space contains the vector
\Psi=\otimes_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}\Psi_{j}
. In fact, it is the subspace of the unrestricted infinite tensor product
[
143
]
that can be approximated by finite perturbations of
\Psi
.
Here, a vector
\Phi
is a finite perturbation of
\Psi
if
\Phi=a\Psi
for an operator
a
that acts trivially on all but finitely many of the multipartite systems.
With each agent
A_{x}
, we associate an AFD factor
{\mathcal{M}}_{x}
on
{\mathcal{H}}
via

{\mathcal{M}}_{x}=\bigotimes_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}\,\big({\mathcal{M}}_{x|j},\Psi_{j}),

(4.47)

where
{\mathcal{M}}_{x|j}=1^{\otimes j-1}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{x|j})\otimes 1^{\otimes N-j}
is the observable algebra of the agent
A_{x}
on the
j
th
N
-partite system (see
[
70
, Sec. XIV.1]
).
More generally, for every collection of agents
\prod_{x\in I}A_{x}
,
I\subset\{1,\ldots N\}
,

{\mathcal{M}}_{I}=\bigvee_{x\in I}{\mathcal{M}}_{x|j}=\bigotimes_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}\,({\mathcal{M}}_{I|j},\Psi_{j}),

(4.48)

defines an AFD factor on
{\mathcal{H}}
, where
{\mathcal{M}}_{I|j}=\vee_{x\in I}{\mathcal{M}}_{x|j}\subset{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{j})
.
Clearly, we have
{\mathcal{M}}_{\{x\}}={\mathcal{M}}_{x}
,
{\mathcal{M}}_{I}\subset{\mathcal{M}}_{J}
if
I\subset J
, and
{\mathcal{M}}_{\{1,\ldots,\}}={\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
.
The commutation theorem for infinite tensor products
[
70
, Thm. XIV.1.9]
implies that Haag duality holds in any bipartition

{\mathcal{M}}_{I}^{\prime}={\mathcal{M}}_{I^{c}},\qquad I\subseteq\{1,\ldots,N\}.

(4.49)

Figure 4
:
Three parties sharing infinitely many entangled states.

Let us consider the class of implementable operations.
As said above, we assume that the agents can only act on finitely many systems.
By
lemma

4.4
, the operations that can be implemented approximately by the agent are precisely the
{\mathcal{M}}_{x}
-inner ones.

The factors that we constructed above are so-called ITPFI (infinite tensor product of finite type
{\mathrm{I}}
) factors, and we may refer to
N
-partite systems constructed above as
ITPFI multipartite systems
.
These form a mathematically well-understood pool of nontrivial examples, in particular, for the bipartite case where
N=2
.
Indeed, if
N=2
, the algebraic properties of
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
are completely understood thanks to the work of Araki and Woods
[
144
,
145
]
.
Let us consider a well-known example. If
{\mathcal{H}}_{j}=({\mathbb{C}}^{2})^{\otimes 2}
and

\Psi_{j}=(1+\lambda)^{-1/2}\big(\lambda^{1/2}|0\rangle|0\rangle+|1\rangle|1\rangle\big),\quad j\in{\mathbb{N}},

(4.50)

then
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}={\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{\prime}
are factors in standard representation.
They are of type
{\mathrm{I}}
if
\lambda=0
, of type
{\mathrm{III}}_{\lambda}
if
0<\lambda<1
, of type
{\mathrm{II}}_{1}
if
\lambda=1
and of type
{\mathrm{III}}_{\lambda^{-1}}
if
\lambda>1
.
Even in the case of more than two parties, ITPFI systems will be very useful as they allow us to construct multipartite embezzling states (see
section

8
).

4.10
Notes

In the following, we briefly review some aspects that have to be mentioned, but that did not receive a detailed exposition due to the constraints of this work.
There is a vast literature that is concerned with properties of von Neumann algebras inspired by information theory.
In the following, we stick to a selection of cases that have a direct operational interpretation.
Due to the extensive literature on the subject, this list remains incomplete.

Bell’s inequalities.

Arguably, the first work in von Neumann algebraic quantum information theory is Summers and Werner’s 1985 paper
[
15
]
on the topic of Bell’s inequalities
[
146
]
in quantum field theory.
A series of papers
[
15
,
23
,
22
,
25
]
followed in which they relate the maximal violation of Bell’s inequalities to algebraic properties of the observable algebras in quantum field theory (see
section

4.9.2
).
35
35

35

In passing, they discovered self-testing (see, e.g.,
[
24
, Lem. 2.2]
), which was later rediscovered in finite-dimensional quantum information theory
[
147
]
.

In their works, Summers and Werner assumed that all measurements in the local observable algebras are, in principle, physically implementable.
While we know today that this assumption leads to contradictions with causality (see the last paragraph of
section

4.9.2
), this assumption does place their work in the context of von Neumann algebraic quantum information theory.
Their results characterize the AFD type
{\mathrm{II}}_{1}
factor and its commutant in standard representation as the universal resource for Bell nonlocality, see
[
24
, Thm. 2.5]
.

Relative entropy and quantum divergences.

In
section

4.7
, we considered the norm distance and the fidelity, which operationally quantify the distance of states.
Both of these are symmetric, i.e., the distance from
\psi
to
\phi
is the same as the distance from
\phi
to
\psi
.
However, as in classical information theory, asymmetric distance measures become necessary in hypothesis testing, where false negatives can be much worse than false positives
[
148
]
.
By far the most popular among these distance measures is the quantum relative entropy.
There is, however, a whole zoo of so-called quantum divergences, with operational meanings in various settings, e.g., quantum cryptography,
[
148
]
.
Many of these have been extended to (or were originally defined in) the von Neumann algebraic setting
[
35
,
121
,
36
,
149
,
150
,
120
]
.
We especially recommend the monograph
[
35
]
of Hiai, which, among other things, includes a nice description of the theory of recovery maps.
Applications to explicit quantum information theoretic problems with systems described by von Neumann algebras have been considered, e.g., in
[
36
,
40
,
121
,
120
]
.
Let us also mention
[
37
]
, where the asymptotic equipartition theorem is generalized to the von Neumann algebraic setting.

Relative entropy of entanglement.

The relative entropy of entanglement is a standard entanglement monotone for mixed states.
It is defined as the smallest relative entropy between a given state and the set of separable, i.e., classically correlated, states.
The relative entropy of entanglement has been considered and estimated in the context of von Neumann algebraic systems arising in quantum field theory in
[
32
]
, where explicit estimates for the entanglement entropies in concrete models can be found.
However, it only makes sense in bipartite systems that admit normal product states, which requires the split property to hold (see
lemma

4.14
).
This rules out the relative entropy of entanglement as a sensible entanglement measure in the settings we are interested in, where the split property generally fails (see
section

4.6
).

Tsirelson’s problem.

Tsirelson’s problem
[
31
]
is a deep problem about correlation experiments, which was solved in
[
151
]
.
It has sparked lots of research, especially on the intersection of operator algebras and quantum information theory in the last 20 years (see, e.g.,
[
31
,
152
,
151
,
153
,
154
,
155
,
156
,
157
,
158
,
159
]
).
The setup is that of a bipartite system in which Alice and Bob are given access to a collection of measurements with a common outcome space
X
.
We label Alice’s measurements by
i
and Bob’s measurements by
j
.
Depending on the state of the full system and the respective choices of measurements, they observe a probability distribution
p(x,y|i,j)
on the joint outcomes
(x,y)
.
Tsirelson’s problem asks whether the correlation functions of finite-dimensional bipartite systems can approximate the correlation functions in the
“commuting operator framework”
, which allows Alice and Bob access to pairwise commuting observables on a joint, possibly infinite-dimensional, Hilbert space.
In
[
151
]
, this was settled in the negative.
Nonetheless, an explicit counterexample is not known.

Given the above and the setup of von Neumann algebraic quantum information theory, the natural question is which algebraic properties a pair of commuting von Neumann algebras
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B})
on a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
must have, such that it allows for counterexamples to Tsirelson’s problem.
It is known that counterexamples cannot be found if
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are AFD
[
31
]
(see
[
153
, Prop. 52]
for a detailed proof).
In fact, counterexamples can only be found if
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are not Connes embeddable
[
152
]
.

Subfactors and index.

A subfactor is an inclusion of factors
{\mathcal{N}}\subset{\mathcal{M}}

[
110
]
.
In his seminal paper
[
110
]
, Jones defined an index
[{\mathcal{M}}:{\mathcal{N}}]
for type
{\mathrm{II}}_{1}
subfactor inclusions, which Kosaki and Longo generalized to subfactors of arbitrary type
[
160
,
92
]
.
A subfactor is irreducible if
{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}\cap{\mathcal{M}}={\mathbb{C}}1
.
For non-irreducible subfactors, the index depends on the choice of a conditional expectation
{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathcal{N}}
.
Roughly speaking, this means an outer quantum channel that describes how to extend states on
{\mathcal{N}}
to states on
{\mathcal{M}}
.
For subfactors of type
{\mathrm{II}}_{1}
, there is a canonical conditional expectation, and for irreducible subfactors, there is a unique one (if it exists).
The theory of subfactors and their index developed into one of the main research areas in operator algebras in the 1990s (see
[
109
]
and the references therein).
In von Neumann algebraic quantum information theory, subfactors, or more generally, inclusions of von Neumann algebras, occur when we consider two agents with partial access to a full quantum system such that one agent is strictly more powerful than the other one (see (
4.12
)).
The index can then be shown to be an operational measure of how much more the powerful agent can do
[
40
]
.
In
[
41
]
this idea was considered in the context of secret sharing, where the more powerful agent tries to hide information from the less powerful one.
In
[
161
]
, the index is characterized using relative entropies and other quantum divergences between states on
{\mathcal{N}}
and
{\mathcal{M}}
with the help of a conditional expectation, although this work does not apply to the type
{\mathrm{III}}
case.
The index has also appeared in work on quantum teleportation and dense coding in
[
162
]
(see also
[
29
]
).

We mention an open problem, also discussed in
[
153
]
:
If
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B})
is an irreducible pair of commuting factors on a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
(see
lemma

4.17
), does the index of the irreducible subfactor
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}\subset{\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}
have an operational significance in entanglement theory?
We can answer this affirmatively for the case where the index is trivial, i.e.,
[{\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{M}}_{A}]=1
.
Indeed, a trivial index is the same as Haag duality
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}={\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}
, which we show to have an operational interpretation in
section

6.6
.

Sufficiency.

Further following the idea that an inclusion
{\mathcal{N}}\subset{\mathcal{M}}
of von Neumann algebras corresponds to a setting with two agents, where one agent has access to more operations than the other one, it makes sense to ask whether the weak agent has access to sufficiently many observables in order to distinguish a given pair
(\omega_{0},\omega_{2})
of states as good as the strong agent.
This is precisely the question, whether the weaker agent’s observable algebra
{\mathcal{N}}
is sufficient in the sense(s) of Umegaki, Petz, Jencova and others (see
[
120
,
35
,
163
,
164
,
165
,
166
,
167
]
and the references therein).
There is a unique minimal sufficient subalgebra. Petz showed that this is precisely the von Neumann algebra generated by the Connes cocycles
(D\omega_{0}:D\omega_{1})_{t}
,
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Instead of asking for the sufficiency of subalgebras, one can more generally ask for the sufficiency of a quantum channel, meaning that the channel is reversible relative to the given states.
We refer to the references above for details.
In the case of abelian von Neumann algebras, these notions of sufficiency reduces to the notions of sufficient statistics in classical probability theory
[
163
]
.

5
Catalytic states on von Neumann algebras

In this section, we consider approximate unitary equivalence of states.
We define a notion of catalytic states on von Neumann algebras, and we see in
section

8
that catalytic states are precisely the marginal states of embezzling states on bipartite quantum systems.
Many of our results on embezzlement of entanglement will be proved by reducing them to statements about catalytic states.
The results in this section are based on
[
3
]
and rely on insights from
[
57
,
168
,
8
]
.

Approximate unitary equivalence of a pair of normal states
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
on a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{N}}
is defined as

\omega_{1}\sim\omega_{2}\quad:\iff\quad\inf_{u\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{N}})}\ \lVert\omega_{1}-u\omega_{2}u^{*}\rVert=0.

(5.1)

If
{\mathcal{N}}
is an observable algebra of a quantum system, approximate unitary equivalence means that, up to an arbitrarily small error, the given states can be transformed into each other with invertible quantum channels.
In finite type
{\mathrm{I}}
factors, approximate unitary equivalence implies exact unitary equivalence. This is in general false, even for factors of type
{\mathrm{I}}_{\infty}
.

To decide approximate unitary equivalence, we need
spectral
information of the two states.
Indeed, in type
{\mathrm{I}}
factors, a pair of states is approximately unitarily equivalent if and only if they have the same spectrum (with equal multiplicities).
This generalizes to general von Neumann algebras if we replace the spectrum by the Haagerup-Størmer spectral state (see
section

3.3.4
).
We say that a nonzero positive linear functional
\psi
on a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
is a
catalyst
for a pair of normal states
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
on a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{N}}
if
\psi\otimes\omega_{1}\sim\psi\otimes\omega_{2}
but
\omega_{1}\not\sim\omega_{2}
.
We refer to
[
169
]
for a recent review of catalysis in quantum information theory (where catalysis is considered in a variety of resource theories).
In the language of
[
169
]
, we could refer to the above notion of catalysis also as
“embezzlement of (im)purity”
.

Definition 5.1
.

A normal state
\psi
on a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
is
universally catalytic
if

\psi\otimes\omega_{1}\sim\psi\otimes\omega_{2}

(5.2)

for all pairs of normal states
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
on a type
{\mathrm{I}}_{\infty}
factor.

We will see later that universally catalytic states satisfy (
5.2
) for all pairs of normal states on an arbitrary AFD factor, even though the definition only asks for type
{\mathrm{I}}_{\infty}
factors.
We quantify how close a given normal positive linear functional
\psi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
is to being universally catalytic by considering the worst-case error:

\kappa(\psi):=\adjustlimits{\sup}_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}{\inf}_{u}\lVert\psi\otimes\omega_{1}-u(\psi\otimes\omega_{2})u^{*}\rVert,

(5.3)

where the optimizations are over normal states on a type
{\mathrm{I}}_{\infty}
factor
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})
and unitaries in
{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})
, respectively.

The main theorem of this section states that
\kappa(\psi)
measures the deviation of the Haagerup-Størmer spectral functional
\hat{\psi}
from being invariant under the flow of weights:

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
\psi
be a normal positive linear functional on a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
. Then

\kappa(\psi)=\sup_{t>0}\,\lVert\hat{\psi}-\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{t}\rVert.

(5.4)

We briefly sketch the proof, which will be given in detail in
section

5.1
below:

Theorem

3.15
, lets us write the RHS of (
5.3
) as
\sup_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}\lVert(\psi\otimes\omega_{1})^{\wedge}-(\psi\otimes\omega_{2})^{\wedge}\rVert
.
The flow of weights of
{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})
can be identified with the flow of weights of
{\mathcal{M}}
itself (see
proposition

3.11
).
We will show that under this identification
(\psi\otimes\omega)^{\wedge}
becomes a smeared out version of
\hat{\psi}
over its orbit:

(\psi\otimes\omega)^{\wedge}=\sum\lambda_{i}\,\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{\log\lambda_{i}},

(5.5)

where
(\lambda_{i})
are the eigenvalues of the density operator
\rho
associated with
\omega
.
This readily implies that
\kappa(\psi)\leq\sup_{t>0}\,\lVert\hat{\psi}-\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{t}\rVert
:

\lVert(\psi\otimes\omega_{1})^{\wedge}-(\psi\otimes\omega_{2})^{\wedge}\rVert=\Big\|\sum\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}(\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{\log\lambda_{i}}-\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{\log\lambda_{j}})\Big\|\leq\sup_{t>0}\ \lVert\hat{\psi}-\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{t}\rVert.

For the converse, consider states of the form
\omega_{1}={n}^{-1}\tr(p({\,\cdot\,}))
and
\omega_{2}=m^{-1}\tr(q({\,\cdot\,}))
for finite-dimensional projection
p,q
on
{\mathcal{K}}
of dimensions
n
and
m
, respectively. Then

\lVert(\psi\otimes\omega_{1})^{\wedge}-(\psi\otimes\omega_{2})^{\wedge}\rVert=\lVert\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{\log n}-\hat{\phi}\circ\theta_{\log m}\rVert=\lVert\hat{\psi}-\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{\log t}\rVert,\qquad t=\frac{m}{n}.

Since
\log{\mathbb{Q}}^{+}
is dense in
{\mathbb{R}}
, this implies the converse inequality
\kappa(\psi)\geq\sup_{t>0}\,\lVert\hat{\psi}-\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{\log t}\rVert
.

In particular,
theorem

5.2
tells us that

\psi
is universally catalytic if and only if
\hat{\psi}
is invariant under the flow of weights.

This generalizes
[
168
, Prop. 3.2]
stating that a
\hat{\psi}
is invariant if and only if
\psi\otimes\langle 0|{\,\cdot\,}|0\rangle\sim\psi\otimes\frac{1}{n}\tr_{n}
, where
\tr_{n}
denotes the standard trace on
M_{n}({\mathbb{C}})
.
It is clear that, if
\psi
is a universally catalytic state on a von Neumann
{\mathcal{M}}
and
\phi\sim\psi
, then
\phi
is also universally catalytic.
If
{\mathcal{M}}
is a factor, the converse holds as well:

Corollary 5.3
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a factor and let
\psi,\phi
be normal states on
{\mathcal{M}}
.
If
\psi,\phi
are universally catalytic, then
\psi\sim\phi
.

Corollary

5.3
follows from the fact that an ergodic flow admits at most one invariant normal state.
Indeed, this implies that all universally catalytic states have the same spectral state, which implies the claim by
theorem

3.15
.

Next, we use optimize
\kappa
over the normal state space in order to obtain algebraic invariants:

\kappa_{\max}({\mathcal{M}})=\sup_{\psi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}({\mathcal{M}})}\,\kappa(\psi),\qquad\kappa_{\min}({\mathcal{M}})=\inf_{\psi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}({\mathcal{M}})}\,\kappa(\psi).

(5.6)

In
section

5.2
, we completely determine these algebraic invariants by examining the corresponding invariants for ergodic flows on abelian von Neumann algebras.
The results are summarized in the following:

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a von Neumann algebra.
Then
\kappa_{\min}({\mathcal{M}})
is either
0
or
2
.
The following are equivalent:

1.

{\mathcal{M}}
admits a universally catalytic state;

2.

\kappa_{\min}({\mathcal{M}})=0
;

3.

the flow of weights of
{\mathcal{M}}
admits an invariant normal state.

In particular,
\kappa_{\min}({\mathcal{M}})=2
if
{\mathcal{M}}
is semifinite and
\kappa_{\min}({\mathcal{M}})=0
if
{\mathcal{M}}
is a factor of type
{\mathrm{III}}_{\lambda}
,
0<\lambda\leq 1
.

In
[
168
]
, the class of AFD factors whose flow of weights admits normal invariant states is characterized as the class of AFD factors with the property that they admit a type
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}
subfactor with conditional expectation.

We see that universally catalytic states only exist on factors of type
{\mathrm{III}}
.
Apart from some type
{\mathrm{III}}_{0}
factors, all type
{\mathrm{III}}
factors admit universally catalytic states.

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a factor. Then

\kappa_{\max}({\mathcal{M}})=\diam\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{M}})/_{\sim}

(5.7)

is exactly the diameter of the state space modulo unitaries (see
eq.

3.8
).
Hence, if
{\mathcal{M}}
has type
{\mathrm{III}}
, then it has subtype
{\mathrm{III}}_{\lambda}
with

\lambda=\bigg(\frac{2-\kappa_{\max}({\mathcal{M}})}{2+\kappa_{\max}({\mathcal{M}})}\bigg)^{2}.

(5.8)

We see that in factors of type
{\mathrm{III}}_{\lambda}
with
\lambda\approx 1
, we have
\kappa(\psi)\approx 0
for all normal states
\psi
.
A special role is played by type
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}
factors, which are precisely those factors with the property that all states are universally catalytic.
In fact, this holds for algebras:

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a von Neumann algebra. Then
{\mathcal{M}}
is of type
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}
, i.e., a direct integral of type
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}
factors, if and only if every normal state is universally catalytic.

5.1
Haagerup-Størmer spectral states of product states

Proposition

3.11
shows that we may identify the flow of weights of
{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{N}}
with the flow of weights of
{\mathcal{M}}
itself.
Note, however, that this identification depends on the choice of a normal semifinite faithful trace
\tau_{0}
on
{\mathcal{N}}
.

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a von Neumann algebra and let
{\mathcal{N}}
be a semifinite factor with normal semifinite faithful trace
\tau_{0}
.
Then (
3.41
) identifies the flow of weights of
{\mathcal{M}}
and
{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{N}}
.
This identification has the property that

(\psi\otimes\omega)^{\wedge}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda_{\omega}(t)\,\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{\log\lambda_{\omega}(t)}\,dt,

(5.9)

for
\psi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
and
\omega\in{\mathcal{N}}_{*}^{+}
, where
\lambda_{\omega}(t)
is the spectral scale of
\omega
relative to
\tau_{0}
(see
section

3.3.4
).

Proof.

We recall that
proposition

3.11
identifies the core of
{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{N}}
as
(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\otimes{\mathcal{N}},\tilde{\theta}\otimes\id,\tau\otimes\tau_{0})
, where
(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}},\tilde{\theta},\tau)
is a core of
{\mathcal{M}}
.
Let
\rho_{\omega}=d\omega/d\tau_{0}\in L^{1}({\mathcal{N}},\tau_{0})
be the density operator
\omega
.
Notice that the dual weight of
\psi\otimes\omega
is simply
\tilde{\psi}\otimes\omega
. Thus,

h_{\psi\otimes\omega}=\frac{d(\psi\otimes\omega)^{\sim}}{d(\tau\otimes\tau_{0})}=\frac{d\tilde{\psi}}{d\tau}\otimes\frac{d\omega}{d\tau_{0}}=h_{\psi}\otimes\rho_{\omega}.

Using the spectral measure decomposition
\rho_{\omega}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}t\,dp_{\omega}(t)
, we obtain

h_{\psi}\otimes\rho_{\omega}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}t\,h_{\psi}\otimes dp_{\omega}(t)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}h_{t\psi}\otimes dp_{\omega}(t).

Thus, the spectral projection
e_{\psi\otimes\omega}=1_{(1,\infty)}(h_{\psi\otimes\omega})
of
h_{\psi\otimes\omega}
is

e_{\psi\otimes\omega}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}1_{(1,\infty)}(h_{t\psi})\otimes dp_{\omega}(t)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}e_{t\psi}\otimes dp_{\omega}(t).

This and (
3.59
) imply

\displaystyle(\psi\otimes\omega)^{\wedge}=(\tau\otimes\tau_{0})\big(({\,\cdot\,})e_{\psi\otimes\omega}\big)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}\tau(({\,\cdot\,})e_{t\psi})\,\tau_{0}(dp_{\omega}(t))

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}\widehat{t\psi}\,\tau_{0}(dp_{\omega}(t))=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}t\,\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{\log t}\,\tau_{0}(dp_{\omega}(t)).

By
eq.

3.21
, the measure
\tau_{0}(dp_{\omega}(t))
is the push-forward measure (see
[
170
, Ex. 1.4.38]
)
\mu
of the Lebesgue measure along the measurable function
\lambda_{\omega}
.
Indeed,
\mu(A)=\lvert\lambda_{\omega}^{-1}(A)\rvert=\int_{0}^{\infty}1_{A}(\lambda_{\omega}(t))\,dt=\tau_{0}(1_{A}(\rho_{\omega}))
for measurable
A\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
.
This and (
3.59
), imply

(\psi\otimes\omega)^{\wedge}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}\widehat{\lambda_{\omega}(t)\psi}\,dt=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}\lambda_{\omega}(t)\,\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{\log\lambda_{\omega}(t)}\,dt.

∎

Equation

5.9
can be expressed as a convolution of
\hat{\psi}
by a measure
\nu_{\omega}
on the multiplicative group
(0,\infty)
with respect to the action
\theta_{\log}
of
(0,\infty)
on the flow of weights.
Indeed, the proof shows that we may write (
5.9
) as

(\psi\otimes\omega)^{\wedge}=\hat{\psi}*_{\theta}\nu_{\omega}:=\int_{(0,\infty)}\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{\log t}\,d\nu_{\omega}(t),

(5.10)

where
\nu_{\omega}
is the measure defined by
d\nu_{\omega}(t)=t\tau_{0}(dp_{\omega}(t))
with
p_{\omega}
denoting the spectral measure of
\rho_{\omega}
.
Note that
\nu_{\omega}
is a positive measure with normalization
\nu_{\omega}((0,\infty))=\omega(1)
.
We can rewrite it in the concise form

\nu_{\omega}(A)=\tau_{0}(\rho_{\omega}|_{A}),

where
\rho_{\omega}|_{A}
is the restriction of
\rho_{\omega}
to the part of the spectrum contained in the Borel set
A\subset(0,\infty)
, i.e.,
\rho_{\omega}|_{A}=\rho_{\omega}\,1_{A}(\rho_{\omega})
.

In the case that
{\mathcal{N}}
is a type
{\mathrm{I}}
factor with
\tau_{0}
the standard trace, (
5.9
) reduces to (
5.5
).
For states with a flat spectrum, we get:

Corollary 5.8
.

In the setting of
proposition

5.7
, let
\omega=\tau_{0}(p)^{-1}\tau_{0}(p({\,\cdot\,}))
for a finite projection.
Then

(\psi\otimes\omega)^{\wedge}=\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{\log\tau_{0}(p)}.

(5.11)

In particular, we have
(\psi\otimes\omega)^{\wedge}=\hat{\psi}
if the projection
p
has trace one.
This is, in particular, the case if
\omega
is a pure state on a type
{\mathrm{I}}
factor with the standard trace.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a von Neumann algebra and let
{\mathcal{N}}
be a semifinite factor with normal semifinite faithful trace
\tau_{0}
.
We identify the flow of weights of
{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{N}}
with that of
{\mathcal{M}}
as in
proposition

5.7
.
If
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
are normal states on
{\mathcal{N}}
, then

\lVert(\psi\otimes\omega_{1})^{\wedge}-(\psi\otimes\omega_{2})^{\wedge}\rVert\leq\sup_{t>0}\ \lVert\hat{\psi}-\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{t}\rVert.

(5.12)

Proof.

Let
\lambda_{i}(t)
denote the spectral scales of
\omega_{i}
,
i=1,2
, relative to
\tau_{0}
.
Then

\displaystyle\lVert(\psi\otimes\omega_{1})^{\wedge}-(\psi\otimes\omega_{2})^{\wedge}\rVert

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda_{1}(t)\,\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{\log\lambda_{1}(t)}\,dt-\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda_{2}(s)\,\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{\log\lambda_{2}(s)}\,ds\bigg\|

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda_{1}(t)\lambda_{2}(s)\,\big(\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{\log\lambda_{1}(t)}-\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{\log\lambda_{2}(s)}\big)\,dt\,ds\bigg\|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda_{1}(t)\lambda_{2}(s)\,\big\|\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{\log\lambda_{1}(t)}-\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{\log\lambda_{2}(s)}\big\|\,dt\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t,s>0}\lVert\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{\log\lambda_{1}(t)}-\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{\log\lambda_{2}(s)}\rVert\ \!\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda_{1}(t)\,dt\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda_{2}(s)\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t>0}\ \lVert\hat{\psi}-\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{\log t}\rVert.\qed

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a von Neumann algebra and let
{\mathcal{N}}
be a semifinite factor with normal semifinite faithful trace
\tau_{0}
.
We identify the flow of weights of
{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{N}}
with that of
{\mathcal{M}}
as in
proposition

5.7
.
If
{\mathcal{N}}
is not a finite type
{\mathrm{I}}
factor, then

\sup_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}\lVert(\psi\otimes\omega_{1})^{\wedge}-(\psi\otimes\omega_{2})^{\wedge}\rVert\geq\ \sup_{t>0}\ \lVert\hat{\psi}-\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{t}\rVert,

(5.13)

where the optimization is over pairs of normal states on
{\mathcal{N}}
.

Proof.

Consider finite projections
p
and
q
in
{\mathcal{N}}
and let
n=\tau_{0}(p)
and
m=\tau_{0}(q)
.
Consider the states
\omega_{1}=n^{-1}\tau(p({\,\cdot\,}))
and
\omega_{2}=m^{-1}\tau(q({\,\cdot\,}))
.
Then, by
corollary

5.8
,

\displaystyle\lVert(\psi\otimes\omega_{1})^{\wedge}-(\psi\otimes\omega_{2})^{\wedge}\rVert=\lVert\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{\log n}-\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{\log m}\rVert=\lVert\hat{\psi}-\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{\log(n/m)}\rVert.

The possible values of
n/m
are exactly the ratios of numbers that occur as the dimensions of finite projections.
If
{\mathcal{N}}
is a type
{\mathrm{II}}
factor, then the possible values of
n/m
are
{\mathbb{R}}>0
(see
section

3.2
), so that the claim follows.
If
{\mathcal{N}}
is a type
{\mathrm{I}}_{\infty}
factor, then the possible values
n/m
are the positive rational numbers (this is true even if
\tau_{0}
is not the standard trace).
If
t>0
is irrational, we may pick a sequence of rational numbers
r_{n}\to e^{t}
.
It then follows that
\lVert\hat{\psi}-\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{t}\rVert=\lim_{n}\lVert\hat{\psi}-\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{\log r_{n}}\rVert\leq\sup_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}\ \lVert(\psi\otimes\omega_{1})^{\wedge}-(\psi\otimes\omega_{2})^{\wedge}\rVert.

∎

As a consequence of
proposition

5.7
, we deduce the following generalization of
theorem

5.2
:

Theorem 5.11
.

Let
\psi
be a normal positive linear functional on a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
, and let
{\mathcal{N}}
be a semifinite factor not of finite type
{\mathrm{I}}
.
Then

\adjustlimits{\sup}_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}{\inf}_{u}\ \lVert\psi\otimes\omega_{1}-u(\psi\otimes\omega_{2})u^{*}\rVert=\sup_{t>0}\ \lVert\hat{\psi}-\hat{\psi}\circ\theta_{t}\rVert,

(5.14)

where the first supremum is over normal states on
{\mathcal{N}}
and the infimum is over unitaries in
{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{N}}
.

Proof.

We use
theorem

3.15
to write the LHS of (
5.14
) in terms of the flow of weights of
{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{N}}
:

\adjustlimits{\sup}_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}{\inf}_{u}\ \lVert\psi\otimes\omega_{1}-u(\psi\otimes\omega_{2})u^{*}\rVert=\sup_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}\ \lVert(\psi\otimes\omega_{1})^{\wedge}-(\psi\otimes\omega_{2})^{\wedge}\rVert,

(5.15)

Therefore,
lemma

5.9
shows the inequality
\leq
, and
lemma

5.10
shows the inequality
\geq
.
∎

Before passing on, we note another consequence of
proposition

5.7
that will be helpful later on.

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a von Neumann algebra with normal positive linear functionals
\psi,\phi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
.
Let
{\mathcal{N}}
be a semifinite factor with normal semifinite faithful trace
\tau_{0}
and let
0\neq p\in{\mathcal{N}}
be a finite projection.
Consider the state
\omega=\tau_{0}(p)^{-1}\tau_{0}(p({\,\cdot\,}))
.
Then

\inf_{u\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}})}\lVert u\phi u^{*}-\psi\rVert=\inf_{u\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{N}})}\lVert u(\psi\otimes\omega)u^{*}-\phi\otimes\omega\rVert

(5.16)

Proof.

We identify the flow of weights of
{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{N}}
with the flow of weights of
{\mathcal{M}}
based on (
3.41
).
Using
theorem

3.15
, we may rewrite the LHS as
\lVert\hat{\psi}-\hat{\psi}\rVert
and the RHS as
\lVert(\psi\otimes\omega)^{\wedge}-(\phi\otimes\omega)^{\wedge}\rVert
.
The claim now follows from
corollary

5.8
:

\lVert(\psi\otimes\omega)^{\wedge}-(\phi\otimes\omega)^{\wedge}\rVert=\lVert(\hat{\psi}-\hat{\phi})\circ\theta_{\log\tau_{0}(p)}\rVert=\lVert\hat{\psi}-\hat{\psi}\rVert.\qed

5.2
Ergodic flows on abelian von Neumann algebras

In this subsection, we consider ergodic flows on abelian von Neumann algebras.
Based on
theorems

3.15
and
5.2
, the results that we derive here have direct consequences for catalytic states on von Neumann algebras.

If
\theta:{\mathbb{R}}\curvearrowright{\mathcal{A}}
is a point-
\sigma
-weakly continuous flow on a general abelian von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
, we define the
\theta
-regular state space

\mathfrak{S}_{*}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}})
as in (
3.61
):

\mathfrak{S}_{*}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}}):=\big\{\chi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{A}}):\chi\circ\theta_{t}\geq e^{-t}\chi,\ t>0\big\}.

Clearly, this is a convex space.
Its diameter
\diam\mathfrak{S}_{*}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}})
is defined with respect to the norm distance.
We measure how much normal positive linear functionals on
{\mathcal{A}}
deviate under the flow
\theta
with the lower semicontinuous function

\kappa_{\theta}:{\mathcal{A}}_{*}^{+}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{+},\qquad\kappa_{\theta}(\chi)=\sup_{t>0}\,\lVert\chi-\chi\circ\theta_{t}\rVert.

(5.17)

Note that
\kappa_{\theta}(\chi)=0
if and only if
\chi
is
\theta
-invariant, and that every
\theta
-invariant state is
\theta
-regular.
If
\theta:{\mathbb{R}}\curvearrowright{\mathcal{A}}
is the flow of weights of a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
, then
theorem

5.2
implies

\kappa_{\theta}(\hat{\psi})=\kappa(\psi),\qquad\psi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}.

(5.18)

The main result of this subsection is the following:

Theorem 5.13
.

Let
\theta:{\mathbb{R}}\curvearrowright{\mathcal{A}}
be an ergodic point-
\sigma
-weakly continuous flow on an abelian von Neumann algebra.
Let
T\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
be the minimal period of
\theta
(
T=\infty
if
\theta
is aperiodic), then

\diam\mathfrak{S}_{*}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}})=\sup_{\chi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}})}\kappa_{\theta}(\chi)=2\frac{1-e^{-T/2}}{1+e^{-T/2}}.

(5.19)

If
T<\infty
, then
{\mathcal{A}}
admits both a
\theta
-invariant normal state, and it admits a
\theta
-regular normal state that is as non-
\theta
-invariant as possible in the sense that
\kappa_{\theta}(\chi)=\diam\mathfrak{S}_{*}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}})
.
If
T=\infty
, exactly one of the following is true:

•

There exists an invariant normal state on
{\mathcal{A}}
and
\kappa_{\theta}(\chi)<2
for faithful
\chi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{A}})
.

•

There exists no invariant normal state on
{\mathcal{A}}
and
\kappa_{\theta}(\chi)=2
for all
\chi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{A}})
.

We begin by following
[
57
, Sec. 6]
in noting that

{\mathcal{A}}_{\theta}=\{a\in{\mathcal{A}}:\lim_{t\to 0}\,\lVert a-\theta_{t}(a)\rVert=0\}

(5.20)

is a
\sigma
-weakly dense
C^{*}
-subalgebra.
Thus,
\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{A}})\subset\mathfrak{S}({\mathcal{A}}_{\theta})
is weak*-dense, where
\mathfrak{S}({\mathcal{A}}_{\theta})
denotes the state space of the
C^{*}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}_{\theta}
.
For
\omega\in\mathfrak{S}({\mathcal{A}}_{\theta})
, we set

\chi_{\omega}=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t}\,\omega\circ\theta_{-t}\,dt\in\mathfrak{S}({\mathcal{A}}_{\theta}).

(5.21)

Note that
\chi_{\omega}\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{A}})
if
\omega\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{A}})
.

Lemma 5.14

(
[
57
, Sec. 6]
)
.

1.

\omega\mapsto\chi_{\omega}
is an affine bijection of the convex sets
\mathfrak{S}({\mathcal{A}}_{\theta})
and
\mathfrak{S}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}}_{\theta}):=\{\chi\in\mathfrak{S}({\mathcal{A}}_{\theta}):\chi\circ\theta_{t}\geq e^{-t}\chi,\ t>0\}
.

2.

{\mathcal{A}}_{*}
is linearly spanned by
\mathfrak{S}_{*}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}})
.

We briefly sketch the easy part of the proof. Namely, we check that
\chi_{\omega}\in\mathfrak{S}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}}_{\theta})
for all
\omega\in\mathfrak{S}({\mathcal{A}}_{\theta})
:
If
t>0
, then

e^{t}\,\chi_{\omega}\circ\theta_{t}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!e^{t-s}\omega\circ\theta_{t-s}\,ds=\int_{-t}^{\infty}\!e^{-s}\omega\circ\theta_{-s}\,ds\geq\int_{0}^{\infty}\!e^{-s}\omega\circ\theta_{-s}\,ds=\chi_{\omega}.

Lemma 5.15
.

\sup_{\chi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}})}\kappa_{\theta}(\chi)=\sup_{\chi\in\mathfrak{S}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}}_{\theta})}\kappa_{\theta}(\chi)
.

Proof.

The inequality
“
\leq
”
is trivial.
Given
\varepsilon>0
, let
t>0
,
\chi\in\mathfrak{S}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}}_{\theta})
be such that
\lVert\chi-\chi\circ\theta_{t}\rVert
is
\varepsilon
-close to the supremum on the RHS.
By
lemma

5.14
,
\chi=\chi_{\omega}
for a unique
\omega\in\mathfrak{S}({\mathcal{A}}_{\theta})
.
Let
(\omega_{\alpha})
be a net of normal states on
{\mathcal{A}}
that weak*-converges to
\omega
.
Then
\chi_{\omega_{\alpha}}\in\mathfrak{S}^{\theta}_{*}({\mathcal{A}})
. This and weak*-lower semicontinuity of the norm on
{\mathcal{A}}_{\theta}^{*}
imply

\RHS-\varepsilon\leq\lVert\chi_{\omega}-\chi_{\omega}\circ\theta_{t}\rVert\leq\varliminf_{\alpha}\lVert\chi_{\omega_{\alpha}}-\chi_{\omega_{\alpha}}\circ\theta_{t}\rVert\leq\LHS.\qed

Lemma

5.15
reduces much of the analysis that we have to do to the point-norm continuous flow on
{\mathcal{A}}_{\theta}
.
In general, if
\theta:{\mathbb{R}}\curvearrowright{\mathcal{B}}
is a point-norm continuous action on a
C^{*}
-algebra
{\mathcal{B}}
, then the map
{\mathcal{B}}^{*}\times{\mathbb{R}}\ni(\omega,t)\mapsto\omega\circ\theta_{t}\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}
is jointly continuous when the dual space is equipped with the weak*-topology.
36
36

36

This follows from the triangle inequality: If
(\omega_{\alpha},t_{\alpha})\to(\omega,t)
then
\lvert(\omega\circ\theta_{t}-\omega_{\alpha}\circ\theta_{t_{\alpha}})(a)\rvert\leq\lvert(\omega-\omega_{\alpha})(\theta_{t}(a))\rvert+\lVert\omega\rVert\lVert(\theta_{t}-\theta_{t_{\alpha}})(a)\rVert\to 0
.

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
{\mathcal{B}}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra and let
\theta:{\mathbb{R}}\curvearrowright{\mathcal{B}}
be point-norm continuous
{\mathbb{R}}
-action.
For a state
\omega
on
{\mathcal{B}}
, denote by

\per_{\theta}(\omega)=\inf\{t>0:\omega\circ\theta_{t}=\omega\}\qquad(\text{with}\ \inf\emptyset=\infty)

(5.22)

its minimal period under
\theta
.
Then
\per_{\theta}:\mathfrak{S}({\mathcal{B}})\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
is weak*-lower semicontinuous.

Proof.

We show that the sublevel sets of
\per_{\theta}
are weak*-closed.
Let
T>0
and let
(\omega_{\alpha})
be a weak*-Cauchy net of states on
{\mathcal{B}}
with minimal period
\leq T
.
For each
\alpha
, let
t_{\alpha}
be a period for
\omega_{\alpha}
in the interval
(T/2,T]
.
To see that these exist, take any period
s_{\alpha}\in(0,T]
of
\omega_{\alpha}
, which exist since
\per_{\theta}(\omega_{\alpha})\leq T
, and let
t_{\alpha}=2^{n_{\alpha}}s_{\alpha}
, where
n_{\alpha}
is the smallest integer
\geq\log_{2}(T/s_{\alpha})-1
.
We can pass to a subnet of
(\omega_{\alpha})
such that
t=\lim_{\alpha}t_{\alpha}
exists since
[T/2,T]
is compact.
We claim that
t
is a period for
\omega=\lim_{\alpha}\omega_{\alpha}
.
Indeed, joint continuity of
(s,\nu)\mapsto\nu\circ\theta_{s}
implies

\omega\circ\theta_{t}=\lim_{\alpha}\omega_{\alpha}\circ\theta_{t_{\alpha}}=\lim_{\alpha}\omega_{\alpha}=\omega.

(5.23)

Since
t\leq T
is a period for
\omega
, the minimal period of
\omega
is bounded by
T
.
∎

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
\theta:{\mathbb{R}}\curvearrowright{\mathcal{B}}
be a point-norm continuous flow on an abelian
C^{*}
-algebra
{\mathcal{B}}
.
Let
\omega
be a pure state that is aperiodic under
\theta
.
Then
\chi=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t}\omega\circ\theta_{-t}dt\in\mathfrak{S}^{\theta}({\mathcal{B}})
satisfies

\lim_{t\to\infty}\,\lVert\chi-\chi\circ\theta_{t}\rVert=2.

(5.24)

Proof.

We follow ideas from
[
57
, Secs. 6 & 10]
.
If
t>0
, we have

\displaystyle\lVert\chi-\chi\circ\theta_{t}\rVert

\displaystyle=\lVert\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-s}\omega\circ\theta_{-s}ds-\int_{-t}^{\infty}e^{-s-t}\omega\circ\theta_{-s}ds\rVert

\displaystyle=\lVert\int_{0}^{\infty}(1-e^{-t})e^{-s}\omega\circ\theta_{-s}ds-\int_{-t}^{0}e^{-s-t}\omega\circ\theta_{-s}ds\rVert.

The assumption that
\omega
is aperiodic and the fact that distinct pure states on abelian
C^{*}
-algebras are orthogonal imply that this is the norm difference of a pair of orthogonal positive linear functionals on
{\mathcal{B}}
. Therefore, we can continue the calculation as

\displaystyle=\lVert\int_{0}^{\infty}(1-e^{-t})e^{-s}\omega\circ\theta_{-s}ds\rVert+\lVert\int_{-t}^{0}e^{-s-t}\omega\circ\theta_{-s}ds\rVert

\displaystyle=(1-e^{-t})\,\lVert\int_{-t}^{0}e^{-s}\omega\circ\theta_{-s}ds\rVert+e^{-t}\,\lVert\int_{-t}^{0}e^{-s}\omega\circ\theta_{-s}ds\rVert

\displaystyle=1-e^{-t}+e^{-t}\int_{0}^{t}e^{s}ds=1-e^{-t}+e^{-t}(e^{t}-1)=2-2e^{-t}\to 2.\qed

Next, we need a dichotomy for
{\mathbb{R}}
-actions on abelian von Neumann algebras based on
[
83
, Sec. 4.5]
.
The next Proposition and its proof were communicated to the author by Amine Marrakchi:

Proposition 5.18
.

Let
\theta:{\mathbb{R}}\curvearrowright{\mathcal{A}}
be a (not necessarily ergodic)
{\mathbb{R}}
-action on an abelian von Neumann algebra.
The following are equivalent:

1.

there exists no
\theta
-invariant normal state on
{\mathcal{A}}
;

2.

there is a faithful normal state
\chi
on
{\mathcal{A}}
such that
\kappa_{\theta}(\chi)=2
;

3.

for every normal state
\chi
on
{\mathcal{A}}
it holds that
\kappa_{\theta}(\chi)=2
.

Proof.

Let
(X,\mu)
be a standard measure space such that
{\mathcal{A}}=L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
.
Let
\sigma:{\mathbb{R}}\curvearrowright X
be the
\mu
-nonsingular action such that
\theta_{t}(f)(x)=f(\sigma_{t}(x))
.
We denote by
\sigma_{t}(\mu)
the push-foward measure, i.e.,
\sigma_{t}(\mu)(A)=\mu(\sigma_{t}^{-1}(A))=\mu(\sigma_{-t}(A))
,
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
While
\sigma_{t}
does not preserve
\mu
, it does preserve the measure class of
\mu
, i.e.,
\sigma_{t}(\mu)\sim\mu
,
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
We define the positive measurable function
m_{t}=\sqrt{d\sigma_{t}(\mu)/d\mu}:X\to{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
.
The operator
U_{t}f(x)=m_{t}\cdot(f\circ\sigma_{-t})
is a unitary operator on
L^{2}(X,\mu)
, which implements the action
\theta:{\mathbb{R}}\curvearrowright{\mathcal{A}}
in the sense that

(U_{t}^{*}fU_{t}g)=\theta_{t}(f)\cdot g,\qquad t\in{\mathbb{R}}

(5.25)

(this is the so-called Koopmann representation of the dynamical system
\sigma:{\mathbb{R}}\curvearrowright X
).
For every
\chi\in{\mathcal{A}}_{*}^{+}
, there is a unique positive
\mu
-absolutely continuous measure
\nu_{\chi}
on
X
such that
\chi(f)=\int fd\nu_{\chi}
,
f\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)={\mathcal{A}}
.
Note that the
1
-norm distance of measures is the norm distance on
{\mathcal{A}}_{*}
, i.e.,
\lVert\chi-\chi^{\prime}\rVert=\lVert\nu_{\chi}-\nu_{\chi^{\prime}}\rVert_{1}
, and that the measure corresponding to
\chi\circ\theta_{t}
is the push-forward measure
\sigma_{t}(\nu_{\chi})
.
37
37

37

The
1
-norm is defined on signed measures as is twice the total variation norm, i.e.,
\lVert\mu\rVert_{1}=2\sup_{A}\lvert\mu(A)\rvert
, where the optimization is over measurable subsets
A\subset X
.

Thus, we have
\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{\theta}(\chi)=\sup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\lVert\nu_{\chi}-\sigma_{t}(\nu_{\chi})\rVert
.
We set
g_{\chi}=(d\nu_{\chi}/d\mu)^{1/2}\in L^{2}(X,\mu)^{+}
, so that
\nu_{\chi}(f)=\langle g_{\chi},fg_{\chi}\rangle_{L^{2}}
.
It follows that
U_{t}g_{\chi}=g_{\chi\circ\theta_{t}}
.
Then
lemma

5.19
below implies that

\inf_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\,\langle g_{\chi},U_{t}g_{\chi}\rangle_{L^{2}}=0\iff\kappa_{\theta}(\chi)=\sup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\,\lVert\nu_{\chi}-\sigma_{t}(\nu_{\chi})\rVert_{1}=2.

(5.26)

Thus,
1

\Leftrightarrow

2
follows from
[
83
, Prop. 4.5]
.
Next, we show
3

\Rightarrow

2
based on an argument from
[
83
, Lem. 4.4]
:
Let
t_{n}
be such that
\langle g_{\chi},U_{t_{n}}g_{\chi}\rangle_{L^{2}}\to 0
.
Since
\chi
is faithful,
g_{\chi}\in L^{2}(X,\mu)^{+}
has full support.
For
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)^{+}
, set
g_{j}=f_{j}g_{\chi}\in L^{2}(X,\mu)^{+}
.
Then
g_{j}\leq\lVert f_{j}\rVert g_{\chi}
implies
0\leq\langle g_{1},U_{t_{n}}g_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}}\leq\lVert f_{1}\rVert\lVert f_{2}\rVert\langle g_{\chi},U_{t_{n}}g_{\chi}\rangle\to 0
.
If we decompose non-positive functions
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
into linear combinations of positive functions, it follows that
\langle g_{1},U_{t_{n}}g_{2}\rangle\to 0
for all
g_{1},g_{2}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)g_{\chi}
.
Since
g_{\chi}
has full support, the latter is a dense subspace of
L^{2}(X,\mu)
. Thus,
U_{t_{n}}\to 0
in the weak operator topology.
In particular, we get
\langle g_{\chi^{\prime}},U_{t_{n}}g_{\chi^{\prime}}\rangle\to 0
for all
\chi^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{A}})
.
The claim follows from (
5.26
).
∎

Lemma 5.19
.

Let
(X,\mu)
be a
\sigma
-finite measure space and let
\nu,\nu^{\prime}
be
\mu
-absolutely continuous probability measures. Let
g^{(\prime)}=(d\nu^{(\prime)}/d\mu)^{1/2}\in L^{2}(X,\mu)^{+}
.
Then

1-\delta\leq\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle_{L^{2}}\leq(1-\delta^{2})^{1/2},\qquad\delta=\frac{1}{2}\lVert\nu-\nu^{\prime}\rVert_{1}.

(5.27)

Proof.

The lower bound follows from the standard estimate
\lVert g-g^{\prime}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\lVert\nu-\nu^{\prime}\rVert_{1}
:

1-\delta\leq 1-\frac{1}{2}\lVert g-g^{\prime}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}=1-(1-\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle_{L^{2}})=\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle_{L^{2}}
.
To obtain the upper bound, consider the density operators
\rho^{(\prime)}=|g^{(\prime)}\rangle\langle g^{(\prime)}|
on
L^{2}(X,\mu)
.
Then
(\nu-\nu^{\prime})(f)=\tr(\rho-\rho^{\prime})f
,
f\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
, implies

\displaystyle\delta=2\lVert\nu-\nu^{\prime}\rVert\leq 2\lVert|g\rangle\langle g|-|g^{\prime}\rangle\langle g^{\prime}|\rVert_{1}=(1-\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle^{2})^{1/2}.

Rearranging shows the upper bound.
∎

Proof of
theorem

5.13
for periodic flows.

The case where
T=0
is trivial.
Let
0<T<\infty
be the minimal period of
\theta
.
By
[
57
, Lem. 10.2 & 10.3]
,
\diam\mathfrak{S}_{*}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}})=\diam\mathfrak{S}({\mathcal{A}}_{\theta})=2(1-e^{-T/2})/(1+e^{-T/2})
.
It is clear that
\kappa_{\theta}(\chi)\leq\diam\mathfrak{S}_{*}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}})
for all
\chi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}^{\theta}
.
It remains to show that there exists a normal state
\chi
on
{\mathcal{A}}
such that
\kappa_{\theta}(\chi)=2(1-e^{-T/2})/(1+e^{-T/2})
.
By
[
57
, Lem. 10.2]
, we may assume
{\mathcal{A}}_{\theta}=C({\mathbb{R}}/T{\mathbb{Z}})
with
\theta_{t}(f)=f\circ\sigma_{t}
,
\sigma_{t}(s)=s-t
with addition mod
T
.
Periodicity of the flow implies that there is a unique
\theta
-invariant normal state
\omega
on
{\mathcal{A}}
, which is necessarily faithful.
38
38

38

Indeed, take any normal state
\omega_{0}
on
{\mathcal{A}}
and set
\omega=T^{-1}\int_{0}^{T}\omega_{0}\circ\theta_{t}dt
.
Invariance is clear, faithfulness holds because
\supp(\omega)
is a
\theta
-invariant nonzero projection, hence the identity, and uniqueness holds because
d\omega^{\prime}/d\omega
is
\theta
-invariant, hence constant, for every other
\theta
-invariant normal state
\omega^{\prime}
.

It follows that
\omega(f)=T^{-1}\int_{0}^{T}f(t)dt
,
f\in C({\mathbb{R}}/T{\mathbb{Z}})
because the only
\sigma
-invariant Borel probability measure on
{\mathbb{R}}/T{\mathbb{Z}}
is the uniform distribution.
Faithfulness of
\omega
on
{\mathcal{A}}
and
\sigma
-weak-density of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\theta}\subset{\mathcal{A}}
lets us identify
{\mathcal{A}}
with
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}/T{\mathbb{Z}})
.
Let
\chi
be the normal state on
{\mathcal{A}}=L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}/T{\mathbb{Z}})
with

\chi(f)=\int_{0}^{T}p(s)f(s)\,ds,\qquad p(s)=(1-e^{-T})^{-1}e^{-s}.

We claim that the state
\chi\circ\theta_{t}
has the density
g_{t}(s)p(s)
, where

g_{t}=e^{-t}1_{[0,T-t)}+e^{T-t}1_{[T-t,T)},\qquad t\in{\mathbb{R}},

with addition modulo
T
.
Indeed,

\displaystyle\chi(\theta_{t}(f))

\displaystyle=(1-e^{-T})^{-1}\bigg(\int_{t}^{T}f(s-t)e^{-s}ds+\int_{0}^{t}f(s+T-t)e^{-s}ds\bigg)

\displaystyle=(1-e^{-T})^{-1}\bigg(\int_{0}^{T-t}f(s)e^{-s-t}ds+\int_{T-t}^{T}f(s)e^{-s-t+T}ds\bigg)=\int_{0}^{T}f(s)g_{t}(s)p(s)\,ds.

Since
g_{t}\geq e^{-t}
, it follows that
\chi
is
\theta
-regular.
Next, we compute the norm distance of
\chi
and
\chi\circ\theta_{T/2}
:

\displaystyle\lVert\nu-\nu\circ\theta_{T/2}\rVert

\displaystyle=\lVert p-g_{t}p\rVert_{L^{1}}=(1-e^{-T})^{-1}\int_{0}^{T}\lvert 1-g_{t}(s)\rvert e^{-s}ds

\displaystyle=(1-e^{-T})^{-1}\bigg(\int_{0}^{T/2}\lvert 1-e^{-T/2}\rvert e^{-s}ds+\int_{T/2}^{T}\lvert 1-e^{T/2}\rvert e^{-s}ds\bigg)

\displaystyle=(1-e^{-T})^{-1}\Big((1-e^{-T/2})(1-e^{-T/2})+(e^{T/2}-1)(e^{-T/2}-e^{-T})\Big)

\displaystyle=2(1-e^{-T/2})/(1-e^{T/2}).

It follows that

2\frac{1-e^{-T/2}}{1-e^{T/2}}=\diam\mathfrak{S}_{*}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}})\geq\kappa_{\theta}(\chi)\geq\lVert\nu-\nu\circ\theta_{T/2}\rVert=2\frac{1-e^{-T/2}}{1-e^{T/2}}.

Thus, we have equality everywhere, which finishes the proof.
∎

Proof of
theorem

5.13
for aperiodic flows.

Clearly, we have
2\geq\diam\mathfrak{S}_{*}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}})\geq\sup_{\chi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}})}\kappa(\chi)
.
We have to show that the RHS equals
2
.

Case 1: There exists an invariant normal state.

The claim that
\kappa_{\theta}(\chi)<2
for all faithful normal states
\chi
on
{\mathcal{A}}
follows from
proposition

5.18
.
It remains to show
\sup_{\chi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}})}\kappa(\chi)=2
.
By
lemma

5.15
, we can equivalently optimize over states
\chi\in\mathfrak{S}({\mathcal{A}}_{\theta})
.
We claim that
{\mathcal{A}}_{\theta}
necessarily admits aperiodic pure states.
In this case, our claim is implied by
lemma

5.17
.
To see that
{\mathcal{A}}_{\theta}
has aperiodic pure states, we identify
{\mathcal{A}}_{\theta}=C(X)
with
X
being the set of pure states equipped with the weak*-topology.
On the set of pure states, the flow acts as
\omega\mapsto\omega\circ\theta_{t}
, which defines a continuous flow
\sigma:{\mathbb{R}}\curvearrowright X
.
This flow is ergodic since the only
\sigma
-invariant continuous functions on
X
are the constant functions (because
({\mathcal{A}}_{\theta})^{\theta}\subset{\mathcal{A}}^{\theta}={\mathbb{C}}1
).
Let
\omega
be an invariant normal state on
{\mathcal{A}}
.
Ergodicity implies that
\omega
is faithful since
\supp(\omega)
is a
\theta
-invariant nonzero projection.
Let
\mu
be the
\sigma
-invariant Borel measure on
X
such that
\omega(f)=\int_{X}f\,d\mu
,
f\in C(X)={\mathcal{A}}_{\theta}
.
Since
\omega
is faithful and
{\mathcal{A}}_{\theta}
is
\sigma
-weakly dense in
{\mathcal{A}}
, we may identify
{\mathcal{A}}=L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
.
By
lemma

5.16
, the minimal periodicity
\per_{\sigma}:X\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
,
\per(x)=\inf\{t>0:\sigma_{t}(x)=x\}
is lower semicontinuous, hence Borel-measurable.
Since
\per_{\sigma}
is a
\sigma
-invariant function, i.e.,
\per_{\sigma}(\sigma_{t}(x))=\per_{\sigma}(x)
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, ergodicity implies that it is constant
\mu
-almost everywhere, i.e.,
\mu
-almost every point
x\in X
has period
T
for some fixed
T\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
.
If
T<\infty
, then
\theta
would be
T
-periodic on
{\mathcal{A}}
, which contradicts our assumption.
Therefore,
\mu
-almost every pure state
\omega
on
{\mathcal{A}}_{\theta}
is aperiodic.

Case 2: There exists no invariant normal state.

proposition

5.18
implies that every normal state
\chi
on
{\mathcal{A}}
has the property
\kappa_{\theta}(\chi)=2
.
Since
2\geq\diam\mathfrak{S}_{*}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}})\geq\kappa(\chi)=2
for every
\chi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}})
, this implies
\diam\mathfrak{S}_{*}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}})=2
.
∎

We now prove
theorems

5.4
,
5.5
and
5.6
based on the above:

Proof of
theorem

5.4
.

Let
\theta:{\mathbb{R}}\curvearrowright{\mathcal{A}}
be the flow of weights of
{\mathcal{M}}
.

Theorem

5.2
implies
\kappa_{\min}({\mathcal{M}})=\inf\kappa_{\theta}(\chi)
, where the infimum ranges over
\theta
-regular normal states
\chi
on
{\mathcal{A}}
.
Thus, the equivalence between the existence of a
\theta
-invariant normal state and
\kappa_{\min}({\mathcal{M}})=0
follows from
proposition

5.18
.
Since every
\theta
-invariant normal state is
\theta
-regular, it is the spectral state of a state on
{\mathcal{M}}
, which by
theorem

5.2
is universally catalytic.
This finishes the proof.
∎

Proof of
theorem

5.5
.

Let
\theta:{\mathbb{R}}\curvearrowright{\mathcal{A}}
be the flow of weights of
{\mathcal{M}}
.
The statement in
theorem

5.5
directly follow from those in
theorem

5.13
because of the following:

•

\psi\mapsto\hat{\psi}
is an isometric bijection
\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{M}})/_{\sim}\,\to\mathfrak{S}_{*}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}})
(see
theorem

3.15
),

•

\kappa(\psi)=\kappa_{\theta}(\hat{\psi})
and
\psi
is a universalö catalytic if and only if
\hat{\psi}
is
\theta
-invariant (see
theorem

5.2
),

•

the flow of weights is periodic with minimal period
0\leq T<\infty
if and only if
{\mathcal{M}}
is a type
{\mathrm{III}}_{\lambda}
factor with
\lambda=e^{-T}>0
. ∎

Proof of
proposition

5.6
.

The fact that the flow of weights of a direct integral is the direct integral of the corresponding flows of weights (see
lemma

3.10
) implies that a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
is of type
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}
if and only if its flow of weights
\theta:{\mathbb{R}}\curvearrowright{\mathcal{A}}
is trivial, i.e.,
\theta\equiv\id
(in this case,
{\mathcal{A}}=Z({\mathcal{M}})
).
If the flow of weights is trivial, then
theorem

5.2
implies
\kappa(\psi)=0
for all
\psi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
.
Conversely, if
\kappa(\psi)=0
for all
\psi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
, then
theorem

5.2
implies that
\kappa_{\theta}(\chi)=0
for all
\chi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}})
.
By
lemma

5.14
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{*}
is spanned by
\mathfrak{S}_{*}^{\theta}({\mathcal{A}})
.
Thus,
\chi\circ\theta_{t}=\chi
for all
\chi\in{\mathcal{A}}_{*}
, which implies
\theta_{t}=\id
, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
∎

5.3
Catalysis of state transitions on general factors

Proposition 5.20
.

Let
\psi
be a normal positive linear functional on a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
, and let
{\mathcal{N}}
be an AFD factor.
If
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
are normal states on
{\mathcal{N}}
, then

\inf_{u\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{N}})}\ \lVert u(\psi\otimes\omega_{1})u^{*}-\psi\otimes\omega_{2}\rVert\leq\kappa(\psi).

(5.28)

We prove
proposition

5.20
by reducing the general case to the semifinite case via a martingale Lemma inspired by
[
57
, Thm. 8.1]
.
To state it, we need the concept of a conditional expectation:
A conditional expectation onto a von Neumann subalgebra
{\mathcal{N}}
of a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
is a surjective ucp map
E:{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathcal{N}}
such that
E^{2}=E
.

Lemma 5.21
.

Let
{\mathcal{N}}
be a factor and let
{\mathcal{N}}_{\alpha}
be an increasing net of subfactors, indexed by a directed set
I
such that
\bigcup_{\alpha}{\mathcal{N}}_{\alpha}
is
\sigma
-weakly dense in
{\mathcal{N}}
.
Let
E_{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in I
, be conditional expectations onto
{\mathcal{N}}_{\alpha}
, such that
E_{\alpha}E_{\beta}=E_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha\leq\beta
, and such that

\lim_{\alpha}\ \lVert\psi\circ(\id-E_{\alpha})\rVert=0,\qquad\psi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}.

If
proposition

5.20
holds for all
{\mathcal{N}}_{\alpha}
, then it also holds for
{\mathcal{N}}
.

Proof.

We apply the results of
[
57
, Sec. 7]
to the conditional expectations
P_{\alpha}=\id\otimes E_{\alpha}
onto the subalgebras
{\mathcal{M}}\otimes{\mathcal{N}}_{\alpha}\subset{\mathcal{M}}\otimes{\mathcal{N}}
.
In particular, we use that

\lVert\hat{\phi}_{1}-\hat{\phi}_{2}\rVert=\lim_{\alpha}\,\lVert\hat{\phi}_{1,\alpha}-\hat{\phi}_{2,\alpha}\rVert,\qquad\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in({\mathcal{M}}\otimes{\mathcal{N}})_{*}^{+},

(5.29)

where
\hat{\phi}_{i,\alpha}
,
i=1,2
, denotes the spectral state of
\phi_{i,\alpha}=\phi_{i}\restriction{\mathcal{M}}\otimes{\mathcal{N}}_{\alpha}
(note that this is a normal positive linear functional on the flow of weights of
{\mathcal{M}}\otimes{\mathcal{N}}_{\alpha}
).
If we apply this to
\phi_{i}=\psi\otimes\omega_{i}
,
i=1,2
, for normal states
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{N}})
and
\psi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
,
theorem

5.11
implies

\displaystyle\lVert(\psi\otimes\omega_{1})^{\wedge}-(\psi\otimes\omega_{2})^{\wedge}\rVert=\lim_{\alpha}\ \lVert(\psi\otimes\omega_{1,\alpha})^{\wedge}-(\psi\otimes\omega_{2,\alpha})^{\wedge}\rVert\leq\kappa(\psi),

where
\omega_{i,\alpha}=\omega_{i}\restriction{\mathcal{N}}_{\alpha}
,
i=1,2
.
Together with
theorem

3.15
, this implies the claim.
∎

Proof of
proposition

5.20
.

By
theorem

5.11
, the claim holds for semifinite
{\mathcal{N}}
.
It remains to show the claim for type
{\mathrm{III}}
factors.
If
{\mathcal{N}}
is an AFD factor of type
{\mathrm{III}}_{0}
, a net of conditional expectations
E_{\alpha}
onto an increasing net of semifinite subfactors
{\mathcal{N}}_{\alpha}
as in
lemma

5.21
, exists according to
[
57
, Prop. 8.3]
.
Thus, the claim follows from
lemma

5.21
.
If
{\mathcal{N}}
is an AFD factor of type
{\mathrm{III}}_{\lambda}
,
0<\lambda\leq 1
, then it is an ITPFI factor
[
171
,
172
,
70
]
.
Thus, we may write
{\mathcal{N}}
as
{\mathcal{N}}=\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}({\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{n}),\omega_{n})
, where
\dim{\mathcal{H}}_{n}<\infty
, and where each
\omega_{n}
is a faithful state on
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{n})
.
For
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we set
{\mathcal{N}}_{\alpha}=\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}_{n=1}^{\alpha}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{n})
and
E_{\alpha}=\id\otimes\,(\otimes_{n>\alpha}\omega_{n}):{\mathcal{N}}\to{\mathcal{N}}_{\alpha}
.
Then
E_{\alpha}
is a sequence of conditional expectations that satisfies the properties in
lemma

5.21
.
Since each
{\mathcal{N}}_{\alpha}
is semifinite,
lemma

5.21
implies the claim.
∎

Remark 5.22
.

We expect that
proposition

5.20
holds in full generality without the assumption that the factor
{\mathcal{N}}
is AFD.
To prove this stronger statement, one needs to relate the spectral states of
\psi\otimes\omega_{i}
,
i=1,2
, to the spectral states of
\psi
and
\omega_{i}
, which requires, in particular, an understanding of the flow of weights of
{\mathcal{M}}\mathbin{\overline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{N}}
in terms of the flow of weights of
{\mathcal{M}}
and
{\mathcal{N}}
.
This is fully understood for semifinite factors (see
proposition

5.7
in
section

5.1
).
However, our argument for type
{\mathrm{III}}
factors in the proof of
proposition

5.20
was a reduction to the semifinite case via a martingale-type argument, which does not generalize to the general non-AFD type
{\mathrm{III}}
case.

6
Bipartite quantum systems

In this section, we initiate our systematic study of bipartite systems and lay the groundwork for our discussion of bipartite entanglement theory.
The material of this section is partly based on
[
3
,
4
]
, but also contains new results such as the operational characterization of Haag duality in
section

6.6
.

6.1
Definition and basic facts

Let us begin with a formal definition.

Definition 6.1
.

A
bipartite system
is a triple
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
of a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
and a pair of commuting von Neumann algebras
{\mathcal{M}}_{A/B}
on
{\mathcal{H}}
such that

{\mathcal{M}}_{A}={\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}.

Although we are mostly interested in the case where both
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are factors, we use the above definition that only assumes Haag duality (the property
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}={\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}
, see
section

4.5
) because most of our results apply to the non-factorial case.
We refer to
section

4.5
for a discussion of factoriality and Haag duality from an operational point of view.
In
section

6.6
, we prove an operational interpretation of Haag duality in the context of pure state local entanglement theory and local operations.

From a structural point of view, our definition of a bipartite system is equivalent to concretely represented von Neumann algebras
({\mathcal{M}},{\mathcal{H}})
via the identification
({\mathcal{M}},{\mathcal{H}})\equiv({\mathcal{M}},{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{H}})
.
Conceptually, however, our definition places the two subsystems on the same hierarchical level, which reflects how we think about physical bipartite systems.
We import some definitions from von Neumann algebras to bipartite systems.

Definition 6.2
.

A bipartite system
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
is:

•

factorial
if
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and, hence,
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
, are factors,

•

semifinite
if
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and, hence,
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
is a semifinite von Neumann algebra,

•

of
type
\rm X
if
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are of type
\rm X
, where
\rm X={\mathrm{I}}
,
{\mathrm{I}}_{n}
(
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
),
{\mathrm{II}}
,
{\mathrm{II}}_{1}
, etc.

If
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
is factorial, we define the
minimal type
as the minimum of the types of
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
, relative to the naive ordering (
2.2
).

If
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
,
({\mathcal{N}}_{A},{\mathcal{N}}_{B},{\mathcal{K}})
are bipartite systems, then the tensor product and direct sum

({\mathcal{M}}_{A}\otimes{\mathcal{N}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B}\otimes{\mathcal{N}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}),\qquad({\mathcal{M}}_{A}\oplus{\mathcal{N}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B}\oplus{\mathcal{N}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}}\oplus{\mathcal{K}})

(6.1)

are again bipartite systems.
Moreover, every bipartite system uniquely decomposes into a direct sum

({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})=({\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\mathrm{I}},{\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\mathrm{I}},{\mathcal{H}}^{\mathrm{I}})\oplus({\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\mathrm{II}},{\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\mathrm{II}},{\mathcal{H}}^{\mathrm{II}})\oplus({\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\mathrm{III}},{\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\mathrm{III}},{\mathcal{H}}^{\mathrm{III}})

(6.2)

of bipartite systems of types
{\mathrm{I}}
,
{\mathrm{II}}
, and
{\mathrm{III}}
, respectively.
The disintegration of von Neumann algebras induces a disintegration of bipartite systems:
Every bipartite system is of the form

({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})=\int_{X}^{\oplus}({\mathcal{M}}_{A_{x}},{\mathcal{M}}_{B_{x}},{\mathcal{H}}_{x})\,d\mu

(6.3)

for a measure space
(X,\mu)
such that
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{B}\cong L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
, and a measurable field of factorial bipartite systems
({\mathcal{M}}_{A_{x}},{\mathcal{M}}_{B_{x}},{\mathcal{H}}_{x})
,
x\in X
.
39
39

39

This is really just the disintegration of von Neumann algebras since a bipartite system is nothing but a representation of a von Neumann algebra from a structural point of view. A
“measurable field”
of bipartite systems is just a measurable field of von Neumann algebras.

The following fundamental fact will be useful on several occasions.
It states that ,, for all unit vectors
\Psi,\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
, the two parties agree on which of the two marginal states has a larger support projection.

Lemma 6.3

(
[
54
, Prop. V.1.10]
)
.

If
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
is a bipartite system on
{\mathcal{H}}
and
\Psi,\Phi
are unit vectors in
{\mathcal{H}}
, then
40
40

40

In (
6.7
), we write
[{\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime}\Psi]
instead of
[{\mathcal{M}}_{B}\Psi]
(which is the same thing since we assume Haag duality) to emphasize that the projection
[{\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime}\Psi]
is intrinsic to the algebra
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
. In fact,
[{\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime}\Psi]
is exactly the support projection
\supp(\psi_{A})
of the state
\psi_{A}
that
\Psi
induces on
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
; see
eq.

3.10
.

[{\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime}\Psi]\succeq[{\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime}\Phi]\iff[{\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}\Psi]\succeq[{\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}\Phi].

(6.4)

By (
3.10
), the statement (
6.4
) is the same as
\supp\psi_{A}\preceq\supp\phi_{A}\iff\supp\psi_{B}\preceq\supp\phi_{B}
, where
\psi_{A/B}
and
\phi_{A/B}
denote the respective marginal states.
We will see later that
eq.

6.4
is moreover equivalent to the statement that
\Phi
can be obtained from
\Psi
with approximate stochastic LOCC (see
section

7.2
).
For factorial bipartite systems,
lemma

6.3
was in the first paper of Murray and von Neumann
[
58
, Lem. 9.3.3]
, where it is used to define the coupling constant for representations of type
{\mathrm{II}}_{1}
factors.

6.2
Standard bipartite systems

In the following, we consider so-called standard bipartite systems.
Roughly speaking, these are bipartite systems, in which the two von Neumann algebras are equal in size.
We define a
standard bipartite system
as a bipartite system
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
with the property that
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and, therefore,
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are in standard representation on
{\mathcal{H}}
.
As a consequence of the properties of standard representations (see
section

3.3.1
), we will establish an operational characterization of the standard form in terms of the existence of arbitrary purification for both
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
.

If
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
is a bipartite system, we say that a vector
\Omega\in{\mathcal{H}}
is
bicyclic
if it is cyclic for
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and for
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
(see
section

3.1.4
).
Since we assume Haag duality, this is equivalent to
\Omega
being separating for both
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
.
41
41

41

If Haag duality is not assumed, bicyclicity is stronger than the assumption of being cyclcic and separating for one of the two parties.

Proposition 6.4
.

A bipartite system
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
is standard if and only if one (hence all) of the following equivalent properties holds:

1.

Bicyclic vector: There exists a vector
\Omega
that is bicyclic in the sense that it is cyclic for
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and for
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
(equivalently,
\Omega
is cyclic and separating for
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
),

2.

Exchange symmetry: There exists a conjugation
J
such that
J{\mathcal{M}}_{A}J={\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}
and
JzJ=z^{*}
for
z\in{\mathcal{M}}_{A}\cap{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
,

3.

Purifications: For every state
\omega_{A}
on
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
there is a vector
\Omega\in{\mathcal{H}}
such that
\omega_{A}(a)=\langle\Omega,a\Omega\rangle
,
a\in{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
, and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
has the same property.

In particular, if these equivalent properties hold, then
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
have the same type and subtype.

Proof.

The equivalence of standardness and
1
is shown in
lemma

3.8
(note that
\Omega
is bicyclic if and only if it is cyclic and separating for
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
).
Thus,
1

\Rightarrow

2
and
1

\Rightarrow

3
follow from
theorem

3.5
.
The implication
2

\Rightarrow

3
is shown in
[
54
, Thm. V.3.15]
.

3

\Rightarrow

1
:
In particular, faithful states on
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}={\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime}
are implemented by vectors in
{\mathcal{H}}
.
Thus,
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
admits both a separating vector and a cyclic vector.
By
[
54
, Cor. V.1.14]

{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
also admits a cyclic separating vector.
∎

As a consequence of
theorem

3.5
, if
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
is a standard bipartite system, then one can pick a self-dual positive cone
\mathcal{P}\subset{\mathcal{H}}
and a conjugation
J
with
J{\mathcal{M}}_{A}J={\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}
such that
({\mathcal{H}},J,\mathcal{P})
is a standard form for both
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
.

The following sufficient condition for standardness is often useful:

Lemma 6.5

(
[
77
, Thm. III.2.6.16]
)
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
be a bipartite system.
If
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are both properly infinite (types
{\mathrm{I}}_{\infty}
,
{\mathrm{II}}_{\infty}
or
{\mathrm{III}}
), then
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
is standard.

We consider standard subsystems induced by bipartite pure states.
We say that an anti-linear operator
J
on
{\mathcal{H}}
is a
partial conjugation
if there is a closed subspace
{\mathcal{H}}_{0}\subset{\mathcal{H}}
such that
J=J_{0}\oplus 0
with respect to
{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{0}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\perp}
, where
J_{0}
is a conjugation on
{\mathcal{H}}_{0}
.

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
be a bipartite system.
Let
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
be unit vector and let
\psi_{A/B}
its marginal states on
{\mathcal{M}}_{A/B}
.
We set
p_{A/B}=\supp(\psi_{A/B})
,
q=p_{A}p_{B}
,
{\mathcal{H}}_{\Psi}=q{\mathcal{H}}
, and
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Psi,A/B}=q{\mathcal{M}}_{A/B}q
.
Then:

1.

({\mathcal{M}}_{\Psi,A},{\mathcal{M}}_{\Psi,B},{\mathcal{H}}_{\Psi})
is a standard bipartite system with
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\Psi}
a bicyclic vector.

2.

There exists a partial conjugation
J
on
{\mathcal{H}}
such that

(a)

J\Psi=\Psi
(and hence
\psi_{A}=\psi_{B}(J({\,\cdot\,})^{*}J)
),

(b)

J^{2}=q
,

(c)

J{\mathcal{M}}_{A}J={\mathcal{M}}_{\Psi,B}
and
J{\mathcal{M}}_{B}J={\mathcal{M}}_{\Psi,A}
.

3.

A normal state
\phi_{A/B}
on
{\mathcal{M}}_{A/B}
has a purification in
{\mathcal{H}}_{\Psi}
if and only if
\supp(\phi_{A/B})\leq\supp(\psi_{A/B})
.

Proof.

1
:

\Psi
is cyclic for both
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Psi,A/B}
since these are precisely given by the projections onto the cyclic subspaces associated with
{\mathcal{M}}_{A/B}
.
Haag duality
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Psi,A}={\mathcal{M}}_{\Psi,B}^{\prime}
holds because taking commutants is compatible with cutdowns
[
173
, Prop. 5.5.6 & Cor. 5.5.7]
.
Standardness follows because
\Psi
is bicyclic.

2
:
Let
J_{0}
be the modular conjugation for
({\mathcal{M}}_{\Psi,A},{\mathcal{M}}_{\Psi,B})
induced by the bicyclic vector
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\Psi}
.
By definition,
{\mathcal{H}}_{\Psi}
is a closed subspace of
{\mathcal{H}}
, and we obtain an operator
J
with the desired properties via
J:=J_{0}\oplus 0
with respect to the direct sum decomposition
{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{\Psi}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{\Psi}^{\perp}
.

Item

3
follows from
1
and
proposition

6.4
.
∎

Definition 6.7
.

If
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
is a bipartite system and
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
, we refer to the bipartite system
({\mathcal{M}}_{\Psi,A},{\mathcal{M}}_{\Psi,B},{\mathcal{H}}_{\Psi})
in
proposition

6.6
as the
truncated bipartite system
associated with
\Psi
, and to the operator
J
in as
the partial modular conjugation induced by
\Psi
, and we write
J=J_{\Psi}
.

6.3
Distance of pure states up to local unitaries

This subsection is devoted to showing that the local distinguishability of marginals of bipartite pure states, up to local unitaries, is as good for Alice as it is for Bob.
We will show this for both the fidelity and the norm distance.
In
section

6.6
, we will show that a weaker version of this statement, the uniqueness of purifications up to local unitaries, characterizes Haag duality.

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
be a bipartite system.
Let
\Psi,\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
be vectors and let by
\psi_{A/B},\phi_{A/B}\in({\mathcal{M}}_{A/B})_{*}^{+}
be the induced marginals on
{\mathcal{M}}_{A/B}
.
Then

\inf_{u_{A}}\,\lVert u_{A}\phi_{A}u_{A}^{*}-\psi_{A}\rVert=\inf_{u_{B}}\,\lVert u_{B}\phi_{B}u_{B}^{*}-\psi_{B}\rVert,

(6.5)

and

\sup_{u_{A}}\,F(\psi_{A},u_{A}\phi_{A}u_{A}^{*})=\sup_{u_{A},u_{B}}\lvert\langle\Psi,u_{A}u_{B}\Phi\rangle\rvert^{2}=\sup_{u_{B}}\,F(\psi_{B},u_{B}\phi u_{B}^{*}),

(6.6)

where the optimizations are over unitaries
u_{A/B}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{A/B}
.

In
eq.

6.6
,
F
denotes the fidelity (see
section

4.7
).
As a consequence of (
6.6
), we note that a pair of vectors is approximately equivalent up to local unitaries (LU) if and only if the respective marginals are approximately unitarily equivalent:
42
42

42

This uses that convergence in Fidelity is equivalent to norm convergence, which follows from the Fuchs-van de Graaf inequalities, see (
4.29
).

Corollary 6.9
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
be a bipartite system.
Let
\Psi,\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
be vectors and let by
\psi_{A/B},\phi_{A/B}\in({\mathcal{M}}_{A/B})_{*}^{+}
be the induced marginals on
{\mathcal{M}}_{A/B}
.
The following are equivalent:

1.

\Psi
and
\Phi
are approximately LU equivalent, i.e., for all
\varepsilon>0
there are unitaries
u_{A}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
u_{B}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
such that
u_{A}u_{B}\Psi\approx_{\varepsilon}\Phi
;

2.

\psi_{A}\sim\phi_{A}
, i.e., for all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a unitary
u_{A}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
such that
u_{A}\psi_{A}u_{A}^{*}\approx_{\varepsilon}\phi_{A}
;

3.

\psi_{B}\sim\phi_{B}
.

Corollary

6.9
will allow us to show that embezzlement of entanglement is equivalent to universal catalyticity, its single-system analog.
We now come to the proof of
proposition

6.8

Proof of
proposition

6.8
.

We begin by showing
eq.

6.5
:

Step 1.

If
\Omega\in{\mathcal{H}}
is a vector, we denote by
\mathrm{LU}(\Omega)
its closed local unitary orbit, i.e., the closure of the set of vectors
u_{A}u_{B}\Omega
, where
u_{A/B}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{A/B}
are unitaries.
We note that both the LHS and the RHS of (
6.5
) remain unchanged if, instead of
\Psi,\Phi
, we take other vectors
\Psi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{LU}(\Psi)
,
\Phi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{LU}(\Phi)
.

Step 2.
We assume that
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
is a standard bipartite system.
Let
J
be a conjugation and let
\mathcal{P}\subset{\mathcal{H}}
be a
J
-invariant self-dual positive cone such that
({\mathcal{H}},J,\mathcal{P})
is a standard form for
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
(and hence
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
), see
section

3.3.1
.
By step 1 and
corollary

3.23
, we may assume
\Psi,\Phi\in\mathcal{P}
.
Then

\displaystyle\inf_{u_{A}}\lVert u_{A}\phi_{A}u_{A}^{*}-\psi_{A}\rVert

\displaystyle=\adjustlimits{\inf}_{u_{A}}{\sup}_{a}\lvert\langle\Psi,u_{A}^{*}au_{A}\Psi\rangle-\langle\Phi,a\Phi\rangle\rvert

\displaystyle=\adjustlimits{\inf}_{u_{A}}{\sup}_{a}\lvert\langle\Psi,Ju_{A}^{*}JJaJJu_{A}J\Psi\rangle-\langle\Phi,JaJ\Phi\rangle\rvert

\displaystyle=\adjustlimits{\inf}_{u_{B}}{\sup}_{b}\lvert\langle\Psi,u_{B}^{*}bu_{B}\Psi\rangle-\langle\Phi,b\Phi\rangle\rvert=\inf_{u_{b}}\lVert u_{B}\phi_{B}u_{B}^{*}-\psi_{B},\rVert

where the suprema are over the set of contractions in the respective algebra.

Step 3.
We show the claim by reducing it to the case where the bipartite system is standard.
Let
{\mathcal{K}}
be an infinite-dimensional separable Hilbert space, let
\Omega\in{\mathcal{H}}
be a unit vector, and let
\omega
be the induced normal state on
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})
.
By
lemma

6.5
,
({\mathcal{M}}_{A}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})\otimes 1,{\mathcal{M}}_{B}\otimes 1\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}),{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}^{\otimes 2})
is a standard bipartite system.
The marginals of
\tilde{\Psi}=\Psi\otimes\Omega^{\otimes 2}
and
\tilde{\Phi}=\Phi\otimes\Omega^{\otimes 2}
are
\tilde{\psi}_{A/B}=\psi_{A/B}\otimes\omega
and
\tilde{\phi}_{A/B}=\phi_{A/B}\otimes\omega
.

Lemma

5.12
, implies that

\inf_{u_{A/B}}\,\lVert u_{A/B}\phi_{A/B}u_{A/B}^{*}-\psi_{A/B}\rVert=\inf_{\tilde{u}_{A/B}}\,\lVert\tilde{u}_{A/B}\tilde{\phi}_{A/B}\tilde{u}_{A/B}^{*}-\tilde{\psi}_{A/B}\rVert,

where
\tilde{u}_{A/B}
ranges over unitaries in
{\mathcal{M}}_{A/B}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}})
.
By step 2, we have

\inf_{\tilde{u}_{A}}\,\lVert\tilde{u}_{A}\tilde{\phi}_{A}\tilde{u}_{A}^{*}-\tilde{\psi}_{A}\rVert=\inf_{\tilde{u}_{B}}\,\lVert\tilde{u}_{B}\tilde{\phi}_{B}\tilde{u}_{B}^{*}-\tilde{\psi}_{B}\rVert,

which finishes the proof.

Equation

6.6
follows from Uhlmann’s Theorem (
theorem

4.20
) and
corollary

3.23
.
∎

6.4
The coupling constant of semifinite bipartite systems

In this subsection, we investigate factorial semifinite bipartite systems.
In particular, we discuss how traces on
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are coupled to each other and how the coupling constant
c({\mathcal{M}}_{A}:{\mathcal{M}}_{B})
measures the relative size of
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
.
At the end of the subsection, we comment on the non-factorial case.
