Mathematically, the material of this subsection is well-known and goes back all the way to Murray and von Neumann
[
58
,
60
]
.
However, coming from bipartite pure state entanglement theory, we will look at these classical results from new perspectives.

Recall that a semifinite factor
{\mathcal{M}}
(i.e., a type
{\mathrm{I}}
or
{\mathrm{II}}
factor) has a normal semifinite faithful
\tau
which is uniquely determined up to scaling.
In case of a factorial semifinite bipartite system
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
, this means that we have, a priori, two normal semifinite faithful traces
\tau_{A/B}
that are only determined up to scaling.
However, we can canonically fix their relative scaling.
This is a consequence of the following classic structure result on the representations of semifinite factors due to Murray-von Neumann
[
58
,
60
]
(see also
[
54
, Thm. V.3.8]
):

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a semifinite factor on
{\mathcal{H}}
.
For each pair of normal semifinite faithful traces
\tau,\tau^{\prime}
on
{\mathcal{M}}
and
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
, there exists a number
0<C<\infty
such that
\tau[{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\Psi]=C\cdot\tau^{\prime}[{\mathcal{M}}\Psi]
for all
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
.
In particular,
[{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\Psi]
is a finite projection in
{\mathcal{M}}
if and only if
[{\mathcal{M}}\Psi]
is a finite projection in
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
.

We make the following convention, which we call the
“trace coupling convention”
, regarding the joint scaling of the traces on a factorial semifinite bipartite system:

Convention
.

For a factorial semifinite bipartite system
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
, the traces
\tau_{A}
and
\tau_{B}
are chosen such that

\tau_{A}([{\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime}\Psi])=\tau_{B}([{\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}\Psi])\qquad\forall\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}.

(6.7)

In the case of
{\mathrm{I}}
factors
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}={\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{A})\otimes 1
,
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}=1\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{B})
on
{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{A}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{B}
, our convention says that the projections onto the subspaces
V_{A}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{B}
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{A}\otimes V_{B}
have the same trace if and only if
\dim V_{A}=\dim V_{B}
.
In particular, the convention is satisfied by the standard traces (i.e., minimal projections have trace 1).
In the study of type
{\mathrm{II}}_{1}
factors and their representation, the different convention that all traces are unital is usually used
[
110
]
.

Next, we consider standard bipartite systems, where we show that the trace coupling convention agrees with the trace coupling induced by the modular conjugation:

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
be a factorial semifinite bipartite system.
If the bipartite system is standard with conjugation
J
(as in
proposition

6.4
), then the trace coupling convention is equivalent to

\tau_{A}(a)=\tau_{B}(Ja^{*}J),\qquad a\in{\mathcal{M}}_{A}.

(6.8)

Proof.

Let
({\mathcal{H}},J,\mathcal{P})
be a standard form of
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
.
Let
\omega_{A}
be a state on
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
with a finite support projection, and let
\Omega\in\mathcal{P}
be the purification.
Note that
\supp(\omega_{A})=[{\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime}\Omega]=[J{\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}J\Omega]=[J{\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}\Omega]=[J{\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}\Omega]J=J\supp(\omega_{B})J
, where
\omega_{B}
is the marginal state of
\Omega
on
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
.
This gives

\tau_{B}([{\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}\Omega])=\tau_{B}(J[{\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime}\Omega]J).

Thus, the trace coupling convention (which sets the LHS equal to
\tau_{A}([{\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime}\Omega])
) is equivalent to (
6.8
) (which sets the RHS equal to the same value).
∎

A benefit of our convention is that it also makes sense when one or both of the factors is non-finite, which is not the case for the convention where all traces are taken to be unital (see above).

Definition 6.12

(Coupling constant for bipartite systems)
.

The coupling constant of a factorial semifinite bipartite system
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
is defined as

c({\mathcal{M}}_{A}:{\mathcal{M}}_{B}):=\frac{\tau_{A}(1)}{\tau_{B}(1)}\in[0,\infty],

(6.9)

for some, hence all, choice of coupled traces
(\tau_{A},\tau_{B})
.
For a factorial bipartite system of type
{\mathrm{III}}
, we set
c({\mathcal{M}}_{A}:{\mathcal{M}}_{B})=1
.

In (
6.9
) and below, we use the following conventions for degenerate ratios:

\frac{\infty}{r}=\infty,\quad\frac{r}{\infty}=0,\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\infty}{\infty}=1,\qquad\text{ for $0<r<\infty$.}

(6.10)

The coupling constant measures the size of
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
relative to
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
.
It is related to the Murray-von Neumann coupling constant
c({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{H}})\equiv\dim_{{\mathcal{M}}_{A}}{\mathcal{H}}
(cp.
[
58
,
110
]
) via

c({\mathcal{M}}_{A}:{\mathcal{M}}_{B})=\frac{1}{\dim_{{\mathcal{M}}_{A}}{\mathcal{H}}}=\dim_{{\mathcal{M}}_{B}}{\mathcal{H}}.

(6.11)

It is a direct consequence of the definition that

c({\mathcal{M}}_{B}:{\mathcal{M}}_{A})=c({\mathcal{M}}_{A}:{\mathcal{M}}_{B})^{-1},

(6.12)

which also holds if the coupling constants are
0
or
\infty
if the convention (
6.10
) is used.
In case of a tensor product bipartite system with Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{A}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{B}
, we have

c\big({\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{A})\otimes 1:1\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{B})\big)=\frac{\dim{\mathcal{H}}_{A}}{\dim{\mathcal{H}}_{B}}.

(6.13)

Lemma 6.13

(
[
54
, Sec. V.3]
)
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
be a factorial bipartite system.

1.

c({\mathcal{M}}_{A}:{\mathcal{M}}_{B})\geq 1
(resp.
\leq 1
) if and only if
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
(resp.
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
) admits a cyclic vector.

2.

({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
is standard if and only if
c({\mathcal{M}}_{A}:{\mathcal{M}}_{B})=1
.

3.

Suppose
({\mathcal{N}}_{A},{\mathcal{N}}_{B},{\mathcal{K}})
is a bipartite system such that
{\mathcal{N}}_{A}\cong{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
. Then
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})\cong({\mathcal{N}}_{A},{\mathcal{N}}_{B},{\mathcal{K}})
holds if and only if

c({\mathcal{M}}_{A}:{\mathcal{M}}_{B})=c({\mathcal{N}}_{A}:{\mathcal{N}}_{B}).

(6.14)

Proof.

Clearly
1

\Rightarrow

2
.

Item

1
holds trivially in the type
{\mathrm{III}}
case.
The semifinite case of the
items

1
and
3
are shown in
[
54
, Thm. V.3.13]
and
[
54
, Thm. V.3.11]
, respectively.
43
43

43

To be precise, the results of
[
54
]
are only shown under the assumption of finiteness. The reason for this is that
[
54
]
only defines the coupling constant under this assumption, where it is defined via
lemma

6.10
with the convention that the traces are unital. With our convention of defining the coupling constant, the proofs of
[
54
]
apply to the non-finite case.

The type
{\mathrm{III}}
case of the third item is shown in
[
54
, Cor. V.3.2]
.
∎

To state our next result, we need conventions for traces on cut-downs:
If
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
is semifinite and
p_{A}\in\proj({\mathcal{M}}_{A})
is a projection, then the cut-down
p_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}p_{A}
is a semifinite von Neumann algebra on
p_{A}{\mathcal{H}}
, which we equip with the restricted trace.
The commutant of
p_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}p_{A}
, taken as a von Neumann algebra on
p_{A}{\mathcal{H}}
, is precisely
p_{A}{\mathcal{M}}_{B}p_{A}

[
173
, Cor. 5.5.7]
.
If
p_{B}
is a projection in
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
, then
p_{B}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}p_{B}
is a von Neumann algebra on
p_{B}{\mathcal{H}}
whose commutant is
p_{B}{\mathcal{M}}_{B}p_{B}

[
173
, Prop. 5.5.6]
.
In this case, we equip the cut-down
p_{B}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}p_{B}
with the trace induced by this isomorphism
a\mapsto p_{B}a
.
Hence, if
q=p_{A}p_{B}
with
p_{A/B}
as above, the commutant of
q{\mathcal{M}}_{A}q\subset{\mathcal{B}}(q{\mathcal{H}})
is
q{\mathcal{M}}_{B}q
, so that
(q{\mathcal{M}}_{A}q,q{\mathcal{M}}_{B}q,q{\mathcal{H}})
is a bipartite system.
It can readily be checked that these conventions imply that
\tau_{q{\mathcal{M}}_{j}q}([(q{\mathcal{M}}_{j}q)^{\prime}\Psi])=\tau_{{\mathcal{M}}_{j}}([{\mathcal{M}}_{j}^{\prime}\Psi])
,
j=A,B
, for all
\Psi\in q{\mathcal{H}}
, where we introduced the notation
\tau_{q{\mathcal{M}}_{j}q},\tau_{{\mathcal{M}}_{j}}
to distinguish the original from the induced trace.

Lemma 6.14
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
be a factorial semifinite bipartite system and let
p_{A/B}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{A/B}
be projections. Set
q=p_{A}p_{B}
,
{\mathcal{K}}=q{\mathcal{H}}
, and
{\mathcal{N}}_{A/B}=q{\mathcal{M}}_{A/B}q
.
Then the induced traces on
{\mathcal{N}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{N}}_{B}
satisfy the trace coupling convention, and

c({\mathcal{N}}_{A}:{\mathcal{N}}_{B})=\frac{\tau_{A}(p_{A})}{\tau_{B}(p_{B})}.

(6.15)

In particular,
({\mathcal{N}}_{A},{\mathcal{N}}_{B},q{\mathcal{H}})
is standard if and only if
\tau_{A}(p_{A})=\tau_{B}(p_{B})
.

Proof.

As stated above, we have
\tau_{{\mathcal{N}}_{j}}([{\mathcal{N}}_{j}^{\prime}\Psi])=\tau_{{\mathcal{M}}_{j}}([{\mathcal{M}}_{j}^{\prime}\Psi])
,
j=A,B
, for
\psi\in{\mathcal{K}}
.
Thus, the claim follows from the assumption that the trace coupling convention holds for the traces on
{\mathcal{M}}_{A/B}
.

Equation

6.15
follows directly from the definition of the coupling constant.
∎

Lemma 6.15
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
be a factorial semifinite bipartite system with coupled normal semifinite faithful traces
(\tau_{A},\tau_{B})
and let
\psi_{A}
be a normal state on
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
.
Then
\psi_{A}
has a purification in
{\mathcal{H}}
if and only if

\tau_{A}(\supp(\psi_{A}))\leq\tau_{B}(1),

(6.16)

where both sides may be infinite.

Proof.

If
\Psi
is a purification in
{\mathcal{H}}
, then (
6.7
) implies that
\supp(\psi_{A})
and
\supp(\psi_{B})
have the same trace. Both of them are obviously bounded by the trace of the identity operator.
Now suppose that (
6.16
) holds.
Set
p_{A}=\supp(\psi_{A})
and let
p_{B}
be a projection in
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
with
\tau_{B}(p_{B})=\tau_{A}(p_{A})
.
Then
lemma

6.14
implies that the reduced bipartite system on
p_{A}p_{B}{\mathcal{H}}
is standard.
Thus,
proposition

6.4
implies that
\psi_{A}
has a purification in the reduced bipartite system and, therefore, also in the original one.
∎

Remark 6.16

(Non-factorial bipartite systems)
.

The results presented above can be generalized to the case where
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}={\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime}
are general von Neumann algebras instead of factors.
For this, traces and coupling constants have to be replaces by so-called center-valued traces and coupling functions (see
[
54
, Def. V.2.33, Def. V.3.9]
).
This essentially amounts to a disintegration over the center
L^{\infty}(X,\mu)=Z({\mathcal{M}}_{A})=Z({\mathcal{M}}_{B})
and then applying the above results to the factorials bipartite systems associated with the points
x\in X
.

6.5
Schmidt spectrum and entanglement entropies

We continue our discussion of semifinite factorial bipartite systems.
We use the trace coupling discussed in the previous subsection to generalize the notion of the Schmidt spectrum of bipartite pure states to the von Neumann algebraic setting.
As an application, we can define entanglement monotones such as the Schmidt rank or the entanglement entropy.

Before discussing the generalization to the von Neumann algebraic case, let us briefly recall the Schmidt spectrum for bipartite systems of the form
{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{A}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{B}
.
The story starts with the Schmidt decomposition:
If
\Psi
is a state vector in
{\mathcal{H}}_{A}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{B}
, then there exists a unique number
r(\Psi)\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{\infty\}
, called the Schmidt rank (after Erhard Schmidt
[
174
]
), a unique decreasing
\ell^{1}
-sequence
(\lambda_{n})_{n=1}^{r(\Psi)}
, called the Schmidt spectrum, and orthonormal families of vectors
\{\Phi_{n}^{A/B}\}_{n=1}^{r(\Psi)}\subset{\mathcal{H}}_{A/B}
such that

\Psi=\sum_{n=1}^{r(\Psi)}\lambda_{n}^{1/2}\,\Phi^{A}_{n}\otimes\Phi^{B}_{n}.

(6.17)

Entanglement monotones such as the entanglement entropy, which is defined as

H(\Psi):=\sum_{n=1}^{r(\Psi)}\eta(\lambda_{n})=\tr\eta(\rho_{A/B}),\qquad\eta(t)=-t\log t,

(6.18)

only make sense because we know from the Schmidt decomposition that
\rho_{A}
and
\rho_{B}
have the same spectrum, namely the Schmidt spectrum of
\Psi
, which implies that (
6.18
) is independent of the choice
A/B
.

In the following, we will see that the above generalizes to semifinite bipartite systems.
In particular, we will be able to generalize the usual entanglement monotones (e.g., the Schmidt rank and entanglement entropies) to the setting of semifinite bipartite systems.
It will be shown in
section

7
that these generalizations are indeed entanglement monotones.

Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
be a general bipartite system and let
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
be a unit vector. We denote by
\psi_{A/B}
its marginal states.
By
proposition

6.6
,
\Psi
is a bicyclic vector for the truncated bipartite system
({\mathcal{M}}_{\Psi,A},{\mathcal{M}}_{\Psi,B},{\mathcal{H}}_{\Psi})
associated with
\Psi
(see
definition

6.7
).
The modular conjugation
J_{\Psi}
induces an anti-isomorphism

j_{\Psi}:{\mathcal{M}}_{\Psi,A}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{\Psi,B}

such that

\psi_{B}(j_{\Psi}(a))=\psi_{A}(a^{*}),\qquad a\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\Psi,A}.

(6.19)

Since the truncated bipartite system is standard, we may regard
{\mathcal{H}}_{\Psi}
as a Haagerup
L^{2}
-space
L^{2}({\mathcal{M}}_{\Psi,A})
on which
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Psi,A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Psi,B}={\mathcal{M}}_{\Psi,A}^{\prime}
act by left/right multiplication and in which the vector
\Psi
is a positive self-adjoint operator with full support.
This is the generalization of the Schmidt decomposition, which works for general bipartite systems.
In the case of a type
{\mathrm{I}}
bipartite system on
{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{A}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{B}
, it reduces to the Schmidt decomposition considered above, where
{\mathcal{H}}_{\Psi}
becomes the subspace spanned by the ONB
\{\Phi_{n}^{A}\otimes\Phi_{m}^{B}\}_{n,m=1}^{r(\Psi)}
(see (
6.17
)).

We now use the above generalization to define a notion of Schmidt spectrum.
We consider a factorial semifinite bipartite system
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
with coupled normal semifinite faithful traces
\tau_{A}
and
\tau_{B}
(see
section

6.4
).
We define the
Schmidt spectral scale
(or
Schmidt scale
for short) of the unit vector
\Psi
as

\lambda_{\Psi}(t):=\lambda_{\psi_{A}}(t)=\lambda_{\psi_{B}}(t),\qquad t>0,

(6.20)

where
\lambda_{\psi_{A/B}}
denote the spectral scales of
\psi_{A/B}
relative to the traces
\tau_{A/B}
(see
section

3.2
).
The Schimdt scale depends only on the joint scaling of the coupled traces
(\tau_{A},\tau_{B})
.

Lemma 6.17
.

The Schmidt scale in (
6.20
) is well-defined.

Proof.

We consider the truncated bipartite system
({\mathcal{M}}_{\Psi,A},{\mathcal{M}}_{\Psi,B},{\mathcal{H}}_{\Psi})
as above.

Lemma

6.14
implies that the truncated traces
\tau_{\Psi,A}
and
\tau_{\Psi,B}
satisfy the trace coupling convention.
By
propositions

6.6
and
6.11
,
j_{\Psi}
identifies the truncated traces:

\tau_{\Psi,A}(a^{*})=\tau_{\Psi,B}(j_{\Psi}(a)),\qquad a\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\Psi,A}.

Together with (
6.19
), this shows
\lambda_{\psi_{A}}(t)=\lambda_{\psi_{B}}(t)
.
∎

By construction, we can compute arbitrary trace-functions of the marginal states of
\Psi
in terms of the Schmidt scale.
A trace-function of an operator
\rho
is an expression of the form
\tau(f(\rho))
for some Borel function
f:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
:

Corollary 6.18
.

Let
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{+}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
be a Borel measurable function. Then

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}f(\lambda_{\Psi}(t))\,dt=\tau_{A}\bigg(f\bigg(\frac{d\psi_{A}}{d\tau_{A}}\bigg)\bigg)=\tau_{B}\bigg(f\bigg(\frac{d\psi_{B}}{d\tau_{B}}\bigg)\bigg),

(6.21)

where all terms may be infinite.

The above allows us to generalize the Schmidt rank and the entanglement entropies from finite-dimensional quantum information theory
[
13
,
81
,
46
]
:
The Schmidt rank of a unit vector
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
is defined as

r(\Psi):=|\supp(\lambda_{\Psi})\;\!|,

(6.22)

where
\lvert{\,\cdot\,}\rvert
denotes the Lebesgue measure. By
corollary

6.18
, we have

r(\Psi)=\tau_{A}\bigg(\supp\bigg(\frac{d\psi_{A}}{d\tau_{A}}\bigg)\!\bigg)=\tau_{B}\bigg(\supp\bigg(\frac{d\psi_{B}}{d\tau_{B}}\bigg)\!\bigg).

(6.23)

The
entanglement entropy
of
\Psi
is defined as

H(\Psi):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}\eta(\lambda(t))\,dt,

(6.24)

The
\alpha
-Renyi entanglement entropy

H_{\alpha}(\Psi)
,
\alpha\in(0,1)\cup(1,\infty)
of a state vector
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
is defined as

H_{\alpha}(\Psi):=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\log\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}\lambda_{\Psi}(t)^{\alpha}\,dt.

(6.25)

In
section

7
, these quantities will be shown to actually be entanglement monotones.

In general, there is no canonical choice for the joint scaling of the two traces.
If we scale both traces by a factor
c>0
, the trace coupling conventions continues to hold, but the entanglement entropies are shifted by
\log c

H_{\alpha}(\Psi)\longrightarrow H_{\alpha}(\Psi)+\log c.

(6.26)

However, differences of entanglement entropies remain unaffected and have an intrinsic meaning
44
44

44

The story is similar for the Schmidt rank: Scaling the trace means scaling all Schmidt ranks, but ratios are unaffected. This fits into the entropy picture because the logarithm of the Schmidt rank is the max-entropy.
:

H_{\alpha}(\Psi)-H_{\alpha}(\Phi)\longrightarrow H_{\alpha}(\Psi)-H_{\alpha}(\Phi).

(6.27)

This is analogous to the role of entropy in classical statistical mechanics, where only entropy differences are meaningful (this can be traced back to the fact that the Lebesgue measure on phase space has no canonical normalization).
Another similarity with the entropies of continuous distributions on the phase space is that the entanglement entropies above can become negative.
However, as in classical statistical physics, the sign is irrelevant as the entropy is only meaningful up to an additive constant.

6.6
An operational characterization of Haag duality

In this subsection, we provide an operational interpretation of Haag duality in terms of pure states and local operations.
In
corollary

3.23
, we saw that vectors in a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
induce the same marginal state on an observable algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
if and only if they are related by unitaries in the commutant
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
(up to arbitrarily small error).
Here, we show the converse:

Theorem 6.19
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
be commuting von Neumann algebras on a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
.
The following are equivalent:

1.

Haag duality
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}={\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}
;

2.

if
\Psi,\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
are unit vectors with
\psi_{A}=\phi_{A}
, then
\Psi=v_{B}\Phi
for a partial isometry
v_{B}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
;

3.

if
\Psi,\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
are unit vectors with
\psi_{A}=\phi_{A}
, then, for all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a unitary
u_{B}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
such that
\Psi\approx_{\varepsilon}u_{B}\Phi
.

Lemma 6.20
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
be commuting von Neumann algebras on a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
.
The following are equivalent:

1.

Haag duality
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}={\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}
;

2.

[{\mathcal{M}}_{A}\Omega]=[{\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}\Omega]
for all
\Omega\in{\mathcal{H}}

3.

\overline{{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}}_{A})\Omega}=\overline{{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime})\Omega}
for all
\Omega\in{\mathcal{H}}
.

Proof.

1

\Rightarrow

3
is trivial.

3

\Rightarrow

2
follows from the fact that a von Neumann algebra is spanned by its unitary group.
It remains to show
2

\Rightarrow

1
:
Recall if
p
is projection and
{\mathcal{N}}
is a von Neumann algebra on
{\mathcal{H}}
, then
p\in{\mathcal{N}}\iff{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}p{\mathcal{H}}\subset p{\mathcal{H}}
(
p{\mathcal{H}}
is invariant for
{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}
).
We start by observing the following:

[{\mathcal{M}}_{A}\Omega]\in{\mathcal{M}}_{B}\quad\iff\quad[{\mathcal{M}}_{A}\Omega]=[{\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}\Omega].

(6.28)

Indeed,
“
\Leftarrow
”
is clear, and the converse follows from
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}\Omega}\supseteq\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{A}\Omega}\supseteq{\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}\overline{{\mathcal{M}}_{A}\Omega}\supseteq{\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}\Omega
.
By (
6.28
), our assumption implies
[{\mathcal{M}}_{A}\Omega]\in{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
for all
\Omega\in{\mathcal{H}}
.
Thus, if
p\in\proj({\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime})
and
\{\Omega_{n}\}
is a basis of
p{\mathcal{H}}
,
then
p=[\cup_{n}{\mathcal{M}}_{A}\Omega_{n}]=\vee_{n}[{\mathcal{M}}_{A}\Omega_{n}]=\vee_{n}[{\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}\Omega_{n}]
shows that
p\in{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
.
Since
p
was arbitrary, this implies
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}={\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime}
.
∎

Proof of
theorem

6.19
.

1

\Rightarrow

2
is shown in
corollary

3.23
, and

2

\Rightarrow

3
follows from
corollary

3.17
.

3

\Rightarrow

1
:
We check condition
3
of
lemma

6.20
.
Let
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
be a unit vector.
For any unitary
u_{A^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime})
, the vector
\Phi=u_{A^{\prime}}\Psi
is such that
\psi_{A}=\phi_{A}
.
Using our assumption, we obtain
\Psi\in\overline{{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}}_{B})\Phi}
.
Since
u_{A^{\prime}}
was an arbitrary unitary, this implies
\overline{{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime})\Phi}=\overline{{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}}_{B})\Phi}
.
∎

Example 6.21
.

We consider an example due to Naaijkens
[
41
,
40
,
91
]
of a setup where Haag duality is known to fail.
We consider the toric code on an infinite 2D spin lattice
\Gamma={\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
(see
[
42
]
and
section

4.9.1
). Let
C_{1},C_{2}
be two disjoint cones.
We define
A=C_{1}\cup C_{2}
and
B=\Gamma\setminus A
.
Then Haag duality fails
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}\subsetneq{\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}
.
Using the structure of the toric code, one can define unitaries that create a pair of anyonic excitations that are localized in the respective cones. By construction, these unitaries commute with
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
. They are, however, not elements of
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}={\mathcal{M}}_{C_{1}}\vee{\mathcal{M}}_{C_{2}}
.
Let
\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
denote the ground state vector, let
u\in{\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}
be a unitary that creates anyonic excitations in
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
, and let
\Psi=u\Phi
.
Then
\Psi
and
\Phi
have the same marginal state on
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
.
Since the anyonic excitations cannot be removed with unitaries in
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
, and, since they can be measured locally, we even know that
u_{A}\Psi
and
\Phi
are orthogonal for all
u_{A}\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}}_{A})
.
In particular,
\Psi
cannot be approximated by acting on
\Phi
with unitaries from
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
.

7
Bipartite pure state entanglement theory

In this section, we consider pure state entanglement theory in von Neumann algebraic bipartite systems.
We characterize both LOCC and stochastic LOCC transitions, and show the non-type
{\mathrm{III}}
part of
C
in
section

2
.
The section is largely based on the article
[
4
]
, and extends ideas from
[
16
,
30
,
39
]
.

Before going into details, we discuss a few generalities.

As long as we are considering LOCC protocols, we assume that all instruments involved have Kraus rank 1, i.e., that every instrument
(T_{x})
is of the form
T_{x}=k_{x}^{*}({\,\cdot\,})k_{x}
for Kraus operators
\{k_{x}\}
.
We can make this assumption without loss of generality because general instruments arise from coarse-graining of instruments with Kraus rank 1 (see (
4.8
)).
Therefore, instruments with Kraus rank 1 only yield more information, which the agents can then choose to ignore.
Since the set of instruments with Kraus rank 1 is closed under products, the Kraus rank 1 property is passed on to the total instrument
(T_{x})
of an LOCC protocol.

Consider a pair
\psi,\phi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{H}})
of pure states with implementing vectors
\Psi,\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
.
In
section

4.6
, we defined what it means that
\psi
can be transformed to
\phi
with LOCC
We write

\Psi\xrightarrow{\mathrm{LOCC}}\Phi,

to denote that
\psi
can be transformed to
\phi
with LOCC, as defined in
section

4.6
.
Note that
T
is a quantum channel on
{\mathcal{H}}
and if
\{k_{x}\}
is a collection of Kraus operators for
T
such that
T_{*}(\psi)=\phi
, then
k_{x}\Psi
is equal to
p_{x}^{1/2}\Phi
up to phase, where
p_{x}=\psi(k_{x}^{*}k_{x})=\lVert k_{x}\Psi\rVert^{2}
, for all
x
.
By adapting the phase of the Kraus operators, we can assume
k_{x}\Psi=p_{x}^{1/2}\Phi
for all
x
.
We thus have the following:

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
\Psi,\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
be unit vectors. Then
\Psi\xrightarrow{\mathrm{LOCC}}\Phi
if and only if there is an LOCC protocol whose total instrument has Kraus operators
\{k_{x}\}
such that

k_{x}\Psi=p_{x}^{1/2}\Phi,\quad p_{x}=\lVert k_{x}\Psi\rVert^{2}\qquad\forall x.

(7.1)

Next, we will show that we may assume that an agent
A
with von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
can apply subnormalized operations to states on which these are normalization preserving.
A subnormalized quantum channel on a system with Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
is a normal subunital cp map
T
on
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
(Heisenberg picture) or a normalization-nonincreasing cp map
T_{*}
on the predual
{\mathcal{B}}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{H}})
(Schrödinger picture) with the two versions related by (
4.2
).
A subnormalized channel on
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
is inner for a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
if it admits a Kraus decomposition (
4.4
) with operators
\{k_{x}\}
in
{\mathcal{M}}
(of course,
\sum k_{x}^{*}k_{x}=1
is then replaced by
\sum k_{x}^{*}k_{x}=T(1)
).

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a von Neumann algebra on
{\mathcal{H}}
and let
\psi,\phi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{H}})
.
If there is an
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner subnormalized channel
T
on
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
such that
T_{*}(\psi)=\phi
, then there is an
{\mathcal{M}}
-inner channel
S
with
S_{*}(\psi)=\phi
.

Proof.

We set
k_{0}=(1-T(1))^{1/2}\in{\mathcal{M}}
and
S=T+k_{0}^{*}({\,\cdot\,})k_{0}
.
Clearly,
S
is an inner ucp map with
S_{*}(\psi)=\phi+k_{0}\psi k_{0}^{*}
.
Since
S_{*}
is normalization preserving, we have
\lVert k_{0}\phi k_{0}^{*}\rVert=k_{0}\phi k_{0}^{*}(1)=S_{*}(\phi)(1)-T_{*}(\phi)=\phi(1)-\psi(1)=0
, which shows
S_{*}(\psi)=\phi
.
∎

Based on the Lemma, we will make statements like
“the agent applies a partial isometry
u
to the state
\Psi
”
. This is justified by the Lemma if
u
acts isometrically on
\Psi
, i.e., if
u^{*}u\Psi=\Psi
, because the agent can always implement an extension
\tilde{T}
of the subnormalized channel
u^{*}({\,\cdot\,})u
, which gives the same output state.
The choice of this extension is irrelevant as long as we do not simultaneously consider other initial states.
The same reasoning can be applied to instruments.

7.1
One-way LOCC

A one-way LOCC protocol is an LOCC protocol that with two rounds.
Thus, it consists of an instrument of the first agent, who then communicates the outcome to the second agent, who performs an operation conditioned on what they received (it is irrelevant whether the second agent communicates the outcome since the first agent is not allowed to act afterward).

One of the fundamental results of pure state entanglement theory is that, if an LOCC protocol takes a pure state
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
to a pure state
\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
, then there exists a one-way LOCC protocol that does the same thing
[
175
]
.
An approximate version of this was generalized to the case of semifinite von Neumann algebraic bipartite systems in
[
30
, Thm. 4.11]
.
In this subsection, we show the following exact version of the statement for general bipartite systems:

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
be a bipartite system and let
\Psi,\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
be unit vectors.
If
\Psi\xrightarrow{\mathrm{LOCC}}\Phi
, then there is a one-way LOCC protocol taking
\Psi
to
\Phi
, in which Alice performs an instrument with Kraus operators
\{k_{x}\}
and Bob performs a conditional partial isometry
u_{x}^{\prime}
such that

u_{x}^{\prime}k_{x}\Psi=p_{x}^{1/2}\Phi.

(7.2)

The partial isometry
u_{x}^{\prime}
acts isometrically on
k_{x}\Psi
.

Recall from
lemma

7.2
that it makes sense to say that Bob performs a partial isometry on a vector, provided that it acts isometrically.
For the proof, we use the following Lemma:

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a von Neumann algebra on
{\mathcal{H}}
, and let
\pi
be the representation of
{\mathcal{M}}
that puts it in standard form on
L^{2}({\mathcal{M}})
.
For a given pair
a,\Psi
of an operator
a\in{\mathcal{M}}
and a vector
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
,

•

let
w^{\prime}:L^{2}({\mathcal{M}})\to{\mathcal{H}}
be the partial isometry appearing in the polar decomposition
\Psi=w^{\prime}\Omega_{\psi}
of
\Psi
(see
section

3.4.2
),

•

let
v^{\prime}\in\pi({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})
be the partial isometry appearing in the polar decomposition of
\pi(a)\Omega_{\psi}=v^{\prime}\Omega_{a\psi a^{*}}
of
\pi(a)\Omega_{\psi}\in L^{2}({\mathcal{M}})
,

•

let
u\in{\mathcal{M}}
be the partial isometry with
\pi(u^{*})=j(v^{\prime})
, and let
a^{\prime}=w^{\prime}j(\pi(u^{*}a))w^{\prime*}=w^{\prime}v^{\prime}j(\pi(a))w^{\prime*}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
.

Then
u
is isometric on
a\Psi
and

a\Psi=ua^{\prime}\Psi.

(7.3)

Proof.

Step 1.
We assume
{\mathcal{H}}=L^{2}({\mathcal{M}})
,
\pi=\id
, and
\Psi=\Omega_{\psi}
.
Since
\Omega_{a\psi a^{*}}
is
J
-invariant,
v^{\prime*}a\Omega_{\psi}=\Omega_{a\psi a^{*}}
implies
uj(a)\Omega_{\psi}=\Omega_{a\psi a^{*}}
.
Since
v^{\prime*}
acts isometrically on
a\Omega_{\psi}
,
u=j(v^{\prime*})
acts isometrically on
j(a)\Omega_{\psi}
and hence on
a^{\prime}\Omega_{\psi}=j(u^{*}a)\Omega_{\psi}
.
Thus, we have

a\Omega_{\psi}=v^{\prime}\Omega_{a\psi a^{*}}=v^{\prime}uj(a)\Omega_{\psi}=uj(u^{*}a)\Omega_{\psi}=ua^{\prime}\Omega_{\psi}.

Step 2.

In the general case, step 1 implies that
\pi(a)\Omega_{\psi}=\pi(u)j(\pi(u^{*}a))\Omega_{\psi}
.
We have

a\Psi=w^{\prime}\pi(a)\Omega_{\psi}=w^{\prime}\pi(u)j(\pi(u^{*}a))w^{\prime*}\Psi=uw^{\prime}j(\pi(u^{*}a))w^{\prime*}\Psi=ua^{\prime}\Psi.

Since
\pi(u)
is isometric on
\pi(j(u^{*}a))\Omega_{\psi}
,
u
is isometric on
a^{\prime}\Psi=w^{\prime}\pi(j(u^{*}a))\Omega_{\psi}
.
∎

Corollary 7.5
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
be a bipartite system and let
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
be a vector.
For every collection
\{k_{x}\}\subset{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
of Kraus operators of Alice, there exist partial isometries
u_{x}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
of Alice and a collection
\{k_{x}^{\prime}\}\subset{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
of Kraus operators of Bob such that

k_{x}\Psi=u_{x}k_{x}^{\prime}\Psi.

(7.4)

The partial isometries
u_{x}
act isometrically on
k_{x}^{\prime}\Psi
.

Proof.

If we define
w^{\prime},v^{\prime}_{x},u_{x},k_{x}^{\prime}
as in
lemma

7.4
(applied to
a=k_{x}
), then (
7.4
) holds.
We only have to check that
\sum k_{x}^{\prime*}k_{x}^{\prime}\leq 1
(
\leq
is enough by
lemma

7.2
).
We have

\displaystyle\sum_{x}k_{x}^{\prime*}k_{x}^{\prime}=\sum_{x}w^{\prime}j(\pi(k_{x}))^{*}v_{x}^{\prime*}w_{x}^{\prime*}w_{x}^{\prime}v_{x}^{\prime}j(\pi(k_{x}))w^{\prime*}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{x}w^{\prime}j(\pi(k_{x}^{*}k_{x}))w^{\prime*}=w^{\prime}w^{\prime*}\leq 1,

where we used
v_{x}^{\prime*}w_{x}^{\prime*}w_{x}^{\prime}v_{x}^{\prime}\leq 1
.
∎

Proof of
proposition

7.3
.

We consider the LOCC protocol, which takes
\Psi
to
\Phi
.
Whenever it is Bob’s turn, we use
corollary

7.5
to replace the instrument that Bob chooses in the given round by an instrument of Alice, followed by a conditional partial isometry of Alice without changing the post-measurement states of the initial state
\Psi
.
This constructs a new instrument in which all communication goes from Alice to Bob and in which
B
only acts with partial isometries.
Each of these partial isometries acts isometrically on the post-measurement state of the previous round.
Thus, we can define a new protocol in which Alice performs the product instrument of all her instruments in the first round and then sends the output to Bob, who performs the product
v_{x}^{\prime}
all these partial isometries in the second round.
By construction,
v_{x}^{\prime}
is a contraction in
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
that acts isometrically on
\Psi
. Thus, we can replace it by an isometrically acting isometry
u_{x}

lemma

3.16
.
By construction, this one-way protocol has the same post-measurement states as the initial one (on the input state
\Psi
).
∎

7.2
Stochastic LOCC

The idea of stochastic LOCC (SLOCC) is to consider a relaxed version of LOCC transitions in which it is only required that the state transitions are possible with some nonzero probability, see
[
13
]
for details.
We begin our consideration of SLOCC in the multipartite case.
Afterward, we characterize the approximate SLOCC transitions in bipartite systems and show that the Schmidt rank is a complete monotone for approximate SLOCC transitions.

Consider a quantum system with Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
together with a collection of agents
A_{1},\ldots A_{N}
that have partial access to the full quantum system with Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
.
We assume that the operations that can be implemented by these agents are described by von Neumann algebras
{\mathcal{M}}_{1},\ldots{\mathcal{M}}_{N}
on
{\mathcal{H}}
.
Moreover, we assume that these agents are pairwise independent, which translates to pairwise commutativity of the von Neumann algebras
{\mathcal{M}}_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots N
.
If
\psi,\phi\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{H}})
are states of the full system, we say that
\psi
can be transformed to
\phi
with SLOCC
by the of agents
A_{1},\ldots A_{N}
, if there exists an LOCC protocol with overall instrument
(T_{x})
such that the probabilities
p_{x}=\psi(T_{x}(1))
for which
\psi\circ T_{x}=p_{x}\phi
add up to a nonzero number, called the overall transition probability.
In this case, we write

\psi\xrightarrow{\mathrm{SLOCC}}\phi.

Moreover, we say that
\psi
can be transformed to
\phi
by means of
approximate SLOCC
, denoted

\psi\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathrm{SLOCC}}}\phi,

if, for every
\varepsilon>0
, there is a state
\phi^{\prime}\approx_{\varepsilon}\phi
such that
\psi\xrightarrow{\mathrm{SLOCC}}\phi^{\prime}
.
It is, at first, not clear that approximate SLOCC does not trivialize. After all, it includes cases where it is not possible to perform the desired state transition with any nonzero probability.
If
\psi
and
\phi
are pure states, we use the same notations for the implementing vectors
\Psi,\Phi
.

Next, we show the following Lemma, which, in particular, states that a pure state SLOCC transition never requires communication between the agents:

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
\Psi,\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
be unit vectors. Then

\Psi\xrightarrow{\mathrm{SLOCC}}\Phi\quad\iff\quad\exists a_{j}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{j}\ \text{s.t.}\ \Phi=a_{1}\cdots a_{N}\Psi.

(7.5)

Proof.

“
\Rightarrow
”
:
If
\Psi\xrightarrow{\mathrm{SLOCC}}\Phi
, there must be at least one nonzero Kraus operator
k_{x}
of a total instrument associated with an LOCC protocol such that
k_{x}\Psi\propto\Phi
.
Since each Kraus operator of the total instrument is a product
k_{x}=a_{1,x}\cdots a_{N,x}
of operators from the local algebras
a_{j,x}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{j}
, the claim follows.

“
\Leftarrow
”
: Let
k_{1,j}\propto a_{j}
be nonzero rescalings such that
\lVert k_{1,j}\rVert\leq 1
for each
j
.
Now consider the LOCC protocol where each agent applies the instrument with Kraus operators
(k_{1,j},k_{2,j})
(no communication is needed).
By construction, this LOCC protocol implements the state transition from
\Psi
to
\Phi
with nonzero probability.
∎

We now restrict our attention to bipartite systems.

Theorem 7.7
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
be a bipartite system and let
\Psi,\Phi
be unit vectors in
{\mathcal{H}}
.
The following are equivalent

1.

\Psi\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathrm{SLOCC}}}\Phi
;

2.

\supp(\phi_{A/B})\preceq\supp(\psi_{A/B})
;

3.

for each
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a unitary
u_{A}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
k_{B}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
such that
u_{A}k_{B}\Psi\approx_{\varepsilon}\Phi
.

In
item

2
,
“
\preceq
”
denotes the Murray-von Neumann ordering of projections (see
section

3.1.2
).
Before we come to the proof, we evaluate the above for factorial bipartite systems:

Corollary 7.8
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
be a factorial bipartite system and let
\Psi,\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
be unit vectors.
Then:

1.

If
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and, therefore,
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are of type
{\mathrm{III}}
, then
\Psi\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathrm{SLOCC}}}\Phi
.

2.

If
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and, therefore
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are semifinite, then

\Psi\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathrm{SLOCC}}}\Phi\quad\iff\quad r(\Psi)\geq r(\Phi),

(7.6)

where
r
denotes the Schmidt rank relative to some choice of coupled normal semifinite faithful traces
\tau_{A/B}
on
{\mathcal{M}}_{A/B}
(see
section

6.5
).

The results of
section

6.5
imply that the RHS of (
7.6
) does not depend on the choice of the coupled traces.
We need the following Lemma, which is surely known but which we could not locate in the literature.

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a von Neumann algebra.
If
\phi_{n},\phi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{*}^{+}
and if
\supp(\phi_{n})\preceq p
for a projection
p\in{\mathcal{M}}
, then
\phi_{n}\to\phi
implies
\supp(\phi)\preceq p
.

Proof.

By disintegration, we may assume
{\mathcal{M}}
to be a factor.
If
{\mathcal{M}}
is type
{\mathrm{III}}
, the statement is trivial because all nonzero projections are Murray-von Neumann equivalent.
For semifinite
{\mathcal{M}}
, consider a normal semifinite faithful trace
\tau
on
{\mathcal{M}}
.
If
\lambda_{\phi_{n}},\lambda_{\phi}
denote the respective spectral scales relative of
\tau
(see
section

3.2
), then
theorem

3.3
implies
\lambda_{\phi_{n}}\to\lambda_{\phi}
in
L^{1}
-norm. We find

\varliminf_{n}\tau(\supp(\phi_{n}))=\varliminf_{n}|\supp(\lambda_{\phi_{n}})|\geq|\supp(\lim_{n}\lambda_{\phi_{n}})|=|\supp(\lim_{n}\lambda_{\phi})|=\tau(\supp(\phi)),

(7.7)

where
\lvert{\,\cdot\,}\rvert
denotes the Lebesgue measure on
{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
and where we used the lower-semicontinuity of the measure of the support on
L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{+})
.
Thus,
p\succeq\supp(\phi_{n})
implies
\tau(p)\geq\varliminf_{n}\tau(\supp(\phi_{n}))\geq\tau(\supp(\phi))
, which implies
p\succeq\supp(\phi)
because
{\mathcal{M}}
is a factor.
∎

Proof of
theorem

7.7
.

The two versions of
2
are equivalent by
lemma

6.3
.

3

\Rightarrow

1
follows from
lemma

7.6
.

1

\Rightarrow

3
:
Let
\varepsilon>0
, then
lemma

7.6
implies that there are operators
a_{A/B}
such that
\Phi\approx_{\varepsilon/2}a_{A}a_{B}\Psi
.
We may assume
\lVert a_{A}\rVert\leq 1
.
Applying
lemma

7.4
, we get an operator
b_{B}
and a partial isometry
v_{A}
, which acts isometrically on
b_{B}\Psi
, such that
v_{A}b_{B}\Psi=a_{B}\Psi
.
By
corollary

3.17
, there is a unitary
u_{A}
such that
u_{A}b_{B}\Psi\approx_{\varepsilon/2}v_{A}b_{B}\Psi
.
Setting
k_{B}=b_{B}a_{B}
, we have
u_{A}k_{B}\Psi\approx_{\varepsilon}\Phi
.

3

\Rightarrow
(
2
,
“
B
”
):
Let
u_{A,\varepsilon},k_{B,\varepsilon}
be as in the statement and set
\Phi_{\varepsilon}=u_{A,\varepsilon}k_{B,\varepsilon}
.
The
B
-marginal of
\Phi_{\varepsilon}
is
\phi_{B,\varepsilon}=k_{B,\varepsilon}\psi_{B}k_{B,\varepsilon}^{*}
, which converges in norm to
\phi
as
\varepsilon\to 0
and satisfies
\supp(\phi_{B,\varepsilon})\preceq\supp(\psi_{B})
for all
\varepsilon>0
.
By
lemma

7.9
, we get
\supp(\phi_{B})\preceq\supp(\psi_{B})
.

2

\Rightarrow

3
:
Let
v_{A}
be a partial isometry such that
v_{A}v_{A}^{*}=\supp(\psi_{A})
,
v_{A}^{*}v_{A}\geq\supp(\phi_{A})
.
Then
[{\mathcal{M}}_{B}v_{A}\Psi]=[{\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime}v_{A}\Psi]=\supp(v_{A}\psi_{A}v_{A}^{*})=v_{A}^{*}v_{A}\geq\supp(\phi_{A})=[{\mathcal{M}}_{A}^{\prime}\Phi]
implies that
\Phi
is in the closure of
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}v_{A}\Psi
.
By
corollary

3.17
, we can approximate
v_{A}
with unitaries in
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
acting on
\Psi
, which implies the claim.
∎

7.3
Nielsen’s theorem

Entanglement theory for bipartite mixed states is extremely hard
[
13
]
.
Essentially, this follows from the problem that the class of LOCC protocols is intractable.
For pure states, the story is quite different as the reduction to one-way LOCC
[
175
]
shows.
As Nielsen noted in
[
46
]
(see also
[
81
,
30
]
), the latter allows for a complete characterization of pure state LOCC transitions in terms of the marginal states.
Nielsen’s Theorem was generalized to semifinite factors in
[
30
]
.
In the following, we begin by showing an exact version of Nielsen’s theorem for general bipartite systems.
Afterward, we characterize approximate LOCC transitions in factorial bipartite systems in terms of majorization for the Schmidt scales.

Theorem 7.10

(Nielsen’s Theorem)
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
be a bipartite system and let
\Psi,\Phi
be unit vectors in
{\mathcal{H}}
.
Then

\Psi\xrightarrow{\mathrm{LOCC}}\Phi\quad\iff\quad\psi_{A/B}=\sum_{x}p_{x}\,u_{x}\phi_{A/B}u_{x}^{*}

(7.8)

for a probability distribution
(p_{x})
and a collection of partial isometries
u_{x}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{A/B}
.

Note that the equality on the RHS ensures that the partial isometries act isometrically on
\phi_{A/B}
(see
corollary

3.17
).

Lemma 7.11
.

If
\Psi\xrightarrow{\mathrm{LOCC}}\Phi
, there are partial isometries
u_{x}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and a probability distribution
(p_{x})
such that

\psi_{A}=\sum_{x}p_{x}\,u_{x}\phi_{A}u_{x}^{*}.

Proof.

By
proposition

7.3
(applied with
A
and
B
swapped), there are partial isometries
u_{x}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and Kraus operators
k_{x}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
such that
k_{x}\Psi=p_{x}^{1/2}u_{x}\Phi
.
Therefore, we have

\sum_{x}p_{x}\,u_{x}\phi_{A}u_{x}^{*}=\sum_{x}\langle k_{x}\Psi,({\,\cdot\,})|_{{\mathcal{M}}_{A}}k_{x}\Psi\rangle=\langle\Psi,({\,\cdot\,})|_{{\mathcal{M}}_{A}}\sum_{x}k_{x}^{*}k_{x}\Psi\rangle=\psi_{A}.\qed

Lemma 7.12
.

Let
\phi
be a normal state on a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
, let
(p_{x})
be a probability distribution, and let
u_{x}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
be a collection of partial isometries such that
u_{x}\phi_{A}u_{x}^{*}
is normalized.
Set
\psi=\sum_{x}p_{x}u_{x}\phi u_{x}^{*}=\sum_{x}\psi_{x}
,
\psi_{x}=p_{x}u_{x}\phi u_{x}^{*}
, and set
k_{x}=u_{x}^{*}(D\psi_{x}:D\psi)_{-i/2}
.
If we consider
{\mathcal{M}}
in standard form, then

k_{x}j(u_{x}^{*})\Omega_{\psi}=p_{x}^{1/2}\Omega_{\phi},\qquad\sum_{x}k_{x}^{*}k_{x}=\supp(\psi).

(7.9)

In the Lemma,
(D\psi_{x}:D\psi)_{-i/2}
denotes the analytic continuation of the Connes cocycle.

Proof.

The analytic continuation of the Connes cocycle makes sense and satisfies

(D\psi_{x}:D\psi)_{-i/2}\Omega_{\psi}=\Omega_{\psi_{x}}=\Omega_{p_{x}u_{x}\psi u_{x}^{*}}=p_{x}^{1/2}u_{x}j(u_{x})\Omega_{\psi}.

This is shown in
[
35
, Lem. A.58]
in the context of Haagerup
L^{p}
-spaces (we apply the Lemma with
p=1
).
Applying
u_{x}^{*}j(u_{x}^{*})
to both sides shows the first equality in (
7.9
).
To see the second inequality, we consider
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
, we note that
\supp(k_{x}^{*}k_{x})\leq\supp(\psi)
.
Now let
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
. Then

\displaystyle\langle x^{\prime}\Omega_{\psi},\sum_{x}k_{x}^{*}k_{x}y^{\prime}\Omega_{\psi}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{x}\langle x^{\prime}k_{x}\Omega_{\psi},y^{\prime}k_{x}\Omega_{\psi}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{x}p_{x}\langle x^{\prime}j(u_{x})\Omega_{\phi},y^{\prime}j(u_{x})\Omega_{\phi}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{x}p_{x}\langle x^{\prime}u_{x}j(u_{x})\Omega_{\phi},y^{\prime}u_{x}j(u_{x})\Omega_{\phi}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{x}p_{x}\langle\Omega_{u_{x}\phi u_{x}},x^{\prime*}y^{\prime}\Omega_{u_{x}\phi u_{x}^{*}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\Big(\sum_{x}p_{x}\,u_{x}\phi u_{x}^{*}\Big)^{\prime}(x^{\prime*}y^{\prime})=\psi^{\prime}(x^{\prime*}y^{\prime})=\langle x^{\prime}\Omega_{\psi},y^{\prime}\Omega_{\psi}\rangle,

where we used the notation
\omega^{\prime}=\langle\Omega_{\omega},({\,\cdot\,})\Omega_{\omega}\rangle|_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}}
.
Thus, we have
\sum_{x}k_{x}^{*}k_{x}=\supp(\psi)
.
∎

Corollary 7.13
.

Under the assumptions of
lemma

7.12
, let
{\mathcal{M}}
act on a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
and let
\Psi,\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
be purifications of
\psi,\phi
.
Let
\Psi=w^{\prime}\Omega_{\psi}
and
\Phi=v^{\prime}\Omega_{\phi}
be the polar decompositions of
\Psi
and
\Phi
(see
corollary

3.22
).
Let
u_{x}^{\prime}=v^{\prime}j(\pi(u_{x}^{*}))w^{\prime*}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
, where
\pi
is the representation that puts
{\mathcal{M}}
in standard form.
Then

k_{x}u_{x}^{\prime}\Psi=p_{x}^{1/2}\Phi.

Proof of
theorem

7.10
.

The two implications are shown in
lemmas

7.11
and
7.13
, respectively.
∎

We now introduce a stabilized version of pure state LOCC transitions.
Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
be a bipartite system.
For unit vectors
\Psi,\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
, we say that
\Psi
can be transformed to
\Phi
with
approximate LOCC
, if for all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a
\Psi^{\prime}\approx_{\varepsilon}\Psi
that can be reached from
\Psi
with LOCC.
We denote this

\Psi\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathrm{LOCC}}}\Phi.

(7.10)

For states
\psi,\phi
on an arbitrary factor
{\mathcal{M}}
, we define majorization
\psi\preceq\phi
via
\psi\in\overline{\conv}\{u\phi u^{*}:u\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}})\}
.
By
theorem

3.3
, this is in accordance with the usual definition of majorization, whenever defined.

Theorem 7.14
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
be a factorial bipartite system and let
\Psi,\Phi
be unit vectors in
{\mathcal{H}}
.
Then

\Psi\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathrm{LOCC}}}\Phi\quad\iff\quad\psi_{A/B}\preceq\phi_{A/B}.

(7.11)

In particular, if
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are semifinite, this is equivalent to
\lambda_{\Psi}\preceq\lambda_{\Phi}
, where the Schmidt scales are taken with respect to a coupled pair of normal semifinite faithful traces
(\tau_{A},\tau_{B})
.

The majorization relation for the Schmidt scales is the usual one, i.e.,
\lambda_{\Psi}\preceq\lambda_{\Phi}
is defined as
\int_{0}^{t}\lambda_{\Psi}(s)\,ds\leq\int_{0}^{t}\lambda_{\Phi}(s)\,ds
for all
t>0
.

Proof of
theorem

7.14
.

If
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
is a general bipartite system, then the definition of approximate LOCC transitions and
theorem

7.10
directly imply

\Psi\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathrm{LOCC}}}\Phi\quad\iff\quad\psi_{A/B}\in\overline{\conv}\{u\phi_{A/B}u^{*}:u\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}}_{A/B})\}.

In the factorial case, the RHS is our definition of
\psi_{A/B}\preceq\phi_{A/B}
.
The statement about the Schmidt scales follows from
theorem

3.3
since
\lambda_{\Psi}=\lambda_{\psi_{A}}
and
\lambda_{\Phi}=\lambda_{\phi_{A}}
(see
section

6.5
).
∎

Remark 7.15
.

In
[
30
]
, only approximate LOCC transitions are considered.
However, the definition of approximate LOCC transitions above is similar but not the same as the one considered in
[
30
]
.
Their definition, which is arguably more natural, only asks that for all
\varepsilon>0
, there is an LOCC protocol taking the pure state
\omega_{\Psi}=\langle\Psi,({\,\cdot\,})\Psi\rangle
to a (possibly mixed) state that is
\varepsilon
-close to
\omega_{\Phi}
.
The data processing inequality show that our notion of an approximate LOCC transition implies theirs notion, but the converse is less clear.
However, for the case of semifinite factors, their version of Nielsen’s theorem
[
30
, Thm. 5.3]
shows that both notions are, in fact, equivalent.
In the type
{\mathrm{III}}
case, our notion of approximate LOCC transitions trivializes (see
section

7.5
) and, hence, so does their notion.
Thus, the two notions are, in fact, equivalent for factorial bipartite systems.

7.4
Pure state entanglement monotones

In this subsection, we note that the usual construction of pure state entanglement monotones based on Nielsen’s theorem
[
46
]
and monotones for majorization theory extends to the case of semifinite von Neumann algebraic bipartite systems.
The basics were already discussed in
section

6.5
, where we defined the Schmidt scale
\lambda_{\Psi}
in factorial semifinite bipartite systems.

We begin by defining entanglement monotones:
A pure state entanglement monotone for a bipartite system
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
is a scalar function
E
, defined on unit vectors
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
, such that

\Psi\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathrm{LOCC}}}\Phi\quad\implies\quad E(\Psi)\geq E(\Phi).

In
section

7.2
, we have seen that the Schmidt rank
r(\Psi)
of a factorial semifinite bipartite system is a monotone for approximate SLOCC transition.
Thus, it is, in particular, an entanglement monotone.

Proposition 7.16
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
be a factorial semifinite bipartite system, and let
\tau_{A/B}
be coupled normal semifinite faithful traces on
{\mathcal{M}}_{A/B}
.
For a convex non-decreasing function
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{+}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
and a unit vector
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
, define

E_{f}(\Psi):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}f(\lambda_{\Psi}(t))\,dt,

(7.12)

where
\lambda_{\Psi}
is the Schmidt scale of
\Psi
(see (
6.20
)).
Then the functions
E_{f}
with
f
as above form a complete family of entanglement monotones:

\Psi\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathrm{LOCC}}}\Phi\quad\iff\quad E_{f}(\Psi)\geq E_{f}(\Phi)\quad\forall f.

(7.13)

Proof.

By
theorem

7.14
, the approximate LOCC transition is equivalent to majorization
\lambda_{\Psi}\preceq\lambda_{\Phi}
of the Schmidt scales.
Thus, the claim follows from
theorem

3.3
.
∎

Corollary 7.17
.

The entanglement entropy and its
\alpha
-Renyi variants (see
section

6.5
) are entanglement monotones.

7.5
LOCC and the type classification of factors

In this subsection, we show that the minimal type of semifinite bipartite systems is in one-to-one correspondence with certain operational properties in entanglement theory.

Let us fix a bipartite system
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
. We make the following definitions:

•

A unit vector
\Phi
in
{\mathcal{K}}_{A}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{B}
can be
distilled
from a unit vector
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
if there is a unit vector
\tilde{\Psi}\in{\mathcal{H}}
such that

\Psi\otimes|0\rangle|0\rangle\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathrm{LOCC}}}\tilde{\Psi}\otimes\Phi,

for some unit vectors
|0\rangle\in{\mathcal{K}}_{A/B}
.

•

A unit vector
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
is
maximally entangled
if

\Psi\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathrm{LOCC}}}\Phi\qquad\forall\Phi,

where
\Phi
ranges over unit vectors in
{\mathcal{H}}
.

•

The
one-shot entanglement
of a unit vector
\Psi
is the maximal log-dimension at which a (finite-dimensional) maximally entangled state can be distilled from
\Psi
.
I.e. the one-shot entanglement is
\log_{2}n
, where
n
is the largest integer, such that
\Phi_{n}
can be distilled from
\Psi
, where
\Phi_{n}=n^{-1/2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}|j\rangle|j\rangle\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
.

•

LOCC trivializes
, if
\Psi\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathrm{LOCC}}}\Phi
for all pairs of unit vectors, or, equivalently, if all unit vectors are maximally entangled.

If the system is finite-dimensional, our definition of the one-shot entanglement reduces to the
min-entanglement entropy

E_{\infty}(\Psi)
, which is defined as the
-\log\lambda_{\max}(\Psi)
, where
\lambda_{\max}(\Psi)
denotes the largest Schmidt coefficient of
\Psi
.
45
45

45

This is a direct consequence of Nielsen’s theorem.

The main Theorem of this subsection is the following:

Theorem 7.18
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
be a factorial bipartite system.
Then the operational entanglement properties defined above only depend on the minimal type and are given by:

operational property/ minimal type

{\mathrm{I}}_{n}

{\mathrm{I}}_{\infty}

{\mathrm{II}}_{1}

{\mathrm{II}}_{\infty}

{\mathrm{III}}

one-shot entanglement

​​​
\leq
\log_{2}n

​
<
\infty

\infty

\infty

\infty

maximally entangled state

LOCC trivializes

(7.14)

Lemma 7.19
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
be a bipartite system of type
{\mathrm{III}}
factors.
If
\Psi,\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
are unit vectors, then
\Psi
can be transformed to
\Phi
with approximate LOCC.

Proof.

By
[
76
, Lem. 4.3]
(or
[
57
, Lem. 9.3]
), we have
\psi_{A}\in\overline{\conv}\{u\phi_{A}u^{*}:u\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}})\}
for all pairs
\psi_{A},\phi_{A}
of normal states on
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
.
Thus,
theorem

7.14
implies the claim.
∎

Lemma 7.20
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
be a factorial bipartite system. If
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are not of type
{\mathrm{I}}
, then every unit vector
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
has infinite one-shot distillable entanglement.
If
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are of type
{\mathrm{I}}
, no unit vector
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
has infinite one-shot entanglement.

Proof.

The type
{\mathrm{III}}
case follows from
lemma

7.19
and the fact that
{\mathcal{M}}_{j}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{j})
is of type
{\mathrm{III}}
whenever
{\mathcal{M}}_{j}
is,
j=A,B
.
We consider the type
{\mathrm{II}}
case.
We set
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}={\mathcal{M}}
, and pick coupled normal semifinite faithful traces
(\tau,\tau^{\prime})
on
{\mathcal{M}}
and
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
.
Clearly, it is sufficient to show that a sequence of vectors with unbounded entanglement entropy can be distilled from any given vector
\Psi
.
We let
\Phi_{n}
denote the maximally entangled state in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
.
By
theorem

7.14
, we have to show that for each
n
, there exists a unit vector
\tilde{\Psi}
such that

\lambda_{\Psi\otimes|0\rangle|0\rangle}=\lambda_{\Psi}\preceq\lambda_{\tilde{\Psi}\otimes\Phi_{n}}.

Since the marginal state of
\Phi_{n}
is the tracial state on
M_{n}({\mathbb{C}})
, we have
\lambda_{\Phi_{n}}(t)=n^{-1}1_{[0,n)}(t)
.
With this, it can readily be checked that

\lambda_{\tilde{\Psi}\otimes\Phi_{n}}(t)=\frac{1}{n}\lambda_{\tilde{\Psi}}\bigg(\frac{t}{n}\bigg).

We claim that, for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there is a unit vector
\tilde{\Psi}
with
\lambda_{\tilde{\Psi}}(t)=n\lambda_{\Psi}(nt)
.
By the above, this will show the claim.
Since
|\supp(n\lambda_{\psi}(n({\,\cdot\,})))|\leq|\supp(\lambda_{\psi})|\leq\tau(1)
, there is a normal state
\tilde{\psi}
on
{\mathcal{M}}
with spectral scale
\lambda_{\tilde{\psi}}=n\lambda_{\Psi}(nt)
(see
section

3.2.2
).
The trace coupling implies
\tau(\supp(\tilde{\psi}))\leq\tau(\supp(\psi))=\tau(\supp(\psi^{\prime}))
, where
\psi^{\prime}
denotes the
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
-marginal of
\Psi
).
Thus,
lemma

6.15
implies that there is a purification
\tilde{\Psi}\in{\mathcal{H}}
of
\tilde{\psi}
. Since
\lambda_{\tilde{\Psi}}=\lambda_{\tilde{\psi}}
, this finishes the proof.

Finiteness of the one-shot distillable entanglement for type
{\mathrm{I}}
was shown in
[
27
]
.
In fact,
[
17
]
shows the same statement also for mixed states and allows for a larger class of maps than LOCC.
∎

The proof above also shows the following:

Corollary 7.21
.

If
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
is a factorial semifinite bipartite system with
{\mathcal{M}}_{A/B}\neq{\mathbb{C}}
, there exist unit vectors
\Psi,\Phi
such that
\Psi
can be transformed to
\Phi
with approximate LOCC but not vice versa.

Proof of
theorem

7.18
.

We show the three rows of the table separately.

One-shot entanglement: For non-type
{\mathrm{I}}
factors, the statement is shown in
lemma

7.20
.
For systems with minimal type
{\mathrm{I}}_{n}
, the most entangled state that can be distilled is
\Phi_{n}
. Hence, the one-shot entanglement of every unit vector is
\leq\log_{2}n
.

Maximally entangled states:
In the type
{\mathrm{III}}
case, every unit vector is maximally entangled since LOCC trivializes.
We now consider the semifinite case. Without loss of generality, let
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
be the
“smaller”
of the two factors (see
section

6.4
) so that minimal type equals the type of
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
.
Thus, every normal state on
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
has a purification (see
section

6.4
).
Therefore,
theorem

7.14
implies that a unit vector
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
is maximally entangled if and only if its
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
-marginal state
\psi_{A}
is maximally mixed in the sense that
\psi_{A}\preceq\phi_{A}
for all
\phi_{A}\in\mathfrak{S}_{*}\hskip-0.6pt({\mathcal{M}}_{A})
.
By
theorem

3.3
, a semifinite factor has a maximally mixed state if and only if it is finite, i.e., of type
{\mathrm{I}}_{n}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, or of type
{\mathrm{II}}_{1}
.
This shows the claim.

LOCC trivialization: This is shown in
lemma

7.19
.
∎

Instead of the triviality of LOCC, one can use LOCC-embezzlement to distinguish types
{\mathrm{I}}
and
{\mathrm{II}}
from type
{\mathrm{III}}
.
This will be discussed in
section

8.1
(see
proposition

8.6
).

8
Strong forms of infinite entanglement

In this section, we study particularly strong entanglement phenomena.
The results we obtain here allow us to distinguish different von Neumann algebraic bipartite (or multipartite) systems with operational entanglement properties, even when both systems are infinitely entangled.

Section

8.1
is based on the article
[
3
]
and heavily relies on the results of
section

5
(except for
proposition

8.6
, which is taken from
[
4
]
).

Section

8.2
generalizes results from
[
6
]
, and
section

8.3
generalizes results from
[
3
]
.

8.1
Embezzlement of entanglement

Embezzlement of entanglement is a phenomenon in entanglement theory that was discovered by van Dam and Hayden in
[
47
]
.
The idea of embezzlement is that an entangled resource state admits the extraction of arbitrary entangled bipartite states with local unitaries so that the resource system returns to its initial state.
More precisely, let
\Psi
be the resource state and let us consider a finite-dimensional bipartite system in a state
\Phi_{1}
with little entanglement, e.g., a product state
\Phi_{1}=|0\rangle|0\rangle
.
The task is then to prepare a more entangled state
\Phi_{2}
with local unitaries
u_{A}
,
u_{B}
on the joint system and without disturbing the resource state
\Psi
, i.e.,

u_{A}u_{B}(\Psi\otimes\Phi_{1})=\Psi\otimes\Phi_{2}.

(8.1)

This appears paradoxical, since local unitaries cannot generate entanglement.
Indeed, if
\Psi
has finite one-shot entanglement, embezzlement is impossible.
However, if the resource state has infinite one-shot entanglement, it is less clear why embezzlement should be impossible.
Indeed, we know from logic paradoxes like Hilbert’s hotel
[
176
]
that the impossible can become possible in the presence of infinite resources.

Although embezzlement of entanglement is impossible with finite-dimensional resource systems, it is possible in an approximate form, where the resource state depends on the error.
Indeed, the discovery of
[
47
]
was that the sequence of bipartite states

\Psi_{n}=c_{n}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\alpha^{-1/2}\,|\alpha\rangle|\alpha\rangle\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n},

where
c_{n}=(\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\alpha^{-1})^{-1/2}
, admits approximate embezzlement of bipartite pure states of Schmidt rank
d
in the sense that

\lVert u_{A}u_{B}(\Psi_{n}\otimes|0\rangle|0\rangle)-\Psi_{n}\otimes\Phi\rVert\leq\varepsilon_{d,n},\qquad\varepsilon_{d,n}=\left(\frac{2\log d}{\log n}\right)^{1/2},

for suitable local unitaries
u_{A},u_{B}
.
46
46

46

This follows from their estimate
F(u_{A}u_{B}(\Psi_{n}\otimes|0\rangle|0\rangle),\Psi_{n}\otimes\Phi)^{1/2}\geq 1-\frac{\log(d)}{\log(n)}
and the Fuchs-van de Graaf inequalities (
4.29
).

Thus, if
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}
are bipartite pure states with Schmidt rank
d
, then

\lVert u_{A}u_{B}(\Psi_{n}\otimes\Phi_{1})-\Psi_{n}\otimes\Phi_{2}\rVert\leq 2\varepsilon_{d,n}

for suitable local unitaries.
A sequence of entangled states
(\Psi_{n})
with the above properties for some
\varepsilon_{d,n}
with
\lim_{n}\varepsilon_{d,n}\to 0
for all
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
is known as a
(universal) embezzling family

[
177
,
178
]
.
In finite-dimensional systems, it is impossible to perform any sort of embezzlement with an error of
\varepsilon=0
.
In fact, the same holds in infinite-dimensional bipartite systems
{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{A}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{B}
in the traditional, Hilbert space-based, framework of describing quantum systems
[
179
]
.
Exact embezzlement of specific entangled states
\Phi
has been shown to be possible with resource systems described by C*-algebras and local operations described by *-automorphisms
[
179
,
157
,
180
]
.
47
47

47

In
[
3
]
, it is shown that separability of the Hilbert space renders exact embezzlement of all bipartite pure states impossible.
Moreover, it is shown that exact embezzlement of all bipartite pure states is possible in certain non-separable settings.

We consider here embezzlement of entanglement with resource systems described by von Neumann algebras.
This approach was suggested in unpublished notes of Haagerup, Scholz, and Werner
[
8
]
.
As above, we restrict our attention to the bipartite case for now.

Definition 8.1
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
be a bipartite system. A vector
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
is
embezzling
if

\Psi\otimes\Phi_{1}\mathrel{\,\xleftrightarrow{\ \overline{\mathrm{LU}}\ }}\,\Psi\otimes\Phi_{2}

(8.2)

for all Hilbert spaces
{\mathcal{K}}_{A},{\mathcal{K}}_{B}
and all unit vectors
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{A}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{B}
, where LU is considered relative to the bipartite system
({\mathcal{M}}_{A}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{A})\otimes 1,{\mathcal{M}}_{B}\otimes 1\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{B}),{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{A}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{B})
.

As a consequence of
corollary

6.9
, we observe that a bipartite pure state
\Psi
is embezzling if and only if one, hence both, of its marginal states are universally catalytic (see
section

5
).

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
be a bipartite system and let
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
. The following are equivalent:

1.

\Psi
is embezzling;

2.

\psi_{A}
is a universally catalytic state on
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
;

3.

\psi_{B}
is a universally catalytic state on
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
.

This observation, although based on a more pedestrian argument, was already made in
[
8
]
.
On a factor
{\mathcal{M}}
, all universally catalytic states are approximately unitarily equivalent (see
corollary

5.3
). This implies:

Corollary 8.3
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
be a bipartite system.
If
\Psi_{1}\in{\mathcal{H}}
is embezzling and
\Psi_{2}\mathrel{\,\xleftrightarrow{\ \overline{\mathrm{LU}}\ }}\,\Psi_{1}
, then
\Psi_{2}
is embezzling as well.
If
{\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
factors on
{\mathcal{H}}
, then the converse holds:
If
\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2}
are embezzling vectors with the same norm, then
\Psi_{1}\mathrel{\,\xleftrightarrow{\ \overline{\mathrm{LU}}\ }}\,\Psi_{2}
.

As a consequence of
eq.

6.5
, we learn that the marginals
\psi_{A}
and
\psi_{B}
of a bipartite pure state
\Psi
are equally good or bad at the task of universal catalysis:

\kappa(\Psi):=\kappa(\psi_{A})=\kappa(\psi_{B}),\qquad\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}.

(8.3)

By
corollary

8.3
,
\kappa(\Psi)
quantifies the optimal performance of the state
\Psi
at the task of embezzling entanglement from it (see
remark

8.5
for a discussion of the analogous quantifier in fidelity).
This quantification is operational in terms of local distinguishability, i.e.,
\kappa(\Psi)
is precisely the worst-case error for the detection of whether the embezzlement of arbitrary states
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}
was successful.
As a consequence of (
8.3
), the worst worst-case error among all resource states of the bipartite system is
\kappa_{\min}({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}}):=\inf\,\kappa(\Psi)
, where the infimum is over unit vectors
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
, equals the corresponding local quantities:

\kappa_{\min}({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})=\kappa_{\min}({\mathcal{M}}_{A})=\kappa_{\min}({\mathcal{M}}_{B}).

(8.4)

Similarly, we have

\kappa_{\max}({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})=\kappa_{\max}({\mathcal{M}}_{A})=\kappa_{\max}({\mathcal{M}}_{B}),

(8.5)

where
\kappa_{\max}({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})=\sup\kappa(\Psi)
, where the supremum is over unit vectors
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
.

The above allows us to fully characterize the invariants
\kappa_{\min}
and
\kappa_{\max}
for bipartite systems of factors, based on
theorems

5.4
and
5.5
:

Theorem 8.4
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
be a bipartite system of factors.

1.

The value of
\kappa_{\min}({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
is either
0
or
2
.
The following are equivalent:

(a)

there is an embezzling vector
0\neq\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
;

(b)

\kappa_{\min}({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})=0
;

(c)

the flows of weights of
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and, therefore,
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
admit invariant normal states.

These equivalent properties hold for all factors of type
{\mathrm{III}}_{\lambda}
,
0<\lambda\leq 1
. They are false in the semifinite case.

2.

The value of
\kappa_{\max}({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
equals the diameter of the state space of
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
(and, therefore,
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
) up to unitary equivalence.
If
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and, therefore,
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are of type
{\mathrm{III}}
, this gives

\kappa_{\max}({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})=2\frac{1-\sqrt{\lambda}}{1+\sqrt{\lambda}}

(8.6)

where
\lambda
labels the subtype (i.e.,
\lambda
is such that
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and, hence,
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are of type
{\mathrm{III}}_{\lambda}
).

The first item also holds if
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and, therefore,
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are not factors but general von Neumann algebras.

Together with the results in
section

7.5
, this proves
C
in
section

2
.

Remark 8.5

(Quantiying embezzlement in fidelity)
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
be a bipartite system.
In the above, we quantify the capability of a unit vector
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
at the task of embezzling entanglement in terms of the local distinguishability in norm.
An alternative quantification, which is arguably more natural in the context of bipartite pure state entanglement theory, would be in terms of the fidelity

\vartheta(\Psi)=\adjustlimits{\inf}_{\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}}{\sup}_{u_{A},u_{B}}\,\big|\langle\Psi\otimes\Phi_{1},u_{A}u_{B}(\Psi\otimes\Phi_{2})\rangle\big|^{2},

(8.7)

where
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}
are unit vectors in
{\mathcal{K}}_{A}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{B}
for infinite separable Hilbert spaces
{\mathcal{K}}_{A},{\mathcal{K}}_{B}
, and where
u_{A/B}
are local unitaries from the bipartite system
({\mathcal{M}}_{A}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{A})\otimes 1,{\mathcal{M}}_{B}\otimes 1\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{K}}_{B}),{\mathcal{H}}_{A}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{A}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}_{B})
.
This quantification has the benefit that it measures directly how close the initial and final bipartite pure states are.
For a normal state
\psi
on a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
, let us denote by
\vartheta(\psi)
the analog of
\kappa(\psi)
in fidelity:

\vartheta(\psi)=\adjustlimits{\inf}_{\phi_{1},\phi_{2}}{\sup}_{u}F(u(\psi\otimes\phi_{1}))u^{*},\psi\otimes\phi_{2}),

(8.8)

where the infimum is over pairs of normal states on a type
{\mathrm{I}}_{\infty}
factor and the supremum is over unitaries in
{\mathcal{M}}
.
By
proposition

6.8
, we then have

\vartheta(\Psi)=\vartheta(\psi_{A})=\vartheta(\psi_{B}).

(8.9)

It is a consequence of the Fuchs-van de Graaf inequalities (
4.29
) that
\vartheta(\Psi)\approx 1
if and only if
\kappa(\Psi)\approx 0
is embezzling and that
\vartheta(\Psi)\approx 0
if and only if
\kappa(\Psi)\approx 2
.
Thus, the operational quantifications of embezzlement in terms of
\kappa
and
\vartheta
agree in the two extremal cases.
As with
\kappa
, we can define
\vartheta_{\max}
and
\vartheta_{\min}
, which only depend on the bipartite system.
The above and our results on
\kappa
imply that
\vartheta_{\max}
is either
0
or
1
with the value
0
(resp.
1
) occurring if and only if
\kappa_{\min}=2
(resp.
0
).
It would be most interesting to work out the value of
\vartheta_{\min}
for facts of type
{\mathrm{III}}_{\lambda}
.
The problem that keeps us from applying our methods to
\vartheta_{\min}
is that the machinery of Haagerup-Størmer spectral states is, at this point, only available for the norm distance of unitary orbits.
Extending this machinery to the fidelity and possibly other quantum divergences (see
section

4.10
) would be of independent interest.

Finally, we comment on a variant of embezzlement of entanglement, where the agents are allowed to communicate classically.
We refer to this as
LOCC-embezzlement
.
LOCC-embezzlement in finite-dimensional systems was studied in
[
181
]
.
We show that LOCC-embezzlement is possible if and only if the bipartite system is type
{\mathrm{III}}
, in which case every unit vector is an LOCC-embezzling state:

Proposition 8.6
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
be a factorial bipartite system, and let
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
be a unit vector.
The following are equivalent:

1.

For all unit vectors
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
, we have

\Psi\otimes\Phi_{1}\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathrm{LOCC}}}\Psi\otimes\Phi_{2},

(8.10)

2.

{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are of type
{\mathrm{III}}
.

Proof.

Suppose
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are type
{\mathrm{III}}
.
Then the same is true for
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}\otimes M_{n}({\mathbb{C}})
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}\otimes M_{n}({\mathbb{C}})
.
Hence,
lemma

7.19
implies (
8.10
) for all
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}
if
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
.

To see the converse, we assume that
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are semifinite and that (
8.10
) holds for all
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}
.
In particular, (
8.10
) holds for
\Phi_{1}=|0\rangle|0\rangle
and
\Phi_{2}=n^{-1/2}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}|j\rangle|j\rangle
.
Following the arguments in the proof of
lemma

7.20
, this implies
\lambda_{\Psi}\preceq\lambda_{\Psi\otimes\Phi_{n}}
,
\lambda_{\Psi\otimes\Phi_{n}}(t)=n^{-1}\lambda_{\Psi}(t/n)
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Since the converse majorization holds trivially, this shows

\lambda_{\Psi}(t)=\frac{1}{n}\lambda_{\Psi}\left(\frac{t}{n}\right).

Using this equation for two numbers
n,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we get

\lambda_{\Psi}(t)=\frac{m}{n}\lambda_{\Psi}\left(\frac{mt}{n}\right).

Thus, right-continuity of the Schmidt spectral scale, which follows from right-continuity of the spectral scale of states semifinite factors (see
section

3.2.2
), implies
\lambda_{\Psi}(t)=c\lambda_{\Psi}(ct)
for all
c>0
.
Hence,
\lambda_{\Psi}(t)=t^{-1}\lambda_{\Psi}(1)
, which contradicts
\lambda_{\Psi}\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{+})
, or
\lambda_{\Psi}=0
, which contradicts
\Psi\neq 0
.
∎

8.2
LU transitive multipartite systems

In the following, we consider multipartite systems in which all pure states are approximately equivalent up to local unitaries (approximately LU equivalent).
We first consider such systems abstractly and fully characterize the bipartite case.
Afterward, we use a multipartite embezzling family from
[
178
]
to construct nontrivial examples.
Consider a collection
({\mathcal{M}}_{x})_{x=1}^{N}
,
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, of pairwise commuting von Neumann algebras on a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
.
We say that a pair of unit vectors
\Psi,\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
is
approximately LU equivalent
, denoted

\Psi\mathrel{\,\xleftrightarrow{\ \overline{\mathrm{LU}}\ }}\,\Phi,

(8.11)

if for all
\varepsilon>0
, there exist unitaries
u_{x}\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}}_{x})
such that
\lVert\Psi-\prod_{x}u_{x}\Phi\rVert<\varepsilon
.
We say that the collection
({\mathcal{M}}_{x})_{x=1}^{N}
is
LU transitive
if all pairs of unit vectors are approximately LU equivalent.
In the following, we consider a collection
({\mathcal{M}}_{x})_{x=1}^{N}
as above, which we assume to be
nontrivial
in the sense that
N\geq 2
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{x}\neq{\mathbb{C}}1
,
x=1,\ldots N
.

Lemma 8.7
.

If
({\mathcal{M}}_{x})_{x=1}^{N}
is LU transitive, then each
{\mathcal{M}}_{x}
is a type
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}
factor.

Proof.

Since the center
Z({\mathcal{M}}_{x})
commutes with the unitary groups
{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{M}}_{y})
for all
x,y
, all pairs of approximately LU equivalent unit vectors induce the same states on each of the centers
Z({\mathcal{M}}_{x})
.
It is easy to see that if a von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}
acts on a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
, then
{\mathcal{H}}
contains unit vectors that induce different states on the center
Z({\mathcal{M}})
, unless the center is trivial.
Thus, LU transitivity implies that each
{\mathcal{M}}_{x}
is a factor.
To see that each
{\mathcal{M}}_{x}
is of type
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}
, we note that LU transitivity implies that all pairs of normal states
\psi,\phi
on
{\mathcal{M}}_{x}
that admit purifications in
{\mathcal{H}}
are approximately unitarily equivalent.
This is clearly false for semifinite factors other than
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
. If
{\mathcal{M}}_{x}={\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
, we have
{\mathcal{M}}_{y}={\mathbb{C}}1
for
y\neq x
, which we excluded. Thus, each
{\mathcal{M}}
is a type
{\mathrm{III}}
factor.
Since type
{\mathrm{III}}
factors are always in standard representation (
lemma

6.5
), we know that all normal states on
{\mathcal{M}}
have purifications in
{\mathcal{H}}
(see
section

3.3.1
).
Thus, the assumption of LU transitivity implies that all normal states on
{\mathcal{M}}
are approximately unitarily equivalent, which is the case if and only if
{\mathcal{M}}
is a type
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}
factor
[
182
]
(see
section

3.1.2
).
∎

Proposition 8.8
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B})
be a pair of commuting von Neumann algebras on
{\mathcal{H}}
.
Assume that
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}\neq{\mathbb{C}}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}\neq{\mathbb{C}}
.
The following are equivalent:

1.

({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B})
are LU transitive,

2.

{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are type
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}
factors in Haag duality:
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}={\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

1

\Rightarrow

2
:
Haag duality
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}={\mathcal{M}}_{B}^{\prime}
follows from
theorem

6.19
.
The type
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}
factor property is shown in
lemma

8.7
.

2

\Rightarrow

1
:
Let
\Psi,\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
be unit vectors. By
corollary

6.9
,
\Psi
and
\Phi
are approximately LU equivalent if and only if the marginal states
\psi_{A}
and
\phi_{A}
on
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
are approximately unitarily equivalent.
This is the case since
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
is a type
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}
factor
[
182
]
(see
section

3.1.2
).
∎

In the following, we give a construction of nontrivial LU transitive
N
-partite systems based on the embezzling family of Leung, Toner and Watrous (LTW)
[
178
]
, which is the only known example of a multipartite embezzling family, and the idea of ITPFI multipartite systems discussed in
section

4.9.3
.

Proposition 8.9
.

For each
2\leq N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists an LU transitive collection
({\mathcal{M}}_{x})_{x=1}^{N}
of
N
pairwise commuting type
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}
factors on a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
.

Proof.

We use the notation from
[
6
]
to which we refer for details on the LTW construction.
The construction yields a sequence of unit vectors
\Omega_{k}\in({\mathbb{C}}^{D_{k}})^{\otimes N}
in
N
-partite systems with local dimension
D_{k}
,
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, which have the following embezzlement property:
For all
d
, unit vectors
\Psi,\Phi
in
({\mathbb{C}}^{d})^{\otimes N}
and
\varepsilon>0
, there exists some index
k
and a local unitary
u=\prod_{x=1}^{N}u_{x}
on
({\mathbb{C}}^{D_{k}})^{\otimes N}
such that

\lVert\Omega_{k}\otimes\Psi-u(\Omega_{k}\otimes\Phi)\rVert<\varepsilon.

(8.12)

The LTW family is the sequence of tensor products
\Omega^{(n)}=\otimes_{k=1}^{n}\Omega_{k}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
By (
8.12
), the sequence
\Omega^{(n)}
can be used to embezzle arbitrary
N
-partite state up to arbitrarily small error.
In the following, we will use no other property of the LTW family than the embezzling property (
8.12
).
The tensor product form of the sequence
\Omega^{(n)}
allows us to take the limit
n\to\infty
.
As in
section

4.9.3
, we consider the restricted infinite Hilbert space tensor product

{\mathcal{H}}=\bigotimes_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\,\big(({\mathbb{C}}^{D_{k}})^{\otimes N};\Omega_{k}\big)

(8.13)

on which we have
N
commuting factors
{\mathcal{M}}_{1},\ldots{\mathcal{M}}_{N}\subset{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}})
corresponding to the
N
agents.
By construction, the vector
\Omega=\otimes_{k=1}^{\infty}\Omega_{k}
is embezzling in the sense that for all
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and all unit vectors
\Psi,\Phi\in({\mathbb{C}}^{d})^{\otimes N}
, we have

\Omega\otimes\Psi\mathrel{\,\xleftrightarrow{\ \overline{\mathrm{LU}}\ }}\,\Omega\otimes\Phi,

(8.14)

where the local unitaries are taken from the factors
{\mathcal{M}}_{x}\otimes(1^{\otimes x-1}\otimes M_{D_{k}}({\mathbb{C}})\otimes 1^{\otimes N-x})
on
{\mathcal{H}}\otimes({\mathbb{C}}^{d})^{\otimes N}
.
For each
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we can consider the
tail
Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}_{\geq k}
formed as in (
8.13
) but restricting to integers
n\geq k
.
On the tail Hilbert space, we have ‘tail’ factors
{\mathcal{M}}_{x,\geq k}
with
{\mathcal{M}}_{x}\cong M_{D_{1}}({\mathbb{C}})\otimes\ldots\otimes M_{d_{k-1}}({\mathbb{C}})\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{x,\geq k}
.
Since (
8.12
) requires arbitrarily large indices for small errors
\varepsilon>0
, the ‘tail’ sequence
\{\Omega_{l}\}_{l\geq k}
inherits the approximate embezzlement property.
Thus, the unit vector
\Omega_{\geq k}=\otimes_{l\geq k}\Omega_{l}
in
{\mathcal{H}}_{\geq k}
is an embezzling vector in the sense of (
8.14
).
We use this fact to show that the multipartite system
({\mathcal{M}}_{x})_{x=1}^{N}
on
{\mathcal{H}}
is LU transitive.
Let
\Psi,\Phi\in{\mathcal{H}}
and
\varepsilon>0
be given.
Since
{\mathcal{H}}
is a restricted infinite tensor product, there are an index
k
and unit vectors
\Psi_{<k},\Phi_{<k}\in\bigotimes_{l=1}^{k-1}({\mathbb{C}}^{D_{l}})^{\otimes N}
such that

\Psi\approx_{\varepsilon}\Psi_{<k}\otimes\Omega_{\geq k},\qquad\Phi\approx_{\varepsilon}\Phi_{<k}\otimes\Omega_{\geq k}.

The fact that
\Omega_{\geq k}
is embezzling now implies

\Psi_{<k}\otimes\Omega_{\geq k}\mathrel{\,\xleftrightarrow{\ \overline{\mathrm{LU}}\ }}\,\Phi_{<k}\otimes\Omega_{\geq k}.

Since this works for all
\varepsilon>0
, it follows that
\Psi
and
\Phi
are approximately LU equivalent.
∎

8.3
Universal embezzlers

A
universal embezzler
is a system in which all pure states are embezzling states.
We begin by considering multipartite systems, showing that every LU transitive system is a multipartite embezzler.
Afterward, we focus on the bipartite case, where we show that a bipartite system is a universal embezzler if and only if it is of type
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}
.
As a consequence, we know that (bipartite) universal embezzlers exist in well-studied physical models, e.g., in conformal field theory
[
18
]
, relativistic quantum field theory
[
19
,
2
]
, critical spin chains
[
17
,
5
]
, and critical fermion chains
[
5
]
.

Consider a nontrivial (see
section

8.2
) collection
({\mathcal{M}}_{x})_{x=1}^{N}
of commuting von Neumann algebras on a Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}
.
A unit vector
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
is
embezzling
if for all
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
and all pairs of unit vectors
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}\in({\mathbb{C}}^{d})^{\otimes N}
, the vectors
\Psi\otimes\Phi_{1}
and
\Psi\otimes\Phi_{2}
are approximately LU equivalent with respect to joint the
N
-partite system on
{\mathcal{H}}\otimes({\mathbb{C}}^{d})^{\otimes N}
.
The collection
({\mathcal{M}}_{x})_{x=1}^{N}
is a
universal embezzler
if every unit vector
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
is embezzling.

Lemma 8.10
.

If
({\mathcal{M}}_{x})_{x=1}^{N}
is LU transitive and nontrivial, then it is a universal embezzler.

Proof.

By
lemma

8.7
, each
{\mathcal{M}}_{x}
is an infinite factor.
Thus, for each
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we can find an
{\mathcal{M}}_{x}
-unitary
u_{x}:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
.
Thus, if
({\mathcal{N}}_{x})_{x=1}^{N}
denotes an finite dimensional
N
-partite system with Hilbert space
{\mathcal{K}}=({\mathbb{C}}^{d})^{\otimes N}
, then there is a local product
u=\prod_{x}u_{x}:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}\otimes({\mathbb{C}}^{d})^{\otimes N}
with
u{\mathcal{M}}_{x}u^{*}={\mathcal{M}}_{x}\otimes{\mathcal{N}}_{x}
.
Thus,
({\mathcal{M}}_{x}\otimes{\mathcal{N}}_{x})_{x=1}^{N}
on
{\mathcal{H}}\otimes({\mathbb{C}}^{d})^{\otimes N}
is LU transitive as well.
Consequently, if
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}\in({\mathbb{C}}^{d})^{\otimes N}
are unit vectors, then
\Psi\otimes\Phi_{1}
and
\Psi\otimes\Phi_{2}
are approximately LU equivalent.
∎

As a direct consequence of
lemmas

8.10
and
8.9
, we obtain the existence of multipartite universal embezzlers:

Corollary 8.11
.

For each
N\geq 2
, there exists a collection
({\mathcal{M}}_{x})_{x=1}^{N}
of pairwise commuting factors, which is a universal embezzler.

The converse to
lemma

8.10
is false.
Indeed, LU transitivity requires that each
{\mathcal{M}}_{x}
is a factor, which is not required for universal embezzlers.
This follows from the following structure result, which was conjectured in
[
8
]
, describing those bipartite systems (this assumes Haag duality) that are universal embezzlers.

Proposition 8.12
.

A bipartite system
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B},{\mathcal{H}})
is a universal embezzler if and only if
{\mathcal{M}}_{A}
and, therefore,
{\mathcal{M}}_{B}
are von Neumann algebras of type
{\mathrm{III}}_{1}
.

Proof.

Recall that a unit vector
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
is embezzling if and only if
\kappa(\Psi)=0
(see
section

8.1
). Thus,
({\mathcal{M}}_{A},{\mathcal{M}}_{B})
is a universal embezzler if and only if
\kappa(\Psi)=0
for all
\Psi\in{\mathcal{H}}
.
Thus, by (
8.3
), the assertion follows directly from
proposition

5.6
, which is the corresponding statement about catalytic states on a single von Neumann algebra.
∎

Propositions

8.12
and
8.8
imply
D
in
section

2
.

Remark 8.13
.

In the bipartite case, we know that LU transitivity implies Haag duality (see
proposition

8.8
).
It is an interesting open question whether bipartite universal embezzlers necessarily satisfy Haag duality.

Remark 8.14
.

The proof of
proposition

8.9
shows the following partial converse to
lemma

8.10
:
If
({\mathcal{M}}_{x})_{x=1}^{N}
is an ITPFI multipartite system (see
section

4.9.3
) on
{\mathcal{H}}
, which is a universal embezzler, then
({\mathcal{M}}_{x})_{x=1}^{N}
is LU transitive.
It would be pleasing to get rid of the ITPFI assumption.
